# Kalaignar's Poems

## கலைஞரின் கவிதைகள் — English Translation

<!-- Reader-facing assembly from reviewed Phase 4 translation batches 01–18. -->

## Item 1 — Give Me Your Heart, Anna

<!-- scan 18 -->

Give Me Your Heart,
Anna

<!-- scan 19 -->

<!-- scan 20 -->

Give Me Your Heart,
Anna

(A tearful poetic tribute offered by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi to Perarignar Anna
on Chennai Radio on 9 February 1969.)

More tender than the tenderness of a flower-petal,
a sacred heart — a loving heart —
a mother's heart that gathers one into its embrace! His
flower-petals speak Tamil —
even the three fruits, mango, jackfruit and banana, would be defeated —
his eye-flowers become spear, become sword
if any harm should come to Mother Tamil!

Though his foot-flowers wither, his arduous journey will not stop;
his hand-flowers bind together the younger brothers, the Kazhagam!
Those very flowers forgive enemies
and make their heads bow in shame like ripened paddy!
Let the flower of democracy sway,
let the flower of social equality spread fragrance,
let the mother tongue Tamil itself become the grove of life —
the breeze that comes dancing,

<!-- scan 21 -->

O flower-crown sought by all! O crown of fame! Amid the heaps
of greetings that come seeking you — each day
I shall fly as the bee that comes singing;
for you I shall give up myself; the great leader
celebrated in the song sung by
my one crore Tamil youth....

Listen, as I sing the greatness
of that man of action and valour, who made
the dear lives of still-young youth swell
with Tamil ardour packed into the drum!
The wounds made by the spear that lodged and burned,
he soothed with the healing heat called love,
and with his red-fruit lips applied medicine —
listen, as I sing that grace!

Where is the sword in the broad battle-hand; where the jewelled chest?
Where, where are the shoulders of the patron
who would never turn his back, whoever came at the battlefront —
listen to how he asked the younger brothers!
Listen!

“While Kanchi stands, Kalinga reels —
O love-dazed women, Kazhar-chēnni;
while Kanchi stands, Kalinga is lost —
open, that the field-war may be sung!”

To wake the love-bewildered women from sleep,
he set himself to action and sang —
Kalap-parani... *Kalingathu Parani*,
a praise-parani of immeasurable valour — I,
raising a poetic parani, sang a Kanchipuram Parani;
today I stand singing
a parani of anguish...!

<!-- scan 22 -->

As meaning in poetry, as sweetness in sugarcane,
as light in the sunbeam, as flower in the grove,
as coolness in moonlight, as wealth upon the earth,
as language murmured by a cascading waterfall,
as music mounting the wandering wind,

as foam dancing on the wave-rising sea —
the poet-leader whose gathered art breathes fragrance,
Anna — as I sing his sacred glory,
alas, the tears the Earth-maiden pours!
A flood!
A flood!
A mighty flood!

In nineteen hundred and thirty-eight,
if some sowed the seed for a language revolution in the motherland,
then under the peerless leadership of
the hero of Erode,
our Anna entered the field, clad in Kanchi!

For two or three months he withered in prison,
giving Tamil as the supporting staff,
and by those rare three letters became Anna....
That day, back in fifty-two,
in Chennai I told you —
do you remember?

There is a special glory in three letters,
the fragrance of Muttamil is there...
Three kings, three flags, three fruits —
the Tamils made the three drums resound; the Tamil
`வாழ்வு` — life — they lived has three letters; the
`அன்பு` — love — that is the foundation of that life has three letters...
the `அறிவு` — knowledge — that stands beside love has three letters;

<!-- scan 23 -->

`காதல்` — love — rising among the wise has three letters...
the fierce `வீரம்` — valour — praised by lovers has three letters...
the `களம்` — field — where valour is made to grow has three letters...
the `வெற்றி` — victory — seen upon entering the field has three letters;
and the peace-filled `அண்ணா` — Anna —
who urges us all toward that victory has three letters —
that is what I told you to know!

Tamil thunder to all eight directions,
a rain of words everywhere,
asking for plans so those without means, those without place,
may live —
he circles like a bee, mounting the stage!

If Anna writes,
every paper, every home is Tamil,
the whole land.... the fragrance of newness is there....
“Hey, younger brother — pick up the pen....!”
“Do not sharpen the knife; sharpen your intellect.”
A treasure-chest of thought, a casket of camphor!

“A corpse upon a tree-branch!”
“A spear in a seared wound!”
Could we ever forget those heart-winning phrases?
“On the roadside, the unemployed —
strange thoughts in the hearts of the unemployed —
O King! That is the sign of the times!”
Who but Anna could have such a beautiful gait?
A gait of reason!

“The line rose; the hill fell” —
is that not an editorial Tamil Nadu will never forget!
“If this army loses, which army will win?”
“He who has a younger brother fears no army”....

<!-- scan 24 -->

Even if *Manonmaniam* is the work
that uttered incomparable lines —
was it not Anna who inscribed them in our hearts!
“Even jasmine from the rival's garden has fragrance” —
what beauty, teaching political culture!
“Let us forget — let us forgive” —
what dignity, bearing an opponent's abuse!
Who taught us this?
As one builds a wall and then paints a picture,
Anna said that politics should be conducted
with cultivated grace —
and if, forgetting that,
four or five men sprang about,
Anna saw them and laughed!
Because one mother's womb
could not bear giving birth to everyone,
“Younger brothers born of separate mothers!” he called them,
showing love like fruit-sweetness, sugar-candy, honey-syrup,
and embracing them like a bee settling upon a dewy flower.
The master of speech, Sethu Pillai,
and Somasundara Bharathi — in the battle of words
he made them swoon — poor men; he caught them fast!
The Justice Party, behind the high walls of a fortress —
by his principle that it must not remain
a party of the moneyed caste —
he brought it out into the street!
“Cast away the titles given by the white men,”
he moved the resolution at the Salem conference;
unable to resist
Anna's warlike bearing,

<!-- scan 25 -->

those without principle fell,
like blind men without staffs!
Valiant Anna brought forward
the resolution changing the name to Dravidar Kazhagam
at a conference crowded with the brave!
When Annal Gandhi, following the path of virtue,
removed British rule,
Anna rejected Periyar's declaration calling it a day of sorrow
and began his battle!

A day of joy! A sweet day!
Indians reduced to bone and skin,
by beloved Gandhi's grace today
rose, cutting through the fetter of slavery!
“Let us lift the drum of freedom and make it roar” —
that is what Anna said, that very day....

From that day, in the Anna — Ayya — relationship,
a scratch appeared;
in forty-nine it became a separation,
and like a full moon tearing through cloud and emerging,
he made the Munnetra Kazhagam blossom!

Unable to live together with the Leader,
when he came out with the majority of comrades,
our steadfast Anna said it that day.
It feels as though he said it only yesterday!
“There is no leader for the Munnetra Kazhagam —
my Leader's chair will remain empty! In it,

<!-- scan 26 -->

the Leader himself will come and sit someday....
Until then I shall wait,” he said.

Like a flower garden in bloom
shedding morning dew,
those who had waited and heard the meeting
shed tears.
“Drops of tears! Jewels of the nation's eyes!” he called,
and marshalled them to shed red blood!
Has anyone ever heard it?
Has the world ever told it?
Within eighteen years, a movement,
like a tiger crouching before its leap — the story of its coming to rule?
Not even two years had passed since my Anna took office —
yet he set about so many distinguished deeds!
He undertook the effort
to raise all the rich-field farmers
and heap a beautiful life before them.
The illustrious President who came
to inaugurate the World Tamil Conference said,
“I saw an incomparable sight; I saw grandeur;
never in my life have I seen its like,”
and the town itself acclaimed Anna's fame!
Andhra's Brahmananda Reddy too —
“Ah! You alone are the true Gandhian,”
he worshipfully praised him — not even a year had passed!
“You are making prohibition more rigorous; in many states
they have washed their hands of it and tied up the pot;
you alone are the offspring of the Mahatma,” he said.
If Giri means mountain — is it not a mountain among
those trees!
Our Anna became the cloud embracing the mountain!

<!-- scan 27 -->

This is the explanation of V. V. Giri's greeting.

International scholars stood in praise — that day,
those who had stood as peaks of the South:
what did they say
of Anna?
The Pallavaram savant of ancient Tamil
stood spellbound by Anna's Tamil speech —
a thousand words of praise from that Maraimalai.
Pasumalai Bharathi — Pandiyan in bearing,
whom the whole land calls Navalar — he said:
“To guard the Tamil land felled by men of crookedness,
we have found a leader without stain in his heart,”
and told Anna's greatness that day!

Two thousand years ago, as young as she is today,
our Mother Tamil,
to live for countless crores of years,
gave birth to poets who made thousands of books; yet
our Mother, goddess of the arts,
bore Valluvar as her foremost son.

If flower — it is the lotus;
if king — it is Karikalan;
if book — it is the *Tirukkural* —
he gave that book of virtue,
praised in such words;
all humankind rejoiced!
Along the mountain-edge that touches the sky —
rising within and beyond our sight —
like a poisonous tree, in the cultivated life of Tamils,
alas, doctrines wounded in the back began to blaze!

<!-- scan 28 -->

On Tamil soil where the Kural rang out without seeing castes,
midway came crores upon crores of castes!

Shrines fragrant with knowledge disappeared,
and temples housing a thousand gods took their place.
Creatures with only appetite of mouth, with no feeling for language —
whether Tamil died or lived, what did it matter to them?
On the great land where the path of virtue had stood as the goal,
they sowed other paths; they heaped up errors.
Like a field gone without rain, the Tamil mind was changed;
envy, `avar`, anger, hurtful speech — without those four,
some Tamils went about as though no work could be done!
“For those without wealth, there is no world,” they said,
and in a land that had amassed wealth and flourished — today
darkness gathered and distress planted its flag.
Mother Tamil withered; Mother Tamil sang a song of sorrow;
Mother Tamil danced in burning fire...
She ran... she ran... no path could be found!

Leaving the town of earth, he lives in the town of fame —
the great sovereign of poetry's city, the master of the Kural!
She went to him and said, “Dear son!
I have brought one request.”
“What is it, Mother?” asked the Kural-master.
“In Tamil Nadu, where gold comes wherever one digs,
you must be born again,” she said.
“To dig for gold?” he asked.
“To make the minds and lives of Tamils
golden,” she said.
“What has happened now?” he asked.
She said danger threatened the mountain-like language;
he said the poor had no hut to go to;
not knowing how to raise the rod swiftly and cast it gently,

<!-- scan 29 -->

“the killing staff has risen high,” she said; and told him too
the idle stories told by calves in intellect.

Wiping the eyes that had wept,
sweet-speaking Valluvar asked,
“Mother, where shall I be born?”
Kissing the crown of the worshipful son's head,
with arms like hanging banyan roots, she held him close.
“A ploughed field must not dry for want of seedlings —
not from now on,” she thought;
her whole heart leapt full with joy
like a farmer whose mind is glad.
“To pour gruel into the dried stomach —
O sugar-candy! O honey-syrup! O Tirukkural!
be born in Kanchi!” she said.

He was born as our Anna;
as a king of reason.
As the breeze from Pothigai Hill,
as the meaning of a hymn
that flowers into song,
as the war-song of the ancient Tamils,
as the fresh fragrance of Tamil consciousness,
Anna rose into birth — like
a ray risen in the eastern sky —
and to sing his fame,
I too, the younger brother he nurtured,
taking the Tamil he gave me,
have come here; this is the truth —
they call him Leader, they call him philosopher,
they call him actor, they call him king of drama,
they say he possessed savour-rich power of speech and writing.

<!-- scan 30 -->

They call him human, they call him a jewel, they call him
minister of the State;
they call him mother, guardian of noble speech,
they call him politician — and those who have no time
to say each one separately — let them, in their hearts,
call him by the one word “Anna” —
that is why his mother
gave him the name.

To honour that clan of mothers:
a statue for Avvai,
a statue for Kannagi who nurtured virtue,
a statue for Bharathi of the unbending heart,
a statue for Bharathidasan of the unbowed crown,
a statue for Veeramamunivar,
a statue for Caldwell and Pope,
a statue for Kambar,
a statue for the valiant Tamil who steered the ship,
a statue for Tiruvalluvar, to spread the Kural with love to all eight directions.
Because he placed ten statues — so the world might know
Anna's attachment to Tamil — when
a statue for that Anna himself
was placed in Chennai,
only the raised index finger
stood pointing...
We thought our Anna was issuing an order.
Alas — today alone do we understand
that the one finger meant,
“I shall live only one more year!”
Our Anna... king of hearts....
“You have a younger brother who fears no army,”
you told us;

<!-- scan 31 -->

why did you leave us and continue the great journey....?
We grew in the warmth of the light of your eyes!
Why did you turn all our eyes into pools?
We thought you were our shade; but you have gone
beneath the sea-land seeking shade — is that justice?
Not every pearl beneath the sea is a pearl;
are you sleeping on the shore saying, “I alone am the good pearl, my boy”?
Why did you roll up the tongue that poured out music?
With a movement of your finger,
you made wonders in the world of letters;
why did you fold that finger away?
I have seen the great beauty of you thinking with eyes closed;
today what cruelty is this earth closing over you, barring us from seeing you!
You brought cruelty to an end; why did you go,
leaving us to cruelty?
You said we needed a heart that could bear anything;
where, Anna, is the heart in us to bear even this!
Enough of taking the sea breeze on the shore, Anna!
Rise, our Anna!
You will not come; you will not come;
we know nature's conspiracy, Anna... until
I come seeking the place where you are,
lend me your heart, Anna....
When I come, I shall bring it with me in my hands
and place it at your flower-feet, Anna......

[Decorative printer's ornament centered below the closing verse]

<!-- scan 32 -->

## இனமான ஏந்தல்கள் — Bearers of Dignity

<!-- anthology divider: Tamil = இனமான ஏந்தல்கள் -->

## Item 2 — The Tale of the Southerner

<!-- scan 34 -->

**The Tale of the Southerner**

Short beard; squat beard; long beard; white beard;
with beards of every kind like these...
a crowd of pūsuras, spreading out a bazaar of Puranas,
tasting the sour old grove, getting drunk and dancing.
At the edge of a realm once without stain or blemish,
they slaughtered cows, goats and human beings,
heaping mounds of corpses for sacrifice!
By an Aryan invasion?... No...
by a swaying waist... a sidelong glance... by the sages'
wine of pleasure — in the southern land gone dark —
like a lamp newly lit
rose the king of Lanka!
If I begin to tell his history... every muscle dances, ayyo!
The lord of southern Lanka whose fame reached all eight directions,
forefather of the Dravidians!
Our grandfather who held fast to love of ancient Tamil.
The island of Lanka like the pupil within the eye,
the deep seas its dark iris! The great warrior
who brought renown to Tamil soil.
A lion in form, a heart of gold! He lived
with none his equal in the mode of the yāzh —
a man of sacred Jaffna, a mountain-Tamilian!

<!-- scan 35 -->

His companion, flower-tressed where bees would settle,
Mandodari — jewel of womankind — a speaker
whose words could pluck an answering veena!... Ravana's yāzh-music
was defeated only before the fruit-sweet speech
of his chaste wife in the bedchamber!
There are eyelid-doors to guard beautiful eyes;
so was Ravana to Lanka!
And she to him!

In the forests, along our border-lines, Aryans
sought sacrifice for their god; saying
such cruelty must not be, he drew his sword!
Invitations from forest hermitages kissed his feet;
the laughter of those young women flowered like a palmyra spathe!
"Ha! Would I touch an Aryan scorpion?"
That bull raised a cry of resistance!.....
Wherever foxes filled the forest, wherever deceivers roamed the land:
guard! guard! To those armies
Ravana was the cock that announced the hour! The cock!

Tamil queen Muthunagai, Ravana's sister,
blew the conch against the ascetics of Dandakaranya!
She sprang forth like a vengai, a woman of a warrior clan;
the Ganges seemed to stream from the eyes of the Veda-chanters!
Having understood that strength of shoulder could not
defeat the Tamils, they turned to treachery and intrigue:
with the younger Lakshmana and Sita, his wife, following behind,
the king called Rama set out,
a man who, after wronging his brother and seeking the crown,
was scorned throughout the land as a betrayer of trust
and had gone away to the forest!

<!-- scan 36 -->

One day,
as he wandered by the forest, sugarcane-like Muthunagai
slept from weariness beside an orchard of fruit trees.
The vile man, his reason lost to lust,
went near the pure woman sleeping with lotus eyes closed,
intending wickedly to violate her — his younger brother
beside him as accomplice.
Sita Devi stood there as a ready hand for guard!
Muthunagai, who had fallen asleep without a guard,
flared up to protect the fire of her chastity...
one woman alone like a fruit tree — against two monkeys!
What could she do?

As the price of chastity she lost a hand! Lost her nose!
Lost the teeth that scattered Muthunagai's pearls! Alas,
lost her breast — yet her chastity
did not lose its ground!
A Tamil woman who would guard honour even if her head were lost,
Ravana's own younger sister!

The ear-burning news that a guileless sister
had been made into Surpanakha reached him —
he looked as though a wound had opened in his eye.
The king who ruled sea-girt earth — was he not
Muthunagai's elder brother?
Even if there were no spreading bond of kin,
was she not a citizen of Lanka?
He rose to take revenge,
storing in his eyes the redness of the young morning sun...!

Hearing that the king was coming, beautiful-eyed Sita Devi
demanded a winsome deer
and sent her husband racing after it!

<!-- scan 37 -->

"In the elder brother's absence, do you think
to cast a net for your sister-in-law, younger brother?

Run after the elder one, you too!" — she drove him away
and waited for the Southerner to arrive.

With eyes throwing sparks, the warrior came like a chariot.
He saw Sita, pouring honeyed speech, come to the street entrance.

"The elder and younger have both run away —
you, the one suited to me, have now come —
I give myself to you," she said,
and twined herself around Ravana's broad shoulders,
green-bangled Sita Devi like a spreading jasmine creeper!

"You are the wife of the man who harmed
my own life-blood sister!
Rather than give your body to crow and vulture
and lusting bear,
I will not even let my hand touch it.
Board the ship," he said.

The companion of the man born in a lineage
that had loaded its honour upon a ship said,
"Let my body accept the gift,"
and leaned upon his strong chest —
thinking that somehow she would gain the boon,
she boarded the vessel and stood,
a she-fox behind the lion of proud gait!

"If he carries Sita Devi away as prisoner,
sets her upon the throne and makes her crowned queen,
and if Sita bears him a son,
that son too will have a share in Lanka's sceptre.
If a country for Aryans can come
like palmyra fruit, without labour —

<!-- scan 38 -->

what harm if ten thousand Sitas go?"
But the Aryans' thought did not come to pass.
For Sita the conjurer,
the king of Lanka arranged a prison!

"Those who proclaim 'Veda!' and 'sacrifice!' —
go break their ribs
as you would split firewood!" he ordered.

The victorious king had a younger brother, Vibhishana...
a parasite upon a vengai tree,
waiting to bring down his elder brother and rule.
Into the hand of a fifth-column force called Hanuman
he placed a firebrand:
"Destroy the great city of Lanka," he said...
all southern Lanka became a forest of fire, a forest of corpses!
Through the cooperation of corrupt Vibhishana,
the dwarf-fox Aryans found a road to advance their race!

[Line illustration occupies the lower portion of the page]

<!-- scan 39 -->

The cruel man who, by the scheming of the darbha-grass men,
had killed his elder brother Vali, a Tamil warrior —
Sugriva, who priced away self-respect —
fell at the feet of the eye-rolling Aryans.

The gold who had trusted his younger brother,
the lion who had ruled until the whole earth trembled,
saw enemy forces riding the waves of the sea
and called, "Own brother, Vibhishana! Come at once!"

"Do not play with a cobra lifting its hood —
you will tremble like a debtor's heart, Anna!
Let us go together and surrender; come!"
Thus the younger brother stood singing the praise
of Aryans who displayed their liquor jars.

The Southerner saw the monkey-show; his eyes
became pools.
"You who came to ruin the clan!
Handle of the axe!
Thank me that I have not cut away your wicked tongue —
walk out!" he said,
and strode toward the battlefield....!

The noble one steeped in the same womb,
mighty in the sleep of thought,
the beautiful younger brother Kumbakarna,
like the sword hanging at his elder brother's waist,
sprang after him toward the place where armies clashed!

The youngest brother came and stood in his path.
"Gathering armies to defeat
the Aryans who live with matted hair is useless —
all will be ruined," he said.

"I am not a brother who lives by setting up a shop of betrayal!
Birthplace of destruction! Walk on!"
He surged away like a flood that had burst its embankment!

<!-- scan 40 -->

"My dear warriors! My comrades rising high!
I am Ravana, who rules this southern Lanka today —
men of base occupation, men stripped of character,
men who live by cheating,
men who lie at their feet —
I have put on the armour of war
to destroy them all in half a moment!

Friends like tigers! I had thought
the wicked priests had no army!
They have made maidens into encampments
and gathered every army they need.
With young women of kovai-fruit lips,
guava cheeks and blanket-bodies,
all the kings of our race
have leaned toward the sages!

Showing full-moon faces,
the wives of ascetics have tormented crowned kings —
fortresses where the Tamil flag once flew
have become monasteries for begging Aryans, ayyo!

There is the thick-skinned one, thicker even than a buffalo,
who harmed his dear younger brother and fled to the forest;
that degraded Sugriva is his companion —
the man who brought death to his elder brother!

Another is Rama.
With the traitor called Hanuman
stands my own brother who set Lanka on fire!

If Rama and all these men oppose me,
must there not be some mystery in this?

They demand that I
give Sita a son and then hand Lanka's sceptre
to that very son —
firebrand-like conditions
from wicked Rama's band!

My own companion is a golden daughter like a flower-ball,
fresh Tamil beyond even the reach
of poets who weave verse —

<!-- scan 41 -->

my great queen,
cool moon! block of diamond!
For this yāzh-player,
why a ruined veena called Sita?

Not even for a few days will I open a path
for our land to touch the Aryans' feet and bow!

Warriors whose eyes themselves display valour!
Swear upon our language and set forth —
we will avenge ourselves upon the porcupines and return!"
Thus thundered the war-drum
of the man with hill-like shoulders!

Hearing him, everyone roared!....

On the battlefield smelling of death,
the armies on both sides poured blood!
Those with shattered heads,
those with torn bellies —
all were Tamils, one clan!

By mountain-side, by tree-side, hiding,
enemy Rama fought like a rat!

"Never on this earth have I seen such a method —
can bow and arrow not reach?"
Kumbakarna flared in anger.
Seeing Rama leap and flee,
he cried, "Coward, turn back!"
He roared like an elephant:
"Is life some sugarcane to cling to?"

He had not finished speaking...
from behind him
Lakshmana poured a shower of arrows!

The dear younger brother fell like an elephant.
The pure Tamilian leading the army saw it!
Every thought, every atom of him scattered fire.
"Cats!... stand where you are!"
he shouted and loosed the war-arrow!

Was it rain pouring from a cold cloud?
Was it thunder cracking in summer?
He took on storm-speed — a new speed!

<!-- scan 42 -->

Like a tiger struck by a spear — like an arrow released —
like floodwater freed from a dam —
the man whose chest had borne a thousand swords
fought!...
With heels at their napes, the cowards hid!

"The enemy is finished!" he laughed aloud
and turned back along the road.
From hiding, the Aryans shot a single arrow.
He cut away the shaft lodged in his chest
and, lifting his sword, turned:
"Who is the coward?"

Rama's band, trapped with no road to run,
would soon have no flesh even left to search for!....

"My land is sugarcane-sweet;
to you who have trapped it in misery —
this sweep of my sword
is lightning!" he cried and whirled it.

The man famed for facing down even a cyclone!

Vibhishana, who had understood
that the Aryan situation had become desperate,
had a spear in his hand!... He hurled it
toward his elder brother's back!

That spear, flying from hiding,
pierced his mountain-rock back,
thrust its head out through his chest
and poured tears!

As though the Himalaya itself had fallen,
Ravana fell!

"The man who killed me is my younger brother,
shoulders like a palmyra, feet at the Brahmin's feet —
in his hand there is medicine to save life!
But I die having heard
that my own blood-brother fought from hiding."

Hearing this, the people of Lanka
became as though dead themselves!

[Decorative printer's ornament centered below the final verse]

## Item 3 — Indrajit

<!-- scan 43 -->

**Indrajit**

Across the earth valour leaps — in women's
eyes chastity speaks;
the shadow of the flag that rises into the sky
conquers the enemy; every mode of the country's music
displays honour;
countless powers bloom in this fortress of warriors —
white clouds hold the royal parasol,
while the broad sea performs penance,
kissing coolly the golden-walled fort.
Their own lives are not the greater thing
to the people who live here!
"To guard the people's lives is the grammar of great Tamils" —
so speaks the guide of southern Lanka's great clan;
lamp of self-respect — leader of the Tamils;
Ravana, who lives forever upon the page of fame!

Lightning-waisted great queen Mandodari was his wife.
The two of them,
longing to hear a child's sugarcane-sweet speech,
without performing harsh austerities,
without bathing in the Ganges,
created a fruit — singing poetry in the bedchamber!
A new flower discovered in a waterfall of love —
a beauty of a man — a pearl they gained
by plunging into an ocean of delight —

<!-- scan 44 -->

they touched him to their eyes and rejoiced;
they forgot their worries.
A cradle inlaid with rubies
would shatter beneath the child's foot;
the very next day would become a festival
at the sight of the son's valour!
If he drooled from his mouth,
mother and father would drink it as nectar!

He crawled through the hall — behind him
the great warrior followed, singing Tamil:

"They dig into the earth
and lift out nailed-gold and jewel-clusters;
can all those equal a child,
praised like the Kural?
The slack-jawed ones scorn household life!
What can I say of their wisdom?
Any epic or literature that denies
the great bliss a child gives — I call it cactus!
I call it a forest twig useless even for a fork!
Is not a child's kiss the gathering of the three fruits?
Is not its smile the taste of Muttamil!"

So he cooed and drew his graceful woman into his arms.
"The child is watching," the maiden would say and move away.
"He strings pearls with the pearls of his laughter!" he would say,
then, "May you have speech! May you have a good heart!
Come, my darling!"
and upon the scarred chest struck by arrows
he would gather his gold into his embrace.

Like a wave rising high
upon the foaming sea in season,
he planted his foot upon the earth
and leapt —
"to catch the mountain-cloud," said

<!-- scan 45 -->

the great queen, pouring cool words!

"Will the wealth born to a warrior of speed
live like a crow or chicken?
Will he listen if one says,
'Cling lovingly to life'?
Without fearing death he leapt upon the white cloud!
So from now I will call him Meghanada!"

The festival of naming the prince of sacred Lanka —
on that day when peacock flocks danced
to the sound of anklets,
he held the beautiful child close
and shed tears of joy, saying,
"Long live the lamp of our lineage, born to rule this land!"
All the women stood smiling;
a great day!

Like the splitting of a palmyra spathe,
the warriors read valour upon the young prince's face!
Like grain sprouting in the pāli of a wedding house,
Meghanada grew and grew upon the earth,
like a mountain!

Before twice eight years were complete,
he entered the forest,
captured two lions
and kept them at the palace like watchdogs!
In his twentieth year
he broke an iron pillar with one hand!
He built a fortress no enemy could enter in Lanka.
He established a beautiful hall for literary Tamil.
Then Indra, king of the devas,
fought him and was defeated —
and so he became worthy of the name Indrajit.

"Sweet one! My nectar!
Our son's greatness has returned to my heart
a new rising, a new feeling, a new youth —

<!-- scan 46 -->

fruit-sweet one! In memory of it,
give me an old kiss! —
when I stand alone I cannot bear
this trouble called love!"

Saying this,
Ravana, guardian of the Tamil field,
found her jasmine-teeth and held her close.

"My life! My beloved! I will tell you one thing;
a many-coloured flower, when it matures,
becomes fruit —
we too are walking toward that stage,
is it not true?
Thinking of all the songs we sang
in moonlit days,
it is best that, in good time,
we seek a companion in love
for our son, our cool nectar."

Thus spoke bee-haunted flower-tressed Mandodari!
Hearing it, Ravana nodded yes —
but did not let go —
the two of them won that night together;
they rose when dawn's light struck.

At that moment a companion came running:
a mango-like young woman, she said,
was standing in Meghanada's palace,
shy as a water-lily creeper!

The parents stood astonished — yet
the king rejoiced as though tasting athirasam.
"Life of my life! Go and see your daughter-in-law!
Is she a garlanded king's daughter? — a tender vine?
A dark-tressed beauty? — one suited to our darling?
Learn all this and return in a moment," he said.

She went — she came back!
"What is it, mirror-eyed one! Your golden feet
hardly touch the earth — you are flying!
Tell me, sweet Tamil!"
Thus the man beside her stretched his ear in excitement.

<!-- scan 47 -->

The queen of Lanka came forward
to tell exactly what was there:

"As though sandalwood were turned and carved into a statue,
then lips fitted with the colour of burning flame,
beneath brows like Indra's bow
two beautiful blue gems set within eyelids,
a sacred mouth fragrant with classical Tamil made to move,
and life breathed into it
so it might forever sing praise —
what wonder is this?

Queen of beauty! Nectar-speech! Love-speech!
There is no word in the manuscript of familiar Tamil
that can show a comparison!
I will tell you one thing.

[Large reclining-figure illustration]

Do not be startled when you hear it —
she is the enemy's daughter!
A red paddy-ear our son brought away
without scattering, without spilling!
She is the daughter of the Naga king
who once tried to face us with an army
and escaped alive!
Her name is Sulochana —
a sweet-filled
segment of jackfruit! One day —

<!-- scan 48 -->

as that good maiden wandered
beside the garden of a jewelled tower,
Meghanada went there like a cat
that had spotted a sweet cake!

'I suffer from a love that will not cool, beloved,'
he said, lowering before her
the head that bows to no one,
standing there to beg alms of love!

'Because I lacked the courage,
I did not begin first —
pure one, I heard your name
and for many days I have not slept.
Look at my neck, which wears no tiger-claw;
bring it at once!
From now on I want no one else —
if I have this love, that is enough,' she said!

Is your son not a lion?
Would a surging tiger give birth to a cat?
He lifted the gold itself
and carried her upon his shoulder;
chill delight spread through every limb
and she clung to him.
With the new strength her body gave him,
he crossed the fortress wall of the Naga land!

This, king, is the story the maiden told;
know it," she said, heart rejoicing!

Ravana too said it would be best
to celebrate through the streets
where fierce-handed chariots parade
and make Sulochana
the daughter-in-law of the island of Lanka;
and she said, "Good!"

"Return my daughter!" demanded the Naga king.
Meghanada tore apart his furious palm-leaf message.
"I will cut off his head," said the Naga father-in-law,
waiting to repay the insult!

Meanwhile, to destroy Ravana,
the northern Aryans undertook a degrading intrigue —
a conspiracy-play behind the curtain
to finish the Dravidian!

<!-- scan 49 -->

One younger brother who had seemed a dove
turned into a wicked eagle —
for Rama, who had sent
a fifth-column force called Hanuman,
Meghanada found a way to teach a lesson!
He cut the tail of the one who tried to burn the city,
gave his hide a taste of punishment,
and drove him away!

The flood of betrayal surged upon Lanka!....

"A grove has no beauty without flowers!
A poem has no beauty without meaning!

<!-- scan 50 -->

A tongue has no beauty without grace!
Therefore it is best to go down and speak with Rama,"
said cruel Vibhishana,
the one who had "become seven with Guha"!

Hearing it,
"You have well spoken the grammar that guards the tongue,
uncle!
But after war has begun to guard the country,
to kneel in order to guard our own heads
is not our custom — it is no act of honour.
Walk on," said Meghanada,
with a heart that knew no trembling.

The impetuous northerners began the war!
Whenever hardship came,
the great people of this land laughed at it
and leapt into the battlefield;
the deed of the brother who came
like a disease that kills its own blood
shook the whole mind of resolute Ravana.

"Refined gold! Eye that I fathered!
I am sending you to war!
Go and return, my son," he said.

"These battle-itching shoulders
have found their food!
For the wicked we will build
a ladder to the city of death!"
The bull rose roaring.

"To guard the motherland I will give my head —
I will teach a lesson to sinners
who open the road for the enemy-country's dogs!
I will return, my mother!
Send me on my way!
I will bring the news of victory," he said
and flew away.

His companion came before him.
"I set out for war," he said.

"Not even half a year has passed since our wedding —
son of kings for whom ruling the earth was not enough,
who rule the sea as well!
To this simple woman whose love
cannot find a way across its own banks —
is it just to say that the war-drum
has sounded for a journey? Tell me!" she said.

<!-- scan 51 -->

"It is not beautiful to pour tears like hanging roots!
We were born in a lineage
that ploughs with the bow and raises a crop of valour.
We bow in life to the learned,
not to enemies!

Women should weep to stop the deceit of men,
not to stop the event of entering the battlefield!
Damage may come to body, wealth and life —
not to the culture of ancient Tamil!
When dawn comes,
give me leave to depart," said
the vengai longing to see the face of war.

"When you bent me like a creeper,
threw me across your shoulder
and crossed the long fortress wall,
I was not afraid — yet when I think of tomorrow's dawn,
why does my heart tremble so, beloved?"

She wept. He said:

"This is a land where a child hearing thunder
claps its hands and laughs!
A Tamil land that does not know
the word 'impossible' —
can you speak without reading this, green parrot?
Lay me in your lap."

With the words shed by that fearless heart as comfort,
the woman, unable to find any road to consolation,
clung like a beautiful flower
to the bull-embracing chest of her man.

"Kiss this hard chest
where the mud of blood will settle," he asked;
one or two became a hundred —
she poured down a rain of kisses!
Even the moon that had hidden
peeped out to watch —
peerless warrior Indrajit became shy!

They heard he was coming to war.
"He will come like a river without a dam," they said.
"The charioteer of his chariot is our man!

<!-- scan 52 -->

No need for useless fear — cast it away!
I will go into the town and return as a spy!"
Thus Vibhishana slipped away in hiding.

"Without your brow shrinking,
without your blue eyes clouding,
conquer the long enemy, beloved!
Great treasure of this motherland rich in worth!
The unlearned fools set out like swarming termites
to show what their hands can do —
my king! Guard the honour of the clan!"

So beautiful Sulochana Devi,
for the warrior already clothed for battle,
placed the garland, marked the tilak
and stood blessing him!!

"Upon this earth I will let no enemy enter —
my ten-and-a-half-touch pure gold!"
He received one "seal" and went forth!

"At the place called Nikumbala is a great encampment.
There are weapons there capable of destroying whole masses!
That is Indrajit's great army;
we must destroy it by strategy."
Vibhishana said this in the camp of the worthless men!

They all danced in excess of joy!
With his army, the tiger-like Tamilian
entered the Nikumbala encampment!
Weapons known to no one,
he took and placed into the warriors' hands!
The war-drum roared outside the camp!
The camp shook as enemies arrived!
The warrior suddenly trapped inside the cave
spat smoke like a volcano, enraged!
Enemies surrounded him everywhere.

"This is the work of my shameless uncle!
He has completed his share of betrayal —
I will sever his head!"

<!-- scan 53 -->

He whirled in his chariot like a revolving sword!
His eyes appeared as flame.
With the strength of a thousand elephants he resisted!
Like a temple tower walking,
like a mountain moving,
he stormed like a cyclone,
came like a surging sea —
were these the eyes that spoke tenderness
to fish-eyed women?
They were sparks of fire
to destroy the alien lineage utterly!

Were these hands that embraced a golden statue?
They were hands that reaped the heads of the arrogant!
Everywhere, he!
In everything, he!
He was the sunbeam that tears the night!

Just as he was about to finish the battle
and cut off Rama's head,
the charioteer's conspiracy began at once!
The wheels had lost their linchpins —
in that instant the chariot lurched and fell,
and the four horses sprang in panic!
The whole undercarriage scattered apart!

Meghanada fell through deceit —
and poison-hearted Vibhishana cut off his hand.
There was no time even to think
of the treachery his uncle had done...
with both arms pouring rivers of blood,
he fought!

Clenching a spear in his mouth,
he tormented the enemies.
All the enemies gathered in a mass.
Even with both hands lost,
his furious power of battle
made the enemy itself praise him —
everyone except one sinner, Vibhishana!
Before the prince!

<!-- scan 54 -->

Unable to bring down the enemy by spear or arrow,
with feet and head alone
he scooped away many lives!

On learning that her beloved's hand
had been severed on the battlefield,
flower-creeper Sulochana too understood —

"Where is the arm that leapt to embrace me?
Sinner that I am — how shall I bear to see my beloved?"

Lamenting, she ran there.
She saw one man standing among a thousand,
displaying Tamil valour:

"Beloved!" she cried —
"My love," he answered —
and for a moment he weakened!

Then Lakshmana, the great coward,
loosed an arrow that did not miss —
and to the cry "Long live the motherland!"
he swayed upon the battlefield
like a vessel without a sail.

The base-natured Naga king,
who had nursed an old grudge,
now settled the ancient vengeance
by cutting off his head!

"I have gained a place
on the golden page of the green-Tamil people;
green parrot, I am coming!"
he said —
and only his head fell into her hands...
Sulochana, that priceless jewel,
died as well!

"I have no other life left
to lose in this war," said
the king of Lanka and collapsed —
Alas, only to die after him
had Mandodari opened her long lashes!

✤

## Item 4 — Hiranyan

<!-- scan 55 -->

Hiranyan

Upon the neck of Earth, the woman,
the Southland rolls like a strand of pearls;
a lovely vīṇā taken up and plucked!
A grove of art where gatherings of poets weave garlands of verse;
Lanka, like a hand into which the people of the world
heap tribute and offerings!
In those old days when the honey-sweet sound of Tamil,
delightful to the ear,
spread Tamil valour throughout the world....

A warrior whose shoulders were like twin mountains,
a body rich in splendour;
honey to the Dravidians — moonlight to them —
a weapon for sweet Tamil, a great one who shone!
He had a moustache like a sheath in which war-swords slept!
He had an unquenchable longing
that the Tamil land should live in a way the world would praise;
and whenever he saw the Aryans march bearing grass,
there was the roar of a lion in his voice!
To this unwavering Tamilan
his parents gave the name **Perunthagai Vēndhu** — the noble king!
The base-minded Aryans called him **Hiranyan**!
A king who understood all three divisions of the *Tirukkural*;

<!-- scan 56 -->

he guarded his rule so that neither the Book of Wealth
nor the Book of Virtue strayed beyond the bounds of governance!
The final book, the Book of Love,
he found with his sweet queen!
And as its fruit....
so that baby-speech surpassing flute and yāzh might unfold,
a jewel-casket appeared within the queen's womb —
and the people of the country roared in joy!

A young bull with eyes of beauty and a face that spoke poetry;
because his crescent forehead surpassed even feminine beauty,
his parents called him **Pirainuthalān**, the crescent-browed one;
the backward Aryans called him **Prahlada**!
At a time when they had found no path by which to destroy the king,
if the banner of the clan should sprout in the warrior-king's line,
they said, ruin would come upon the Veda-chanting clan itself;
and like fire flaring in a sacrificial pit
the Aryans boiled up to devise a plot!
They made their jackal-plan and dispersed —
those grass-bearers who had come to turn the country into a forest!

As the king's son grew day by day,
changing with every passing hour....
a jackal crossed their path saying,
"I alone am skilled enough
to teach him swordcraft, archery and the arts of learning."
The king flared up:
"You coward who would fear even
to stroke a screw-pine leaf against its grain!
You fool who does not know how to hold a sword
even to cut a banana tree!
Go back the way you came!"

"It is not good that he should go away with a wounded heart;

<!-- scan 57 -->

show no difference of caste when giving education, O king!"
The crowned queen stood sulking as she said it,
faulting the victor's "narrow outlook";
she had learned a false definition of broad-mindedness....
She asked that milk and eggs be offered to a snake!
The mark of a narrow outlook — the sacred thread upon the chest!
A crooked heart from which little-jackal deeds arise —
an Aryan doctrine-text in the hand!
Yet, in the name of a large-minded outlook,
she asked him to accept the emissary of Aryanism
who had come close only to destroy from within!
Because answering word for word against
the round-faced great queen was not his custom,
the great warrior who showed tiger-speed and storm-speed in battle
agreed in an instant and went away.

To the wavering crowd that lives thinking
Tamil **vāḷ** — "sword" — means merely a monkey's **vāl**, its "tail";
that **vēl**, "spear," means the karuvēlam tree;
and **kaḷam**, "field of battle," means only a place where paddy is heaped —
there came as their ally,
a creature the source mocks as `ōthiyamilār-ōṇān kuñju`,
who sat like an incomparable teacher and delivered lessons;
the prince sat obediently before him and studied.
The teacher's eldest daughter, Asha Devi,
stayed nearby and made friends with him!
Even the womb of Aryanism carries intrigue —
who on this earth has not been caught in its whirl?
He began with the source-form chant, **"ari namōthu sindham"**!
He told with relish the tale in which a fox became a horse!
The maiden ripened into fruit; the prince became a parrot!
He taught the tale of the red-matted one who wears the moon,
and the sacred sport he played with a devotee's wife....

<!-- scan 58 -->

The tale of Brahma, maker of the three worlds,
uniting with his daughter Tilottama....
The tale of Kannan overturning the pots of young maidens
by the seashore, riverbank, forest-edge and house-yard....
He taught every fevered tale of erotic desire!
The girl became a pawn in the gambling game her father played!
Without tiring, she taught the young prince the arts of pleasure!

Believing his son would return a warrior from Vivekapuri,
the king asked,
"Have you finished archery? Elephant-riding?"
The son replied with scorn:
"Why, father, all that useless misery?
I have tasted the pleasure given by Inbavalli,
in whom every beauty is gathered....
I have found the path to behold
the lotus-feet of the Lord!"
Hearing his son speak so,
Hiranyan sprang at him in fury:
"With shoulders eager for combat,
I have stood firm as diamond within the palmyra trunk,
shaking the directions!
I took up the sword to split the mouths
of those base men who, upon a people that worships Nature,
smuggle in an idol under the name of God!
Show me, then — who is your god?
If not, in a forest torrent.... in mid-ocean.... in blazing fire....
I shall plunge you down, grind you to powder
and scatter you upon the earth...."
His words flew like a gunshot,
cutting one thing cleanly into two;
the prince, who had returned with the brains of a goat,
stood frightened!
Had the queen not stepped between them there....
Tamil honour would have remained;
Hiranyan's son would have died....

<!-- scan 59 -->

"If by tomorrow night you do not show me God,
the mother who bore you as a burden....
and the beloved who has turned hard as stone....
will cry in terror as you mount the funeral bier!
No son — no warrior —
no more glory to my line because of you!
Go! Go! Disappear!"
He stood lamenting....

Even when armies clashed like mountains,
even when enemies swarmed around him like locusts,
he was the great warrior whose mind never shook;
a golden page in the history of martial Tamils.
But hearing that his son had gone over to the Aryans,
the prince slipped away in hiding, broken and afraid!

Before the Vedic priest seated beside the sacrificial pit,
he told all that had happened:
"My life has reached the point of departure....
Ask your gods to come!"
"Do not fear — come!" said
Asha Devi, green-parrot girl,
drawing him onto her lap
and raining kiss after kiss....
To change his mind,
she wrapped him against her breast and held him!
Having completed every kind of lesson
in the pleasures of desire,
she began a new display of arts;
she composed a new dictionary of sexual delight!
She played and rejoiced —
a wasp that transforms the senses of the worm it seizes!
She stung him.... with kovvai-red lips!
The prince born to rule the great land
where Avvai's Tamil is sung
subdued himself within her grip!

<!-- scan 60 -->

They judged that this was the moment for a rare conspiracy....
a plan to make Aryanism endure....
and in the council of the sacrificial pit
it received immediate approval!
Prahlada, unaware of it,
went before his father and declared,
"I shall show you the Lord!"
"Where?" roared the Hiranyan-lion.
"In this pillar!" he said;
it was a word spoken at Asha's prompting.

"In the pillar? — shame, shame!
Had you said, 'He dwells in the hearts of the pure,'
the world of reason would not deny it!
Fool — in this pillar?
In the pillar...?"
With a thunderous roar,
he raised his iron-shod foot to smash the pillar to dust!
By the traitors' arrangement
the whole pillar had been filled with magnetism —
a fact even the guards did not know!
The son who had lost his rational sense stood there too;
the villains' arrangement had vanished from his mind
under the pleasures the maiden had given him!
The raised iron shoe clung to the magnetic pillar;
the broad-shouldered one sank into astonishment!
Then a deceiver hidden there,
disguised in the form of a lion,
so that no one might know him as an Aryan,
thrust a spear into the breast of the southern sun!
Blood surged out in torrents!
They cut open his entrails; cut out his tongue;
hacked the body; destroyed the form....
Then they sold a story for people to believe:
"Our Lord came in the form of Narasinga!"
A thick fog closed over it —
the mouth that had thundered against Aryanism
was silenced;

<!-- scan 61 -->

this was the sacred theatre of Aryanism!
The son too, understanding it only as
"the god himself took my father's life," disappeared into its fold!
He did not know the full shape of Aryanism —
he stood eager to dance upon the throne!
That treacherous son is not alive today;
so what?
In this country, along his path,
are there not countless Prahladas
at the feet of Aryanism?

✤

## Item 5 — King Vali

<!-- scan 62 -->

King Vali

Golden hill of noble thought, good man of Kishkindha;
unmatched master in wrestling and sword-fight!
Tara, Tamil woman, guards the husband she has chosen;
a maiden who brings delight with sugar-candy speech!
Pearls seem picked from her teeth!
A bow seems drawn across her brows!
Even the moon learns from her
a new art of giving light!
By strength of shoulder, by strength of sword,
all the enmities that gather rank upon rank
are crushed to dust — trouble disappears!
In giving, he is Vēl Pāri!
In falling at the feet of Tamil and paying homage, a Valluvar!
Under the rule of that mighty man there is no poverty;
therefore there is no suffering.
Yet is it not nature that even the good bear one blemish?
A mountain-serpent that comes to kill; a savage beast;
the total form of boundless selfishness without principle;
a complete fool —
the ill-named Sugriva happened to be born his younger brother!
A minister called Hanuman was his great companion;
a religious monkey fighting for the order of Manu!

<!-- scan 63 -->

Into sugarcane sweet at every joint
he planned to mix neem-oil atom by atom;
to destroy southern Tamil, fine as millet-flour,
he became the traitorous ally of directionless Aryanism.
"If you bring down your elder brother,
the throne will lie at your feet — understand that, Sugriva!"
Thus he urged the fool on,
and the two who had waited for their moment spoke together.
They sent a brute called Avunan against the elder brother.

"There is no one who can defeat me in wrestling;
let any who deny it come and compete!"
Beating his chest, Avunan stood in challenge.
The great king who ruled the earth,
lamp of the martial Tamil clan,
welcomed him with a smile and sounded the war-drum!
"How many days will this wrestling last?
Both are great warriors," said one crowd.
"He looks even stronger than the king;
will some harm come to our ruler?" worried another crowd.
"He looks like a mountain;
his two shoulders are like mortars.
The king will surely lose," another crowd faltered.
But before any shock the people expected could occur,
the great warrior Avunan fell senseless from a single blow!
The next blow.... "Ammaadi!".... "Appaadi!"...!
After that there was no one to catch him....
he hid himself inside a mountain cave!
Sugriva, whose intrigue was not yet complete,
said to his brother:
"It would be good to capture Avunan,
bring him back and make a spectacle for the country!

<!-- scan 64 -->

Elder brother, follow him!"
He stood pointing out the cave.
The brother who would refuse to believe
that his younger brother's whole heart was a mountain of fire
walked toward the cave!
So that the lion who entered the cave
would die without returning,
the hostile schemers planned — and acted!
They brought elephants, rolled boulders into place
and sealed the cave-mouth!
They thought, "We have destroyed the lion."
They laughed, "We have attained the throne."
Taking their seats in state, they began to proclaim:

"People of great Kishkindha!
The news that your father, my elder brother, has died
is a terrible wonder!
Even to think of it pains our hearts!
By fighting craftily and feigning flight,
Avunan the warrior has sent that incomparable hero
to the other world — alas! Misery! Misery!
There is no use in weeping! There is no one to console us!
Worshipping the Lord is now my only work;
I shall become an ascetic."
So saying, he shed false tears in streams!

"Do not go, young lord! Do not go!
Take the right to rule the earth;
accept the duty of protecting us!"
The uncomprehending people of the land lamented and blocked his way.
"I cannot refuse, O minister! O Maruti!
Bring the jewelled crown — put it upon my head!
I tried to avoid responsibility — but will the people let me?
I cannot bring myself to reject my brother's country!"

<!-- scan 65 -->

The people standing around rejoiced
at the words of Sugriva, master of intrigue.
Anjaneya flew like a whirlwind,
bustling through the work of coronation —
and at that moment, like a storm-wind,
like a great fire, Tara Devi burst in!

"A hare's horn is the crown upon your head, Sugriva — know that!
You joined with Hanuman so that an alien doctrine
might spread through Tamil land,
and you brought down your own elder brother!
Exception to every rule that defines a younger brother!
Traitor to trust! There is no feeling in your blood!
I would say you do not have even the attachment
a bee bears toward a flower
for this pure Tamil land.
Had you told my husband you wished to rule,
would he not, that man of gold, have given it freely?
A battlefield coward without even the strength
to win it by fighting!
You have sat as charioteer of an empire like a chariot —
shame! shame!
If you have courage, lift your sword!
Answer the opposition of this plume-wearing woman
and then rule the country!
Where is Hanuman, whose thighs tremble?"

To Tara who thundered thus,
base Sugriva said:
"Even if you are wife to a great warrior
who fights until armies tremble,
you are still a woman whose waist sways as she walks!
Inside the room!
A hen brooding eggs has no work in the public hall —
go! go!"

"Who did you say that to?"
Tara sprang up
and began pouring words like tongues of flame:
"Cockerel-chick hatched from the egg
of that brooding hen!

<!-- scan 66 -->

Useless male!
Little jackal! Tiny ant!
You whose thick tongue dares insult womankind!
Go and ask the race of dogs to teach you the meaning of gratitude!
Get out of here now!" she lunged.

"You decided there was no one to restrain your arrogance!
Because I showed you respect as crowned queen,
you mistook it for fear!
You have given me the occasion to invoke the law —
guards, this insolent woman must be put in prison —
let the work be done at once!"
With his two hard eyes he gave the order.

"Stop there, younger brother!
Newcomer to the rule of the land!
You took a woman for something feeble —
know that she can spring like a leopard!
You leap in delight thinking,
'We sealed him in the black mountain cave
behind a door of rock' — good!
This is no dream, younger brother; I am your elder brother —
the king of Kishkindha whose time has not yet ended!
See how I kicked away the boulder
with which elephant, Hanuman and loving younger brother
had sealed me in!"
Roaring so, King Vali entered.
Seeing his elder brother,
the younger fled into hiding with Hanuman,
wandering the forest rejoicing that they had saved their lives.

One day there,
the "great warrior" Rama, who had lost his wife,
came to rest with his younger brother Lakshmana.
Sugriva received them and served tender coconuts.

<!-- scan 67 -->

"Had this been the house of the rishis,
there would have been a sweeter drink.
Never mind — it will do," said Lord Rama, drinking.
Then he said:

"My wife is a young-breasted, drum-waisted woman —
a sculpted beauty who enters the field of desire and wins!
All the pleasure one sees in soma-intoxication,
I would say, can be found in her glance!
Her name is Sita! Breasts like red-lotus buds!
She is not with me now.
Find where she is and help bring her back
to satisfy my hunger."

Sugriva replied:
"We shall give help — we shall give an army — but one thing!
My elder brother is an incomparable warrior;
none equals him in sword-fight or wrestling!
There is no one to defeat him in the casting of the **vāḷi**;
from that he won the special name **Vali**!
If you tell me a way to take his life by deceit,
his country will come under my control —
and after that I shall help you."

They devised a plot to seize the sacred land;
dark-hearted Sugriva set out for Kishkindha!
Grace-faced King Vali, amid the chamber's delight,
stroked the curling hair of the life dear to him and found pleasure.
Outside,
a shout whose meaning was not yet clear:
"Will you come out to fight?"
Hearing the cry, the tiger rose from the bed
and was astonished to see his younger brother standing opposite!

<!-- scan 68 -->

The younger brother knew all his elder's strength;
yet seeing him slap his shoulders without fear,
the queen withered in doubt. But what of it?
Could she restrain the warrior?
She gave him leave and sent him forth.
With tears flooding her eyes,
she fell upon the silk-cotton bed!

"Wrestling? Bow-fight? Sword-fight?"
the king asked.
"Wrestling is what I want," Sugriva said.
They collided like two mountains,
both roaring like thunder!
King Vali calculated that the fight would end in moments!
He lifted his younger brother in his hands —
Sugriva became a hand-ball to Vali,
spun round and round;
in another instant life would leave him —
victory stood with Vali.

Hidden in a thicket was the great warrior Rama.
He took aim at Vali's chest and loosed an arrow.
Like a flood bursting a dam,
a torrent of blood rushed from the noble chest;
like a spring exploding from split earth,
the fountain of blood rising from his heart struck the clouds.

"Who is the warrior who attacked me from hiding?
Ah! Rama? Messenger of dharma?
Look at Tamil blood pouring upon the earth!
Why do you wander uselessly wearing the name of warrior?
To receive a wound on the front is valour —
to rise in that splendour is honour —
the Tamilan is one who says honour is greater than life!
Whoever denies it is an enemy!
The younger brother who keeps company with that enemy —
the world of reason will turn away rather than look at him!....

<!-- scan 69 -->

Hero who won fame by breaking a rotten bow!
Champion who seeks another's help to recover a noble woman!
Because there are some Tamils
who believe everything white is milk,
you strut about as a mighty hero!
A coward without courage to stand face to face and fight —
if the grain is lost while only chaff remains,
can life still be of use?
If I die and you remain,
will valour spread?"

Thus King Vali questioned —
with his questions he sounded a war-drum!
Before he could finish, he fell — a corpse!

The news spread — a red fire caught.
Red-lotus Tara Devi came and collapsed!
"My king! When I am here for you to embrace,
have you embraced Mother Earth?"
She wept; she wailed.
With her tears she defeated
the river of blood that ran from him —
and went with her husband!
Rama saw the culture of Tamil land —
and bowed his head!

✤

<!-- scan 70 -->

## கவியரங்கக் கவிதைகள் — Poetry-Assembly Poems

<!-- anthology divider: Tamil = கவியரங்கக் கவிதைகள் -->

<!-- scan 71 -->

## Item 6 — Freedom Fighters

<!-- scan 72 -->

Freedom
Fighters

A presiding poetic introduction by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled
“Freedom Fighters,”
broadcast by Tiruchirappalli Radio
on 15 August 1967.

★

My life-breath Anna, mothers and fathers
born of Tamilakam ringed by the sounding sea,
golden-radiant brothers, renowned poets,
all guardians of precious Tamil —
receive my greetings on this sacred day.

Ah...
Where is the glorious land where the Cheras lived?
Why did the kingdom of the valiant Pandyas fall?
In the tireless land where the Cholas once moved,
how did cowardly doctrines gather so thick?
Questions burst in a grieving heart.
Let us drink deep — give life itself for the nation!

<!-- scan 73 -->

The empty sermon that says “we do not want freedom”
is the howl of jackals.
Bulls seized by love of country,
tear away the panic-stricken men;
let us inscribe a newness
at the summit of a tiger's life!
Thus bristling, surging and rising,
the warriors for freedom given by Tamilakam
have come, at the radio station's invitation,
to be born again in fragrant verse;
the mothers have arrived — and I am their leader!

Let us rise to hear, in the language of poets,
the story of Tamils in whose eyes valour smiles,
who sang the anthem of freedom;
let us press their feet-flowers to our eyes and worship.
First...
“They told us: curb the hunger for freedom;
put down the fire of independence.”
Against them,
a lion-bull thundered, “Beat the drum aloud!”
and fell to death at the hands of the wicked —
let us hear that history.

“The sky pours, the earth yields;
why tribute to a king?”
“Once-ruling race, besieged again —
dying worm, cast off the crown!”
Such fire-spilling breath
was Kattabomman's life-breath.
Because it cooled upon the hanging rope,

<!-- scan 74 -->

we call that place Kayathar.
The history of that ground is being served;
let us feast upon it.

(Poet Tiruchitrambala Kavirayar completes
a historical poem on Kattabomman.)

Chairman Kalaignar:

We thought Kattabomman had died;
this Chitrambalam has given birth to him again — live long, well done!
Along that path,
there are mothers still here
to bear child after child;
we stand eager to lift them up and rejoice.

To end the bondage that gives ruin
and win freedom,
“My son, give your head,” said a mother —
and gave it.
The two Marudhu brothers, tiger-sons,
became fragrance in the garland of freedom they had strung;
like an arrow loosed, with speed,
Poet Natarasa takes words ripened in fire,
weaves verse,
and sends the two Marudhu brothers walking before us again —
very well indeed:

(Poet T. Ku. Natarasan completes
a historical poem on the Marudhu Pandiyars.)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Our friend has brought both Marudhus here
and given us a history rich in valour.

<!-- scan 75 -->

Now, to show the great poet Bharati,
as though the two Marudhus were born as a single Marudhu,
poet Murugusundaram comes forward;
let us listen.

“Bharati did not rise high
by looking at old style, old verse, old Tamil books
and merely writing from them.
If the world has not known
the radiant beauty of Bharati's poetry,
the fault lies with the world.
For the weary and for Tamils who had not studied,
he peeled the fruit ripe,
and with segment-sweet Tamil
gave poetry — Subramania Bharati,
the moon that came singing to end the long sleep,
a word-camphor that came to perfume the forest.”

<!-- scan 76 -->

So sang Bharathidasan,
making peacock-Tamil dance before me;
what will this poet sing? Let us see.

(Poet Murugusundaram completes his poem on Bharati.)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Like a waterfall, did he not pour
upright verse upon Bharati? Good, good.
Yet once,
a waterfall's mischief
made Tamil Nadu bear a great storm of shock!
V. V. S. Aiyar, that truth-filled man of action,
was caught in the fierce whirl of a waterfall
and vanished one day;
today another poet
has come to raise him again.

He rose to destroy the foreign forces
that built walls around freedom,
sealed it in,
and stood guard over it;
he rose, disappeared midway —
and here he comes again,
brought to life by the beautiful,
precious classical Tamil of Abdul Rahman:

(Poet Abdul Rahman completes his poem on V. V. S. Aiyar.)

Chairman Kalaignar:

In Tamil Nadu, where ancient wisdom lives in an ancient people,
we have now seen V. V. S. Aiyar,
born of a lineage that wove fame,
a son of a house steadfast in freedom —

<!-- scan 77 -->

and next, the radiant leader
who launched a ship upon the sea off Tuticorin:

“My spotless wife,
in whom fortune, fame and all excellence abide!
Of my dear life, of the life within my heart,
I gladly gave you one lasting half;
the remaining half
I gave beneath the feet of our great nation —
this is the truth!”

With this *agaval* he opened his heart —
a worthy great one, a leader of Tamils.
To sing of the abundant V. O. C.,
our young poet-friend Ilanchezhiyan
comes here with sweet Tamil words,
a good man who dwells in the heart:

(Poet Ilanchezhiyan completes his poem on V. O. C.)

Chairman Kalaignar's concluding poem:

They lived in prison; they pulled the oil-press —
to drive out the white rulers of this land,
many received shortened lives and suffered killing.
Like a sword leaping out beyond its sheath,
without attachment to life, they fought.
On this day when we have assembled to speak their greatness,
to speak the greatness of all who gave their lives for freedom,
I do not possess poetic wealth like these men;
I have not learned to sing poetry.

<!-- scan 78 -->

But because I sing the story of warriors who broke shackles,
I too sing without shackles;
because they slapped their thighs and felled traitors,
my song too slaps its thigh!

Let my poem
go without *yāppu* if it must — but our country,
our language, honour and feeling
must not go without protection.
Holding only to that principle,
even if I have had no opportunity to study prosody,
I will not stand like a tree that never bears fruit;
I turn the feelings soaking my heart into lines
and say one thing for the axes that change their principles to understand:

How many lives, how much blood,
given without end to win freedom!
After digging this earth and labouring so greatly,
after obtaining gold of ten-and-a-half purity,
is it right, instead of showing craft,
setting it as jewellery
and giving it to women like paintings,
to throw it into a rubbish pit?

In a grove where birds cry,
a fine rose plucked while thorns scratched —
should it give fragrance
upon the heads of sword-bearing warriors,
in the hair of modest women,
or should it be handed
to the cramped fist of a wicked monkey?

We shall cherish and protect the freedom we won;
we shall add honour to the warriors who won it.
If one or two relatives alone
live in prosperity — is that freedom?

<!-- scan 79 -->

The learned and the unlearned,
those who toil in the fields,
helpers, smiths, small workers,
good women and jewel-like students —
for all to delight in a life free of evil:
that is freedom.

Let this principle shine sweetly;
let sluggish doctrine disappear;
let us sound it today in rhythmic Tamil.
Golden hill, Tamil, Mother!
We have spoken of the greatness of your lion-sons;
so that Tamilakam may reap victory everywhere,
grant us your grace here, Mother!

Greetings!
Long live!

✤

## Translator notes

- `பத்தரை மாற்று` is retained conceptually as “gold of ten-and-a-half purity,” a traditional purity/value metaphor; the source's rhetorical continuity with earlier items is preserved.
- Names, quoted historical rhetoric and patriotic claims are translated as Kalaignar's source presentation rather than externally harmonised or fact-checked.
- Parenthetical notices marking the other poets' completed contributions are part of the source structure and remain explicit; their poems are not present in this canonical item and therefore are not reconstructed here.

## Item 7 — The Five Senses

<!-- scan 80 -->

The Five Senses

A presiding poetic introduction by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled “The Five Senses”
held at Islamiah College, Vaniyambadi,
on 24 March 1968.

★

Elders of this great college assembly,
those filled with affection,
young lions!
Receive my greetings with hearts in which a mother's love grows,
as I come to sing Tamil that bows to no enemy,
falls before no army,
and lives through many ages.

We have arranged a *kaviyarangam*
on the five senses — body, mouth, eye, nose and ear.
Friend Rahman said lovingly, “Sir, come.”
He said with his mouth that it was a poetry gathering;
my whole body thrilled — had they really counted me a poet?
I asked, “Really?”
He winked and said yes — perhaps it was a joke!
Then came a letter;
I seized it and read it — with my eyes, of course!
Again the invitation: come sing.

<!-- scan 81 -->

A small treatment for the nose injured in a car accident;
I stayed silent, wondering whether I could go.
My friend did not let go.
The telephone rang sharply;
in my ear I heard Rahman's voice.
He invited me by mouth and thrilled my body,
sent a letter for my eyes to see,
and though there was illness in Kalaignar's nose,
said he would not let me off even three-quarters of the way;
without minding expense he called by telephone to cool my ear.
After all that, if I failed to come,
would you leave me untouched?
Fearing otherwise,
I tied up this unopened bud of poetry
and ran here this morning;
I came in the evening believing it would bloom by evening!

They say one beautiful moon makes the lily bud bloom;
here there are five or six beautiful poet-moons,
so surely even the bud I brought will bloom?
If it does not, blame the moon;
blame those who thought me a poet.
In North Arcot, where colourful hills stand,
there is a friend, a jasmine branch in my grove of friendship,
whose heart trembled whenever trouble came to me —
that sugar-cane-sweet “Mullai.”
When coming here there was no holding me back;
so I came close.

“They say the body dances and subsides,
the mouth sings and subsides,

<!-- scan 82 -->

the eye seeks and subsides,
the nose withers and subsides,
the ear listens and subsides;
therefore controlling the five senses is good.”
How can that be?
Will the ear reject the song of the green bird?
Will the body reject the embrace of the green parrot?
Will the mouth refuse to speak ancient Tamil?
Will the eye reject a moon like a jackfruit segment?
Will the nose reject the fragrance of a dew-wet flower?

All those great sages of the worlds
who performed penance to subdue the five senses —
what suffering they went through!
Viswamitra gave up throne and jewelled crown
and lived as an ascetic in the forest;
do you not know how he became Menaka's faithful friend?
Likewise, when eye, ear, nose, mouth and body —
the five senses — ran like unbridled horses,
like a flood in a wild river,
Arunagiri stood helpless,
blind though he had eyes,
deaf though he had ears,
with wounded body and lamenting mouth.

Everything must remain within measure,
and that measure must be steady.
I told these two stories for that reason.
If a knife is in your hand,
do not stab with it;
but do not hesitate, with intelligence,
to cut fruit and eat it.
To pretend, “I shall attain liberation,”
and refuse to use the five senses is deceit;

<!-- scan 83 -->

to let all five loose and wander like a temple bull is also wrong.
You who have come on stage,
each taking one sense apiece — one word!
Leave that evil aside.

In this college hall, those who let poetry mount the stage
will say their own theme is greatest.
Poets! When you argue that your chosen subject is the greatest,
a warning:
if the body is too large, people call it an elephant;
if the eye is too large, an owl;
if the nose is too large, a capsicum;
if the mouth is too large, they say chatterbox;
if the ear is too large, they mutter “winnowing tray.”
So speak of your subject knowing the proper measure.

If trouble comes, I shall ward it off;
if trouble comes among poets, I shall rejoice!
Take up the staff of Tamil and play a duel of words;
take up arrows of verse and attack one another;
at the same time, take up ripened love and support one another.

Taste, light, touch, sound and smell —
expound the kinds of the five.
Put enmity, blame, victory and boast in your poems;
put laughter, friendship and the feeling of “we” in the ledger of the heart;
then rise to battle!
A little cockfight is for money;
a king's fight is for a country;
your fight is for song;
the fight we watch is for Tamil.

First, on the theme of the body —

<!-- scan 84 -->

Sahul Hameed will pour a poem flowing like ghee.
What will he say?
Without the body there is no eye, no nose,
no mouth, no ear.
He will say the body is the support of all.
Is that true?
That is — is it true that he is going to say that?
Valluvar too stands beside him:
“Touching the bodies of one's children is delight to the body.”
Between the debate over truth and falsehood,
let us taste guava fruit.
Let us wait a little;
let us hear Sahul Hameed's Tamil.

(Poet Sahul Hameed completes his poem on the “body.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
Murugusundaram, a man of Salem —
will the iron there never melt?
Not for one month or two, not for one year or ten;
from the day the Congress came to rule,
we have all been melting, but their heart did not melt!
Honey-iron: if that does not melt,
with the honey-Tamil he has learned
he will sing so that our hearts melt.
Murugusundaram comes...
He will argue that one cannot sing without a mouth
and ask me for judgement.
I shall be bewildered.

<!-- scan 85 -->

He will say even the judgement must be spoken by mouth!
I shall answer: why not write it with the hand?

I will give his mouth, as a morsel,
Valluvar's thought:
“When one gets those able to speak in ordered sweetness,
the world quickly listens and acts.”
He will sing of the mouth;
we shall listen with our mouths shut —
for is the mouth not the doorway through which food enters?

(Poet Murugusundaram completes his poem on the “mouth.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
Rajendran comes to the eye of this assembly to sing of the eye.
I have seen his poetry with my own eye;
it is honey, sugar, rock-candy.
He will argue:
“What use are words from the mouth
when eye meets eye?”
Valluvar too gave the word “eye” abundant space in the Kural:
“Is she Death? Is she an eye?” in one;
a verse beginning with cruel brows and ending in “her eye”;
another proclaiming “two glances dwell within her painted eye”;
another on the flower-like-eyed woman;
and “The great ornament called gracious vision —
by that truth this world exists.”

<!-- scan 86 -->

Do not think that because so many Kurals stand on his side
I cannot give judgement.
With the brain that is trapped in none of these five senses,
we shall think, open our eyes and decide;
until then let us close our eyes and lose ourselves in his poetic delight.

(Poet Rajendran completes his poem on the “eye.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
Tamilanban is going to break the nose!
Though the spectacles needed by both eyes
are carried by the nose between them,
it generously surrendered the right to be called “nose-glasses”
and let them remain “eye-glasses” —
from where did such broad-heartedness come?
He will beat the drum for the nose and enter the battle.
Valluvar too spoke of the nose
that makes the anicham flower wilt merely by smelling it.
Without the nose there is no breath;
so if my friend asks judgement in his favour,
I shall simply exhale through my mouth and show him.

Rather than stand before the friend who sings of the nose,
let us think about the course of his poem.
Betel is presented with a wedding invitation;
here we have been invited with betel to drink the fragrance of Tamil.
Let us drink.

(Poet Erode Tamilanban completes his poem on the “nose.”)

<!-- scan 87 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
like a sea that sings in waves,
like Mamallapuram that sings of art,
there is Ponni Valavan who sings poetry.
Valavan of the Kaveri was Karikala Valavan;
this one is dark-bodied Valavan — yet
majestic-bodied Valavan.
He comes to speak of the ear.

“Wealth among wealth is the wealth of hearing;
that wealth is chief among all wealth.”
He will show this Kural and ask for the prize.
True — for argument's sake! But...
“Words of such quality that they bind those who hear
and make even those who do not hear desire them” —
only when the mouth speaks can they enter the ear and become wealth.
So I shall save myself with that reply.
Let his argument proceed;
let classical Tamil song enter our ear and sweeten it.
We shall lend our ears; come, sir, with your poem!
Valavan has arrived.
See the complexion I described; see the poetic skill.
If you place the two of us side by side,
you will see a two-coloured flag.

(Poet Ponni Valavan completes his poem on the “ear.”)

Chairman Kalaignar's concluding poem:

In a land that drapes poets in silk,
body, mouth, eye, nose and ear — Tamil

<!-- scan 88 -->

has woven a silk cloth and draped us all.
We listened to songs roaring as they attacked one another.
If the moon and Ganga quarrel
upon Siva's red matted hair adorned with konrai,
it becomes a headache for Siva;
but under my leader(ship),
this poets' fight is a battlefield fight —
a fight joined with Tamil rock-candy!

The one who said the body is greatest will not wish to live without a mouth;
the one who said the mouth is greatest will not wish to be deaf;
the one who praised the beauty of the eye will not dare reduce the beauty of the nose;
and will the one who praised the nose say, “I will go about bumping into things without eyes?”

Those who climbed this stage and sang possess all of them.
So is this a useless quarrel?
No — it is a quarrel of vīṇā-Tamil,
a quarrel that sharpens knowledge.
If this quarrel takes place throughout the country,
learning will grow; novelty will arise;
Tamil will gain new life.
Continue this pure work!
As long as breath passes through the nose,
serve Muttamil!
Even after it ceases, guard Tamil through your fame.
Turn everything the eye sees into poetry;
turn it into poetry of awakening,
and begin the duty of putting it into action —

<!-- scan 89 -->

begin the duty, begin the duty!
Hear what is good with the ear; cast away what is not;
open the mouth to sing the welfare of classical Tamils.
Even if you say only one thing, say a good thing.
If a body exists without all I have said,
then that body is not *mey* — truth — but *poi*, falsehood.

Greetings.

✤

## Translator notes

- Tamil `மெய்` carries both “body” and “truth”; the closing line explicitly exploits that double meaning, retained with the Tamil terms in the final couplet.
- Much of the poem depends on chains of puns among the five senses and on Tirukkural citations; English keeps the argumentative structure even where the exact sound-play cannot be reproduced.
- Parenthetical notices marking other poets' completed contributions are retained as source structure; their separate poems are not present in the canonical item.

## Item 8 — The Pilavanga Year

<!-- scan 90 -->

The Pilavanga Year

A presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at a *kaviyarangam* broadcast by Chennai Radio
on 13 April 1968.

★

Greetings.

Pushing the “Pilavanga” year behind,
“Keelaka” has burst open like a clove pod.
Let us wish that people everywhere in the world
may live without riots and without rancour.
Though I say the year's birth is in Thai,
this has entered common practice, so let us offer greetings.
Why practise such thrift in blessing,
blessing only one day in a whole year?
With full mouth and full heart I will bless on many days,
and I greet the poets who have come to sing on the radio.

Sleep has broken; long eyes have opened.
The maiden Chithirai, a golden woman bearing a seal,
has risen.
Poets of ten-and-a-half purity,
and learned Valluvar whom this earth praises —
let us enter the Kural he composed
and taste the thought it offers here.

<!-- scan 91 -->

There is no fame merely by birth;
fame comes from the excellence one earns —
