so he sang in the Book of Virtue.
Good people have come to open that book in song
so that we do not forget it.

I am a bird that does not fly,
a chick of poetry distressed because wings of grammar have not sprouted;
a bird that does not fly,
a bird that does not fly from nest to nest,
a poetry chick whose wings have not sprouted —
let only a tail not sprout, as it has for some!
That is the little poet I call myself.

Do not think I say this from modesty.
Feet, metre, linkage, ornament and prosody —
all of them only block my song!
You must grant me an exemption;
otherwise I shall rise saying I must walk away from you.

One day while asleep I saw a dream.
Do not ask, “Then what — do dreams come while awake?”
Gandhi dreamed while awake and won freedom;
Anna, smiling through the abuse of many,
dreamed while awake of good government,
and soon obtained it.
Let that stand.

What was this servant's sleeping dream?

<!-- scan 92 -->

You are eager to know!
Six women, six lovely maidens,
with bodies like hammered gold,
soft as lily flowers,
beauties whom anyone would long to drink in with the eyes.
South of the village called Honey-Pond,
under the shade beside the public tank,
I sat in a car with my eyes closed in sleep.

“We are jackfruits held within the root;
is there anywhere in the world a maiden like us?” they said.
“What is your request? Tell me,” I asked
those women with kovai-fruit lips.
Before I could finish,
“Choose one beauty from the six;
live your life rejoicing with her.
Tell us now without delay —
time is passing without interest!” they said.

“Are you girls from a Chettiar household?” I asked.
“There is no caste that butts and clashes, no religion —
we are humankind, that is all,” they said.
I too had desire,
but the choosing bewildered me.
All six had one complexion, one nature,
no difference in age;
in the twin fruit trees upon their dew-flower bodies,
in the grace of their walk,
in the spear-army of their eyes,
in the shopfront of their lips,
there was no contradiction; even their smiles were one.
If one woman's body was silk,
all six were silk;
if one was crystal,
all six were crystal;
if one spoke in parrot-speech,
all six were parrots.

<!-- scan 93 -->

If one spoke the path of grace,
so did all six;
if one spoke in the language of the Kural,
all six spoke the same.

What was I to do? How could I choose?
With spear-eyes they wounded my heart,
made music in my chest for love,
and by a wonder that made earth itself,
the six maidens together
plucked out my eyes and left me suffering in confusion.

Very well, I thought: ask their names,
then choose the one we want.
Each gave a name:
“Possession of Grace” was one;
“Self-Control” another;
“Sweet Speech” another;
“Forbearance” said a flower-maiden;
“Freedom from Anger” smiled another;
“My name is Not Doing Hurt,”
said the sixth, stepping forward.

If self-control is present, grace comes dancing;
where sweet speech lives, forbearance reigns;
a mind without anger
becomes a thumbai-grove where non-harm flowers.
Only the names differ — what else differs?
“Good women of equal age, equal nature, equal face —
who is the mother who bore you?” I asked.
“Tamil,” they said.
And their father? Valluvar!

<!-- scan 94 -->

They embraced me crying, “Anna!”
“Dear sisters, to take your hands
I shall bring six poets here.
After hearing the poems they speak,
you may garland them and rejoice,” I said.
They agreed, and the poets arrived.
But — one of those poets was a woman!
“Where is the male poet for the sixth woman?” they asked.
I started awake.
With the dream shattered, I came to this poetry gathering.
The poet who broke the dream, Soundara Kailasam,
is now in the middle of this assembly.
So, since it is good for her now to speak to the assembly,
and since she will speak on “Sweet Speech,”
I shall speak no more and step aside.
The lady will thunder on the theme “Sweet Speech.”

(Mrs. Soundara Kailasam completes her poem on “Sweet Speech.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, self-control.
Knowledge stands as the field in which self-control bears fruit;
like a chutney it cures disease —
it cures the disease of arrogance too.

<!-- scan 95 -->

We say feet are for walking, not kicking!
If a knife is only for folding away, it is unnecessary;
a knife is needed to cut fruit, not take life.
Courage rising from restraint is true courage;
the loud courage of the tongue is counterfeit courage.
The five senses must be drawn in like a tortoise,
but does that mean wandering the town
mute, blind and deaf?
The eye restrained at the eyelid-door;
the tongue restrained in the prison of the mouth;
breath restrained in the air;
the body restrained in the grave;
a good mind restrained in the light of reason.

To thank those who ordered me to preside is assembly-modesty;
to bless those who begged me to preside would be swelling of the head.
“Guard the tongue, whoever you are” — the first step of restraint;
to keep a “mad dog” inside the mouth is the upper stair of arrogance.
If it rolls out from there, the heart-pressure itself may collapse without form!
To offer all this in the assembly,
Manikkavasagam comes.
Let us receive him.

(Poet Manikkavasagam completes his poem on “Self-Control.”)

<!-- scan 96 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, forbearance.
The crescent waits and becomes the moon;
those with forbearance wait and come to rule the land.
The crane waits until prey comes;
do not call that forbearance —
it is strategy the bird learned upon the earth.
The one who makes enduring insult the highest virtue,
Valluvar said, is like earth that bears those who dig it.
Those who insult possess neither true greatness
nor the art of scribbling abuse.
Following behind those who praise is bad;
attacking those who insult is worse.
The heart of one rich in forbearance is a wave-filled sea;
those who strike it seeking to stain it
will end up gasping like fish on dry land.
It is easy to stab with deficient words;
it is hard to bear them with a full heart.
To pour this out under the title “Forbearance,”
comes the eager poet Aatral Arasu.

(Poet Aatral Arasu completes his poem on “Forbearance.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, freedom from anger.
“Anger kills the self,”
said Tamil sage Valluvar in a Kural.
Those in anger leap up, eyes reddened;
and those floating in drink stagger,

<!-- scan 97 -->

eyes reddened — the two are alike.
Jealousy is no good;
helplessness is no good;
ignorance destroys a country;
like non-killing, one great goodness
is freedom from anger.
If alienation enters, home is ruined, country is ruined.
Anger has a hundred faces:
teeth grinding,
lips pressed,
eyes bulging —
a hundred forms.
Its hundred faces shift like drunken frenzy;
Noor Muhammad's bright song will cool it.

(Poet Noor Muhammad completes his poem on “Freedom from Anger.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, not doing hurt.
This too is an “-amai,” but a good one;
not learning it is a great evil.
“If struck on one cheek, show the other,” said Jesus.
“To punish those who do hurt, do them good
until they are ashamed,” said the Kural.
All this talk of *innaa* — hurt —
those who pour out whatever comes to the tongue,
saying “my tongue is simply trained so,”
within the ranks of intelligence —

<!-- scan 98 -->

will stand neither in front nor behind;
they will lie in a corner.
Suratha, poet of flavour,
will explain all this with stacked words.

(Poet Suratha completes his poem on “Not Doing Hurt.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, possession of grace.
“This world is for those with wealth;
the other world for those with grace” —
Valluvar lit a lamp in darkened hearts.
There are many inquiries into what that “other world” is.
We know those who say it is a good world
where some living beings leave this one.
Should we believe fibre can be peeled from stone?
People peeled words and called them heaven and hell,
scattered sesame and said one could travel there.
Let the whole truth bloom like a rose born among thorns.
Where is knowledge? Where is fruit? All becomes waste.
Heaven written in palm-leaf and letters —
helping the poor here is heaven;
practising simplicity is sweet heaven;
speaking sweet words here is heaven;
living after death through one's work is deathless heaven.
For attaining this world, he said, one needs grace;
pleasant poet Thooran has come to explain it.

(Poet Thooran completes his poem on “Possession of Grace.”)

<!-- scan 99 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

I thought he would heap flowers — poetry-flowers;
I was deceived.
I waited thinking that if he gathered flowers
I could string them into a garland.

Instead,
he has beautifully tied one garland for each person:
one for the feet of Mother Tamil;
one for Valluvar's shoulders;
one each for Virtue, Wealth and Love;
and because one garland remains,
what great person shall we seek to wear it?
On this New-Year day let us all wear it.
The world cannot be reformed in one day;
let us strive on many days — first
reform ourselves, then set out to reform the country.
“If a ruler is easy of access and not harsh of speech,
is he then fit to rule?” —
he would not hesitate to enter the witness-box.
Easy of access, disciple of Gandhi;
not harsh in speech, servant of Buddha;
it is good that such a one rule;
he is fitting testimony to the majesty of the Kural.

He speaks sweetly.
He does no hurt.
He guards restraint.
He honours grace.
He does not fall short in forbearance.
He does not know the surge of anger.
This

<!-- scan 100 -->

path of love will make peace prevail.
On the other path turmoil is born:
lifting knives, raising riots,
turning mustard seed into mountain —
a storm that disturbs the sea of loving peace.
To turn it into a breeze requires clarity,
great knowledge.
That is what we spread through the Kural.
We shall see victory in our principle.

Greetings.

✤

## Translator notes

- `பிலவங்க` and `கீலக` are traditional cyclic year-names; the title retains **Pilavanga** rather than replacing it with a Gregorian equivalent.
- The six women in the dream personify six Tirukkural virtues: grace, self-control, sweet speech, forbearance, freedom from anger and non-harm.
- The source uses repeated Tamil suffix/pun patterns (`-ஆமை`, including `ஆமை`, tortoise) that cannot be reproduced fully in English; the conceptual and comic progression is retained.
- Parenthetical poet hand-offs are source structure; the guest poets' poems themselves are not contained in this canonical item.

## Item 9 — Love or Valour?

<!-- scan 101 -->

### Love or Valour?

A poetic debate held in Salem on 4 August 1968
on the theme “In Tamil life, which is greater — love or valour?”
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi's presiding poetic introduction.

What comes to a field that the plough does not reach?
Grass and weeds.
If the plough comes, paddy wealth comes.
What comes when clouds do not arrive in the sky?
Severe famine.
If clouds come, the fruit of delight comes.
What comes from the “Ayan” hill when the Chera does not come?
What comes from Servarayan hill? Iron comes.
If all come together, stability comes;
if all join, this good state comes.
Stability comes.
A chariot moves when many pull it; likewise
honey-iron comes here in Salem.

<!-- scan 102 -->

Whenever I come to this town I wonder:
will the iron here never melt?
Before a demand dries into a fallen leaf,
let us show the swelling support that asks the government to fulfil it.
I came to speak of the wealth of the country and city,
its forests and fruits;
I have turned back toward honey-iron.
It is not because I forgot!

Honey means love;
iron means valour.
Do you understand my course now?
In Salem, filled with people who weave,
poets whose trade is weaving poetry have come
to sing the Tamil way of life that divided land
as neithal, kurinji, mullai, marutham and paalai.

“When will rain fall at Marakkara?”
“Irrigation must be carried out without fail.”
“Mettur must reach fullness.”
I wander with these as my aim;
yet they told me to harvest poetry too.

Gathering poets who conquer time through thought,
to draw the form of poetry,
Ganapathi, head of the Salem Tamil Manram,
welcomes with fragrant Tamil words this quarrel:
Is the valour that fights with a spear-like hand greater,
or the love that fights with spear-like eyes?

<!-- scan 103 -->

One side will say the love given by a fish-eyed woman is greatest;
the other will thunder that valour is the crown of worldly fame.
I am the chairman between the two.
Shall I build a bridge between them or kindle a fire of enmity?
You will say, build the bridge; you will ask for roads —
because I became minister for highways!
But if I build here at the beginning
a bridge between poets dripping Tamil honey,
there will be no path for ideas to leap like deer.
The organ in our head that does not sleep even when this flesh sleeps
is called the brain!
Many in this world do not have it.
Some who have it have only a sesame-seed's worth,
and even that is full of liquor — so they scribble as they please.
These poets, however,
have heads full of brain and have come to this assembly without losing balance.
I will not build a bridge and stop the Tamil battle between them.
When hostility rises among poets, Tamil gains novelty;
so I sow the seed of quarrel among them!
Let us wait and see who wins.

One poet sang, “Jasmine, a white conch on which the bee blows...”

<!-- scan 104 -->

“Not so, not so, Pugazhendi!”
Ottakoothar is said to have leapt up and asked:
“Only after the flower is plucked can a conch be blown at its stem;
is it not strange for a bee to blow upon the white petals?”
Pugazhendi immediately replied:
“Koothar, hear my answer!
Drunk on honey, not knowing front from back,
the bee blew there!”

Did not that quarrel between great poets
give Tamil two more poems?
So poets, I shall cultivate hostility among you — sing!
Like urging racehorses onward,
people under the pavilion, roar!
Some songs may taste like fenugreek;
in these songs let us see valour spitting red flame,
and the manner in which love is spoken in classical Tamil.

Those who were one in kinship like one life
have split into two and clash here.
Will the old bond stick again? you ask.
It will — an old bond that sticks!
Grafted mango kinship is natural in Salem.
So those whose bond will later stick
may now cut and stab each other here.
At the words cut and stab, do not leap up in valour saying,
“Ah, the chairman has praised valour!”
There is an eye-cut even in love;
a body-form that stabs the heart is love's Thai-Pongal.

<!-- scan 105 -->

To fight under the title of love,
a dear one comes — leader in love, Tamilanban.
For a body wrapped only in bone and skin,
is not love the true life?
Our cultured friend, a man of our old town — Erode!
The waves of the Kaveri fight one another
out of love for the woman bathing there.
Erode Tamilanban will speak love with order,
showing not a woman who receives rows of gifts —
jackfruit clusters, ungarlanded red-banana flowers,
a peahen embraced to the chest,
all the gifts a bull might bring —
but two people who receive two lineages,
agree with each other's music,
leave their two lines and consent to become one line,
a bar of bright gold in a giving garden.
Her smile's music will sound as love's music.
Without the music of that season,
can the music of valour's womb arise?
Tamilanban will ask with raised brow.
He stands as one of two formations.

Between two formations I, unable to discern their ranks, am chairman!
Even if neither side digests my thought, I have said it.
These are not merely two teams:
one is love's half-sari,
the other valour's grand procession.
Why should I stand between them?
You ask: is the middle a mortar to stand in?
I say it is an *udukku* drum;
I am today the mischievous priest who beats it
to summon a furious rain of Tamil!
Take up arrows of poetry,
string them with dignity.
Come, friend; come, Tamil-lover;
let this battle begin well.

(Poet Erode Tamilanban completes his poem on “Love.”)

<!-- scan 106 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

He has prepared a notebook of love,
with laughter and throbbing,
plucking flowers from the grove and mixing sweet pollen,
about the disease that buds in the morning,
becomes a full flower through the day
and blooms in the evening,
until women hide their faces behind the ends of their saris.
Young men listened briskly;
students tasted it with a little irritation;
old men regretted their age.

Now comes a man with a book of rebuttal:
“What is love — an eggplant?
Valour is the chilli!”
He shakes his head to collide,
and comes to show the path of valour in argument.

When a warrior asked an old mother,
“Where is your son even now?”
she, swelling with joy, replied:

<!-- scan 107 -->

“I do not know where the tiger went;
he must have gone to the battlefield.
This is the cave where that tiger lived,”
and showed her womb.
That *Purappaatu*, that praise-song,
that song of valour honoured by noble Tamils!
The warrior who threw his spear in battle and killed an elephant,
then grew weary seeking another spear against the enemy,
rejoiced when he saw the spear embedded in his own chest —
the Kural that says this is valour.

With gold-fast Tamil, the man from Vaniyambadi will argue thus.
If I name the town, should I not name the man?
He is renowned Abdul Rahman,
rich in words like a swelling sea,
a pure man who does not let any harmful deed in the country
rest like a drop of dew upon bamboo leaf.
His song has not left my mind.
He cannot bear the claim that “love is greatest”; he has not languished —
he has risen like a lion.
He will not leave love alone;
he will not let it win!
He comes for a valiant battle.
Do not fear that he will take a sword;
he will take a sheet — the sheet on which he wrote his poem.
With flashing lightning he will wound love badly.
He will say there is no time to love — no, no,
he will say there is no courage to love!
To see what happens to love,

<!-- scan 108 -->

you need not eyes but ears.
In this field-battle,
will love die?
Will valour die?
We shall see in the skill of those who perform.

(Poet Abdul Rahman completes his poem on “Valour.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
T. Ku. Natarasan comes to say love is sweet.
Is not Nataraja, moving with Ganga upon his head,
the god of the art of love?
If I say this, this Natarasan will wink and say:
“See! Siva placed love on his head
and valour under his foot” —
and make the man beneath that foot his witness,
claiming victory for love.
He looks valiant, yet speaks of love!
Two scorpions live upon his face — they are called a moustache;
his aim may be elsewhere, yet he has come as a character in this play.
He may call a duck a swan
to compare the graceful walk of beautiful women,
and say “in sweet love...”
He is of the age before domestic sourness;
let us make way for his longing. Come, younger brother!

(Poet T. Ku. Natarasan completes his poem on “Love.”)

<!-- scan 109 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
to return the blow struck by the cock of love,
the cock of valour rushes in as Murugusundaram.
Murugu means beauty; Sundaram means the same!
Such beauty upon beauty —
what wonder that instead of singing love
he sings valour?
Sword-flash,
whirling round,
enemy drained,
victory-bride,
poem about it —
let us hear pearl-Tamil,
Murugu-Tamil.

(Poet Murugusundaram completes his poem on “Valour.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
Ponni Valavan.
What connection has he with love?
Plenty — because until now he has made no marriage alliance anywhere!
So there is a connection.
In stringing garlands of Tamil flowers
he is a Sambandar of the Tevaram.
A speaker sweet as sugar; but if approached treacherously,
he is of Nakkeerar's principle in cutting off the tongue!
Even if he speaks of love, he will speak fiercely.
He will beat the drum of separation's anguish as though it were valour.
Lover, come;
give us lines of poetry.

(Poet Ponni Valavan completes his poem on “Love.”)

<!-- scan 110 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
Rajendran.
His very name holds valour and historical greatness:
Rajendra who took the Ganga;
Rajendran who saw Sivaganga.
Our friend remains in our thought,
a statue in the heart.
He will sing of valour.
Though his body shows bone, his heart is steel.
This dear friend, filled with love,
will answer what was said before and make Tamil valour resound.

(Poet Rajendran completes his poem on “Valour.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
advocate for valour Abdul Rahman
will offer another one or two arguments of substance;
I shall give judgement afterward.

(Poet Abdul Rahman delivers his concluding argument.)

<!-- scan 111 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
advocate for love Erode Tamilanban
will argue.
My judgement follows.

(Poet Tamilanban delivers his concluding argument.)

Chairman Kalaignar's judgement poem:

Beloved people, elders!
Do not think what has happened so far was empty quarrelling;
it was vigorous quarrelling.
I rejoice and bless this kind of quarrel to happen throughout the country.
To the friend who argued valour is greatest,
love is not something bitter.
The friends who plead that love is greatest
may be cowards — no fault in that!
But only cowards love cowards.
Did not *Mullaikkali* thunder:
“The maiden will not embrace, even in another birth,
the man who fears the horns of the killing bull”?
The Tamil woman garlanded the warrior
who feared not even death while subduing the charging bull.
She told him:
“As you place your face upon a woman's breast,
place your head between the bull's horns
and win the glorious wound.”
Only after that happened did she consent in heart and marry him.
Yet it would be wrong to argue
that valour is therefore merely the first step in the epic of love.
They possessed courage unafraid even of death
in order to attain love.
Remembering this,
does love not appear greater?

<!-- scan 112 -->

“Where is the sword in the battle-hand?
Where the gem-like chest?
Where are the diamond shoulders
that never turned their back
whoever came on the face of battle?”
*Kalingathu Parani* roars so.
Yet the same *Kalingathu Parani* sings love:
“Dazed by the joy of union,
when the moon's light withdraws,
they take moonlight itself for cloth and dress in it...”
*Palaikkali* tells of the warrior
who set out toward the battlefront,
heard his maiden's sorrow,
melted and returned.
The Kural that shows the man
who has sent his hand-spear with the elephant
also pours honey of love:
“When eye meets eye...”
And *Kalithogai*, giving love among deer
like a fruit-segment inside honey,
tells of the stag that gives its own shade
to the doe suffering for lack of pleasant shade,
while also displaying the elephant's valour
as it battles the vengai tree and giving glory to kurinji.

<!-- scan 113 -->

In the legendary Ramayana story,
Indrajit, rare treasure of Ravana,
became Sulochana's husband through savoury love;
when enemy forces grew, he lost his head and became a hero.
Before losing it he threw his enemies into disorder.
The hero who saw Nikumbala,
after destroying a great mass of foes,
fell as a warrior,
son of crowned Ravana.

In the Bharata, Abhimanyu, Vatsala's beloved,
would give strong kisses — one, ten, a hundred —
upon pearl-like lips,
and receive clusters of love-fruit.
That unripe young shoot entered the battlefront,
spared no life,
and then died with unfading fame.
He loved the golden-lotus woman
whose hair was like the Courtallam falls;
did he sell away valour for that love in battle? No.
He remains a stream in epic song.

A king's daughter was won by a poet-king's son —
Ambikapathi and Amaravathi's love.
What happened then?
A heroic death.

<!-- scan 114 -->

Why only old tales?
You will ask for modern examples.
There are many; I shall say some.
Edward VIII gave up his crown for love —
does that mean every greatness accrues to love?
Love is enough to say, “Let the woman die with me”;
it takes courage, a spirit of sacrifice, to say “Let there be no such end.”
Why go so far?
An innocent fellow called Seeni falls for Soni next door
and hurriedly jumps the fence;
is that not a kind of courage too?
Valour for love; love among the valiant —
it is not fitting to separate the two
like butter and buttermilk.

Those defeated in love hang by a noose around the neck.
When love is refused,
they search pond and river saying death alone is the path,
to throw away life.
They do this because love alone possesses them;
if valour were joined as love's guard,
it would protect lovers like a god.

Bharati accused society:
“In drama and epic, when it is love
the people marvel and say it is good;
but in the house, by the well, in the village,
when love appears they roar
and build the bier to kill it.”

<!-- scan 115 -->

Bharathidasan too sang:
“With the fire of love I would leap the sea toward you;
the chain called caste has bound my feet, girl.”
Are these not for people in whom valour mingles with love?
Therefore, people, love!
Love with valour mixed into it.
Love without valour breeds despair;
valour without love breeds harshness.
There is no courage in life;
there is no tenderness in life.

I announce my judgement to the assembly:
Love and valour are twin children born embracing one another.
For this pair there is no family-planning restriction —
beget them freely!

Greetings.

✤

## Translator notes

- The canonical title is `காதலா - வீரமா?`; the contents witness `காதலா! - வீரமா?` is retained separately and not merged into a hybrid title.
- Salem/iron/honey, weaving, bridge/highways and numerous other passages depend on Tamil sound-play and contemporary political humour; English keeps the argumentative chain rather than inventing replacement puns.
- Classical citations from *Mullaikkali*, *Kalingathu Parani*, *Palaikkali*, *Kalithogai* and Tirukkural are translated in their source-facing role here, not substituted with outside editions.
- References to self-harm occur as part of Kalaignar's argument about love and despair; they are preserved as source rhetoric without elaboration.

## Item 10 — Six in the Noble Scripture

<!-- scan 116 -->

### Six in the Noble Scripture

A presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled “Six in the Noble Scripture,”
held in Chennai on behalf of Tamil Vattam
on 10 August 1968.

★

Learned elders seated in the middle of this assembly,
great people rich in knowledge,
beloved mothers,
and poets of distinction who have come with Tamil-rich verse
to show six figures from the noble scripture, the Tirukkural —
I bow to you.

Twelve months, and four more beyond that —
sixteen months have passed
since I took office as minister.
In between,
at five or six poetry gatherings
they asked me to preside,
keeping me in office without letting me forget classical Tamil!
They did not invite me out of indulgence saying I was a poet.
Every day
countless people send slips;
I wander among petitions

<!-- scan 117 -->

with no rest.
Even a spinning top, after circling, lies down;
I roll from town to town like a kicked ball.
Though the body has grown lean,
life itself has not grown weak;
in the heart —

<!-- scan 118 -->

with Tamil giving flame,
how can weariness settle?
O charging-bull Tamil of language —
sea-rising, mountain-Tamil of delight —
grove-leaf, flavour-filled Tamil —
you climb my body, climb my life and keep me living;
I bow to you.

I have no wish to oppose grammar.
Grammar itself is the beloved child that sulks with me.
Even if not one of my songs agrees with grammar,
I have the courage that the song I give will not kill Tamil feeling.
Like a neem twig entering a sugarcane mill,
I have bravely entered this gathering of poets.
In this flower-garden where rare songs bloom,
your rare songs become flowers in Tamil's garland;
you are bees dancing around them;
I am only a splinter moving among you!
If an ant can move an iron pillar,
if an ant can make an iron pillar move,
then even my bare song can defeat you!

V. K. C. Natarasan, worthy organiser
who founded Tamil Vattam and conducts this Tamil festival,
said, “Come.”
Because he invited me with affection,
I said I must come lest he circle around me again.
So I came to preside.

<!-- scan 119 -->

A *vattam* — circle — is a name for classification,
a component that works to a plan.
The dot on a maiden's forehead is a circle;
the moon pouring light is a circle;
the far-raying sun is a circle;
and the marks some students receive are a complete zero!
For the act of love, the bed is a circle;
for the sweet-speaking child, the cradle is a circle;
for women's life, chastity's path is a circle;
for warriors' greatness, the battlefield is a circle;
for the farmer's plough, the field is a circle;
for a friend's relationship, the house of love is a circle;
for the ascetic's purity, the mind-cage is a circle;
for the base man's life, the circle is unknown —
only after seeing him do we arrive at the title “base man.”

First, under the title “Warrior,”
Ganesan will beat the drum.
“I shall die amid opposition,”
my leader's two eyes shedding tears;
“I shall beg for that death...”
Would the Tirukkural forget?
It did not forget.
The old *Purappaatu* is an opening song
where even deer-kind display valour
and put male-kind to shame.
Poet Sa. Ka. Anjar will speak;
he will sing the “Warrior.”

<!-- scan 120 -->

(Poet Kamban Adippodi Sa. Ganesan, M.L.C., completes his poem on the “Warrior.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

What shall I say of the new speed he gave,
making even reclining letters leap and spring?
The Kural drew the grammar of honour for warriors:
“Like the kavari deer that cannot live when its hair is lost,
they give up life when honour comes under threat.”
Some who forgot it changed the line:
“Like the kavari deer that cannot live when life is lost,
they shave their hair when honour comes” —
and go bald in the election race!
“Better the spear aimed at an elephant and missed
than the arrow that killed a forest hare” —
this is honour's language.
Yet today's “Kural warrior” says:
“If a cat crosses the road, life is finished!”

If a warrior sees the field,
the farmer's son sees the paddy-field.
Going toward war is the warrior's work;
sleeping upon the haystack is the farmer's work.
The warrior's aim is to take the enemy's crown;
the transplanting field's aim is to take a bundle of seedlings.
Farmer and warrior have many likenesses;
there is only one difference:
the farmer makes people live;
the warrior makes people die!

To speak the farmer's full greatness
comes Sarathi, lover of the plough —
Parthasarathi,
turning from charioteer into charioteer of song,
driving a chariot of classical Tamil —
no, no, driving a plough!
He sings of the farmer.

<!-- scan 121 -->

(Poet Na. Parthasarathi completes his poem on the “Farmer.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Because we have said farming is foremost,
the farmer has no master;
and one who sings of the farmer has no master either — know it!
“When she sees those who sit saying ‘we have nothing,’
the good woman called Earth laughs.”
Seeing those who grieve, “I have nothing,”
she says:
“What is lacking, my son?
Look at the soil-richness in my cracked belly;
dig and water-richness will swell;
dig far enough and I shall give diamonds and gems.
Do not live in anger — increase the farming clan!”
Thus the earth-maiden stands laughing.
Farmer's son, understand the earth's smile;
labour and obtain new prosperity at once.

Next,
a friend comes.
Supporting the friends who come after,
loving them,
even while lying down to sleep I have kept them in mind;
even when they resolved to ruin me,
I approached with friendship and sorrow,
and because their fall could no longer be prevented,
all the friendships I ended make a long story.
In what should a friend be first?
In games of pleasure?
Or in the culture of rebuking frankly?
On what should a friend lead?
Professor Ezhil Mudhalvan will string letters into a garland;
a Tamil cuckoo in a grove of poetry.
Let him sing; let the friend speak.

<!-- scan 122 -->

(Professor Ezhil Mudhalvan completes his poem on the “Friend.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

One chapter for friendship,
another for examining friendship,
one for old intimacy,
and separately, with care, chapters for “evil friendship” and “unfit friendship” —
perhaps Valluvar too suffered greatly through friends, as we have!
If a friend is as Ezhil Mudhalvan described,
what better emblem than Pisiranthaiyar and Kopperuncholan?
At a wedding, when the war-scroll arrived,
Mohammed Khan set aside honeyed feast,
joined Desing's army,
and became a page that will not fall from the history book.

We have stood among men long enough —
warrior, farmer,
and companion sowing friendship.
After so much time,
we need a woman to rest our weariness!
A poet comes to obtain one for us.
“When will she be born? When will she grow?
Will it be our grandson's wedding by then?” you ask.
Chandha-Kavimani Tamilazhagan
brings the woman he has given.
Look at her;
lend your ears to the Sindhu-Tamil
inside the little casket given by the rhythm-poet.

<!-- scan 123 -->

(Poet Tamilazhagan completes his poem on the “Woman.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

The poet said of women:
if she says “rain,” rain will fall.
It is not right to place a chaste woman under that harsh test
and throw blame on her if rain does not fall.
The Kural that praises woman by saying
“rain falls when she says fall”
should establish the thought that
a good woman is *like* rain that falls when asked.
If an old idea raises its head, drive it away.

Next: why should renunciation follow woman?
Why should saffron hide all our eyes?
For some renunciates, when a woman arrives,
renunciation itself gets renounced.
There are many in Tamil Nadu who have taken two renunciations:
first renouncing the sweet householder's life,
living on fruit juice, milk, curd and water-pot;
then when the heavy body has grown fat,
openly renouncing renunciation!
The clever ones remain like fruit hidden behind leaves.
Is all this concealment proper?
Poet Thooran, rich in art,
comes near and opens his heart.
Do not think he renounces;
he opens the poetic heart.

<!-- scan 124 -->

(Poet Periyasami Thooran completes his poem on the “Ascetic.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Renunciation may be good,
but it must remain pure.
To continue merely as costume is evil.
For a heart that catches like camphor in fire,
renunciation is cotton in the Aadi wind.
By day he says life is poison;
by night he says panchamirtham is necessary.
The mind needs strength to condemn this without fear.
Do you not know the story of sage Viswamitra,
who gave up royal bed, jewelled crown and ornaments,
went to the forest,
and became Menaka's faithful friend?
“No shaving, no growing the hair is needed
if one abandons what the world condemns.”
One who rejects this is one kind among base men.
To sing their many kinds,
Tiruchitrambalam comes,
bringing sweet Tamil.

<!-- scan 125 -->

(Poet Tiruchitrambalam completes his poem on the “Base Man.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Good people who listened with smiling faces to all the poems spoken,
and my dear Navalar seated like the full moon!
I shall give the conclusion; after it you may leave.
A manly warrior,
a loving woman,
a friend rich in companionship,
a base man surrounded by harm,
an ascetic accustomed to penance,
a man who farms —
these six were chosen from the noble scripture
and they asked that explanations be given;
they left out the minister!
Perhaps they thought it improper to keep him nearby and criticise him.

<!-- scan 126 -->

“The minister is one who masters instrument, time, method
and the difficult deed to be done.”

“The minister is one able to discern,
act after choosing,
and speak decisively.”

“Beside a minister who plots ruin,
seventy crores of enemies may be preferable.”

Better seventy crores of enemies
than a minister who seeks degrading deeds.
Those who do not agree
with these banana-sweet Kural thoughts on ministers —
how can they attain distinction?
Leaving undying light,
they will hang like bats
inside the darkness of the hall.
For those of us who rule the land,
Valluvar said sleeplessness, learning and courage
must never depart.
Though what I learned is little,
I stayed awake all night to write this poem;
there was no other rest.
Because courage is present,
without ornament, metre, linkage or prosodic feet,
I have come to mount the poetry stage.
The Kural spoken for ruling the earth
I altered for ruling poetry!
So, people of the poetry gathering,
I ask you: draw the bow hard — but throw gently.
Why a separate explanation for a minister?
If he does not fear enemies, he becomes a warrior;
if he does not fear blame, he becomes a base man;
in showing love, he becomes a woman.

<!-- scan 127 -->

More than ploughing with a good plough,
if he ploughs well with words, he becomes a fine farmer;
though opportunities are many,
if he gives up the comforts that come, he becomes an ascetic;
and because he understands the Kural that says
“Friendship is not for laughter alone; its purpose is to rebuke when one goes too far,”
he becomes a capable friend.

For a woman, greatness lies in guarding herself;
for a warrior, in the honour-wound;
for a friend, in gratitude;
for an ascetic, in the grave;
for a base man, in every greatness he happens to see;
for the potter, an earthen pot!
My greatness is simply to be here among you.
If that greatness remains, it is enough for me.

Greetings.
Long live.

## Translator notes

- `அருமறையில் அறுவர்` refers to six human types drawn from the Tirukkural; **Six in the Noble Scripture** keeps that compact title without silently expanding it to “Six in the Tirukkural.”
- The poem repeatedly plays on `வட்டம்` (circle/association/zero and related senses), agricultural and martial vocabulary, and ministerial language; English preserves the sequence but cannot reproduce every sound-pun.
- Tirukkural lines are translated in the form required by Kalaignar's immediate argument rather than replaced with an external published translation.
- Parenthetical poet hand-offs remain source structure; the guest poets' full poems are not present in this canonical item.

## Item 11 — New Path

<!-- scan 128 -->

### New Path

(Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the agricultural *kaviyarangam* titled “New Path,”
held at the Moorthy Art Hall, Kumbakonam,
on 13 August 1968.)

Minister for Agriculture,
whose administrative strength enhances the Tamil government here!
Poets who have come with the swordsmanship of verse
to display the great prowess of farming!
Seeing the emerald gems produced by her own child,
Mother Earth rejoices: “This is *nanjai*, wet cultivation.”
Seeing the emerald grains of paddy produced by Thanjavur,
she rejoices again in that fertile *nanjai* land.
At Thanjavur's tail-end, where Puhar lies,
on Thanjavur's coast, where Poompuhar lies,
none here will exceed my words; therefore,
with no thought of complaint — *puhār* —
let us remember the ancient Puhar festival,
the earlier Karthigai festival—

<!-- scan 129 -->

before the rains themselves stood bewildered.
Not for one day or two, but many days,
though the seasonal rain failed,
you stood like the pole star,
lifting Thanjavur's victorious brow. Long live!
Since the world is sustained by what descends from the sky,
is not rain itself nectar?
Is it not rain that becomes food for those who eat,
and itself food for those who drink?
Is it not rain that destroys,
and then becomes support and raises up those it has ruined?
Though the world cannot exist without water,
our forebears knew that water itself could not be ordered without rivers.
They found rivers — and so they found rice.
How many rivers upon this earth
made patterned paddy smile,
spread green silk,
and burned away the demon of poverty!
Good people who stand by the house,
by the forest,
and by the election vote —
come for a while to the riverbank;
come today to the bank of my song!

Seeing the maiden Kaveri dance at Kudamurutti,
Veera Cholan comes rowing,
saying, “This is the place to fall in love —
this is Kollidam, the place for love!”
He bends toward her saying, “Give me your hand” —
and the Vaigai bends;

<!-- scan 130 -->

he murmurs, “Your teeth are pearl-gems” — Muthar.
Kaveri winks:
“My sister-in-law is coming;
hide yourself, dear!”
He replies, “Let Manniyar come.
Is she a Kallanai dam across our love?
Kamini, Bamini, Namini — we are lovers;
yes, now tell the others that our wedding is coming.
Will the fire of love cool, my dear?
Come near — will that fire cool?”
She says, “No one should think wrongly of us;
no one should seek to divide our love;
even if others do not sever our bond, dear,
my shyness holds me back.”
Veera Cholan quickly embraces her:
“Come, let us first drink from the Palar.”
The turmeric-faced maiden, leaning on his lap, asks,
“When is the wedding, dear?”
“It will be grandly held under the leadership of the Periyar,” he says.

Do not think I merely joined rivers together
to weave a love story!
Wherever possible let us set their courses right
and increase the wealth of the rivers.
“The ploughman is the linchpin of the world;
though men toil, farming remains foremost” —
let us rise and thank them,
with Valluvar himself offering his blessing.

<!-- scan 131 -->

His life blossoms in the mud!
The farmer is a king:
a crown of seedlings rests on his head.
The farmer is a warrior:
there is always a “war” in his field — a haystack-war.
The farmer is a painter:
every field is a picture he has drawn.
The farmer is a god,
for he protects living beings.
Let us honour the sacred wealth
that labours beneath the feet,
with the *kuruvai* crop before us.
From the day the land was born you have laboured;
you give your very life for the country's welfare.
As long as strength remains in the body
you lie out in the fields.
You reckon in the old measures — *kalam*, *marakkal*, *padi* —
and when the man who ploughed calculates his account,
you purse your lips and say,
“Not even an *uzhakku* remains.”
Because we asked why this condition persists,
we found a path toward rising progress.
A path found should not remain only on paper;
nor is it good for it to end with a *kaviyarangam* song.
People of Tamil Nadu lying at home, wake up!
On this Independence Day, rise with urgency!
You roam forest and field,
you circle among goats and cattle;
if old methods of cultivation are changed,
there is a way to reap a much fuller yield.
The dark condition in which only an *uzhakku* remained
will disappear like dawn whitening the east.

<!-- scan 132 -->

I am not calling you
to raise a crop in one night like Nandan.
Under the karuvel tree the “mirasudar” shouts,
“Kanda! Kadamba! Karuppa! Munia!”;
cattle are tethered,
the plough breaks the human back,
the manure is what those animals kindly provide,
and the bull goes round because the goad pierces it
while water is poured.
Into this inherited mode of farming
we are joining science,
so that it may move with the speed of a spinning top.
In Western countries educated people sow and harvest;
they plough with scientific machinery.
Here, educated people weep at the thought of growing crops
and fall into queues searching for jobs!
What other work is higher
than the work that gives food?
The old meaning of
“all who learn stand at the head-end of those who plough”
was that the learned bow before the ploughman.
Should we not give it a new arrangement?
“All who learn shall be at the head and tail of those who plough” —
the educated themselves shall farm,
at the head-reach and at the tail-reach!
We have stood behind for many days.
Let us take heart to achieve self-sufficiency in food.
Our government stands in front to reach that condition.
When we say increase production in the country,
some leave that aside
and increase production in the home!

<!-- scan 133 -->

Pearl, jewel,
little bird, casket,
eye, nose —
stop producing children beyond count under such pet names.
Instead produce food:
paddy,
varagu millet,
pearl millet,
sugarcane,
black gram,
pulses,
sesame,
horse gram.
Increase food; produce it!
At this *kaviyarangam* created to make that clear,
a number of farmer-friends have come
to plough with the plough of poetry.
I welcome them.

Do not think a man called Karumbayiram
will sing about sugarcane merely because of his name.
He will sing as sweet as sugarcane;
we can taste it like ants.
There was *durai*-rule before —
before the year forty-seven:
white colonial *durai*-rule.
Twenty years after freedom,
today it is Anna Durai's rule.
That day was the rule of the foreign *durai*;
today it is the Aduthurai variety.
Our poet will sing about it.

<!-- scan 134 -->

For forests to fragrance,
for fame to endure,
for dignity to rise,
for the nation daily
to labour,
for the hand's home to be full —
Aduthurai Twenty-Seven!

(Poet Karumbayiram finishes his poem on
“Aduthurai-27 Paddy.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Born in Aduthurai, has this Twenty-Seven
through love of English become “A.D.T.-27,” sir?
Likewise there is an “I.R.-8” paddy
that defeats another in abundant yield.
We have heard of poets who, before taking a breath,
can produce three hundred or four hundred lines;
here is one who can deliver verse in the blink of an eye.
“Kannimai” — “blink” — is his very name.
He will pour melody about paddy number eight, I.R.-8:

(Poet Kannimai finishes his poem on “I.R.-8 Paddy.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next:
another poet sings about maize.
His name is Nanjappa!

<!-- scan 135 -->

We shall say, “Give us a little Tamil, sir,”
and listen to Nanjappa's song
to drive famine away!

(Poet Nanjappa finishes his poem on “Maize.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
a poet comes carrying a *kambu* staff —
do not fear that he has come to start a quarrel.
He pours a poem
about methods of cultivating *kambu*, pearl millet.
His name is Radhakrishnan:

(Poet Radhakrishnan finishes his poem on
“Cultivation of Pearl Millet.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next —
have you seen the wonder of growing *ragi*
in a lotus pond?
A poet named Chellapandian from Thamaraikulam
brings the methods of cultivating ragi.
Listen with pleasure and put them into practice:

(Poet Chellapandian finishes his poem on “Ragi.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next,
under the name “Moovendhar,”
in the lineage of the poet-king,

<!-- scan 136 -->

Tamilarasu sings,
weaving a silken poem about hybrid sorghum.

Chairman Kalaignar's concluding poem:

Minister for Agriculture!
Good farming people!
Dear farmers of Tamil Nadu!
You have heard six men speak of abundant yield;
you have heard this humble man sing on behalf of the government.
After hearing all this on August Fifteenth,
do not sit carelessly saying, “Let us see what happens.”
The Tamil government here is a government for agriculture,
a new government rushing to work swiftly
to increase the harvest.
Loans will be provided without delay;
fertiliser distribution will not stop even for a moment;
credit and pesticide will be available
to destroy the lives of crop-destroying pests.
This is not merely the fellowship of song;
there is also the help of cooperation.
For double-crop wet land in Thanjavur
we have granted water-cess relief;
we have exempted dry land from tax
and become medicine for the farmer's smile.
We have plans to do much more —
we need only your wholehearted cooperation.
Landowners must have in their hearts
an abundance of generosity
toward those who labour.

<!-- scan 137 -->

Labour and ownership must join
so that the food situation may flourish.
Under the leadership of honourable Gandhi
we drove out the foreigner.
There are no foreigners among us now;
it is wrong for our own people to behave like foreigners.
The man expert in hoarding
kills many lives.
The man skilled in smuggling
surpasses even Yama.
The man who says, “Do not give out the paddy,”
becomes a cruel beast.
Today let farming people
win “freedom” from such men.
For this purpose let us find a New Path.
On August Fifteenth let us proclaim the vow:
Abundant harvest is the ideal of Anna's government —
let us pledge ourselves to it with certainty!

Greetings.
Long live!

## Translator notes

- The river-love passage deliberately strings together South Indian river/place names — Kollidam, Vaigai, Manniyar, Kallanai, Palar, Periyar and others — as puns inside romantic dialogue. The names are retained rather than replaced with generic river terms.
- `nanjai`, *kalam*, *marakkal*, *padi*, *uzhakku*, *kuruvai* and *kambu* are source-active agricultural terms. Short contextual glosses are supplied in English without altering the Tamil witness.
- The Aduthurai/A.D.T.-27 and I.R.-8 passages preserve the source's humour around modern crop-variety names and English abbreviations.

## Item 12 — Ten Possessions

<!-- scan 138 -->

### Ten Possessions

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled “Ten Possessions,”
held at the Children's Theatre, Chennai
(Kalaivanar Arangam),
in connection with Tiruvalluvar Day,
on 16 September 1968.

★

Greetings.

On this fitting sacred day
when we hold a festival for Valluvar,
greetings to all who have come;
greetings to the reception committee;
greetings to the Tamil man
with clustered moustache,
pearl-like speech,
strong ideas,
seed of awakening —
to the man who feasts on Tamil
like a peacock dancing in a flower-garden;
greetings to the *Silambu Selvar*.

<!-- scan 139 -->

Valluvar spoke of ten “possessions.”
I was asked to show them
in peeled, juicy Tamil.
Again and again I scratched my head;
imagination would not come.
I was shaken.
Usually I take up the page,
write, and finish —
that has been my way until now.
But today I stand
like a wandering mendicant with his begging bowl,
after going house to house and gaining nothing.
You ask why the poem will not come.
What shall I say?
Tears come first —
but I shall still say it.

There is an old story that Pattinathar once burned a house,
saying one's own deed will burn oneself
and the rice-cake will burn the house.
Today some “Pattinathars” —
men of Madras city —
are burning many houses.
Do you think only houses are burning?
Are they not the stomachs of the poor?
“Our cot and mansion are only this mud house,”
they say,
living there with their little children.
Men with not a trace of pity,
men like poison-filled snakes,
men like frenzy-struck demons,
do deeds at which even Yama would recoil.
Curse them, Tamil Nadu —
curse them to be blown away like cotton!
Curse also the traitors who offer incense to them;
may they too be scattered to dust!
Curse them!
When I tremble with thoughts such as these,

<!-- scan 140 -->

how can poetry send its messenger to me?
In ungovernable anger I cry,
“Wretch — perish!”
But Valluvar, embodiment of love, restrains me:
“If, knowing wisdom, one learns to restrain oneself,
that self-restraint, knowing its strength,
will bring distinction.”
That is possession of self-control. What can I do?
People have lost cattle, calves,
even the single house they had,
and wander in the middle of the street.
I press down my heart and circle about,
wishing that those base men
who seeded this condition should fall.
“To hearts joined with darkness there is no grace,”
says the master of the Kural,
bestowing the possession of grace;
and then:
possession of love,
possession of knowledge,
possession of energetic action,
possession of character,
possession of modesty,
possession of forbearance,
possession of discipline,
possession of enterprise —
thus Valluvar sets out the ten possessions
and tries to console me.

But the people who possess no property,
trapped like mangy dogs
in the great tongue of blazing fire —
can their ruin be told with one tongue, sir?
Men with black tongues...
If I say, “Curse the scoundrels,”
Valluvar's sacred word joins mine:

<!-- scan 141 -->

“As the sun scorches the boneless worm,
virtue scorches the loveless man.”
Let us sing virtue and destroy unjust men;
let us rejoice in a new song of prowess,
saying we have driven wickedness
from Tamil Nadu that once lived outside the path;
let us bring down evil;
let us wipe out the creatures who set fires.

While four-and-six poets have come here
and make a river of song swell
like Palar, Thenpennai and Kaveri,
pardon me for speaking about the disorder in Madras.
My heart could not bear it;
that is why I lamented.

Tamil Nadu raises a statue to Valluvar and honours him;
it is also a warrior's house
that will put a nose-rope on those who leap against it.
In five letters we say, “Know Valluvar!”
Those who do not know him bark the two-letter “val.”
Put a leg between that “val” and reduce three letters —
we shall take up the *vāl*, the sword,
to smash deceit to powder!
If we take up one sword,
they take up two and bark “vāl-vāl”!
Someone may say,
“I made Valluvar ride the bus free of charge” —
not Ma. Po. Si. sitting here, no, no;
some friends say that after Valluvar's portrait was hung,
accidents became cheaper!

<!-- scan 142 -->

There are strange men even to insult Valluvar,
whose every atom of thought awakens feeling;
fortunately they fell in sixty-seven.
If Tamil cannot be the language of government,
and when asked, “Then should another language dominate?”
what sort of possession of knowledge is it to say yes?
What sort of possession of love for Tamil?
When I think that such men existed,
I wonder how Tamil Nadu could have remained
for twenty years like a painting without a wall.
That Valluvar himself survived among them
is enough to provoke wonder.
Is freedom only for a country that lived and fell?
Freedom also for great Valluvar,
so he may give light to fallen Tamils;
freedom also for flourishing Tamil.
With the whip-strike of freedom's light
let us drive away the dark crowd of destruction.

A mother said, “Give your head,”
and many Tamils gave it,
falling into the mouth of fire.
They were shot and went to the cremation ground;
their wives were left widows.
They were imprisoned, dragged the oil-press,
and died writhing to protect Tamil
like fish cast onto dry earth.
The party that committed all this,
the party that imposed repression —
yesterday I saw an English newspaper
scribble, in irritation, a half-formed piece saying
“not enough sacrifice.”

<!-- scan 143 -->

Where else in India, sir,
did eight people burn themselves alive?
Here in Tamil Nadu they fell into fire for language!
Our hearts boil at that newspaper's lament.
Soon there will be no one even to buy the paper
from the stall where it hangs!
Because it wrote demeaningly
of Tamils who gave their heads so Tamil might live,
I left today's Valluvar theme
and wandered toward those headless men.
Forgive me.

Alliteration and rhyme have not yet been born —
they are only ten months along!
The metre and prosodic bond
have not even had their baby-shower.
There are poets here whose names are inscribed in fame,
who can offer verse like ripe jackfruit rooted deep.
I shall remain among them
like the fibre in a flower-garland and gain its fragrance.
Had Tamil's old kings lived today,
they too would have given these poets world-renowned prizes.
My prize is only this:
to praise them so the town may know them.

Greetings.
Long live!

(In this *kaviyarangam*, poets Athmanathan, Ponnivalavan,
Kudiyarasu, Murugusundaram, Abdul Rahman, Poongundran,
Tamilappithan, Anandan, Muthulingam and Pulamaipithan
sang respectively on the ten themes:
possession of grace, self-control, love, knowledge,
energetic action, enterprise, discipline, character,
modesty and forbearance.)

## Translator notes

- The canonical title `உடைமைகள் பத்து` (“Ten Possessions”) is authoritative. The contents witness `உடன்பிறப்பின் பற்று` is a distinct source-valid variant and is preserved only in metadata, not merged into a hybrid title.
- The sequence around `வள் / வாள் / வாள்வாள்` depends on Tamil letters and sound. The Tamil forms are retained in transliteration where the English pun cannot reproduce the letter-count joke.
- References to the 1965 anti-Hindi agitation, self-immolation and political repression are translated as Kalaignar's source rhetoric; Phase 4 does not fact-check or harmonise those claims.

## Item 13 — The Water Family

<!-- scan 144 -->

### The Water Family

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* of Kandar College,
Paramathi Velur,
on 28 September 1968.

★

Greetings to the assembly;
greetings to the poets who have come to mount the stage,
and to those who founded Kandar College.
Greetings to the respected teachers
and beloved students.
Siruthondar gave his child as flesh;
today Kandar gives children knowledge —
he gives knowledge to the child.
As outward purity is formed by water,
and inward purity is seen through truth,
Kaveri and this college are fittingly joined;
let us call their founder a grove of generosity
and celebrate him always.
Only where there is moisture is there giving;
on this brave bank of Kaveri there is moisture,

<!-- scan 145 -->

there is giving,
and there is the life that earns musical fame.
Under the title “The Water Family,”
poets of enduring renown will sing.
They told me, “Are you not related to the water family?”
and seized me — a man who writes verse with watery letters —
and placed me among poets who write with ornament and metre.

The cloud's writing becomes rain;
rain's writing upon earth becomes river;
the straight writing of the river becomes pond and well.
The root-letter of all these, they say, is the sea.
One man grieves, “What fate is written on my head?”
and sheds tears;
another says, “I shall rewrite fate,”
and conquers it through the battle-script of sweat.

If I said that thunder is the old man of the sky coughing,
lightning the light from his swollen eyes,
and rain the coward's saliva he spits,
we would never welcome that diseased rain.
So I shall offer an image young people may like:
the fish of the sky are women;
their group-talk is thunder;
the sword-like sweep of their eyes is the lightning-streak;
and the rosewater they sprinkle on their round-moon lover
is the raindrop.
For warriors, another image:
when two valiant hearts clash — thunder;

<!-- scan 146 -->

when great warriors' spears scrape — lightning;
when many warriors pour red water — rain.
In general the sky itself is a battlefield —
whether a battlefield, a pleasure-field,
or a cruel field of disease.
Yet the good men of agriculture crudely called
the rain-giving sky “honour” — *mānam*!
Is it not fitting?
Enough of getting drenched in rain;
come, let us go near the sea.

When Vaishnavas hear “sea,”
they remember Vishnu lying in repose.
Saivites smeared head to foot with ash
remember the story of Siva drinking poison.
We remember the history of Poompuhar,
which no army could destroy
but the sea destroyed without hindrance.
Add and subtract all this,
and how many more benefits come from the sea!
Did not the sea give us pearls
so we could say “teeth like pearls,”
and let women speak words sweet as sugarcane?
The blue silk worn by maiden Earth in her season;
the tall, blue-hued handsome man
who raises wave-hands to embrace the moon-maiden;
a sand-mattress on the shore for roaming lovers;
a treasure-box of imagination for wandering poets.
For minds dark as midnight,
all they see on the shore are crab and snail;
but even the harbour where ships stand
like cattle tethered in a pen —
is not the delight of seeing it also given by the sea?

<!-- scan 147 -->

You gained greatness by humbling King Canute's pride,
gained distinction when Mahatma Gandhi made salt,
opened a route for Vasco da Gama's ships,
gave the first history to Tamils who crossed seas in trade,
became a simile for a broad heart,
remained for long an ever-moving mute,
at times became a deluge,
at times an evil to the world,
and then, like an ascetic mastering anger,
turned turtle-like and drew back your roaring waves.
Sea, can we ever finish telling all your glory?
So let us cross the sea and reach the river.

Coming through rain, embracing the mountain,
like a golden cloth passing through a slender waist,
like a palm-leaf strip thrusting its head through foliage,
like a parting drawn through a woman's hair,
you walk in joyous gait through the groves
and become Kaveri,
a woman adorned with jewels.
They called you woman, they called you Pennai;
because wherever you lay your hand delight dances,
they called you Vaigai, pouring song so wealth may flourish.
There is no Godavari in Tamil Nadu —
there is no line of caste coming here as “gothra-varisai”!
No Godavari for the dry field in Tamil Nadu;
for Tamils abroad, there is a place to live —
we define and provide it.
We bow to you, river — live long.
They called me here for six topics,
then asked me to write and sing seven;
I am singing.

Next, let us go to the well.

<!-- scan 148 -->

I am a frog in the well of the village of poetry.
I know the nation's resources;
I do not know the abundance of song as you do, sir!
If the long sky refuses rain,
if long river, pond and pool dry and wither,
if not even a cup of water remains to quench thirst,
then, as one swallows one's own saliva,
we immediately dig:
a spring-well, a seepage-well,
a deep well, a riverbank well.
People say, “When a well is dug, a ghost comes out.”
Do you understand what that means?
Dig a well and earth piles up — one element;
the air trapped under the ground comes out — another;
fresh water wells up — the next;
the bright sky appears upon the water — the fourth;
and the sun's fiery rays purify that water — the fifth.
It is the arrival of these five elements
that the old people expressed beautifully;
intellectual paupers merely scream “ghost!”

<!-- scan 149 -->

A pond gives beauty to a village;
I too have a pond — a heart — for poetry,
and I have a heart for public service.
The lotus pond is great beauty;
the hidden pond is the Tirukkural;
the heart of scripture is the Tirukkural.
There are sacred bathing tanks,
and bridge-building tanks;
there are tanks that have taken some lives.
There are swimming pools in towns —
should there still be pools of “low people” in villages?
An inlet sluice and outlet sluice are needed for a pond;
an inlet of knowledge and outlet of love are needed for the heart.
Every pond needs a stone bathing-step;
even a steel heart needs a touchstone on which to learn.

“Those who leave when the pond dries
are not true kin;
those who remain, like water-lily and lotus
rooted in the same pond,
are the kin who cling close.”

Singing this, I climb out of the pond.

The sea is vast on earth;
rain is sweet to humanity;
the river has great fame in the world;
the well too has many honours;
all ponds are nectar-mouths of the globe.
All that is fine.
But all of them arise only when the cloud-cluster sheds tears:
rain comes, the sea swells,
rivers run, wells spring, ponds fill.
Why did night's darkness vanish,
why did dream-joy break?
The tears shed by the morning flower are dew;
the tears shed by the coconut spathe are toddy;
the banana's tears become milk;
the poor man's tears become a spear,
become a sword.

<!-- scan 150 -->

“Are not the tears shed by those
who suffer beyond endurance
the army that wears away wealth?”
So let us turn swiftly from tears to sweat.
In an oyster's sweat a pearl is born;
in a sculptor's sweat a statue is born;
in a farmer's sweat hunger flies away;
in a warrior's sweat the enemy flies away.
The sweat of the powerful is rosewater;
the sweat of the humble is red water.
In the furnace, flame drinks the sweat that falls;
in the battlefield of love,
every drop of sweat becomes sandal paste and perfume.
Literature refuses to call that “sweat” —
so it becomes fragrance!

When poets of distinction are here
to give such spreading description
from the broad sea down to the pond of sweat,
is it right for me alone to sing so much?
Honey, fruit, millet, nectar,
flesh, life, Tamil, mother —
I offer greetings. Live long!

First, Murugusundaran comes to cross the sea,
giving lines sweet as palm-jaggery,
giving us a sea-song:

(Poet Murugusundaram finishes his poem on “The Sea.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, rain.
Natarasa Pulamai Nambi,
who drops good poems like *naval* fruit,
comes to pour a rain of verse;
if you have an umbrella, hold it up!

<!-- scan 151 -->

(Poet T. K. Natarasan finishes his poem on “Rain.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, river.
Our dear Tamilanban,
lover of tongue-sweet words, word-archer —
the river-topic is his flower.
The topic is river;
let the Tamil-river flow without obstruction,
and over the loudspeaker too let it flow freely:

(Poet Erode Tamilanban finishes his poem on “River.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, well.
Ramarasan digs a well;
he ties a Tamil pot to the rope;
he pours out a new flood.
Drink and rejoice — drink water,
drink well-water and rejoice:

(Poet Ramarasan finishes his poem on “Well.”)

<!-- scan 152 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

One may live without *samsaram*, household life;
but can one live without *minsaram*, electricity?
*Samsaram* has not entered him until today —
but for that reason, do not touch *minsaram*!
He is my friend, so I warn him.

Next, pond.
In olden days Akbar and Aurangzeb saw battlefields;
in their line Abdul Rahman has found a pond.
To speak of it he too has entered a field — the field of poetry.
When we find a pond, shall we leave at once?
Let us squat on the bank and rest:

(Poet Abdul Rahman finishes his poem on “Pond.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, tears.
A Tamil poet-bull named Kandasamy
comes into this assembly
with a grieving heart to shed tears.
Let us ask what troubles him;
if we can, let us remedy the grievance:

(Poet Kandasamy finishes his poem on “Tears.”)

<!-- scan 153 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, sweat.
To show white pearl-like sweat,
do we not need a black background like the centre of the eye?
Ponnivalavan comes to sing in tune —
a man rich as gold in verse.
He will sing with sweat falling
and drive away all our weariness:

(Poet Ponnivalavan finishes his poem on “Sweat.”)

Chairman Kalaignar's concluding poem:

“Because I am the irrigation minister,
this Water Family theme is useful to me.”
So I flew toward Paramathi Velur.
Poets have sung on many topics;
one topic dragged another into playful quarrel
and completed our theme —
completed our head-*bag* too!
I will not give a one-sided judgement.
But just one thing:
from the sea, rain must increase;
rivers must run;
wells and ponds must fill with water.
In the Water Family one thing must decrease,
and another must disappear.

<!-- scan 154 -->

Sweat must decrease —
for those who work,
as facilities increase,
sweat must decrease.
Tears must disappear.
Because we always desire happiness for everyone,
tears must disappear!

Greetings.
Long live!

## Translator notes

- The poem treats sea, rain, river, well, pond, tears and sweat as one “water family.” That structural sequence is retained in full.
- Several stretches depend on Tamil homophony and word splitting (`நீர்`, `நேர்`, `மானம்`, `கோதாவரி`, `சம்சாரம் / மின்சாரம்`, `தலைப்பை / தலைப்-பை`). English cannot reproduce all of these simultaneously; the sense and the Tamil term are both kept where needed.
- Religious, historical and political examples — including Poompuhar, King Canute, Gandhi, Vasco da Gama and caste criticism — remain source rhetoric rather than being externally harmonised.

## Item 14 — Bharathidasan

<!-- scan 155 -->

### Bharathidasan

Presiding address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled “Bharathidasan,”
held at Bharathi Kala Mandram, Bombay,
on 8 December 1968.

★

Friends in Bombay who guard me always
as the eyelid guards the eye!
Brave men who pour out blood like oil
to protect Mother Tamil, cool as moonlight!
Warriors who, when told “cut it and bring it,”
will bind it and bring it back!
Friends who call me here again and again
and rejoice like a poor man
opening a box and looking repeatedly at the money inside!
You stand on the soil of Maharashtra,
land of Shivaji, that mountain-mouse turned great warrior,
with unwavering love and integrity.

There, in Tamil Nadu, is sea-singing Mamallapuram,
art-singing epics and beauty-singing sculptures;
Thanjavur has wet fields — *nanjai* —
and there is a temple there to Siva who swallowed poison.

<!-- scan 156 -->

Madurai once produced so much red paddy
that even tying elephants to thresh it was not enough;
there, because the fish ruled,
there was a fish-emblazoned flag,
and there still towers the temple of Meenakshi.
There are Tirunelveli and Salem,
Courtallam and Coimbatore.
Because life's troubles drove you,
you came to Dharavi to release your great sighs.
Though you live in Matunga,
your whole mind is there, in Tamil Nadu.
I say this as though keeping it ear-to-ear:
for India's unity,
and for Tamil Nadu's rights,
we have adopted both as high principles;
let us work to multiply their benefits.

With this introduction I begin.
Yesterday Ranga said he had repeatedly and painfully invited me:
“Come to Bombay.”
Did he invite me after suffering,
or suffer after inviting me? I do not know!
Still, having undertaken the task,
we have mounted the stage to complete it.
Elders of Bombay who increase flavour through classical Tamil!
You have created a *kaviyarangam*
in the name of lion-poet Bharati;

<!-- scan 157 -->

you have founded a golden Tamil forum.
Poets who have mounted the stage
to thunder jewel-like verse!
Can you stand after climbing a hill?
Can you stand on a hill?
Did I ever say you could not stand
on a hill of fame?
Have I said it even today?

They praise Bharati as a “great poet” — *mā-kavi*.
I do not accept it!
Was Bharati a poet made of ground flour — *mā*?
No, no — was he a *paniyaram* poet?
Was he a poet who filled prosody and ornament to the brim?
He was indeed *Mahakavi* —
a lion-great poet,
a head-poet,
a head-poet of innovation,
a poet of national liberation.
With turban twisted,
punching moustache,
a sun-bright gaze that made enemies tremble and run —
Bharati!
Head —
*pa* —
hand —
with turban twisted and fist clenched,
that moustache and gaze making enemies quake!

“You fools who roam searching,
saying there are a thousand gods” —

<!-- scan 158 -->

have you seen the anger in that song?
“We shall dance, we shall sing the *pallu*;
we shall dance saying we have attained joyous freedom!”
Before freedom arrived he declared it with certainty,
a charging bull, a king whose language thundered.
And there was a younger one to him,
like the younger Marudhu.
The moment Bharati said, “Sing,”
he sang:
“Wherever I look, there is Sakthi, brother;
the seven seas are her colour.”
Bharati praised him as a brave poet.
He shortened the far-travelling name Kanaka Subburathinam
and fitted himself perfectly with the name
“Bharati's servant” — Bharathidasan.
He tightened his war-band to enter the field and protect Tamil.

Upright gaze,
compressed brow,
voice of a waterfall,
moustache like a sheaf of paddy.
In writing poetry he learned Bharati's alchemy —
look at this man:
he learned the alchemy of *adhirasam* too;
how much sweetness lives in his song!

He takes us to the slopes of *Sanjeevi Parvatham*
and makes us listen to musical lovers' talk.
The woman says, “I shall give you a hundred kisses without hurting you.”
The man replies, “Then give me one kiss as the advance!”
Is that not the argument of *adhirasam* sweetness?
Was that not always his way?

<!-- scan 159 -->

“Who owns all those labouring shoulders
that plough and plough,
make the little village, the bunded field,
the river and the well-cut canal yield their grain?”
Is that a question mark — or an exploding volcano?
Are these lines not enough
for the world to garland him as the Revolutionary Poet?
“If this country exists for one man's need alone,
it is better that such a country perish.”
Is this not the song of a whole human being?
The man who hears it and weeps is half a man,
a deficient man,
a half-and-half man.

“The world is in a dark room, brother;
even the man who says caste exists is himself still here!”
He begins so,
and ends:
“Some people roll everything they can get into their own hands.”
He is a rolling poet — a cigar-rolling poet;
a poet who overturns —
a poet who turns upside down every superstitious habit.
Noise, uproar, agitation, procession!
Would not the Pondicherry cuckoo sing
so that the world might live in peace?
“Let us make a new world;
let us uproot the evil world that wages war.”
He calls the world as a poet of peace:
“Is it good that the world flourish by peace,

<!-- scan 160 -->

or good that it weaken by growing religious division?”
He asks, melting like wax in fire
before Gandhi's anguished idea.
“The liar is a coward;
the man who plunders another's land is a giant rat;
he who seeks welfare for all the world's people is wise.”
A poet of ideas — a somewhat dark-hued poet;
a poet of time — a poet of all three times.

What a wonder, the waterfall-song
he found as a form for delightful Tamil!
“When the mullai bud sees a guest coming,
it blooms like the face of a gentle woman — what a wonder!
Whether it sees someone known or unknown,
smiling is simply its habit.”
Hear another:
a woman pleasing to the eye dances;
Dasan loads sweetness into language:
“Anklets coo at her feet;
her lotus hand floats in the sky marking rhythm;
her beckoning finger is a fresh champak bud;
a red coral stalk that captures the eye.”
And another:
“Nothing was caught in his throat;
yet the husband gave a little cough.
The woman came near and said, ‘Dear?’
Would the dear man sleep then? ‘Sit near me,’ he said.”
His own special teasing!
The flavour belonging to his poetry!
They call him the poet of simile —

<!-- scan 161 -->

can one choose only one or two examples?
“The darkness loosened its bundle
like lime mixed into a tub of indigo.”
“When rice is served,
let the poured ghee run down
like sap along a bending banana-stem.”

Revolution? Peace?
Surging simile?
Ideas? Honey that intoxicates through Tamil?
Whatever anyone argues is greater than another,
I will not agree;
I will say each surpasses the other!

How many books he gave!
He lit the Family Lamp in a Dark House,
poured *Isai Amudhu*,
and won us the *Pandiyan Parisu*.
We have the *Kurinji Thittu* too.
While conducting the magazine *Kuyil*,
with *Azhagin Sirippu* he captured the Tamil movement.
He gave peace a sound verdict.
Then, longing for love,
he left the poetic maiden to pine
and parted with death's unexpected kiss.
He flew away saying, “I shall see my teacher Bharati.”

What do you think the two poets,
meeting in the world beyond death,
would have said?
Bharati, overwhelmed, dances:
“People say we are not in Tamil Nadu today —
shall we believe it?
‘When one says the words “land of classical Tamil,”
joyous honey pours into the ear.’
I waited in pain for someone to turn this song into action.
Today,

<!-- scan 162 -->

have you seen the wonder, brother?
Anna gave our forefathers' land the name Tamil Nadu!
We live there — is it not true?”
Bharathidasan greets him and replies:
“The pulp inside fruit,
the juice inside mature cane,
the honey in the dew-wet flower,
the sweetness in boiled syrup,
the milk the rich cow pours,
the cool tender water the coconut gives —
all these I call sweet;
yet Tamil I call my very life.”
“I wrote so.
Today Tamils everywhere take Tamil as their very life
and labour to spread it in all eight directions, sir.
We have not died; we live in the homeland!”
“That is true,” Bharati says,
embracing his own Dasan,
and laughing a Bharati-laugh.
Together they sing:
“Our life and our wealth —
blow the conch that they are undying Tamil!”

Let us join them in song,
see an advancing Tamil Nadu,
remove the foul caste-feud,
and hold festivals for mighty Bharati and his Dasan.
When we hold the festival,
let us hear their festival-ideas.

<!-- scan 163 -->

First, a poet comes to sing on “Simile.”
My friend, my special dear friend,
Anandam — a poet whose Tamil flows like the Porunai river,
who says even a golden flower has fragrance.
A useful tool for my teasing,
who will testify for me like a lizard on the fence,
yet is a mountain of character with no creed of beating his chest for wages.
For the first time he has come to Bombay;
yesterday he flew in an aeroplane for the first time too.
When he sat down neatly I told him, “Put on the seat belt.”
He looked straight at me and tightened the belt around his waist!
The air-hostess laughed until her ribs hurt
and quickly fastened the aeroplane belt.
The old proverb says, “Like a villager seeing an elephant.”
Change it after seeing him:
“Like a villager seeing Bombay.”
The moment he landed he stood open-mouthed,
looking at the sky-high buildings,
looking at the airport;
in doing so he even lost
a costly object he had in his hand!
This is that poet.

<!-- scan 164 -->

He will sing all the beauty of the heart-winning Poet-King's similes.
My guileless friend will give us Tamil in a flood,
like water plunging into a hollow:

(Poet Anandam finishes his poem on “Simile.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, on the beautiful theme of “Joy,”
our dear S. D. Sundaram sings.
The poet whose dream said, “Let joy flourish on earth.”
In politics he stands in the opposite row,
but unlike some, his “opposition row” is not Satan's row!
He will create a new row for Mother Tamil.
A worthy man who writes verse without a row of liquor;
simple dress, simple gait,
a new-minded heart that will not let old ideas bend it.
With lines sharp as tamarindless medicine,
he corrects dirty minds that have never bathed.
Usually his song is public poetry;
today he has been caught here to sing inner poetry.
On the theme “Joy,”
my friend, come and offer your garland of letters:

(Poet S. D. Sundaram finishes his poem on “Joy.”)

<!-- scan 165 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, on “Womanhood,”
comes Ganesan,
a lofty Tamil man of truth,
to offer distinguished verse.
This Chettinad worthy will not lend without interest;
a sacred body without a shirt,
a form of fame that does not care a shirt-button for opposition!
He wears khadi,
a body made splendid by khadi.
He will wrestle — wrestle any enemy who insults Tamil.
He poured sweat for the paddy of freedom;
today he comes to add flavour to words.
With poetic Tamil that can give life even to stone,
he will give us a ripened song:

(Poet Sa. Ganesan finishes his poem on “Womanhood.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Next, should we not add flavour
to an assembly already gathered?
For that comes Abdul Rahman.
His words explode like a gun loaded with shot,
and distil flavour.
He is a skylark;
his home is Vaniyambadi.

<!-- scan 166 -->

If Bharathidasan was servant to Bharati,
this man is servant to that servant
in crafting Tamil verse.
Once a lamp inside a pot,
from now on he is a lamp set upon a hill;
refined gold,
a veena that wins back fame.
He will give a song of flavour.
Taste it — I shall not interrupt:

(Poet Abdul Rahman finishes his poem on “Taste.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Last night in a Bombay hotel
I slept wrapped in my blanket.
A lovely woman of the dream-world seized my hand:
“I waited for you, my dear — have you come?”
“What is your name, lady?” I asked.
With delight she said, “Muthamma.”
“Dream-beauty, explain one thing;
do not misunderstand me —
your face looks like Muthusamy!
Has only the inner masculinity changed into womanhood?”
She burst out laughing
and showed me the moustache she had hidden with her hand!
I recognised the trickster at once.
With no way to escape, the maiden said,
“Dear, I am ready to entrust my heart to you;
but this moustache ruins my desire.”
“If worship could send a moustache away, would it go?
Flower-like girl, be reborn and come as a new woman,” I told her.

<!-- scan 167 -->

She vanished; I woke.
Now you know how yesterday's comic dream arose.
Tamil-loving Muthusamy had announced in this very forum
that he wished to become a woman quickly and marry me,
and that if there were another birth
he was resolved to marry me.
Remembering that, I slept —
and so I dreamed!
When revolutions happen in which Mariyappan becomes Mariamma,
if Muthusamy became Muthamma,
our cabinet would have two women!
Enough of that.

I call my friend Muthu
to sing of the revolution made by the Poet-King's songs.
Do not stay away today out of shyness at yesterday's memory;
come among us and give good Tamil:

(Minister Ma. Muthusamy finishes his poem on “Revolution.”)

Chairman Kalaignar's concluding address:

Bharati's Dasan asked,
“Would you sell your eyes to buy a painting?”
