and said women were “a heap of guava fruit.”
He asked:
“Even a horse locked alone in a stable
has permission to neigh —
should a woman in the home be forced into modesty and fear?”

<!-- scan 168 -->

He said,
“To attain love is the nature of life;
can it be imprisoned in a cot?
Maiden, begin!
Joy — plunder, plunder, plunder in the mango grove!”
He asked,
“Let us open a road for love
and find a door to close the road to pregnancy — what fault is there?”
When did he ask it?
Long before the present.
Today the government campaigns for family planning,
saying, “Do not become like Kuchela.”
The great poet preached that child limitation was necessary
thirty years ago as a progressive idea.

A man, father of four or five children,
lies trembling on the verandah.
His wife comes near.
Listen to how the poet's language flows like the Ganga:
“My dear wife came softly to me;
‘Are you tired?’ she asked,
touching me with her flower-hand.
‘There is frost in the street,’ she said.
I said yes.
I understood her heart — what need was there for words?
Ten months later, one day at noon,
just as a creditor came and tormented me,
while my whole mind worried over the eldest girl's fever,

<!-- scan 169 -->

an old woman came and said,
‘By Muthalamma's grace,
at an auspicious hour your wife has borne a child.’
Contagious poverty, the creditor's trouble,
the storm that keeps striking — and child after child!”

That Bharathidasan honoured womanhood.
Looking at human beings trampled like earth,
he said, “Do not sell yourself!”
“Even if enemies offer me crores, I will not touch it,”
he sounded like a war-trumpet.
He stood as a good ray of light in black darkness.
He called the man who merely lies about without serving “a tortoise.”
He lit garland after garland of poetic lamps.
He became Bharathidasan, heir to Bharati.
For a leader, a poet, or any great scholar,
the true qualification is the lineage that remains after him.
Today wherever we look
we see Bharati's lineage,
the descendants in Bharathidasan's path.
Poets speak word-pearls — poets with many teeth,
and toothless poets too!
When it comes to the vote,
people give it to Bharati and Bharathidasan
with no other aim.
Will the two poets come to rule our kingdom of poetry?
Why should they still need to come and rule?
They already rule our hearts.
Long live they!
Long live their fame!
Greetings!

## Translator notes

- The source repeatedly exploits the sounds of `மா`, `மகாகவி`, `அதிரசம்`, `தாசன்`, political “rows,” clothing, caste and place names. English retains the underlying argument and keeps Tamil terms where the joke would otherwise disappear.
- Quoted lines from Bharati and Bharathidasan are translated as embedded source quotations and are not silently replaced by outside published English translations.
- The Bombay/Muthusamy dream and family-planning passages are preserved as Kalaignar's 1968 stage rhetoric, including humour that reflects its period; Phase 4 does not modernise or censor the source voice.

## Item 15 — Bharathiyar

<!-- scan 170 -->

### Bharathiyar

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the Bharati Festival *kaviyarangam*
held at Vani Mahal, Chennai,
on 12 September 1969.

★

Greetings.
Narana Duraikannan, sculptor of fine writing,
gave the welcome poem.
In elegant Tamil he said here,
“Karunanidhi has reduced his harsh words.”
No, no — I have merely learned
to raise the rod sharply and let it fall softly. That is all!

Elders seated in the midst of this assembly,
learned great ones,
beloved mothers,
and distinguished poets who have come
with Tamil-rich verse to show us the form of rare poet Bharati —
I bow to you.

Twelve months plus another eighteen —
thirty months have now passed
since I assumed office as a minister.
The lion who used to raise angry questions is not here,
but he stands in sculpted form on the seashore.
To sing his fame, Avvai has invited me.

<!-- scan 171 -->

Ah! There are eighteen Avvais —
which Avvai do you mean, you ask?
You shall know:
the nineteenth Avvai,
a jewel among masters of the threefold Tamil arts,
is the one who invited me!

(Next, Kalaignar, chairing the *kaviyarangam*,
sings on Bharathiyar's distinction to complete his introduction,
then calls the participating poets in order
and introduces them to the assembly.)

First, Va. Mu. Sethuraman
will explain through song
the Tamil consciousness Bharati saw.
Va. Mu. Sethuraman, come!
Give us Tamil:

(Poet Va. Mu. Sethuraman finishes his poem on
“Bharati's Tamil Consciousness.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Bharati understood that the whole story
told by the grove-cuckoo about love
was a device of imagination
born from the enchantment of evening beauty.
He asked:
“Even if it is imagination,
if there is some little room
to unfold it as Vedanta and explain its meaning,
will someone speak?”
Accepting Bharati's invitation,
our dear Thooran has arrived.
Let us leave Thooran near that yearning cuckoo
and stand a little farther away;
only when Tamil sounds shall we come close.

(Poet Periyasamy Thooran finishes his poem on
“Bharati's Cuckoo.”)

<!-- scan 172 -->

Chairman Kalaignar:

You have seen the Parasakthi I saw!
The traditionalist asks, “Why did we see her?”
but hides only the news he himself saw.
Taking honey-filled Tamil,
Bharathiyar, in the name of Parasakthi
seen by the deer of devotion,
gives valour reaching to the very sky.
Viswabharathi will explain it:

(Poet Viswabharathi finishes his poem on
“Bharati's Parasakthi.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

I saw Anna —
as mother, as Tamil,
as the eldest child of the homeland,
as a form of love that knew no barrier.
Bharati saw Kannan.
Some say Krishna and Kannan are one;
some say they are not.
Ku. Sa. Ki. will light a lamp of inquiry in the darkness
and tell us without confusion:

(Poet Ku. Sa. Krishnamurthi finishes his poem on
“Bharati's Kannan.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Bharati never saw in person
Panchali, wife of Partha.
But there is one here who has seen her.
Look — he is Bhishma himself!

<!-- scan 173 -->

You who once lay beneath a rain of arrows,
come now and pour a rain of poetry!

(Poet Bhishmar finishes his poem on
“Bharati's Panchali.”)

Chairman Kalaignar:

Eyes that dance beneath artificial brows,
red paint on the lips,
fierce competition in half-skirts and other dress,
Ajanta sculptures piled upon the head,
walking like an exhibition object —
that is not the New Woman!
Then who is she, you ask?
There is a lady here to tell you.
To see the New Woman,
do not shift your seat;
stay exactly where you are:

(Poet Ira. Rasalakshmi finishes her poem on
“Bharati's New Woman.”)

Chairman Kalaignar's concluding address:

Since we are holding a festival
for Subramania Bharati of Pondicherry,
Subramania Kavirayar of Puthaneri did not come,
lest it appear he was arriving in competition!
So I shall go to the conclusion — forgive me.

Bharati honoured womanhood.
Looking at human beings trampled like earth,
he said, “Do not sell yourself.”

<!-- scan 174 -->

“Even if enemies offer me crores,
I will not touch it,”
he gave the cry of freedom.
He stood as a good ray of sunlight
to remove the black darkness of slavery.
He called the man who lies idle without serving “a tortoise.”
He lit lamp after lamp of poetry.
To that Bharati came an heir — Bharathidasan.
For a leader, a poet, or any scholar of distinction,
the lineage that remains after him
is itself the measure of worth.
Wherever we look,
we see Bharati's lineage;
we see descendants in Bharathidasan's path;
we laugh and rejoice with a Bharati-like radiance!

Greetings.
Long live!

## Translator notes

- This is a distinct Bharati *kaviyarangam* item, not a continuation of item 14. The adjacent placement and overlapping literary references are preserved without merging their identities.
- The opening `கடிதோச்சி மெல்ல எறிக` joke and the “nineteenth Avvai” passage are kept as stage rhetoric; English prioritises the source's teasing cadence over literal word-for-word equivalence.
- Embedded descriptions of Bharati's Kannan, Parasakthi, Panchali and New Woman are translated as Kalaignar's source framing, not externally harmonised with other editions or interpretations.

## Item 16 — Pongal Festival Day

<!-- scan 175 -->

A presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled
“Pongal Festival Day,”
broadcast by Chennai Radio
on 12 January 1970.

★

The waters of tanks, ponds and canals
had all turned to vapour;
a storm of drought had come,
and crops had reached the point of death.
Seeing that condition, the Tamil Government
sprang into action and swiftly cooled the torment of heat.
Painted clouds conceived,
came over the earth
and gave birth to the child called rain.
The old government order called food control
was withdrawn.
So, on Pongal — this festival that is an epic of joy —
let all the cuckoos that once called begin calling again;
let Mother Tamil walk over mountains of flowers
scattered by her people;
let every person in our State,
amid the tastes of mango, jackfruit and banana,
eat sweet Pongal to the full.
To make that possible,
we labour as far as our strength permits.
And, to sharpen the mind a little—

<!-- scan 176 -->

Radio said,
“Come to the whetstone of the *kaviyarangam*.”
Since I possess a mind,
I have come;
I have come because I possess a mind,
to have it given a new edge!

Around me are refined Tamil poets
who praise in ordered measure,
ready to take the jackfruit of thought
that lies rooted deep
and set down its sweet bulbs one by one.
I have not mastered all the *yappu* arts
of *thalai* and *thodai*;
like a woman's arm without bangles,
I shall shape and offer a few songs.

Bear with me, bulls among poets!
On this beautiful Tamil Pongal day,
under the theme “The War-Drum of Love,”
here are six of you who have come to sing, sirs!
As the old line says, “With Guha we became five,”
count yourselves, with this son of Tamil,
as seven.
Seven of us are here on this stage;
crying “Rise, Tamilian!”
we shall hoist the flag of Farmers' Day.
To guard the land of the warriors,
we shall beat our chests and swear our pledge!

Today we cry, “Let the milk boil over!”
Is it fresh cow's milk? No, no!
It is the *pāl* given by the charioteer of classical Tamil:
*Arathuppāl*, *Porutpāl*, *Kāmattuppāl* —
and beyond those *pāl*,
what other milk/part should boil over?

“I asked her to bring sugarcane for me to taste”—
but she lifted up the child,

<!-- scan 177 -->

fruit and nectar together, and gave it to me;
that is joy.
“Bring turmeric,” she said.
“What for? Let me rub it across your face!” I said.

Seeing the happiness in my house,
let the neighbours not chew ginger in envy;
uproot it instead,
and tie it to the Pongal pot as custom demands!
There is Pongal even for the bull that butts;
and Pongal too for the strong-shouldered hero
who goes out to seize the bull.

“The man who conquers the bull
is the bull-like man fit for me,”
said the maiden with palmyra-bright teeth:
such lines of literature
bring the old days back to mind.

When light arrives, darkness recedes;
sculptures cut by the chisel gleam;
peacocks dancing in delight spread their plumes;
a young man pulls close, asking for clear honey;
the maiden gives him a single drop
and flies away!

On Pongal day,
so that fresh joy may arise everywhere,
the sun has risen.
To approach all things through love and win,
the Rising Sun has risen high today!

Do you see the green flood,
like a green mat spread before us?
Hear the laughter of the little river singing *sindhu*!
Can the roaring forest-torrent,
charging like a wild tiger,
be allowed to burst beyond its dams?
See the Government standing beside every effort
to cultivate through agricultural credit.
To supply fertiliser cheaply,
a giving hand is needed;

<!-- scan 178 -->

Anna, judging its worth,
left that hand with us before he went.
Some shake their heads and refuse to accept us,
pointing only to holes.
I say: every hole in the *nadaswaram*
exists for good music.
If you are the fingers that move,
even the wind becomes melody today.

If, in factories rising to touch the sky,
some exist only to live for themselves,
our country becomes a head infested with lice.
The medicine I have studied for that is socialism.
Until that condition itself arrives,
it is right, in the present condition,
to find ways for working people to live.
Let us open our mouths and pledge it today.
We have not locked away the principle of common ownership;
nor have we spoken in rejection of it.
That ideal now inside books
must, little by little,
come inside the country.
Before every idea hidden in song
can spread as socialism,
let industry take shape in a fitting way;
let cooperative forms multiply in our land.
On Pongal day let us take up a new path.
Call this sweet path an interim path;
later we shall make even stones ripen.

By the farmer's sweat,
the mother-field became pregnant;
from her womb came a daughter,
the maiden called the grain-ear,
cherished by all.
Like a bride in the wedding hall,
she stands with bowed head
on Pongal day.

<!-- scan 179 -->

Those called Tamils rejoice;
they leap crying “thai, thai!”
Is not *Thai* the month that brings cool radiance?
Does not *thaiyal* mean woman?
Is not *thaiththal* — stitching — also a kind of work?
Some stitch us with thorns;
some stitch caps for us!

Those called Tamils rejoice;
they leap crying “thai, thai!”
They call me and say, “Poet, stitch!”
I say stitching is not my profession;
burying is my profession.
To bury evil is a deed that removes evil;
to bury helplessness is a sweet labour;
to bury ignorance is the work of the wise;
and to bury my face at the feet
of the noble one who gave his life
so that the voiceless might live —
that is my profession.

Two hundred thousand people who received *patta* titles bless us.
We plan so water may be given without obstruction.
For those to whom credit once seemed an unreachable fruit,
we make a special effort so the development bank
may lend without hindrance.
To end the grief of wandering about,
wondering whether an official's eye would ever fall upon them,
one day every month we require officials
to go into villages and render *Manu Neethi*.

We are changing the old condition
in which electricity went only
to the houses of wealthy men glittering with gold;
we labour so that, by 1972,
electric connections may reach everywhere
in my Tamil sacred land.
For praising all this on Pongal day,
I ask for no garlands in heaps,
no prizes.
Poets of this assembly,
if you sing one beautiful song,

<!-- scan 180 -->

I shall stand upright
like the Pandyan who destroyed the enemy field.
In a land where a woman with a winnowing tray
could face a tiger from its cave,
shall we stand bewildered before violence?
We shall overthrow it.
Let us cast away smoky ideas
and honour new thought on Pongal day.
Let us call the greying thought of old men
mere dye for the hair;
and is it not wrong
to accept the inflated claims of liars
without examining them?

Learned in literature,
masters of sweet-Tamil poetry,
our poets will sing on several themes
and offer supporting staffs
for Tamil life.
Selfish greed is the taproot of internal enmity;
violence grows as its branches.
From lack of unity
comes inequality.
And the cause of all these,
we shall say, is poverty of understanding.
A fine poet, rich in substance,
will give us thoughts of the Buddha.

Some great men say
this is the precious hour
to create a movement that drives poverty away.
Is the demon of poverty an ox
that moves at such a slow pace?
If you give it eight steps,
do not let it take even one back!
Chase it and it returns;
threaten it and it returns;
but if truth's roar knocks out its teeth
and casts it down lifeless,
even if you roll it over, it will not rise.

They say imagination thrives in poverty,
that pearl after pearl of poetry is born there.
They give, in clusters, examples:
Sathimuththa Pulavar,
Saththa Kavirayar,
and wandering *panars* of every day.

<!-- scan 181 -->

Yet there are many stories too
of poetry born in prosperity.
What of King Athiveerarama?
What of the southern Pandyan king?
Was he not a lion among poets
with sugarcane-sweet speech?
Those who say poverty multiplies poetry
are often the great wealthy men
who will not permit prosperity to the poor.
To reveal all this through learning,
let the gifted Ki. Va. Ja. come!

(Poet Ki. Va. Jagannathan completes his poem on “Poverty.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Poverty has gone; suffering is cured!
Excellent, excellent — let us praise the friend's poem
with dark eyes widening in delight.
But meanwhile we see another cruelty enter:
the selfish illusion that enslaves.
Against greed as it comes,
with a desire for battle let arrows be loosed;
the distinguished poet Abdul Kafur
will come carrying sharpened Tamil.

(Poet Abdul Kafur completes his poem on “Selfish Greed.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Again and again selfishness returns;
he lashed it with classical Tamil.
Good, good — good indeed!
Next—

<!-- scan 182 -->

let us take internal enmity in hand.
Like a fruit hidden beneath a leaf,
it cannot be seen by the eye;
it appears inside friendship,
taking a new form every day;
even a scorpion would blush at the poison it mixes.
Sword against sword is open enmity;
shoulder joined to shoulder,
while betrayal is done — that is internal enmity.
There is a brave man in this *kaviyarangam*
to seek that enemy
and strike it down the instant it is found.
His name itself is Thooran.
A man with moisture in the heart,
he will speak a poem of leaping valour.

(Poet Periyasamy Thooran completes his poem on “Internal Enmity.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Internal enmity ran away,
searched for another place to enter among us,
withered when it could not,
and danced only to submit!
I draw the line clearly:
like poison mixed skillfully into milk,
it first peers in;
then, like a banyan's aerial roots,
it spreads here and there.
Therefore bolt the door against internal enmity;
guard your distinct character.
Next:
those who murder the principle
“United we live” and crow over it—

<!-- scan 183 -->

who will oppose their acts?
With Tamil still as young as it was then,
Tamil sweet as jaggery when tasted,
there is one to string a poem.
Can there be six tastes without salt?
Can there be this arena without Subbu?
The man of Kothamangalam comes
to peck away at disunity.

(Poet Kothamangalam Subbu completes his poem on “Disunity.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

For long we have explained
that unity must exist within difference.
Those who do not understand writhe;
they explode like bombs.
In Kerala yesterday I saw,
wherever my eye fell,
a coconut palm rising from earth
to compete with the areca climbing toward the sky.
So too, in the Soviet land,
those placed low did not lose
when they competed with those placed high.
To make the stench of high and low wither away,
Thangarasan will offer a poem of strength.

(Poet Thangarasan completes his poem on “Inequality.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Crying out, “Why all this high and low?”
to fill in that foul nursery-bed,
we say violence is not the way;
we say the good path spoken by Mahatma Gandhi is the way.

<!-- scan 184 -->

It is Soundara, who guards the house by gentle means;
let her know her turn has come and sing.

(Poet Soundara Kailasam completes her poem on “Violence.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar, concluding:**

You have completed the *kaviyarangam*;
and within it I circle
like a bee that has drunk honey.
Am I Sadaiyappa Vallal,
who loosened gold for Kamban,
to read again what you have written
and award prizes? No, no.
Some listeners sit like tamarind pounded into a lump;
but can one ever cast aside a stick of red sugarcane
once one has bitten it?
Poets who have distilled and set out
honeyed nectar in this arena,
accept the greetings of this humble man
on this beautiful Pongal Festival Day.

Greetings.
Long live!

## Translator notes

- The source repeatedly exploits Tamil homophones and morphological play around `பால்` (*pāl*: milk / section or division), `தை` (the Tamil month *Thai*), `தையல்` (woman/sewing associations), `தைத்தல்` (to stitch) and `புதைத்தல்` (to bury). The English keeps the Tamil forms where the argument depends on their sound rather than inventing an equivalent pun.
- `உதய சூரியன்` (“Rising Sun”) carries both literal solar imagery and the political emblematic resonance present in Kalaignar's rhetoric; the translation does not neutralise that double register.
- `பட்டா`, `மனுநீதி`, *nadaswaram*, *yappu*, *thalai* and *thodai* are retained/transliterated where a generic English replacement would erase the cultural or rhetorical function.
- References to socialism/common ownership, cooperatives, land titles, rural administrative visits, electrification and agrarian policy are translated as the source's own political claims, not independently fact-checked or harmonised.

## Item 17 — On the Path Called Life

<!-- scan 185 -->

A presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled
“On the Path Called Life,”
broadcast by Chennai Radio
on 14 April 1970.

★

Greetings
to everyone who has come,
and to the poets who have come to shape verse.

The long world that had sunk in sleep
walks forward, showing the light of blue eyes;
year after year rolls on at full breath,
ready to stamp many seals upon time.
In the month of Chithirai
we see abundance smiling,
and we take pride in it.
The year that has gone was *Saumya* —
the year that gave our Saumyan to the Tamils.
Anna too wore “Saumya” as a pen-name
and piled up stories dripping flavour — then went, dead.
As “Saumya” disappeared and “Sādhārana” came,
so that

<!-- scan 186 -->

Saumyan has disappeared,
and this *sādhārana* — this ordinary man — has come before you.

Without an arena many people play round-games in politics;
without monkeys we see plenty of monkey-business in society.
Scratch one sore and it multiplies;
rebuke men of low quality and the same thing happens.
In such a situation,
when some cannot tolerate this ordinary man
coming to rule the earth,
what will become of me
if I also come to rule poetry?

In the old day,
one who poured oblations into a thousand sacrifices ruled;
in this day,
one who knows poetry and composes a thousand songs rules.
Can the mind tolerate it?
Is this proper?
We can stop it!
We can suppress it at once!
We can break it!
Break an arm,
cripple a leg —
so some may mutter.
But this ordinary man thinks instead:
“Will darkness recede and dawn arrive?
Can we still help the poor?”

If the monstrous demon-darkness withdraws,
if the evil attachment to private ownership falls away,
then the principles of an ordered common ownership
may dwell freely everywhere
in the lives of people who work the land —
so this ordinary man thinks,
to enrich the path called life.

<!-- scan 187 -->

Our policy says the path cannot be enriched through violence.
To remove the division called low and high,
we need calm light and peace.
Every revolution must be a revolution of the mind;
every uprising, an uprising within;
every awakening, in writing and speech;
every thought directed toward putting social justice into practice.
Had these existed,
a golden age of advancement,
an age of newness,
would have arrived long ago.

Is there a human being who has understood
what life itself is?
We say birds, animals, creeping things
“live.”
But of rational man we do not merely say he lives;
we say he conducts a life.
One who lacks the confidence
that we ourselves conduct our lives
is hardly human.
Many move life forward saying,
“Somehow we are passing the day.”

When two people meet in the street
and one asks, “How are you — well?”
will the answer ever be,
“Yes, I am well”? Tell me!
“Somehow I am here,” he drags out,
answers in great weariness,
and moves away from his friend.
Why this exhaustion in life?
He says the wife whose lips gave honey is false;
he says the child he gave her is false;
he says, “Ah! one must swim the ocean of family life.”
If you ask when all this begins—

<!-- scan 188 -->

I can tell the age without fail: after seventy!
Poor fellow—
yet you say even between twenty and thirty
there are young renunciates
who call pleasure itself the highest bliss of renunciation.
Except for one or two,
I have doubts about all of them.
They have not subdued the five senses;
I would say one or two senses are simply in famine!

Some others say in daylight,
“All this is false,”
and at night,
“All this is true.”

The strong man who thinks
he alone was born to live,
like a bird swooping low to seize fish,
makes a habit of torturing the weak.
It is good for him to reform himself;
otherwise the laws made to reform him
will reform him,
and stop the swagger of the wicked.

Kuppan, who bends lower each time he is struck,
believing his clan deity will help him,
bends and bends until a hunchback grows upon him.
Medicines that make the hunchback stand upright
will soon arrive —
in the new house where his clan god gets a lamp!

We have seen a crowd of children
scoop handful after handful of sweet sugar
into their mouths.
Some people, instead of enjoying life's sweetness
with a sense of measure,
turn their lives into mud trampled by cattle.

<!-- scan 189 -->

The tiger eats only as much as hunger needs;
the snake, even after hunger has passed,
swallows deer and lies stupefied.
Though we see both scenes in the forest,
those acting in life's drama still do not learn — what can we do?

“Death at six, death at a hundred —
let what must happen happen,”
says the lion-like hero who wages a righteous struggle.
“Death at six, death at a hundred —
let what must happen happen,”
says the one who ceaselessly consumes every pleasure.
He is no hero;
he is lower than an animal.

One who lives only for
his house, his wife, his children, his circle
is not conducting a life either.
One who says,
“Our country, our rights, our language —
I shall do everything I can to protect them all,”
that one is a public human being.

Therefore,
do not float in a jar of honey
like a bee drunk on nectar;
do not preach the renunciate's line,
“What do I need?”
do not squeeze the poor saying,
“Everything is mine!”

On the path called life,
art and education are signboards shown by poets;
office and devotion will be explained by bards;
fame and wealth will be poured out by poets.
First: education.

They say what one has learned is a handful of earth.
For the learned, earth is in the hand;
for the unlearned, I say, it sits upon the head!
From a handful of clay a pot may be shaped—

<!-- scan 190 -->

but the earth piled on the head
is not even fit for the kiln.

When they said, “Learn while young,”
why did some take it to mean,
“Pick up a stone while young and throw it”?
Kamban is called great in learning;
should one who is great in learning
become great in quarrelling?

If the uneducated are “people with two wounds on the face,”
why do the educated shine as people with thirty-two wounds?
Have you thought about it?

I am not one who saw college;
but stone has never soaked into my hand.
Poetry wells in the heart,
a little art wells there too;
fame rises on the earth.
Those close to me said
what I had learned in Tamil was enough
to rule the town called Tamil;
I agreed without heart.
Today, by your grace,
I have escaped!

On the path called life
there is no time for dry stories.
Let us hear Puviyarasu,
who has come to sing
of rich “Education.”

(Poet Puviyarasu completes his poem on “Education.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: office.
To remain in the forest for a lifetime is not so great;
to place one's lips on the tip of a wasp's sting
and endure the pain is not so great;
but to remain in this office—

<!-- scan 191 -->

that is the greatest trouble, O Kanchi Ekambane!

Those whose entrails burn
thinking, “I sit at the summit of the temple tower,”
should know that I do not possess even the happiness
of the flock of pigeons
that build nests and live upon its upper storey.
Offer me sympathy;
keep a sense of friendship.

Office is a cruel knife:
for some it cuts throats.
But if office is accepted as responsibility
only to help others,
can the same life-taking knife
not be used to save many lives?
Call office the knife that cuts fruit;
it is evil when it becomes a sword of vengeance.

King Louis, who strutted arrogantly
thinking he would remain in office forever,
had his head shaved clean off by history,
and a new order arose — we shall not forget it.

If, through good servants fit for Valluvar who speaks virtue,
one rules the earth while rejecting slander,
poets will gather to speak of our merit
and the nation will bless us.

One who clings to office like a monitor lizard
later says, “Never mind the lizard — just release my hand!”
Some began biting because it tasted sweet as sugarcane,
then slipped away saying,
“Good heavens, it burns salty!”

When the poet Rahman is here to sing of office,
I myself have already sung too many lines.
This is not infatuation with office;
it is infatuation with song!
Let us see honey of office drip
from the tongue of our friend Rahman,
one of the good poets who rain upon our country.

<!-- scan 192 -->

Split the Tamil word `பதவி` (*padhavi*, office):
remove the letter `ப`,
and it cries `தவி! தவி!` — “suffer! suffer!”
That is why we suffer when there is office!
Let him sing;
then we shall see.

(Poet Abdul Rahman completes his poem on “Office.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: wealth.
Scholars say it is called *selvam* because it keeps going —
“we go, we go,” *selvōm, selvōm*.
Would it not also be fitting to say
it is called *selvam* because it sits in the iron chests of rich men
until the creeping `செல்` eats it away?

They say Lakshmi is the goddess of wealth;
yet a crore of Lakshmis labour in poverty.
The same Valluvar who said,
“The wealth of wealth is wealth of hearing,”
made us understand too
that this world does not exist for one without material means.
If you are satisfied saying,
“Ear-wealth is enough,”
Radio will fill your ears with four cries of “Long live!”
and tell you, “Go safely,”
without even giving travel expenses!

The wealthy should be like Seethakathi,
like the generous Tamil Kumanan,
like Pari,
and smile in this world.
That is the grammar for those who possess wealth.
For one “possessed by wealth,”

<!-- scan 193 -->

the grammar is Valluvar's line:
“Even if one begs water for a cow,
there is no degradation worse for the tongue than begging.”

When Tamilannal is here
to pour a rain of wealth upon all red-tongued poets,
it is not proper for me
to seize all the wealth myself.
I call him: give us wealth, Tamilannal!

(Poet Tamilannal completes his poem on “Wealth.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: art.
...
After the *kaviyarangam* ends,
one says “the work is to *kalai* — disperse — the hall,”
and offers thanks!
`கலைத் திலகம்` is a title;
but split `கலை - திலகம்`,
and the maiden becomes a tree struck by blight!
Art joins politics in this age;
but when art is made a hostage,
what politics can there be in any age?

In *Kalithogai* there is a love-scene
of art giving its own shadow to the hostage;
and we see every day in the art world
the sight of one fearing one's own shadow.

When the moon's phase decreases,
its light decreases;
we see that every day in the sky.
Where art is full,
there is compassion in the heart,
and the quality that feels another's sorrow.
When an artist sees a poor person in tears,
he sees a character he himself has created,
understands that person's state through imagination,
and performs the duty of removing the sorrow.

<!-- scan 194 -->

Those who said “art for art's sake” have fallen silent.
They understood that beautiful light exists for the house,
not merely for the lamp.

There are so many kinds and counts of art:
beautiful art,
fine art,
dance,
song,
the sixty-four arts —
apart from northern art and southern art!

My friend Vaali has come
to take pearl-like Tamil
and offer all these arts here.
To prove it wrong to say
“Vaali is one with a tail,”
here comes a Vaali without a tail.
If everyone with a tail is Vaali,
are all people with legs *kālis*? No, no!
Let Vaali come,
stringing garlands of thought
in jewel-coloured Tamil.

(Poet Vaali completes his poem on “Art.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: fame.
When crabs raise their heads on the seashore
and you stretch out a hand and run to catch them,
they vanish into hiding before you know where they went.
Fame is like that.

<!-- scan 195 -->

Seek it and embrace it — it runs away.
Give it a crore — it withers.
It does not swoon before mansions;
it does not hesitate when hidden away.
It clings to the person of integrity;
it kicks the arrogant away.
It scatters flowers at the feet of the self-sacrificing;
it bows to the feet of the courageous.
On the greatness of that rare fame,
the worthy poet Mariyadas
will compose in sweet Tamil.

(Poet Mariyadas completes his poem on “Fame.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: devotion.
If devotion means setting out
fruit piled in the palm, *appam*, flattened rice and puffed rice,
those who speak of God will not all agree.
Going on pilgrimages
to holy places, temples and sacred tanks is devotion;
placing holy places, temples and sacred tanks
with grace inside the mind is devotion too.
Kannappa lifting his eye
and placing it upon God is one devotion;
Sakkiyar closing his eyes
and worshipping God by throwing stones is another.
If one cries, “My Lord! Shiva!”
and praises God
but never once looks up at the poor,
where is devotion?

<!-- scan 196 -->

Ekalavya offering his thumb
was the old devotion to a guru;
“Hey, guru! Offer me your hand!”
is the new devotion.
The maiden calling her husband god
is old devotion;
poor husband — kneeling before the maiden
is the husband's devotion!

There are many devotions within devotion.
To let loose their whole sequence
in powerful words,
Mudiyarasu comes.
I invite him without saying,
“Finish quickly, king!”
Come!

(Poet Mudiyarasu completes his poem on “Devotion.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar, concluding:**

You who have come to the path called life,
you who have seen many roads:
receive education — receive it without blemish;
receive office — receive it with culture;
receive wealth — receive it with character;
let art spread fragrance through your life;
let fame gather without your going in search of it;
and if the flood of devotion flows toward
country, language and people,
God's grace will reach you.
He is in the smile of the poor.
Do not forget this.

Greetings.
Long live!

## Translator notes

- `சௌமிய` / `சௌமியன்` and `சாதாரண` / “ordinary man” form a calendrical-name and political-self-description sequence. The English keeps the source names rather than replacing them with external calendrical glosses.
- The poem repeatedly builds arguments out of Tamil morphology: `பதவி` → `தவி`, `செல்வம்` / `செல்வோம்` / `செல்`, `கலை` and Vaali/`வால்`, among others. Tamil forms are retained in notes or in-line where the humour depends on sound.
- The references to private ownership, common ownership, social justice, law, office, religion and devotion are translated as Kalaignar's source rhetoric rather than neutralised or independently harmonised.
- Embedded Tirukkural, literary and devotional references are kept visibly distinct from Kalaignar's surrounding commentary; the English does not silently rewrite the final-cleared Tamil wording.

## Item 18 — Arithmetic

<!-- scan 197 -->

A presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled “Arithmetic,”
held under the auspices of the Salem Tamil Sangam
on 2 August 1970.

★

Bees gathered in Salem city
to taste sweet Tamil fragrant as cardamom!
Poets like red sugarcane,
who have become, with good character,
aerial roots for the extempore poet Bharati
and for Bharathidasan!

Friend Ganapathiyar,
a pure gem in a lofty, adorned face,
has founded this Sangam
so Tamil rain may fall upon the world;
like waves of a roaring sea,
he circles Tamilakam without rest,
pledging lifelong service.
He caught hold even of this spinning-top called me
and dragged me here,
determined to expose my poetic power in public.

When Kamban and Koothan lived,
there was no room for everyone one saw
to wander about calling himself a poet.
But because this Karunanidhi is Chief Minister,
no one has yet dared
criticise my poetry to my face.
So far, that is profit enough!

<!-- scan 198 -->

That is why this humble man too has become bold.
He invited me without quite saying:
“Come punctually by nine-thirty;
do not arrive late
and then speak elegantly afterward.”

Since this is a poetry arena on arithmetic,
I too, who keep accounts in everything,
shall collect travel expenses punctually —
not only for myself,
but for my whole poetic family.
Do not worry, poets!

In former times arithmetic stood first in education;
hence the teacher was called *kanakkāyar*.
Accounts are of many kinds,
and those who keep them are of many kinds.

The capitalist reading the worker's sweat —
that is the money account.
A partner asking wages for his affection —
that is the office account.
A woman marking lines of charcoal in the kitchen
for what remains unpaid —
that is the milk account, the curd account.
Poor child: the account the parents write out for school
so he may somehow escape —
that is the home account.
Political geniuses who care nothing for sin or merit
keep one account before ordinary people:
the vote account!

In the herd: cattle count, goat count;
but do not keep an account only inside the liquor pot!

From Adi to Thai is a year;
so for thirteen months,
on interest at one hundred and three per hundred—

<!-- scan 199 -->

“Altogether five hundred and three!
Pay our debt without delay!”
That is the interest account in Chettiar country.

The treasure of classical Tamil:
the Eighteen *Keelkanakku* works!
The accountant's son does accounts;
the son who goes to marry a woman
does another kind of account!

In that account,
saying twenty plus eighteen is a marriage account;
taking twenty and eighteen together is a love account.
Now: no more than two — that is the family account.
To compete with Kuchela in this count
is a cruel account!

Because the account he made went wrong,
the man called Hitler kissed suicide.
A tiger will erase the account calculated by a deer;
then a hunter will bring down the tiger,
asking, “Why did you make the wrong calculation?”
That is the world!

In this body, eight spans tall,
we move because strings have been tied without count.
Though we have only one tongue
that speaks honeyed words,
through talent we make it into two
and wander about double-tongued.

God has given all of us
two hands in proper count;
why then does God alone
carry the burden of so many hands?
Fortunately,
if humankind had been created
with hands beyond count,
one hand would print counterfeit notes,
one hand would adulterate goods,
one hand would drag young women—

<!-- scan 200 -->

one hand would betray,
one hand would steal,
one hand would hold a knife,
one hand would dare cut a throat.
Today these jobs are done one by one,
and the police we have
can restrain the offenders.
Otherwise,
all those varieties of hands would sprout everywhere,
kill in varieties,
revel and dance;
the country would convulse.

Human beings must live by measure;
so the old saying tells us:
even if you cast something into the river,
measure it first.
There is nothing without calculation.

We cry proudly that man set foot on the moon.
How many crores were spent — calculated exactly?
If even a tiny error arose in the scientific calculation,
would those feet have returned?
Would they not have scattered in space,
leg one way and head another?

If a woman said,
“My husband gave me kisses beyond count,”
and kept a recording beside the bed,
would not even that have a count?
Therefore, there is nothing without arithmetic!

And if I too speak without keeping account of time,
continuing to recite line after line,
the poets who came to sing of arithmetic
may become angry beyond count.

<!-- scan 201 -->

So I call them now.
First: multiplication.

The multiplication of wind becomes a storm;
the multiplication of seedlings becomes a crop;
the multiplication of sweet juice becomes flavour;
the multiplication of a river becomes floodwater.
What becomes of the multiplication of Murugu's song?
Gold,
flowers,
and new poetry!

Salem city has so many distinctions.
When we asked for an iron plant,
people once said, “Salem? A city?”
Today, for Salem to become a great city,
Servarayan hill has given iron ore.
Even if Tamils delight
in that hill-fruit
and in Salem's mango,
my friend Murugu Sundaranar asks:
“Can either equal my crimson-pomegranate poem?”
He will give Tamil for us to drink.
They say the heart melts before *Thiruvasagam*;
his *vāsagam* too is like that.
Friend Murugu!
Tomorrow is Adi Perukku;
for now, sing and make it multiply!

(Poet Murugu Sundaram completes his poem on “Multiplication.”)

<!-- scan 202 -->

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: subtraction.
A thief enters the house while everyone is awake;
only afterward, when he is caught,
does he “wake up,” staring about,
and claim it was merely a game.
My friend Tamilannal has spent his life fruitfully
trying with full breath
to subtract deeds like these,
destroy evils,
condemn them fiercely.
He will speak on “Subtraction”
in classical Tamil overflowing with delight,
mixing honey,
adding taste,
multiplying goodness,
and dividing it out.
Without wasting time,
I clear the road for him.
Come and give it!

(Poet Tamilannal completes his poem on “Subtraction.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: division.
The poet who comes to gather
and offer the poetic nectar called division
is one who lives by the path laid down by the Prophet.
They say Vijayan is a hero
who plays with the bow;
this man is a Tamil hero
who plays with words.
When I return after many victories
to receive an award,
if someone asks what reward I want,
I shall say: “Give me Abdul Rahman.”
He sings now.

(Poet Abdul Rahman completes his poem on “Division.”)

<!-- scan 203 -->

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next: addition.
Kannadasan, who once stood in the subtraction account,
has today joined us in the addition account.
We are friends who joined in Salem.
Whose permission do we need
to join again in Salem?

They call my friend *Kaviyarasu* — King of Poets.
I become angry:
I am only a minister,
and he is a king, is he not?
Even if he is a king,
I shall say he is my spy.
For ten years, sir,
he went around in disguise
circling the enemy camp.
Today he has taken a new birth
and returned here.

I sent him flying as a cuckoo to sing;
but will a cuckoo's song carry
inside a gathering of owls?
So I called him back into my own garden.
Cuckoo who has come to the garden!
Parrot perched upon my shoulder!
Let the poem of addition resound
and plunge us into delight.

(Poet Kannadasan completes his poem on “Addition.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar, concluding:**

Addition and subtraction,
division and multiplication—
we trusted that they would form an alliance,
coo and embrace one another,
and so we gave them our vote!
But—

<!-- scan 204 -->

this alliance,
even if it is an alliance of quarrels,
is in Tamil a golden ornament,
an unyielding lion-band of song.

Which is greater:
addition, subtraction, multiplication or division?
To answer,
I alter one Tirukkural
and announce a new judgement.

The Tirukkural says:
“Shaving and growing are needless
if the world casts away what it condemns.”

My judgement says:
“Division and multiplication are needless,
if addition and subtraction are cast away!”

That is my verdicting voice.

Greetings.
Long live!

## Translator notes

- `கணக்கு` ranges across arithmetic, bookkeeping, social calculation, political calculation and domestic “counting.” The English title **Arithmetic** foregrounds the four operations that organise the second half while the translation keeps “account/count/calculation” where the Tamil shifts register.
- `பதினெண் கீழ்க் கணக்கு` refers to the classical Eighteen *Keelkanakku* works; it is retained as a literary title rather than turned into an arithmetic joke alone.
- The poem depends heavily on Tamil lexical play: `கணக்குப் பிள்ளை`, marriage/family “counts,” the many hands of gods, Salem wordplay, Murugu/*perukku* (“multiply/increase”), and Kannadasan moving from “subtraction” to “addition.” These are preserved as source rhetoric rather than silently replaced with invented English puns.
- The closing verse deliberately rewrites a Tirukkural into the four arithmetic operations. The English distinguishes the quoted Kural from Kalaignar's comic verdict and does not alter the final-cleared Tamil witness.

## Item 19 — Democracy as Nehru Saw It

<!-- scan 205 -->

A presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* titled
“Democracy as Nehru Saw It,”
held in Chennai on 14 November 1970.

★

Navalar of golden character,
ministers of distinction,
poets who have come carrying Sangam Tamil,
learned elders —
accept my greetings.

I bow at the feet of our Anna
who sleeps beside the Bay of Bengal,
and begin this presiding address.

With the punnai tree spreading shade,
with Mother Tamil's lap giving comfort,
with Chennai city shedding light,
the Tamil Government that strives
to drive away former darkness
strings a poem in Muthamil
for Nehru,
elder son of the great Gandhi;
in the arena of poetry
it garlands the Pandit
who gathered fame in the arena of the world.

<!-- scan 206 -->

I entered a garden full of flowers
and found a scene waiting for me;
I turn it into poetry for you.

Champak whispered something
into the ears of the smiling jasmine;
the two ran to chrysanthemum
and spoke into her ear;
then all three murmured to mullai,
and the four began walking
the whole flower-garden together.

Whom were the flower-women seeking?
Were they searching merely for fibre
to bind themselves into a garland?
I wondered.
They spread fragrance in the breeze
and searched and searched;
I could not tell for whom.

“My knees hurt,” said Mullai.
“My feet are red,” said Champak, showing her teeth.
Jasmine and Chrysanthemum too were greatly troubled.
Then, from a corner of the garden,
a sound of lament came.
The flower-women cried, “There! There!”
and flew toward it.

“O lordly flower! Mountain of fame!
Have you gone, my beloved?
O king! King of principle!
Where is the Death that robbed you from me?
You lifted me, kissed me,
and placed me upon your heart —
and today you have left only me
and gone so far away.
What justice is this?
You gave honey to my lips;
in return

<!-- scan 207 -->

I gave you mine — have you forgotten?
You held me to your chest
until the flood of happiness overflowed.
Was your heart stone,
to leave this woman?”

Hearing the cry,
the fragrant flower-women drew near.
Rose stood there,
weeping like a sluice-gate burst open,
on the point of fainting.
Jasmine and Mullai came beside her:

“Why, Rose, are you still crying?
A good man lived so the world might live;
a strong man lived to gather us close.
Will what is gone return?
Stop lamenting.
Queen of flowers — show us a smile!”

They consoled her,
but beautiful Rose found no consolation
and sobbed:

“I called dew the spit of the night;
I called my lord's kiss nectar.
That day he gave me nectar
and stirred desire in me;
today he has left me drenched
in this spittle-dew — what shall I do?
The uncrowned king will never wear me again;
whenever I think of it,
thorns pierce my whole body.
What use is this redness
to one with no smile in life?
What use is fragrance
to one with no joy in the heart?

He sat upon India's throne;
I sat upon the throne of his heart.
He is sleeping; he will rise,
come down the steps,
and call this maiden.
Before then,
in the theatre of his heart—

<!-- scan 208 -->

I put on my red dress to dance.
But before I could,
the monkey-hand of Time
turned his ‘garland’ into a funeral garland
and turned my life into night.

Go away; leave me alone.
On the sacred man's birthday,
to make the river of my tears
an offering at his feet —
that is my duty.”

Hearing Rose's words I too grew weary.
Thinking of her withering
from separation from her king,
I turned back without speaking.
Today I came without plucking any flower,
even if my wife's hair should lose its fragrance.
If I pluck a flower today,
it is only to string a garland
for the great hero Nehru.

He tore down the flag of the white rulers
who tore away our lap and seized our freedom;
he held high the flag of liberation,
a warrior of principle.
To receive blows,
to endure repression,
he laboured tirelessly
along the path laid down by the Mahatma — a lion.

He spent the beauty of youth
inside the darkness of prison — a sacrificer.
His anger could crackle like mustard in hot oil,
yet, we say, he poured that oil
into the sacrifice of freedom.
Though born amid mountains of money,
he stood as a mountain of character
that embraced the poor.

The spinning-top is a figure for swift labour;
the Pandit's labour would defeat the spinning-top.

<!-- scan 209 -->

Thunder in his speech;
youth throbbing in his writing;
a measure of honey in his manners;
an explosive roar in action;
tenacity in his nature;
the freedom flag in his hand;
a little hair upon his head—
(do not look at me and ask what survives here!)

Because he stood face to face
against all that opposed justice,
he became *Nehru* — the one to whom things “occurred” in this play of `நேர்/நேரு`.
When great enemies crossed India's long border,
people worried, “What will happen?”
He wiped the grief from their hearts and said,
“Nothing will happen while I remain” —
and so he was Nehru.

He asked a child,
“If there is courage and purity of heart,
will suffering happen?”
The bud-and-flower answered,
“Only joy will happen, Uncle!”
Thus he became “Nehru Mama.”

His own position on religion and belief in God
was distinct,
yet he governed along a good path
without wounding the minds of others.
At the proper time he wrote in his will
that no religious ritual was needed after his death,
and shed light for the world.
He took non-alignment as a good principle
and shaped a policy suited to the words
our Poongundranar uttered for the world to praise:
“Every place is our town; everyone our kin.”

<!-- scan 210 -->

The strange pattern of time
may seat a donkey as king of the forest;
in an auction some may squat at the summit of a temple tower.
But Jawahar did not become leader of the Indian world that way.

Salem mango,
Chennai seashore,
Korkai pearl,
Kollegal silk,
Tanjore temple,
cool-water Cauvery,
Kanchi at the loom,
Pandyan of broad shoulders—
though differences of thought existed,
when Nehru died,
Anna, who had known the prison cell,
shed tears and wept in grief.

In a land that honours dignified politics,
let us praise Nehru, equal to the eye, in song.

“Army comes leaping —
enemy comes scooping —
firebrand comes —
black mark comes —
Chinese thorn comes,”
some mocked;
yet he said dawn's Venus would come,
plucked the foe away,
and today lies in sacred sleep —
Nehru lies in sacred sleep.

Our *Kalingathu Parani* tells
of mothers beating their breasts and crying
before sons lying with mouths closed,
asking whether any wound marks the jewel-like lips;
of the demonic dance that is the face of war,
of dogs and jackals feeding,
of battlefield cruelty
where fruit, flower and tender bud all fall together.

<!-- scan 211 -->

From that Kalinga,
King Ashoka took up the path of virtue after embracing peace.
Along that moral road,
to protect the world,
Lord Nehru clipped the wings of vultures
and released a white dove into the sky.

“Practise the foremost virtue;
help establish just justice;
let equality be the substance — democracy;
beat the drum and proclaim it.”
This is the poem of the art-rich bard
from wave-beaten Puduvai,
sung to transform our condition.

Jawahar cried, “Democracy, democracy!”
Yet we shall not forget
that some acted to make their own solitary rule flourish.
So that money-rule might fall in a moment,
Nehru, leader by character, strove too;
but time was not enough,
and Time itself would not wait.

If my poem continues longer,
time will not tolerate it
and the poets will not tolerate it either.
So I end this opening poem
and call the first poet.

A male poet greatly eager
to sing of women's rights,
a warrior-poet who has given
many clear-Tamil songs
for the army guarding the rights of the soil:
let him say women must possess
not merely the right to serve food,
but the right to serve in the army;

<!-- scan 212 -->

let him say we will not accept
new rights only in dress.
Poet of freedom,
my handsome poet,
give us a tasty song-*appam*;
let the audience eat it like a home-made *appam*.

(Poet S. D. Sundaram completes his poem on “Women's Rights.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next:
he asks for freedom of speech.
It is his habit always
to speak in the middle of songs anyway;
so now he asks for freedom of speech
for himself alone in the *kaviyarangam*!

Some today ask for the right to abuse;
because of that I fear
the right of Tamil culture itself to breathe may disappear.
If friend Subbu has to ask,
“Time is up — will you give me the right or not?”
and I still have not given it,
that is my fault.

A sprig of coriander scents the curry;
Kothamangalam Subbu gives fragrance without confusion.

(Poet Kothamangalam Subbu completes his poem on “Freedom of Speech.”)

<!-- scan 213 -->

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next:
he asks me for freedom of writing.
He does not know my condition.
*Parabrahmam*, *Thookkumedai*,
the Rising Sun that gives the light of art—
I have lost many writings like these;
he does not know the story
of how even their first letters were banned.
Yet he asks me for freedom of writing.
Because I am now in a place that can grant rights,
I urge Murugu Sundaram to write his poem.
Come — pour Tamil honey.

(Poet Murugu Sundaram completes his poem on “Freedom of Writing.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next:
with creative verbal grace,
a gait like the flow of the Cauvery,
a pure purpose and subtle intelligence,
the poet Rahman strings verses.
He asks now for the right to vote / the right of the word — `வாக்குரிமை`.
I shall not say,
“Take it and then give it only to me”;
if I said that, it would no longer be voting right.
Come, friend.

<!-- scan 214 -->

I have granted the right to vote.
We shall give travel expenses for the song you sing;
only do not ask for a sum for the vote!

(Poet Abdul Rahman completes his poem on “Voting Rights.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar:**

Next:
call him a king,
or call him a thundering drum — neither is wrong.
Though he wandered making playful contact
among those who write in coarse style,
through the friendship that rubs close
he returned again with incomparable affection
to this humble man who bears government responsibility:
Kaviyarasu has returned.

And as soon as he arrives,
he asks for property rights.
Poor man, wounded heart!
There is justice even in his asking:
ten or twelve children!
Never mind — I grant property rights.
Let it grow; come and give.

(Poet Kannadasan completes his poem on “Property Rights.”)

**Chair — Kalaignar, concluding:**

In this poetry arena
we have granted the rights the poets asked for.

<!-- scan 215 -->

Understanding the condition of the nation,
to cherish and protect good rights,
we shall extend a hand for relationship
and raise our voice for our rights.
With that cry let us disperse,
and bow to Nehru,
the complete human being.

Long live!

## Translator notes

- The floral opening personifies Rose as Nehru's bereaved beloved and uses garland/dew/thorn imagery as memorial rhetoric; the English keeps the allegory rather than converting it into biographical exposition.
- Several passages exploit `நேர் / நேரு / நேரும்` and `வாக்கு / வாக்குரிமை` (word/voice/vote) sound relationships. Tamil forms are retained or explained where English cannot reproduce the wordplay without inventing new source meaning.
- `பரப்பிரம்மம்`, `தூக்குமேடை` and `உதயசூரியன்` are preserved as source-named writings/works in the freedom-of-writing passage; Phase 4 does not silently substitute outside bibliographic titles.
- The Nehru, China-border, non-alignment, religion, democracy, war/peace and political-rights passages are translated as Kalaignar's source claims and commemorative rhetoric, not independently fact-checked or harmonised.
- The guest-poet sequence remains distinct: women's rights, speech, writing, voting/voice rights and property rights.

## Item 20 — Thank You, Thank You!

<!-- scan 216 -->

At the Salem exhibition on 1 August 1971,
at the *kaviyarangam* “Kalaignar in Perspective,”
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi took part
and, in thanks for the praise offered to him,
wrote this immediately on the stage
and sang it there.

The poets in this *kaviyarangam*
called me father,
mother,
child,
leader,
servant,
beloved woman,
and beloved man.
I dedicate these lines of thanks to them.

Festival chairman Vezhavendan,
with a noble gaze,
has spread out a poetry-market here
from morning onward.
Poet Kannappan explained in song
the figure of the father.

Like the roar of a shouting sea-wave
you gave your poem;
young poet Muthulingam,
you gave my mother back to me again.
Through the mouth of Aara Amudhan,
I became

<!-- scan 217 -->

a child whose games never end!

One bearing the name of the black-gold Kamaraj leader
accepted me here,
in flashing Tamil,
as a leader too —
a Kamarasan.

Avinasi Mani, the poet
who once worked in my house as a servant-boy,
has today turned me myself
into the servant in his song —
what times these are!

Even after seeing my wife seated beside me,
what courage has this Murugu Sundari
to fall in love with me?

And Kannadasan —
you who separated,
wandered in many directions,
then corrected your heart
and hurried back home —
my beloved man!
Do not go like Kovalan
to sell the anklet.
I, the Kazhagam's Kannagi,
will stand guard at the gate.
Do not be troubled!

Thank you! Thank you! Thank you!
My thanks to everyone!

## Translator notes

- The poem is an improvised stage-response to a sequence of poets assigning Kalaignar family, political, servant and romantic roles. The English keeps those role reversals rather than recasting them as narrative exposition.
- The closing address to Kannadasan uses the *Silappathikaram* Kovalan/Kannagi pair together with `கழகம்` (“Kazhagam”) political identity. The translation retains “Kazhagam's Kannagi” rather than inventing a non-source institutional gloss.
- Personal and political references are translated as source-stage humour; the English does not independently interpret the circumstances behind Kannadasan's “return home.”

## Item 21 — Silver Jubilee

<!-- scan 218 -->

Extracts from the presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* held in Rajaji Hall
on 15 August 1972 by All India Radio, Madras,
for the twenty-fifth anniversary — the Silver Jubilee —
of India's independence.

To everyone gathered in this assembly,
to the poets of beautiful Tamil,
to Navalar and the ministers,
and to Silambu — bone in form, yet love embodied —
my bowed greetings.

People of this sacred motherland,
you have accepted me as your servant.
I bow to you today,
promising that I shall act
without breaking the word I have given.

Young woman of India!
With flowers in the hair you have gathered up,
with darting fish-like eyes below,
you stand in a new picture,
marked with dots and patterns,
writing out for us:
“Silver Jubilee of Freedom.”
Young maiden! Mother of awakening!

<!-- scan 219 -->

Beyond the Vindhyas,
in heroic Tamil Nadu,
I bow in greeting
to the warriors who raced like horses in competition
and leapt ahead of one another
into the fire of sacrifice.

For twenty-five years
we have watched this lamp burn.
We stand worshipfully before our grandfathers,
our forebears,
who poured their blood as oil
and lit that lamp.

In a lovely grove where flocks of cuckoos sing,
shall we listen to the forest shriek of owls?
Shall a woodland where deer roam,
a garden where peacocks dance,
be turned into an elegant pavilion for monkeys?
Shall we build a house for the breeze to enter
and then let fire come in?

Our country — our people — our government.
Even if, like the face of the moon,
there are small blemishes,
it is our country — our people — our government.
We shall tell the world in surging Tamil
that lion-hearted warriors and golden women
will guard it.

It is impossible today
to speak of every one of those people.
So the friends at the radio
asked that a counted few be apportioned among poets.
Their invitation fell upon my ear like honeyed sound,
and leaving behind a world of clamorous noise,
I came running to hear the ordered music of poets.

A Bharati has come to sing of Bharati.
The sweet Tamil songs of Subramania Bharati

<!-- scan 220 -->

may be heard anew
through the new song of Suddhananda Bharati.
Though he is one who has renounced everything,
there is one attachment he has not renounced —
his attachment to Tamil.
Though he has learned many languages,
he has not forgotten his mother tongue.
He cannot shine here
if he does not sing of Bharati here.
So now he sings.

*(Poet Yogi Suddhananda Bharati sings on Bharathiyar.)*

Next —
V. O. Chidambaranar,
our noble one who ran a Swadeshi ship from Thoothukudi
until the British trembled,
then became destitute,
and pulled the oil-press in Coimbatore prison
while guarding his principles.

We already earned honour
by raising a statue to him in Chennai.
On the fifth day of next month,
Prime Minister Indira Gandhi
has gladly agreed
to unveil another statue in Thoothukudi.
Our postal department too
has thought of commemorating his surpassing fame.

Like Chidambaranar, who went to prison in sacrifice,
our friend Sundaram,
who also knew imprisonment and sacrifice
and whose mind creates classical Tamil verse,
has come to offer a poem.

*(Poet S. D. Sundaram sings on V. O. C.)*

<!-- scan 221 -->

Next —
V. V. S. Aiyar,
a warrior of action and truth,
was caught in a furious whirl of water
and one day disappeared from life.

Who is this young shoot
who has come today
to make that freedom fighter rise and walk before us again?
The highly gifted Soundara Kailasam,
who sings fitting Tamil like an Avvai of our own age,
has sent her poem gracefully to us
through her daughter.
Young woman, sing the lullaby you have learned.

*(On behalf of poet Mrs Soundara Kailasam, her daughter sings on V. V. S. Aiyar.)*

Next —
Subramania Siva,
the incomparable martyr
whose lion's voice made those who ruled us tremble;
who, despite the cruelty of disease,
travelled the motherland on foot day after day
to break the Mother's chains;
who was imprisoned in the Tiruchirappalli fortress;
who was like a rising sun that drives away darkness.

Who is fit to sing of him?
Can food without salt be satisfying?
Can a *kaviyarangam* without Kothamangalam Subbu have flavour?
A sprig of coriander scents the curry;
my friend Kothamangalam Subbu's poems

<!-- scan 222 -->

spread fragrance everywhere,
and sweeten the hearts of the Tamils who hear them.
At this age, instead of sitting idle chanting “Siva, Siva,”
you have come eager to sing of Siva.
If you say you will not mount this assembly,
will the assembly not lose its savour?
Do not let it turn tasteless.
Give the gathering flavour, Subbu!

*(Poet Kothamangalam Subbu sings on Subramania Siva.)*

Next —
attachment is not wrong; it is not sin.
But when attachment ripens into frenzy,
it can bring strange and dangerous consequences.

There was a young man who believed
that unless imperialism was driven out of India,
his own body could not rest;
who was bold enough to think
that to drive out one foreign power
it could be right to seek the aid of another;
who became an elder guide to Netaji Bose
and even tried to obtain German military assistance.

He was Champakaraman Pillai,
born in an honourable old family
in the Tamil soil of Nanjil,
who died abroad in fame,
yet possessed the pride to wish
that after his death his ashes
should be scattered in Tamil Nadu.
To sing the valour of that martial Tamil
comes Vaanampadi,
a poet from the flower-rich banks of the Kaveri,
a cultured friend of long standing,
who gladly offers us a feast of verse.

<!-- scan 223 -->

*(Poet Vaanampadi sings on Champakaraman Pillai.)*

Next —
violence and terrorism
are not good methods; they harm a country.
The noble path laid down by Gandhi and Anna
is non-violence.
Yet some among our forebears argued
that in those days,
when foreign rule had to be removed
and the fetters of slavery cut,
seeking another path was not necessarily wrong.

Among those who believed so
Vanchinathan grew like a storm.
In those days a British official named Ashe
was the representative of colonial rule in Tirunelveli.
When the train halted at Maniyachi,
Vanchinathan's group surrounded it as planned.
Vanchi pulled the trigger of his handgun;
the Englishman died.
Driven by a sense of self-respect,
Vanchi then shot himself and died by suicide —
and his family suffered.

If the *Kanchi thinai* is about guarding,
did not Vanchi too guard freedom?
Without forgetting that history,
Arunachalam comes now to give it voice.
Let us hear his poem.

<!-- scan 224 -->

*(Poet K. C. S. Arunachalam sings on Vanchinathan.)*

Without looking to wife,
mother or family,
believing it a duty
to guard the honour of the motherland,
he entered the field of struggle with a sacred heart.

To volunteers who gathered rank upon rank
as people gather to watch a temple chariot,
he was a fitting leader,
a warrior who gave courage,
a generous man who roared;
unafraid of authority or domination,
he held the flag he had taken up,
refused to loosen his grip,
and though beaten,
would not let it go —
and died there.

Kumaran who does not die,
Kumaran who guarded the flag,
a pole star shining in the sky of sacrifice —
Chidambara Raghunathan will show him to us here
with grace.

*(Poet Tho. Mu. Chidambara Raghunathan sings on Tiruppur Kumaran.)*

Next —
we have written down seven names here.
Were these alone the people who brought us
this immeasurable freedom?
How many more great figures,
like the finial of a temple tower,
like a lamp upon a hill,
won immense fame?
How many martyrs remain unseen,
like anchoring roots hidden under the soil?
Naidu, Naicker, Mudaliar —
those of an earlier generation in Tamil Nadu

<!-- scan 225 -->

who sowed the seed of longing for freedom —
Varadarajulu, Periyar E. V. R., Thiru. Vi. Ka., Jeeva —
are all these to be put on a list of people whose names are unknown?
If we continue without fatigue,
will we not find countless martyrs?

To sing generously of their fame,
my close friend,
a lover of Tamil,
wherever he may be,
with classical Tamil surging like the Aadi floods,
a king of poets whose beautiful verses multiply,
Kannadasan —
we have all come patiently,
knowing what is in his mind.

O parrot of freedom!
O cuckoo floating upon comforting words!
Let us hear your gentle music.
Come, come — give us your poem.

*(Poet Kannadasan sings on the unnamed martyrs.)*

“Painted groves, to make you beautiful,
how many comrades once
poured out their blood at your roots?
Lotus-filled tanks, in the time that gave you to us,
how many of our pure brothers
fell away and vanished —
shall we tell the world?”

These are lines of the Revolutionary Poet Bharathidasan.

<!-- scan 226 -->

Holding those lines as good and true,
let us pass victoriously through
the Silver Jubilee of national freedom,
and with hearts rejoicing
wish that the incomparable Golden Jubilee
may come swiftly.

Accept my greetings,
my salutations,
and my thanks.

## Translator notes

- `வெள்ளி விழா` is rendered **Silver Jubilee**, the source's twenty-fifth-anniversary frame for Indian independence.
- The translation preserves the source's named freedom-fighter sequence and its guest-poet hand-offs rather than treating the passage as an external historical summary.
- The Vanchinathan, Champakaraman Pillai and other historical descriptions are translated as Kalaignar's source claims; this layer does not fact-check or harmonise them with outside accounts.
- The final Bharathidasan passage is kept as an embedded quotation and not absorbed into Kalaignar's own voice.

## Item 22 — Anna Is Here

<!-- scan 227 -->

Extracts from the presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* held in Kalaivanar Arangam, Chennai,
on 2 September 1972.

Sandalwood!
Young breeze that comes gliding from southern Podhigai!
Unfailing lamp!
New flower of fragrance!
Great one who taught us
to guard as we guard our eyes
the virtues called dignity,
duty and discipline!

Muthamil itself,
which captured every thought
of lion-hearted Tamils
and now, beside the Bay of Bengal,
with lips closed and eyes closed,
rests in silent penance!

Leader who created a good government
so that every hut might rise
and shine like a temple tower;
so that the darkness of ignorance
and murderous poverty might perish and retreat;
so that everyone might attain every welfare
and live in joy!

<!-- scan 228 -->

Anna of the younger brothers
who understand your thought before it is spoken
and bring it to fulfilment!
My very life,
who made me what I am
and protected me here!

Day after day
we carry out the noble work
of making every one of your dreams real.
In every task we perform,
Anna — you are here!

Mother!
Tree that called me “younger brother”
in a mother's voice!
Whenever my mother calls me,
I hear you calling.
In my mother's call,
Anna — you are here!

The poor are smiling
under the government of your younger brother.
Many say they see God in that smile.
But we —
we see you.
In the smile of the poor,
Anna — you are here!

One day in Arur,
an old woman who had regained her sight
called your younger brother “God.”
And that “God”

<!-- scan 229 -->

raised his hands
and worshipped you there.

Yes, Anna!
When the knife-edge opens
the eyes of those poor people,
in those very eyes —
in that recovered vision —
your smiling face appears, ayya!

Morning and evening,
while we labour without rest,
at every moment,
at every hour,
in everything,
Anna — you are here!

And more than that...
when, after finishing our work,
one day we come
to sleep in your lap,
Anna —
you will be there too.

Our Anna who sleeps
on the wave-washed seashore!
Unquenchable lamp!
Light of love!
Bearing the flame of the lamp you gave us —
the Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam —
we shall drive away
all the deceitful acts of the men of darkness
and raise the flag of victory!

## Translator notes

- `அண்ணன் இருக்கின்றார்` is rendered **Anna Is Here**, preserving the poem's repeated claim of Anna's continuing presence rather than treating the title as a purely locative statement.
- The repeated refrain `அண்ணனே! நீ இருக்கின்றாய்!` is kept as **Anna — you are here!** to preserve its direct-address cadence.
- The references to welfare measures, restored eyesight and the DMK lamp are translated as the poem's political-elegiac rhetoric, without external commentary.

## Item 23 — Anna, a Poetry Assembly

<!-- scan 230 -->

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the Anna Memorial Day *kaviyarangam*
held in Rajaji Hall, Chennai,
on 4 February 1973.

★

I bow at the feet of our Anna
who sleeps beside the Bay of Bengal,
and begin this presiding address.

Eight poets have come,
accepting my chairmanship,
to weave silken poems.
In a Tamil Nadu where some roam about hurling abuse,
let us praise these weavers of poetry.

It has been more than a year
since I last mounted such a stage and sang.
So when Arivazhagan and Veerasamy called,
I did not say, “All right, let us see.”
I said instead, “Is that so? I am coming,”
and agreed at once.

<!-- scan 231 -->

I did not come because
at any cost you must hear my poem.
I came to hear the fine verse
of these poets of rare distinction.

I had no time to write poetry,
yet the artistic heart would not leave me alone.
From morning there were meetings with visitors;
then the launch ceremony
for a trial boat-run on the beautiful Cooum;
by four o'clock another event at the harbour
in the name of our Bharati;
and between all this,
every telephone bell that rang demanded an answer.
Somehow I scribbled something
and came carrying a letter called a poem.

We are holding a *kaviyarangam* for Anna —
but do you know, friends?
Anna himself is a *kaviyarangam*!
On the arena of the world
he made an arena of flowers;
he is the divinity who sleeps in sacred Arangam.
I worship that divinity.
I sing facing that direction.

You may ask:
“What offering did you take for Anna's worship?”

For those who once wriggled on the earth like worms,
we replaced huts
and built houses with upper floors —
is that news not a flower
laid at Anna's feet?

To make it our goal this year
to restore sight to a hundred thousand people —
is that not a plantain fruit
placed in Anna's sanctum?

For leprosy-afflicted beggars,

<!-- scan 232 -->

we established clean homes —
is that not incense and lamp
fit for worshipping Anna?

To tell Anna that no small town,
large town, hamlet or village
shall henceforth remain without electricity —
is that not the light of camphor?

Anna dearly loved betel leaf and areca nut.
Tell him of the law granting house-sites
to agricultural labourers,
and his mouth will redden
as though we had offered him betel.

We devised a plan to make the Cooum fragrant, Anna —
that is the kumkum
with which we decorated your threshold.

Saying these things,
yesterday morning all of us walked together,
bowed at Anna's tomb
where the breeze sings its lullaby,
and returned with every eye turned into a pond,
while Tamil welled up like a river in flood
and hundreds of thousands sang his qualities.

Our Anna,
king of consoling speech,
great ruler of the South,
who unfolded ideas like a lotus in mud
and said every lamp we light
must cast its light upon the poor,
seemed to tell me:

“My younger brother,
enough of praising and glorifying me!
Some people wander about slandering our Kazhagam.
Is it not their crime
to pour oil and set a poisonous wick alight?
Today every rice-bag belly
swings a cardboard sword.
Until Death itself came asking for my life,
I allowed those who gather only to ruin us
no farther than the hall of my own house.
But you —

<!-- scan 233 -->

you led them all the way into the kitchen,
and today you are tasting the result!

What is done is done, younger brother.
All this hostility will fly away like cotton in the Aadi wind.
Work without forgetting the counsel I gave you:
turn opposition into dust with a dismissive smile.
Let them howl like owls with their booming noise;
let their arrogance and haste end in harm to themselves.
Show patience with the poise of Tamil culture.
Gather together the younger brothers
who keep me always in memory.
Call out our principles like the mango cuckoo;
the owls will disappear,
ashamed by the sound of their own voices.”

Thus our Anna gave us his blessing.

Born in Mamallapuram, a place radiant in the mind,
he became a Pallava.
Against conspiracies raised against him,
he became a Chola who could see the enemy's back.
In Kongu, a mountain of virtue,
he became Kumanan in generosity.
In the labour of nurturing beautiful Tamil,
he became a Pandya king.
In wearing the victory garland
and conquering the enemy's field,
he himself became the Chera.

Against those who cleverly claimed
that “namaskaram” was good Tamil,
he brought the Tamil word *vanakkam*
into everyday usage.
He changed an age
when people argued that the eye-bulging phrase
“vivaha subha muhurtham”
was the mark of the learned,
and blended lyre-like Tamil
into the resonant word *manavizha* — wedding celebration.
He planted such words
in the hearts of ordinary people
so Tamil once confined to poets might grow among all.

<!-- scan 234 -->

Was it not Anna
who turned *agrasanadhipathi* into *avai thalaivar* — chair of the assembly?
Otherwise one might have imagined
that the office of *agrasanadhipathi*
stood even above the President of the Republic!

When he mounted a stage,
it sounded like the music of a crown;
when he wrote upon a page,
it became scripture for Tamils.
To make the heads of his opponents
bend like stalks of ripened paddy,
our Anna had mastered the rule of words.
Even lion-like lawyers
could lose before him in argument;
Anna had no skill at all
in stubborn argument for its own sake.

“I bow my head before Kamban's Tamil,
but I do not accept the *Kamba Ramayanam*” —
with such courage and purity of heart
he brought Somesundara Bharathi into agreement.

Anna had not seen Nedunchezhiyan.
He had not seen Anbazhagan.
I can say it with certainty:
Anna had not “seen” them — at Thillai.
Yes:
he had seen them at Annamalai University,
in the city of Thillai,
where he delivered a speech of boundless fame.
Calling out “O fallen Tamil Nadu,”
he classified the greatness by which we once lived
and gave us a deep treasure-chest of ideas —
a chest of ideas,
not a broken-eared box
invented in the imagination of dark-hearted people
to overturn the Kazhagam.

<!-- scan 235 -->

It was a treasure-chest of thought,
a camphor chest given by the sage of Kanchi.

As Periyar's right hand;
as a motor-boat carrying the intellectually poor to shore;
as a warm sun that forgave the faults of the small;
as a master who could make fanatics submit;
he conducted politics with method and principle
and proposed a plan to end the begging bowl.
Some who once sat here with fortune
now go along the street abusing us.

If you quarrel with a boil,
it is your own face that is spoiled.
If you say “never mind” and apply *punugu*,
the boil may fall away.
To the boil that came to spoil the beauty of the face,
what harm is the fragrance of civet?
My friends may roar like tigers,
but if the foul boils can fall away
through the *punugu* called patience,
do not think of it as perfume —
apply it as medicine.

In the movement built through labour,
when there came a difference of opinion
with E. V. R. Periyar,
Anna separated in tears,
saying he would form another Kazhagam;
yet he thundered to the enemy
that we would remain like a double-barrelled gun.

But today some gather loose fragments,
run to slash the mother's belly with a drawn sword,
and fall into the ghostly lap of the enemy.
Those of such degraded character
will soon be crippled.

<!-- scan 236 -->

Whenever Anna waged a righteous struggle
against a ruling party,
he never had the habit
of sending cadres first to test the depth.
He himself entered the field first;
he himself went to prison first.

“The states must remain like deer,
and at the Centre there must roar a tiger
ready to pounce upon them” —
Anna did not accept such a policy for the Republic.
He demanded rights.
Some leap about without understanding
the harm that comes when the tiger's beauty
is defined as hunting the deer.
That was not Anna's policy.
He sounded the drum
for the need to decentralise power.
We shall march in formation along that road,
calling it the good road;
we shall cure the disease of domination.

About this man who was like a university,
poets now sing under many headings in this *kaviyarangam*.

❀

The poets then sang on the following themes:

**Mother Anna** — poet Erode Tamilanban  
**Leader Anna** — poet Ka. Vezhavendan  
**Guardian Anna** — poet Ma. Muthusamy

<!-- scan 238 -->

**Actor Anna** — poet Murugu Sundaram  
**Anna of the Film World** — Dr Avvai Natarajan  
**Chief Minister Anna** — poet Kothamangalam Subbu  
**Writer Anna** — Kaviyarasu Ponnivalavan  
**Orator Anna** — poet Abdul Rahman

### Kalaignar's concluding words

Mother! Beloved leader! Guardian of righteousness!
True actor! Peerless Chief Minister!
One who captured our hearts through writing and speech!

Did the child called Time
pluck you away and run,
saying, “You have ripened in wisdom”?
What can we do, Anna?

Thinking that you are still with us,
we shall defeat cruel deeds,
conspiracies and dances of deceitful hatred;
and to see the Divine in the smile of the poor,
we shall labour forever as we labour today, Anna.
There, in that smile,
we shall see you, Anna.

❀

## Translator notes
