- `அண்ணன் ஒரு கவியரங்கம்` is rendered **Anna, a Poetry Assembly** to retain the poem's governing conceit that Anna himself embodies a *kaviyarangam* rather than merely being its subject.
- Item 23 intentionally owns scans **230–236 and 238**. Physical scan **237 belongs to item 24** and is not inserted here; this non-contiguous provenance is source-authenticated and must remain visible.
- Tamil/Sanskritised public-language contrasts such as `வணக்கம்`, `மணவிழா`, and `அவைத்தலைவர்` are kept partly in transliteration where the political-linguistic contrast would otherwise disappear.
- The `கண்ட தில்லை / தில்லைநகர்` passage depends on Tamil sound and spacing; the English explains its turn conservatively without rewriting the Tamil witness.
- Movement history, welfare claims and state-autonomy/decentralisation rhetoric are translated as source claims, not externally harmonised.

## Item 24 — A Walking Journey for Tamil to Flourish

<!-- scan 237 -->

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the Pari festival on Parambu Hill
on 12 April 1973,
for the *kaviyarangam* titled
“A Walking Journey for Tamil to Flourish.”

Every mountain sings of abundance!
Every Tamil art sings of culture!
Every wave of the sea dances!
Every cluster of enemies trembles and runs!

In the forest where peaks rise,
where swift creatures run and play,
where proud herds of deer move,
where paired cuckoos sing their music,
where forest peacocks spread rhythm and expression,
where birds gather,
where silk banners and victory parasols turn and whirl —
let us worship the fame of generous Pari,
who poured out bounty like a flood,
and who received the circling radiance
of pearl-like Tamil.

<!-- scan 239 -->

You have given us the apt title “walking journey”
and made a way for the Tamil people,
precious as the eye,
to grow.
Live long! Live long!

Let us labour without weariness every day
so the people may live.
Anger is not evil
when harm comes to Tamils.
Are we not a pride of lions?
Shall we bow our heads to the howling of jackals?
Before tigers in full fury,
shall the base make a show of bravado?

Where has my Tamil gone?
Does he stand like a vertical pillar,
without courage to oppose the degradations here?
Does he lie there
with no pulse in his limbs?
Alas, poor fellow —
write a song about him for the Sangam!
Set up a golden statue for him!

The Pandya had a moustache
like two chisels laid above the lips,
bright enough to proclaim
that humiliation never came to him.
The Chola's moustache spread outward
toward the ears,
as though crying the same racial call.
The Chera's long moustache,
curving slightly,
grew toward the eyes
like the swinging trunk of an elephant.

<!-- scan 240 -->

Because those moustaches once trembled,
there are stories of enemy mountains
being ground to dust.
Today we need that courage —
we need it greatly.
Store valour in good action;
banish cruel action entirely;
recover the feeling of the Tamils of old.
Recover the feeling of honour.
Recover the feeling of self-respect.

Poets here,
you will sing of eight paths.
But tell us:
who showed the Tamil people blocked paths?
Those who make a habit of striking us
will hold court without hindrance
for as long as we remain bent.
So let us look here
at some of the paths we can reach.

The path of Kapilar —
not merely a poet's path,
but the finest path of friendship,
a path of compassion.

When Pari,
whose palanquin, sceptre, chariot and army
had been defeated on the battlefield,
fell dead,
Kapilar suffered endlessly
to arrange marriages
for Pari's two daughters,
two ripe mango-fruits.
In the end,
through Kapilar's effort,
the sons of King Kari
became husbands to Pari's daughters.

<!-- scan 241 -->

Having fulfilled his unique duty
to his friend who had ruled a land,
to Pari, lord of Tamil,
the great poet Kapilar,
master of texts,
thought:
“Now nothing prevents me
from joining my Pari.”
He sat facing north
and gave up his life.

Today there are dear friends
who, if a friend is dying,
hurry to place a stone upon his head!
For all of them to change their path,
is not Kapilar's path
a path worth learning?

The path of Avvai —
old Avvai, walking with a staff,
taught paths by which the sceptre of rule
might remain upright.

The path of Ilamperuvazhuthi —
a good path by which the Tamil people may prosper.

Walk the path of Valluvar,
and will enemies dare enter the heart and come near?

The path by which Ilango renounced household life
became the path of *Silappathikaram*,
through which he gave Tamils a noble ethic.

The path of Appar's life
is a path of love.
So too is the path of Vallalar Adigal.

The path that framed pearl-like principles —
that is the path of Arignar Anna!
The precious path of Anna!

Poets have come
to sing of these paths.
Let us give them the way;
let us receive their sweet words.

*(Poet Subramaniam sings on “Kapilar's Path.”)*

<!-- scan 242 -->

Next —
I heard that Adigalar wishes to perform virtue.
Forgetting the old saying
that “to receive is disgrace,”
Abdul Rahman has come
seeking Avvai's path.

I merely said the *Aathichudi*!
Dear Rahman,
“Let anger cool.”
Without abandoning courage,
give us your song.

*(Poet Abdul Rahman sings on “Avvai's Path.”)*

Next —
there is one called Ilancheran.
Like me, his head is bald.
Let it be!
Is not a tender coconut bald too?
Ilanchera, one of my own kind,
find us a pleasant path.

*(Poet Kovai Ilancheran sings on “Vazhuthi's Path.”)*

Next —
Muthu Sambandam,
who takes nourishing Tamil
and makes poems like clusters of fruit —

<!-- scan 243 -->

now let him sound forth,
his song,
Valluvar's song.

*(Poet Muthu Sambandam sings on “Valluvar's Path.”)*

Next —
a Pandyan comes to sell an anklet.
“What is this?
Are you changing the *Silappathikaram*?”
you ask?
No, no!
It is Shanmuga Pandian,
showing Ilango's path.
That is what I meant.

*(Poet Shanmuga Pandian sings on “Ilango's Path.”)*

Next —
“To walk the path, we need the feet of Appar;
to walk the path, we need Appar's feet” —
and Vadivelan comes in beauty:
Singara Vadivelan comes.

*(Poet Singara Vadivelan sings on “Appar's Path.”)*

<!-- scan 244 -->

Next —
Mariadasu.
If he says Vallalar's path
is the path proper to humankind,
is not Mariadasu right?

*(Poet Mariadasu sings on “Vallalar's Path.”)*

Next —
Mudiyarasu, who studies melody and sings,
now studies Anna's path and sings.
Poet-king dear as the eye,
may our government always endure.

*(Kaviyarasu Mudiyarasan sings on “Anna's Path.”)*

They have told us eight paths.
We have listened.
Now —
what is our path?

The Singampunari road,
the Tiruchirappalli road,
the Thanjavur road —
the road our Anna laid down for us.
That is our road.
We are coming along it.

Greetings.

❀

## Translator notes

- `தமிழ் வளர வழிநடைப் பயணம்` is rendered **A Walking Journey for Tamil to Flourish**, preserving both the literal walking-journey frame and the poem's repeated political/literary `வழி` — path/way — architecture.
- Item 24 intentionally owns scans **237 and 239–244**. Physical scan **238 belongs to item 23**; the English provenance therefore preserves the source's interposition and does not create a false continuous run.
- The poem's moustache imagery, courage rhetoric and references to Kapilar, Avvai, Ilamperuvazhuthi, Valluvar, Ilango, Appar, Vallalar and Anna are retained as Kalaignar's rhetorical sequence.
- Several guest introductions depend on Tamil sound-play — notably baldness/tender coconut, `சிலம்பு`/Silappathikaram, and `அப்பரடி`/Appar's feet. The English keeps the governing sense while documenting the loss rather than silently replacing the Tamil conceit.

## Item 25 — For the World to Flourish

<!-- scan 245 -->

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the New-Year *kaviyarangam* broadcast by Madras Radio
on 13 April 1973 under the theme
“Let Us Wish for the World to Flourish.”

You have come to wish
that the world may flourish
through love,
through righteous conduct,
through education,
through agriculture,
through industry,
and through the growth of art.
I too greet you.

Love — **-āl**.  
Righteous conduct — **-āl**.  
Education — **-āl**.  
Agriculture — **-āl**.  
Industry — **-āl**.  
Growth of art — **-āl**.

Since all six headings end in **-āl**,
I have become an *arasu* among the *āl* trees —
a royal tree standing among banyans!

At the birth of the year...

<!-- scan 246 -->

Year... birth...
If stroking a young woman's body
were to produce a birth at once,
the slender woman would soon become thin indeed!
Let the year be born, people of the world;
we do not need another kind of birth every year!

Having driven away Panguni,
Chithirai comes bouncing in to play.
We think her dance will last a month;
then from somewhere another arrives —
Vaikasi — and before we can exclaim,
“Ah, is it you?”
Aadi too has finished her dance.
Month after month the performance continues;
years fall like red-lotus petals,
then bloom again.
We call this the New Year
and string garlands of praise around it.

Ten years ago I was a prison bird,
with plenty of time there
to write many poems and books.
Today I lie like cattle in a shed,
pulled here and there,
my horns sharpened,
and made to stand on all these stages.
Is that good?
I ask the radio people themselves:
why do you refuse to open your mouths?
Even if you leave me untethered,
is your confidence simply that
this is a well-behaved bull that will not butt?

Never mind.
Believing that I will come without being struck
whenever I see Tamil fodder,
you ordered me to take the chair.
I am a bee.
When a flood of honey overflows,
when Tamil flowers spread fragrance,
when every flesh-bound heart melts,
when the sound of the world's Tamil scripture resounds,
when we ask, “Why this pit in life?”
and a path appears to make the poor smile —

<!-- scan 247 -->

when a leaping deer's grace
makes the tiger lose heart and fall,
when a head crawling with lice is no forest,
and the ghost of poverty rises to flee —
then Tamil Nadu itself has risen
to blow the conch.
Let us hear the songs
of the young poets gathered here.

### Through love...

To show how the world can flourish through love,
Valluvar said even one's very bones
belong to others.

Love for you arises from affection —
from your affection...
“Give yourself to me,”
one says to another,
while in a thicket where bees drink flower-milk,
through the heat of desire,
a male and a female speak.
Love lies beneath that too,
but higher still stands
that precious love called a mother's love.

A flower of the bed
once kept a man within her control,
turned him into an unripe fruit,
and then said,
“I want a gift.”
“What?” he asked.
Like a cake cooked without a pan,
she slid closer:
“Will you give it without delay?”
“O sugarcane tied in a bundle,
ask — before you close and open your eyes,
I shall give it,” he said, embracing her.

“Cut out your mother's heart,
bring it here,
and place it in my hand,” she said.
And he went.
His mother,
who had guarded her son like a jewel in a box —

<!-- scan 248 -->

he tore out her heart
and ran back carrying it.
His foot slipped;
he fell.
“Was the blow severe, my son? Alas!”
cried the severed mother's heart,
lying there in the open field —
so the story goes.
Is that not a mother's love?

Mother-love is a love apart,
a golden-fruit love.
There are good people in this world
who do not show even the love of a dog.
May they too live well!

For those with leprosy,
with pus running from their sores,
we build sacred homes.
For poor people whose eyes have lost their light,
we restore sight without charge.
These are temples without equal upon the earth.
God is not in gold or copper;
his refuge is in love.
Know this.

### Through righteous conduct...

Love lays the foundation stone
for building the mansion of righteousness.
Gandhi,
Nehru who followed his path,
and Anna too lived
to protect righteous conduct.
Let us remove the differences
that thought cannot even count,
and make equality dance
in all its colours.

<!-- scan 249 -->

Ownership of the land to the one who tills it;
ownership in industry to the one who labours —
we are putting this voice of rights into action
and laying down true righteousness.
If you see righteousness on the lips
but a little fox wandering in the heart,
it is better to step aside.

### Through education...

Four or five years ago,
if forty-three crores were spent on education,
this year we give one hundred and eight crores
so education may flourish in strength.

If at the beginning
we learn about the world from books,
we can then go on
to read the world itself as a book.
Students must think
of the hardships endured by parents and government,
and of the large sums spent on education;
it is service to avoid crossing limits in agitation,
and a duty to shine
as lamps of the future.

### Through agriculture...

Was it not the farmers
who changed the old condition
when people stood in rice queues,
close to tears?
What a pitiable state our granaries were in then!
Against that,
today rice production alone
stands at fifty-five lakh tons.
This is a government
that protects the farming community,
who rise before the sun and labour,
so that harm does not come to them.

<!-- scan 250 -->

### Through industry...

Across every part
of beautiful Tamil Nadu,
we must see people with work.
Power cuts caused by lack of rain
are not our fault.
The weavers' misery from want of yarn —
we shall quickly seek a way
and remove that hardship.
We shall shine as a government
that protects labour welfare.

### Through the growth of art...

“Tamils are a people of their own —
they possess a character of their own.”
Let us not forget what Namakkal Kavignar said.
Let us not abandon our art,
our sculpture,
our epics.

Only in a heart steeped in art
does the condition of the people appear;
only there does compassion arise.
When someone says “there is none,”
when a poor person's eyes reveal
the sorrow of disappointment,
it is the heart shaped by art
that understands.

For stable government,
there must be rule by art within the heart.
Even if you shout “Kalaignar's rule,”
this rule of art must remain
an administration that never dissolves;
that is why you see a reign of statues
along the seashore,
so it may stand in memory through time.

Anna sleeping in his tomb,
and thousands upon thousands
bowing with tear-filled eyes —
was that not a creation
seen by an artistic heart?

<!-- scan 251 -->

At the city where the Kaveri enters the sea,
we created an art hall
with statues telling Kannagi's story —
that too came from the heart of art.
Even when crushing work breaks my head,
it was artistic passion
that chased me here
to preside over this *kaviyarangam*.

The Pallava king who ruled Kanchi,
the Chola who ruled Thanjavur,
the Chera who ruled Vanji,
and the Pandya who ruled Koodal —
all lived with distinction
through magnificent arts,
and so remain in our thought.

Long live art!
Long live the Tamil mountain!
Long live poetry!
Long live this earth!

With that I call
the line of poets before us.

First —
Arasu Manimekalai.
If I called her simply Manimekalai,
you might think of the renunciant Manimekalai.
So: Arasu Manimekalai.
Her father Arasu
was a friend once very close to me.
Come, jewel,
to offer the gift of love
and turn the fallow land of the heart fertile.

*(Poet Arasu Manimekalai sings on “Through Love.”)*

<!-- scan 252 -->

Next —
friend Ponnadiyan comes to sing of virtue.
There is no time for me to sing of his skill.
Let him sing,
and let us rejoice.

*(Poet Ponnadiyan sings on “Through Righteous Conduct.”)*

Next —
“Let the world receive the joy I have received” —
to show the education he has himself received,
poet Hussain comes with fame reaching the sky.

*(Poet Hussain sings on “Through Education.”)*

Next —
only by labouring in the field
does the grain-head grow,
says Pazhaniyappa Kavirayar as he sings.

*(Poet Sami Pazhaniyappan sings on “Through Agriculture.”)*

Next —
our comrade who makes graceful poetry
will begin with “Through Industry.”
Kallai A. Durai, poet —
tell us a song, ayya!

*(Poet Kallai A. Durai sings on “Through Industry.”)*

The poet Durai who sang today
is to be married tomorrow morning.

<!-- scan 253 -->

I have no time to go and greet him then.
So let all of us together
greet him today!

Next —
to make poetry dance through the growth of art,
to sing Tamil like a swelling flood,
I call Kapilavanan.

*(Poet Kapilavanan sings on “Through the Growth of Art.”)*

### Conclusion

We shall do what we say.
We shall say what we do.
We shall block falsehood.
We shall grow truth.
The smile of the poor —
we shall call that joy.
We shall labour
so that everything is available to everyone.
For the world to flourish,
we shall drive poverty away.

Let this not be something
we sing once a year.
Let us take it up in life
as labour that does not bend.

Greetings.

## Translator notes

- `வையம் தழைக்க` is rendered **For the World to Flourish**; the event's longer theme `வையம் தழைக்க வாழ்த்துவோம்` is retained separately in the context note.
- The opening depends on the Tamil instrumental suffix `-ஆல்` shared by all six themes. The English keeps `-āl` visibly in the wordplay and separately explains the `ஆல்` banyan / `அரசு` royal-tree pun rather than inventing an English equivalent.
- The mother-heart story is preserved as the poem tells it; it is a rhetorical parable inside the source, not an independently authenticated event.
- Education, agriculture, industrial, welfare and artistic-policy figures are translated as source claims without external updating or correction.
- The six-theme structure and every guest-poet hand-off remain explicit so the English does not collapse the original *kaviyarangam* architecture.

## Item 26 — Father Periyar

<!-- scan 254 -->

Introductory presiding poem by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the “Father Periyar” *kaviyarangam*
held by the Tamil Sangam
at Salem Nehru Kalaiarangam
on 11 January 1974.

Honourable ministers,
district comrades — greetings.
To the poets of prosperous Tamil Nadu,
to the elders of Salem, rich in affection,
accept my heartfelt greetings.

Though he knew well
that I have no rest,
our friend sent an order
in the form of a request:
I must come and conduct this *kaviyarangam*.
He is a friend of the plough,
a true friend who labours.

There are those who say,
“We shall overturn us like a boat” —
and traitors who climbed aboard this very boat,
clung to office,
and now say, “We shall grind it to dust.”
There are sinners who declare,
“We shall become the sword
that strikes his back.”
While I am deep in thought,
working to break the conspiracy
of all who surround us in opposition,
he orders me to write poetry!

<!-- scan 255 -->

From yesterday morning until midnight,
more than eight important events in Coimbatore!
After all that,
will the spring-eye of imagination open?
Even so,
like a flag that moves with the wind,
I move at my friends’ command
to sing poetry.

I remember Ganapathi,
whose support and urging,
because he believed I possessed the power to write verse,
brought me to the chair of this poetry assembly.
I remember R. V. Ganapathi;
I continue this beautiful Tamil *kaviyarangam*.

First let us worship
the *sāmi* who let us live and grow upon this earth —
Rām-sāmi,
Periyar Ramasami!

Great people who have come
to sing and to witness this poetry assembly!
Was it not Periyar
who made Tamil consciousness —
which had lain stretched out
like the reclining lord of Srirangam —
rise with a thrill and thunder in all eight directions,
performing many deeds of courage?

Street stage,
art stage,
music stage,
cinema stage —
were they all once nothing but
“ammāmi, aththimbēr?”
and *jalatharangam*?
He changed that,
made them Tamil stages,
stages of our own.
Is it not fitting
that we hold a poetry assembly for him?

That is why the poet-king Bharathidasan sang of him:

> Like crops to the farmer,
> like milk to children,
> like curd to the hungry,
> like a mother to the poor,
> like a faultless penance to the worthy —
> to all people born in the Tamil land,
> he was like life itself.

<!-- scan 256 -->

“If you build a house
only to stuff straw inside it,
why build the house at all?
Why not simply make a haystack?”
Was the E. V. R. who said this merely an atheist?
No, no!

“He is nature’s son,
the bridegroom who wedded this soil,
a messenger of the future greatness of Tamil Nadu” —
so Va. Ra.,
that astonishing great man from the *agraharam*,
once drew him beautifully in an essay.

Like a singular light
born in molten gold,
he surged upward;
the red sky itself,
where the red sun rises,
seemed to flee in shame.
Periyar rose,
and crores of young bulls of modernity
stood firm,
saying blood itself was the stake.

If a limb is diseased,
we agree to cut it off
to save the life.
But when human society suffers
from the diseases of caste and religious division
that cripple life,
we hesitate to remove the disease.
“Is that right?” he asked.
The lion’s voice sounded;
the little foxes howled —
was that not the story that unfolded here?

He had a beard like Tagore of Bengal,
a gaze upright like a raging tiger,
a body with the presence of a red hill,
and in eyes white as conches,
two black-bee pupils —

<!-- scan 257 -->

light lived there until death.

A spinning top rests
once its turning is done.
But even after he had become very old,
he kept circling like a top.

Like a volcano,
a scorching flame,
a drama of nature,
a thunderclap that made opposition tremble,
a great torch of ethnic consciousness,
a cleaving sword that cut down humiliation,
an Ingersoll who denied even God,
a Socrates of Athens who never stopped speaking,
a Voltaire with a diamond heart
who kept asking “Why?” —
so lived our Father Periyar.
Today he has fallen
into the lap of death.

When Mahatma Gandhi said prohibition was needed,
Periyar cut down the coconut palms
standing thick in his own grove
so that liquor pots would not be filled.

Whom did we call the Hero of Vaikom?
As though saying,
“Place the knife at untouchability’s throat!”
he entered the field like a striped tiger,
won victory,
and received from the people of the world
the title **Vaikom Veerar**.

He was a helmsman of the Congress,
a commander for the victory of the khadi movement,
a man of action who stood on Gandhi’s path.
Then the time came to fight within the party,
and at the Kanchipuram conference
he walked out.

V. V. S. Aiyar was called
a distilled patriot.
But when caste feeling invited conflict
at the celebrated Gurukulam in Cheranmahadevi,
when one law was practised for one caste
and another for another,
could Periyar’s heart bear it?
He bent the bow for battle.

<!-- scan 258 -->

He began to make history.
As its result,
he became the father of the Self-Respect Movement,
the guardian leader of the Tamil people —
Periyar became
a man of a new path.

The Justice Party
had set out to perform great work
for the welfare of the backward classes.
But because it remained
at the feet of princes such as Pithapuram and Panagal,
and in the walking-stick grip
of inexhaustibly wealthy men,
he transformed it.
With the aid of the great Anna,
he succeeded in bringing
a party that had lived in mansions
to the people.

When deceitful schemes spread like nets,
trying to make Tamil people
wear Hindi like a monkey’s garland,
was it not Periyar
who cut through them
with an unafraid heart?

Is there a hamlet or village
his feet did not reach?
Even believers who would run a league
at the sound of his name,
once they heard him speak,
would reel as though intoxicated
by a Tamil song.

A rain of reason would fall;
beauty would flow;
darkness would pass.
Clear speeches would shine,
their power flashing —
like the roar of a sea breaking its sluice,
like clouds flowing over a mountain peak.

A rain of reason would fall!
Arrogance would perish!
The system of slavery would end!

<!-- scan 259 -->

When Ayya spoke,
to the Kazhagam people
it was like eating *athirasam*;
to the wicked,
like an atomic bomb exploding!

I joined him,
accepting his leadership,
back in 1938,
when I was still a student in Arur.
Later, when I was attacked in Puducherry,
Ayya himself applied medicine
to my wounds.

During the days
when I studied lessons of principle
in the Gurukulam of the *Kudiyarasu* office,
I never imagined
that I would one day become
the head of a Tamil government,
or that I would give,
with state honours,
my final salute
to the father who raised me.

I still remember the song
I wrote in that Erode Gurukulam:

> The ancient world,
> praised and worshipped for so long —
> look, it shakes beneath the Erode earthquake!
> The word E. V. R.
> is a cyclone rocking
> the very pedestal of Aryanism in this land.
> Let his white beard merely move;
> let the sign in his eyes be seen —
> there are Black Shirts ready,
> swords that return
> only after cutting down cruelty.

I remember it even today.
Periyar did not die young;
he lived to a full old age
and then took rest.
Yet if the Taj Mahal suddenly sank into the earth,
would we console ourselves by saying,
“After all, many years have passed
since it was built”?

<!-- scan 260 -->

If the great tower of Thanjavur tilted and fell,
would it be easy to bear
merely by saying it was very old?

He stood as high as the Thanjavur tower;
he made history like the Taj Mahal.
He will live, he will live —
that is my blessing.

To sing of the Periyar who lives on,
I now call,
one after another,
the poets of rich Tamil.

## Translator notes

- `தந்தை பெரியார்` is rendered **Father Periyar**. `தந்தை` is an honorific and political/familial epithet in the source, not a biological description.
- The scan-255 sequence `சாமி → ராம் சாமி → ராமசாமி` is name/ritual wordplay. The English keeps `sāmi` and the staged Rām-sāmi → Ramasami turn visible rather than inventing an English homophone.
- `அம்மாமி அத்திம்பேர்` is retained as a source-specific Brahmin-household/stage register; the point is the displacement of a culturally exclusive stage idiom by a Tamil public stage.
- The Vaikom passage contains a sound-play between `வைக்கம்` and `வை கத்தி` (“place the knife”). The English translates the rhetorical force and records the wordplay here.
- References to Congress, khadi, the Cheranmahadevi Gurukulam, the Justice Party, anti-Hindi agitation, caste, religion and Aryanism are translated as Kalaignar’s source-framed political history; Phase 4 does not fact-check or harmonise those claims.
- Karunanidhi’s 1938 association, Puducherry injury, `Kudiyarasu`-office apprenticeship and later state-honour reflection are preserved as first-person source claims.

## Item 27 — Akam Creations

<!-- scan 261 -->

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the *kaviyarangam* on the theme
“Akam Creations,”
held on 13 July 1974
at the Muthamizh festival organised
by students of the medical colleges of Madras city.

Greetings.
To those who have come
to make waxing-moon Tamil grow,
to the unwavering tigers of self-respect,
and to Kaviyarasu Kannadasan — greetings.

Greetings too
to those who diagnose the country —
who know its pulse,
and with medicine remove the people's ills
and protect them.

Amid the thousand problems
that dwell within,
they ask me to preside
over a poetry assembly called “Akam Creations.”
These medical students,
trained to explain that even dirt
lodged beneath a fingernail
can injure the health of the body,
are troubling me —
and I have agreed.

In a time when students
holding a festival for Tamil
has itself become a great wonder —

<!-- scan 262 -->

if those learning the art of medicine
prepare a feast for Tamil,
we must bow our heads,
we must join our hands in respect.

Watering the useless weed called strike,
ruining oneself instead of drinking
education that is like milk;
young women with eyes like silk,
while pulling at a sari itself becomes a trade of decay;
going in one bus
and setting another bus on fire —
leaving all this aside,
you have gathered
to make noble Tamil grow.
Long live! Long live!

I too was once a student.
I always walked in such a way
that no condition arose
for my parents to be ashamed.

When I studied,
a love throbbed within me —
a love for Tamil!
Tamil work tasted sweet to me
like bitten sugarcane.

That rare Tamil Nadu Tamil Students' Forum
which I founded in Arur in those days
received a waterfall-like Tamil blessing
from Bharathidasan,
whose stride was that of a lion.
For that festival to flourish,
he sent, with affection,
this greeting:

> In cool-groved Tiruvarur,
> behold the Tamil Nadu Tamil Students' Forum!

<!-- scan 263 -->

> Let it live with generosity and truth!
> Let it become medicine for the people's awakening!
> Those of Tiruvarur are not men of festering wounds —
> behold their tiger-youth,
> shoulders swelling with strength!
> Behold, the pride of Tamil lions has risen,
> searching for the hostile crowd to tear apart!
> In great and fertile Tamil Nadu,
> if non-Tamils stretch out their tails,
> behold — a kick is what they shall receive!

That battle-call of the revolutionary poet —
a poetic thunder sounded by books of *puram* and *akam*,
the great thunder of modern Tamil,
the singular thunder of surging youth!

I think of those days;
I feed on their joy through memory.
Will such a day not come again?
Will our dear students not open their eyes and see?
Will they not join one formation
to guard golden Tamil?
For my heart, aching as it counts these thoughts,
I would call this festival
an apt medicine.

The ancient Tamils divided literature
into creations of *akam*
and creations of *puram*.
*Igambaram* is neither Tamil
nor something belonging to Tamils!
*Akam* and *puram* —
those are Tamil,
and belong to Tamils.
What literature can stand as equal
to the skill of the Tamil
who divided experience into *tiṇai* and wrote it so?

<!-- scan 264 -->

> Beginning with *kaikkilai*
> and ending with *peruntiṇai*,
> the seven modes first declared
> are called the seven *tiṇais*.

Thus speaks the Tolkappiyam,
of undying fame.

Once I debated with Anbazhagan:
“Which is greater — *akam* or *puram*?”
Arignar Anna interrupted
and gave an explanation:

> What can be shared is *puram*, thambi.
> What cannot be shared is *akam*, thambi!

That explanation of Anna
is itself a lamp for the realm of *akam*.

Because the Pandavas and Panchali
did not understand
the dignity of cultured *akam*,
they shared the sphere of *akam*
among five men!
That is a fault of their tradition.
To praise it happily as culture —
that is the fault of those without reason.

Though *akam* and *puram* are only two,
see how they remain
never even a millet-grain apart.
A warrior returns with a spear wound;
his beloved gives medicine
with eyes that themselves shoot spears.

Even if they lie together
like fish within a pond,
if the battlefield calls,
they climb the bank and rise.
Even after placing eye within eye
and tasting the art of union,
if evil threatens the soil,
the warrior clan rises.

To portray the honour of that clan
and pour a beautiful rain of poetry,
the owners of the word-clan have gathered here.
For those who slander the good clan,

<!-- scan 265 -->

for tongues made scarred by abuse,
let the challenge of the mother-clan,
the battle-*parani* of the Tamil clan,
be heard!
Let it tear open
the deaf drum of ears that refuse to hear!

In *akam*,
the hero,
the heroine,
the nurse,
the male friend,
the mother,
the town,
the moon,
the breeze,
the female friend —
all stand as companions,
and Tamil spreads its peacock-feathers and dances.
There are poets here
to give verses that defeat enmity
and win the garland of victory.

In these days
when some headless people
call themselves “leader,”
let us hear a song
about the enduring leader of *akam*.

Is the heroine one
who lies there merely thinking of “fate”?
No! No!
To change her fate,
she makes her friend a messenger,
makes even the nurse an accomplice,
and waits to welcome the lover
carried in by the breeze,
under the moon that stands and burns her.

The male friend is never a traitor
in an *akam* poem!
There are traitors in *puram* poems —
and their treachery becomes visible
right in the opening *puram* itself!

<!-- scan 266 -->

Love does not hide and vanish
because townspeople may blame it.
But today,
in front of the entire town,
on the beach,
inside a car,
in a public hall —
what justice is there
in this mischief of love?
Do they think the townspeople
all possess disabled eyes?
Or do lovers imagine
that because they themselves close their eyes,
the whole world too has closed its eyes?

Making room for love,
making room for duty,
upholding dignity,
and living with discipline —
that is an *akam* creation.
Living with family planning —
that is today's *akam* creation!

If, thinking “creation” in *akam*,
one goes on creating
six, seven, eight —
then what grows from that creation
is only panic!

I said it knowingly.
I said it so it may be understood.
I said it from experience.
Understand it well!

❀

## Translator notes

- `அகம்` (*akam*) is retained in the English title and body because it names the classical Tamil interior/love domain opposed to `புறம்` (*puram*), not merely the everyday sense “inside.”
- `அகத்துறைப் படைப்புகள்` is therefore rendered **Akam Creations**. The final passage deliberately exploits `படைப்பு` (“creation/producing”) for both literary creation and producing children; the neighbouring `பதைப்பு` (“panic/agitation”) is a sound-play that cannot be reproduced cleanly in English.
- The Tolkappiyam quotation and the seven-*tiṇai* terminology are kept source-facing rather than replaced by modern literary categories.
- Anna's `பகிர்ந்து கொள்ளக்கூடியது புறம் / பகிர்ந்து கொள்ள முடியாதது அகம்` explanation is translated as the poem presents it; the Panchali/Pandava criticism is likewise source rhetoric, not an external interpretation supplied by the translator.
- The early student-movement and Bharathidasan material is retained as Karunanidhi's first-person institutional memory; Phase 4 does not fact-check those source claims.
- The poem's closing family-planning satire, including its sexual/public-behaviour humour, is preserved rather than softened into generic advice.

## Item 28 — Pongal Festival

<!-- scan 267 -->

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the Pongal-festival *kaviyarangam*
held by the Madras District DMK
at Kalaivanar Arangam
on 14 January 1975.

Greetings,
people of deep affection,
elders,
and good young treasures
who hold the mother who bore you
and the golden land of your birth
as one!

There was no room
for all of us to be born from one womb;
that is why,
born from the wombs of a crore mothers,
we became sibling Tamils!

To all of you
I offer Pongal New-Day greetings —
accept them!

Poets who have come
to take colourful Tamil
and paint pictures of thought in verse — greetings!

Under the heading Pongal,
you have come overflowing to sing;
I know it well.

<!-- scan 268 -->

Did I say
you came after letting food boil over?
I say you came
with feeling boiling over.
Is that not the truth?

Let us praise the sun!
Let us praise the sun!
The sound of the anklet rings;
it sweetens the mind.
A thousand moons,
a thousand women,
a thousand suns,
a thousand men —
they praised the sun,
they praised the sun.
Tamils became thousands of crores;
they sang many a prefatory song.
Praising the sun,
they gathered at Pongal;
praising the sun,
they danced at Pongal.

Mango-leaf festoons,
turmeric and plantain,
temple drums and festive designs —
they beheld them all.
“Pongalo Pongal!
Pongalo Pongal!”

Though they lived divided
among Kurinji, Mullai, Neithal,
Marutham and Palai lands,
Tamils lived understanding
a common culture.

Though called the ancient people
who arose before stone and soil,
they learned to clear forest into country,
to dig ponds and increase prosperity.

A bow appears on their shoulders —
it displays valour.
Rice appears in their fields —
it displays agricultural skill.

<!-- scan 269 -->

New words appear again and again —
they display the wealth of the language.
Not only letter and word:
Tolkappiyan, like ripened grain,
gathered grammar too
for the prosperous life of Tamils.
With guava-fruit Tamil,
Valluvar wove the Kural for the world.

Let us rejoice
as though rain had fallen after drought,
hearing the poetry of truthful speech!

The curved anklet,
Manimekalai of distinction,
Cintamani,
and Kundalakesi
became ornaments of Mother Tamil
through the work of poets of that age.

How many books!
How many poets!
A treasury for people
who learned and understood,
who felt and knew —
so Tamils lived in those days.

One day,
thinking of the great books of ancient Tamil,
I lay down upon my bed.
A woman lay close beside me,
without the least shyness,
and embraced me.
Though I made no attempt
to gather her into my arms,
I had no mind to push her away either.
Let her embrace me, I thought.

Then another woman arrived —
a rainbow dancer,
a sorceress who gathered music
like a skylark.
“What is this?
Two women — one bed?”

<!-- scan 270 -->

You ask?
Be patient.
The first woman's name was Sleep.
The other was the Dream Woman.

Every scene I saw in that dream
was a Tamil scene,
the splendour of Tamils.

In a hall,
a musician pours a rain of song;
the people sway
like bees drunk on nectar.
As the auspicious concert ends,
some among the audience eagerly ask:
“Sing ten more songs — *Pattuppāṭṭu*!”

The exhausted musician clears his throat:
“I shall sing.
What sum will you give me in addition?”
A poet springs up and answers:
“We shall give you *Eṭṭuttokai* — the Eight Collections!”

The musician understands the play and protests:
“What justice is this?
For my ten songs,
only eight collections?
That is a *Kuruntokai* — a short sum — for me!”

“Oh! Do you want *Ainkurunuru* then?”
the poet snaps.
“I do not want one hundred;
I will not touch it, will not touch it.
Will you give four hundred, great poet?
And let it be four hundred in *akam*,
four hundred in *puram*,”
twists the master of music.

Four hundred in *puram*,
four hundred in *akam* —

<!-- scan 271 -->

“Enough talk of money for song!
We shall give fresh milk for your throat;
if necessary we shall give the *Muppāl* —
the Three Divisions — now sing!” they say.

“O poet who promised the *Muppāl*!
From now on my throat will speak
only for the *Porutpāl* — the division of wealth!”
With that the musician rises and leaves.

My long sleep broke;
I awoke because a *Nedunalvāḍai* —
a long cold wind — had struck me!

Every memory of the dream
was sweet as sugarcane within my heart.
Thinking of Tamil,
soaking my heart in Tamil —

our Tamils once exercised power
across the roaring sea.
Yet today Tamils themselves
have consented to erase
the nectar of ethnic culture,
the dignity of art,
and literature —
like holding dirty spit in the mouth,
brushing filthy teeth with it,
then rinsing and swallowing it
back into the body.

The dirt-filled gut must be cleansed.
For that we need a good washerman called medicine.
Bathing cleanses the outside of the body.
Inner cleanliness comes through medicine;
purity of the mind comes through truth.

Making a path for that truth,
placing explosives beneath the Vedic fortress —

<!-- scan 272 -->

for sixty years,
without fear,
our Anna's leader Periyar fought.

It is on the stove he lit
that today
Pongal boils over,
pretenders fade,
falsehood burns.

“A woman with ten heads,
twenty hands,
a mouth roaring like the sea,
hundreds of eyes,
the face of a cow,
the body of a human,
named Makara Sankranti,
with a mountain of a nose” —
that fabricated tale,
written to deceive,
vanished like a snake before thunder,
destroyed by the roar from Erode.

Following that,
our Anna saw Pongal
as a festival of ethnic consciousness,
as the great festival of the ploughing farmer!

❀

## Translator notes

- `பொங்கல் விழா` is rendered **Pongal Festival**. The poem repeatedly exploits `பொங்கல் / பொங்கு` — Pongal, boiling over, and overflowing feeling — so the English keeps “boil/overflow” visible wherever possible.
- The dream on scans 270–271 is constructed almost entirely from classical Tamil literary titles and number/price meanings: *Pattuppāṭṭu*, *Eṭṭuttokai*, *Kuruntokai*, *Ainkurunuru*, *Akanānūru*, *Puranānūru*, *Muppāl*, *Porutpāl* and *Nedunalvāḍai*. The Tamil names are retained because replacing them with plain English titles would erase the joke; short glosses expose the relevant wordplay.
- `ஞாயிறு போற்றுதும்` and the anklet imagery deliberately activate *Silappathikaram* associations. The English does not silently expand the poem into an external literary commentary.
- The spit/gut/medicine passage and the grotesque Makara Sankranti figure are preserved as the poem's rationalist polemic, not neutralised or presented as independently verified cultural history.
- The Periyar and Anna framing, including the claim of sixty years of struggle and the redefinition of Pongal as an ethnic/farmers' festival, is translated as source rhetoric.

## Item 29 — A Silappathikaram Feast

<!-- scan 273 -->

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the evening *kaviyarangam*
entitled “A Silappathikaram Feast,”
held at the Kotrappandal in Poompuhar
on 25 April 1975.

Greetings.
To our Navalar,
a form of many-coloured Tamil;
to our dear Professor,
a treasury of thought;
to ministers Mannai and Sadiq,
as precious as the eye;
and to all who came by Anna's path — greetings.

To Sivagnana Selvar,
with the sound of the anklet ringing;
to good Ganesan,
rich in ancient Tamil wisdom —
my head-bowed greetings.

To every loving heart
that answered the call
and has come with eagerness
to restore ruined Poompuhar
to beauty —
my humble greetings.

To the poetic hearts
who have brought noble Tamil
to serve us a feast
from *Silappathikaram* —

<!-- scan 274 -->

my affectionate greetings.

O rare Tamil! O nectar!
Fragrance of a sweet flower! Honey!
You came as mother and ruled me;
you came as Anna and made me a man of action.
Bowing at the flower-feet of Kanchi Anna,
I begin this poetry assembly.
Bless us, mother —
may Tamils live,
may Tamil land live!

From the summit of Kudagu hill,
the lovely maiden Kaveri came dancing.
The Karnataka man who ran to reach
the dancing beauty said, “*Aṇai*!” — a dam, an embrace.
She crossed the embrace of his hands,
entered the stone embrace of the Kallanai,
and, guarding her chastity,
leapt toward Thanjavur.
Hearing the grievance of the flower-maiden Kaveri,
the Chola king made the waves of Poompuhar's sea
her husband and protected her.

If a woman's greatness lies
in not forgetting the home of her birth
while making the home she enters prosper,
would that not suit
our eye-jewel Kaveri as well?

Thus the city into which Kaveri entered,
the city where Kaveri enters,
became Kaveripoompattinam, I would say.

Karikala Peruvalathan, Tirumavalavan —

<!-- scan 275 -->

for tender friendship
his scabbard held the sword;
for wicked enmity
the sword rose from its sheath.
With Uraiyur as his capital,
he later married another town as capital too.
For this man with two capital-wives,
the younger one —
sweetest of the two —
is Poompuhar,
welcoming us here today.

“High-maned horses that came by sea,
black pepper sacks that came by land” —
so *Pattinappalai* thunders
of the port-city that shone in those days.

There was Maruvurpakkam,
with warriors who rose in battle;
a quarter where city men,
intoxicated by desire for women,
walked about;
a day-market to which people
from other lands came daily;
a royal street,
a street of flag-bearing chariots,
a merchants' street,
a street of truth-filled farmers;
streets crowded with physicians,
astrologers, bards and performers,
with elephants and horses everywhere.
There was the splendid Pattinappakkam,
spoken of as a painted quarter.

There were halls proclaiming
the Chola's northern victories:
the debate hall,
the pearl pavilion,
the festooned gateway,
the tall-stone hall
affirming Tamil victory in the ancient world,
the Ilanji hall,
the Pavai hall drawing lovers of art;
a Murugan shrine,
near it a Tirumal shrine,
an Arugan shrine,
near it a Buddha shrine;
a precinct where Muthamizh resounded,
and every day,
a Chithirai-festival crowd.

<!-- scan 276 -->

Ilango's Gunavayir Kottam
portrays all these.
All of them, mother,
were destroyed by sea and time.
Yet the wealth of the anklet
given by the Chera —
an imperishable treasury,
an undying sacred lamp — remains.

Awakened by that lamp's light,
with an unforgettable love of language,
there arose a new Puhar —
not one of the empty “complaints”
that useless men raise every day!
A new Puhar, Poompuhar,
created surging from Tamil breath
fragrant with hot blood and valour.

To Kannagi, lamp of the home;
to queenly Madhavi;
to Karikala Chola,
with a diamond-adorned chest;
to Ilango's cultured anklet,
royalty of poetry —
to give all these a beautiful new form,
what thanks can I offer
Tamil Nadu that gave birth to me,
and my own mother and father?

To make many special chapters our right,
we demanded autonomy;
we said we would gladly accept even death for it,
and would endure even the loss of office —
was it not this *Silappathikaram*
that taught us to feel so?

Deceivers came,
prospered through Tamil,
rose in life,
and erased our history.
Today the anklet that caresses us
is at least a tower-lamp
showing us some part of our history.

<!-- scan 277 -->

With the name *Silappathikaram* —
“Let us establish a poem-epic
belonging to all three crowned kings;
Adigal, you must grant it” —
there arose this epic,
an epic of awakening.

Eight incomparable poets are here
to offer a feast from that literature.

“Laughter, weeping, degradation, wonder,
fear, pride, anger and joy —
beyond these are the eight *meyppādu*,”
said the elder grammarian.
Following that path,
they will create eight flavours here:
laughter,
sorrow,
degradation,
wonder,
fear,
valour,
anger,
and joy —
a contest sung without hostile intent.

Through tears one may sometimes gain laughter.
If one marries a husband
who obeys only through fear...!
When people do not grow angry
even after humiliation,
warriors may feel wonder.
Joy and sorrow are children of one mother,
following one another like shadows.

These eight flavours —
Tamil like sweet *pittu*,
curd tied thick,
a bowl filled high,
poured out for you;
a silk flag,
a victory parasol,
a string of pearls —
come to the Chola city!

<!-- scan 278 -->

Come and explain each flavour!
We called — and eight have come.
Would the one who composed *Silappathikaram*
by taking up “eight”
leave the eight flavours out?

> Number and letter,
> five branches of *iyal*,
> four modes of *paṇ*,
> eleven forms of dance born from music —
> on this earth,
> with golden-ankleted Madhavi of Poompuhar,
> Kovalan joined in the evenings,
> weary and entranced,
> caught in desire hard to release.

And the joy he shared
with Kannagi, impossible to destroy,
had no count!

Kannagi and Kovalan too —

> Joined beneath the flower-garlanded canopy,
> they clasped hands as husband and wife;
> all pleasures whose name does not fade
> they drew close to themselves...
> and stood as though they had seen
> the impermanence of life upon the earth.

Not only in joy —
in sorrow too,
in clinging to a person's feet in worship,
the characters Madhavi and Kannagi
compete with one another.

> Kanakan and Vijayan,
> with tongues they could not guard,
> joined the guest-kings
> and spoke without knowing
the power of rare Tamil.
> When the Chera heard those words,
> he said, “Beat the war-drum!”
> and the stone that would be borne
> upon the crowned heads of the cruel —

that scene explains the flavour of valour,
the splendour that raised the Tamil victory flag.

Sweet honey-syrup,
clear nectar that never cloys — Kovalan —

<!-- scan 279 -->

when the Pandya famed in every direction
had him killed as a thief,
Kannagi surged like a wheel of fire.
To make the king who gave that judgement
bow his head,
she took the anklet and broke it.
The Madurai king,
as though his own bone had been broken,
was struck with wonder
and fell dead at once.

“I am a Pandya of a rule
that never strays from justice —
am I that Nedunchezhiyan king
who has now failed in justice?”
How much wonder lies in that question!
How much trembling!

There are beings in this world
who have no kinship at all
with any of the eight flavours —
wonder, laughter, sorrow, anger,
valour, degradation, fear and joy.

When Kalaivanar gave
explosive laughter like thunder
as the flavour of comedy
to the educated and the unlettered alike,
some would sit like pounded tamarind,
unwilling even to bare their teeth!

When great leaders,
who accepted repeated prison terms
and the wilderness of repression
as sacrifice for a beloved principle,
pass away,
thousands, tens of thousands,
hundreds of thousands gather
with grief-filled hearts
at the cremation ground,
longing to pay their final respects.
And if at that moment
a clowning performer walks in —

<!-- scan 280 -->

a foolish crowd may shout!
Clapping,
cheering,
celebratory dancing —
our ears and eyes close by themselves.
Those who do not know
the right place and meaning
for sorrow and joy —
I say they are lower than a donkey!

Tolkappiyar, master of the old treatise,
separated out one hundred and two
manifestations of emotion
and wove his thought.
Of them,
there are poets here
to show the chief eight flavours
and many branch-flavours
through poetic grace.
There is also a woman here
to show them through *abhinaya*.

But if one watches the dancing of some women,
one may wonder whether one committed
a great sin in a former life!

For desire burning like fire,
for anger pouring like honey,
for the emotional “bhava”
that hisses like an enraged cobra,
and for coyness that gathers
pomegranate-red cheeks —
there is not even a small difference,
rise or fall, in the face.
The cosmetic-coated mask
does not move at all.
Have you ever seen,
through *abhinaya*,
even an atom of change
on the golden girl's statue-face,
apart from the mischief of her limbs?

“Thimi-thimi,” they dance,
shaking the hall;
“mithi-mithi,”
they stamp as though trampling mud.
If they keep dancing like that,
the dance-stage will become a broken wall;
the art itself will become murder, I say.

Tolkappiyar placed “joy” at the end
and completed the garland
of eight kinds of expression.
Today, however,
let us begin the poetry assembly with joy
and hear firm valour at the end.

Is valour merchandise for the last stall?

<!-- scan 281 -->

Do not ask so, my dear.
For some party leaders,
valour has indeed become
a commodity whose price can be asked!

When there is war,
ours is the crowd that says,
“I first! You first!” —
not the crowd of people
who make quarrelling their profession.
We come in the line of the fighters of Vaikom!
Can a haystack play with this fire?

Why remember others
in this good assembly?
Let our poetry gathering begin in joy
and end in world-famed valour.
Now do you understand well
why I said we may hear valour “at the end”?

O Tamil maiden,
showing the beauty of the waist in *Manimekalai*
and the beauty of gait in the *Silambu* —
see, the line of poets has set out
to arrange a line of songs before you.
Let their skill begin!
I call the poets.

### The poets are introduced

First,
poet Vairamuthu,
who will sing of **joy**
from the pearls inside the anklet.

*(Poet Vairamuthu sings on “Joy.”)*

Next —
see how sorrow follows joy!
Perunthevan in appearance,
Ilanthēvan in age —

<!-- scan 282 -->

he will scoop and give us
in rich Tamil
the sorrow called “sorrow”
from within the anklet.

*(Poet Ilanthevan sings on “Sorrow.”)*

Next —
there are people who laugh at sorrow.
That stone-hearted poet Kannappan
comes with laughter.
He is not coming like Kovalan,
nor coming to Madurai
with the ornament called an anklet.
He comes to this Poompuhar poetry assembly
to display the **laughter**
that flourishes within the *Silambu*.

*(Poet Kannappan sings on “Laughter.”)*

Next —
there are those who leap in anger
at people who laugh uselessly.
Natarajan of Chidambaram
raises his leg gracefully in dance;
here T. G. Natarajan comes straight,
lifting poetry in the Tamil field.
Without getting angry,
sing to us of **anger**, ayya!

*(Poet T. G. Natarajan sings on “Anger.”)*

Next —
when we see one who is angry,
fear may arise.
To remove it by bringing one who is fit,
we have called poet Tamilanban.

<!-- scan 283 -->

O rare Tamil that speaks in the assembly without fear,
pour poetry in language dripping with flavour!

*(Poet Tamilanban sings on “Fear.”)*

Next —
though the wise know that one must not sleep in fear,
to drive forward a fierce struggle
and win for principle,
fear itself is evil; it is not manliness.
To warn the Tamil people
that if fear arrives, **degradation** follows,
Abdul Rahman comes.
The flavour he sings is degradation —
but what he gives is a downpour of Tamil!

*(Poet Abdul Rahman sings on “Degradation.”)*

Next —
Tamil honour says
that if degradation comes, death is better.
Kuruvikkarambaiyar wonders
how so many remain alive
even after degradation has come.
The little village that gave us Kuthoosi Gurusamy
has also given us precious Shanmugam,
and plunges Tamil Nadu into **wonder**.

*(Poet Kuruvikkarambai Shanmugam sings on “Wonder.”)*

<!-- scan 284 -->

Next —
there is a poet to sing of **valour**
who shows valour in action.
My heart always holds moisture enough
to grow this crop of the Ponni.
In the month when Valavan lived,
let us hear the poem sung by Ponnivalavan.

*(Kaviyarasu Ponnivalavan sings on “Valour.”)*

### Kalaignar's conclusion

From morning until this poetry assembly,
I have listened to Tamil sounds
sweet as sugarcane.
I have enjoyed them;
I remain unable not to enjoy them.
For a while,
with a happy heart,
I have passed time in peace.
After drinking, step by step,
the Tamil honey nurtured
by crowned kings and ancient poets,
how can I now manage
to give a conclusion?

People who came to Poompuhar!
People who gained new feeling!
Before I wish you farewell —
see the full Chithirai moon tonight.
Give kisses on the seashore
to children who are stamped-gold treasures.
Do not ask,
“Only to the children?”
That is a matter
between maiden and bull!

Look at the Poompuhar moon.
Think how it resembles
the victory parasol
of the Chola king who ruled the Tamil world.

<!-- scan 285 -->

To reach our ideal,
set the literature of the anklet
firmly within your minds.
And while you stand
beside the sea of wandering waves,
offer your greetings too
to the government
that walks on Anna's path.

Greetings!

## Translator notes

- `சிலப்பதிகார விருந்து` is rendered **A Silappathikaram Feast**. *Silappathikaram* and related Tamil literary titles are retained rather than replaced by English-only labels because they are active components of the poem's literary rhetoric.
- Scan 274 exploits `அணை` as both a dam and an embrace in the personified Kaveri/Karnataka passage. The English exposes both senses instead of fabricating a new pun.
- The Puhar city description draws on source-visible classical references, including *Pattinappalai*. The translation preserves Kalaignar's reconstruction and etymological/political claims as source claims rather than harmonising them with outside scholarship.
- `மெய்ப்பாடு` is retained in explanation as *meyppādu*, the Tolkappiyam category of embodied/emotive expression. The eight source labels are kept distinct across the poem and guest-poet sequence: `உவகை`, `அழுகை`, `நகை`, `வெகுளி`, `அச்சம்`, `இழிவு`, `வியப்பு`, `வீரம்`.
- The embedded *Silappathikaram* passages are translated only to the degree required by this poem's source text; Phase 4 does not silently substitute wording from a separate edition of the epic.
- The modern political comments about autonomy, repression, parties, Vaikom and the Anna-path government are translated as Kalaignar's source rhetoric.
- All eight guest-poet hand-offs remain explicit because their order is the organising structure of the latter half of the *kaviyarangam*.

## Item 30 — On Anna's Path

<!-- scan 286 -->

Presiding poetic address by
Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
at the Anna *kaviyarangam*
held at Kalaivanar Arangam, Madras,
on the evening of 15 September 1975.

O Tamil! Honey! Golden fruit!
Nectar! Love! Beautiful art!
Grace! Knowledge! Lamp of thought!
Grove! Fame! Jewel of gold!
Sacred treasure! Form! Coolness of the moon!
Tree! Shade! Comfort that gives shade!
Mother — holding fast to your feet,
I have come to sing the presiding poem.

To labour without weariness on Anna's path,
to let music resound and continue the journey,
raise aloud the vow
to guard the Kazhagam like the eye,
until it strikes the sky!

It feels as though it happened today —
that sight of the cow separated
from the calf of the Kazhagam.
As though a spear able to pierce a mountain
had been aimed and driven through the heart —

<!-- scan 287 -->

Anna, who would never leave us,
left us that day. Alas!
Seven or eight years have passed!

Avvai has a song called *Kondraivendan*;
our king of principle
has a song that can never perish.

The stride of the swelling sea,
the soft walk of women —
he showed both in his new Tamil style.
He trapped those who spoke evil
inside their own words.
At the very moment he took a pinch of snuff,
his smile would reduce opponents to powder.
He drove away those of rotten character.
He placed a golden crown upon Mother Tamil.

Anna held a pen.
On paper it became a plough,
to change the written fate
of deceived Tamils.
In our time,
to make those who had wept smile,
it became the sceptre of the government he gave.

On the racing-horse platform of public speech,
he offered guava-sweet Tamil.
When others scorched the Tamil language,
he became the root-force of the language struggle,
entered the field,
awakened those who watched,
and possessed the grace
to praise even those who insulted him.
He destroyed the frenzy
of linguistic domination.

*Palthāram patchanam* —
*pāṇi grahaṇam* —
*vaduvara graha-pravesam* —
*bandhu-mithrar* —
*namaskaram* —
*ubhaya kusalopari* —
*nāmāvali* —
*dhvajarohanam* —

<!-- scan 288 -->

should colourful Tamil,
which gives taste to the tongue,
wither beneath a Sanskrit invasion
whose words cannot even enter the tongue?

Betel, areca and lime
would redden his mouth.
If another language surged forward
to attack Tamil,
his eyes would redden.

He let the *agrasanadhipathi*
walk among us as **avai-thalaivar** — chair of the assembly.
The old *prasangam*
became today's **sorpōzhivu** — public address.
*Namaskaram*, *kimaskaram* and all the rest
bloomed as the good Tamil **vanakkam**.
Once a pure-Tamil **flag-hoisting festival** had arrived,
what work was left there
for *dhvajarohanam*?

If truth alone triumphs,
must it ask *Satyameva Jayate*
for permission?

I would call Anna a rich man:
his friends were the wealth he possessed.
I would call Anna an ascetic:
his whole life wore the saffron of renunciation.
I would call Anna a god:
even traitors now show him incense and lamps.
I would call Anna a Krishna:
even Putana lifts him and gives him milk!

He made our hearts his throne.
Some unknowing people
opposed him at first,
not understanding him.
Only later did they realise:
he had come as the Marx of the South
to teach true equality.

<!-- scan 289 -->

The tale of a fox becoming a horse,
the tale of Narada becoming a woman,
the tale of Krishna breaking the hanging pot —
old tales once praised by the town.

He was the great scholar given by Periyar,
a man who treated those tales
as straw in the fire.

Kamban portrayed Ravana
as a demon without even the substance called compassion.
Anna refused to let that portrayal pass.
With moving pictures drawn in words,
and questions that pierced like needles,
he dragged the births of heaven down into the street.

Was the great sage
who abandoned Menaka
after the hunger of his body was satisfied
a form of compassion?
Was Indra,
that incomparable great king
who by trickery tasted Ahalya,
a form of compassion?
Kambar!
Was Ravana alone
the hard-hearted guardian?
Who upon this earth
can answer the questions Anna raised?

Taking Anna's writings,
I weave a little story:

On **One Night**, under **Moonrise**,
the **Good Younger Brother's** **Servant Maid**
runs toward **Chandramohan**.
In the **Flame of Love**,
she sees the **Gateway to Heaven**
and drinks deep of **Kambarasam**!

The parents who saw it
grew fiercely angry
and went to court
to establish justice.

<!-- scan 290 -->

“Should a fence be built
to stop love?
Must I forget the man
who tied the *thāli* around my neck?
Should a mother tie the *thāli* for her daughter?”
So argued **Kumarik Kottam**.
What would a garden of money
become before her?

**Needhidevan Mayakkam** —
the Judge-God's Delusion — was dispelled;
he stood upright,
blessed them,
“Live well,”
and went away!

Some say God exists;
some say he does not.
I have no worry about God!
“One caste, one God” —
to accept that, he said,
is true theism.
For one person not to deceive another —
that, he said, is atheism!

I say what is good:
for us, God is
in the smile of the afflicted.
Make this your principle, he said;
raise the flag of reason, he said.

Was it not Anna
who showed through familiar Tamil similes
that the Kazhagam is the true communist?
Was not the path he showed
the good path for abolishing poverty?
For rights to increase,
for truths to stand firm —
was not what he taught
a new way?
For the country to flourish,
for good Tamil to grow —
was not each movement of his tongue a drumbeat?
Is he not, every day,
the ruler who still rules us?

Among the days of the week
you may choose Wednesday.
But of the days Anna lived,

<!-- scan 291 -->

which one shall I choose
and remember?

Shall I remember Trichy,
where he called Navalar,
“Come, thambi — come take the leadership”?
Shall I remember how,
to think through an unsolved problem,
he would go to the Professor's house?

Shall I remember the day
when, after eighteen years
of separation that neither heart desired,
we went with him
to see Periyar's face again?

Or the seashore,
where he placed a ring upon me,
saying,
“Though many arrows strike,
may this *kanaiyāzhi* guard you”?

Whatever I remember, our Anna,
your form rises before us,
a sacred form of greatness.
Thinking of it,
we take comfort.

You thundered,
“A man with a brother-army
need not fear an army.”
You would say,
“One tree alone cannot become a grove.”
Yet you know the story
of how one solitary tree here
itself became a grove.

The Puranic people say
a Saturn transit is good for some.
Is that true? Is it false?
In our Kazhagam,
a Saturn-*departure*
has indeed brought good results, Anna!

Because we labour on your path,
like the brittle branch of a drumstick tree,
the enemy will snap,
shatter,
and scatter like cotton.

We walk carrying the lamp you gave us.

<!-- scan 292 -->

The Tamil people add the ghee,
set the wick,
and help us.
In hard rain,
without letting it go out,
without letting it tremble in the wind,
we cup our hands
and guard the hand-lamp.

Give us one boon, Anna!
I ask it as though in penance.
For the people at the foundation,
achievement after achievement
has been built up.
Anna,
if only once
you could see with your own eyes
how tirelessly we walk upon your path,
all our worry would fly away.

O cool full moon,
come without refusing.
If you grant this boon,
it will be as though we received
the boon of deathlessness.
Joyful Tamil Nadu will dance;
all our sorrow will disappear.

### Kalaignar's conclusion

Why a conclusion today?
“Long live Anna!”
is for us
both the pearl-address
and the final address.

So let all of us say:

Long live Anna!
Long live Anna!
Long live Anna!

Greetings!

❀

## Translator notes

- `அண்ணா வழியில்` is rendered **On Anna's Path**. The repeated `வழி` carries both literal path/journey and ideological-political lineage.
- Scans 287–288 deliberately catalogue Sanskritised public/ceremonial vocabulary and Tamil replacements. The source forms are kept in transliteration where the contrast itself is the point; `அக்கிராசனாதிபதி → அவைத்தலைவர்`, `பிரசங்கம் → சொற்பொழிவு`, `நமஸ்காரம் → வணக்கம்`, and `துவஜாரோகணம் → கொடியேற்று விழா` remain explicit.
- The rationalist/Kamban/Ravana passage and the mythological examples on scan 289 are Kalaignar's source polemic; the translation does not harmonise them with external epic or religious traditions.
- The sequence beginning `ஓர் இரவு சந்திரோதயத்தில்...` stitches together names/titles associated in the source with Anna's writings. The English highlights them as titles/conscious literary allusions rather than treating the chain as an ordinary literal incident.
- `கணையாழி` in the seashore memory is retained in transliteration in the key line because the Tamil activates both a ring and the surrounding `கணை` (“arrow”) wordplay.
- `சனிப்பெயர்ச்சி` is used as a political pun on Saturn's transit/departure. The English makes the transit/departure turn visible without claiming an exact homophone.
- The personal memories of Navalar, the Professor, Periyar and the seashore ring are translated as Karunanidhi's first-person recollections in this poem.
- The closing lamp, government-achievement and prayer-to-Anna imagery is preserved as elegiac/political rhetoric rather than neutralised.

## Item 31 — I Shall Walk on Our Ayya and Anna's Path!

<!-- scan 293 -->

I shall walk on the path
of our Ayya and Anna!

My sibling, do you know
one truth?

The joy I did not find
in the lap of the mother
who carried and gave birth to me;
the joy I did not find
on the shoulders of the father
who lifted me and raised me;
the joy I did not find
even when my two sisters
stood like organs of breath
keeping me alive;
the joy I did not find
in the affection of the companions
who hold my hand
and pour love upon me;
the joy I did not find
in the speech of my children,
speech that defeats flute and yāzh
and even the Kural that praises them —
I find that joy
when your mouth blossoms
and cries, “Long live!”

Whenever I call, “Come,”
you treat it as an order,
carry it out,
and stand before me.
Before my lips can even move
to say, “Enter the field,”
you have finished the duty
and stand upright.
Then I see it.
I wear joy

<!-- scan 294 -->

throughout my whole heart.
And I am ashamed —
my face itself is ashamed —
that I cannot repay
your love in equal measure.

Seventy-one today —
how many events,
mixtures of pleasure and pain,
in this simple man's life!

Those who know my path know:
it is the path of Periyar and Anna.
Yet how many crores of taunts,
words, resistance and battles
this heart has carried and carried,
and still it stands erect,
still it stands firm!

Not every stone struck by a chisel
becomes a sculpture alive with spirit.
My thanks
to those who carved me into one!

Not every iron bar
hammered in the fierce heat
of the forge
is shaped into sword or spear.
To those who forged me
into sword and spear,
I scatter flowers of thanks
with both hands full!

When you praise me,
a mere sandal beneath the foot,

<!-- scan 295 -->

I rejoice.
But when I see agreements
that would again make the land
a colony,
I grimace.

At the bogeyman threats
of Hindi fanatics,
a volcano and an earthquake
rise in my heart!

When “social justice”
is shown only
as an eye-wiping trick,
my wounded heart throbs.

There, through a political settlement
reached by Arafat,
Palestinians have half a freedom!

In South Africa,
through white darkness,
a black dawn!

Here, Eelam alone
still burns
without being extinguished!

India became two,
then became three.
In what remains,
how many castes,
how many quarrels!

If secular principle dies,
the nation will crumble.

In the search for humaneness,
parties lose the human being himself,
and reel from scene to changing scene.

<!-- scan 296 -->

Amid all this,
on this birthday,
this is the vow I take:
joining with my sibling,
dearer to me than life,
who says,
“Why fear while I am here?” —

to guard the country,
to guard the language,
to guard the rights
of the people of our race,
I shall continue service
without even an atom of time
for weariness.

I shall keep walking —
on the path
of our Ayya and Anna!

## Translator notes

- The canonical title ends with `!`; the contents witness does not. The English follows the canonical title and preserves the Tamil contents witness separately in metadata.
- `அய்யா` is retained as **Ayya**, the movement's familiar honorific for Periyar, rather than flattened into a generic “sir.”
- The Palestine, South Africa, Eelam, social-justice and political-geography passages are translated as the poem's contemporary claims and rhetoric; Phase 4 does not update or fact-check them.
- The opening family-versus-movement-sibling contrast is intentionally intimate and hyperbolic. The English keeps that structure rather than reducing it to a political summary.

## Item 32 — Presiding Poem at the Three Great Celebrations Poetry Assembly

<!-- scan 297 -->

Tamil, granted to us as life itself!
Even the moon bears a stain,
but sweet Tamil,
you are a ray of light without blemish!

Jaggery is needed
to sweeten Pongal;
Tamil of the Podhigai hills,
to make you grow,
what we need in the conch-resounding battlefield
is a good heart.

Tamil that flourishes as valour!
Tamil that yields as victory!
Laugh so the whole land shakes:
let talk of defeat belong henceforth
to the enemy, not to us!

People born from who-knows-where
have come to divide up Tamil life into shares.
The Tamil host must gather without bank or boundary.
We must make the blind and mute awaken and speak,
and make them march toward the field.

For that spark to rise,
you are the *purappāttu* living in our word and deed,
the war-song of Kalinga,
the ancient song of the elder people
who arose before stone and earth,
unvanquished even by the sea's engulfing flood —

<!-- scan 298 -->

the Tamil of mango, jackfruit and banana,
giving flavour to the new song I sing today.
Live long!

We have seen the three great seas,
seen the threefold Tamil,
seen the three great kings,
seen three flags flying,
seen the glory of the three Sangams,
tasted the three great fruits —
now let us behold the Three Great Celebration!

Is there a day
when we do not think of Anna,
Periyar and the Kazhagam?
Today let us take a vow
to guard the policy that is our very eye.

Gold? Wealth? What use are they?
Counterfeits wander only for those!

Erode was once like a head full of lice;
had there been no Erode comb
to clean and beautify it,
Tamil Nadu itself
might by now have gone bald!

We know his birthday,
but not his star.
He never worried about stars either.
Yet if one asks his *lagna* —
it must surely have been Leo!
Before that lion,
jackals could not wag their tails.

He was a surging volcano;
its lava rushed toward the citadel of Vedic orthodoxy.
He was a forest fire;
in it the great serpents of Sanatana order

<!-- scan 299 -->

were scorched.
He was a wild flood breaking its banks;
in that torrent
the mud houses of old almanacs
vanished without a trace.

He was a war-sword;
in a single sweep,
priestcraft lost its breath.

If even small singing streams,
when they rage,
can make ruin hard to stop,
he was the Periyar — the great river!
If that river rises in anger,
will not the taproots of epic and purana
be torn out?

Like a singular radiance
born in molten gold,
making the red dawn itself blush,
Periyar surged up.
Crores of young bulls of modernity
stood firm and declared:
our blood itself is the stake.

The man of this land shall live here,
a man of fame who can outshine any land.
The Tamil who had bowed his head —
see, he stands upright now!

Those already winnowed out
are the little fox-pack now.
With rational thought he condemned
piety, religion and purana:
“ruin! ruin! ruin!”
A new idea arose — a revolutionary idea.
If even a worm becomes a tiger when trodden on,
what will a tiger become?
Yet some still do not understand!

<!-- scan 300 -->

But consider this:
pigs caked in filth,
mangy dogs,
herds of donkeys —
all have the right
to run and play in streets
where “high-caste” people walk.
Yet the last Shudra or Panchama,
said to have been born from the god's feet,
becomes pollution merely by walking there.
Such was the unjust “justice” called Manu's law,
with no one to reject it
before Periyar was born in Erode.

That is why he said:
strike after seeing the snake —
and the skull of that bitter doctrine
split under the blow.

For Valli and Parvati,
for Bama and Radha and all their kind,
you conduct marriage festivals every year.
“What became of last year's marriage, ayya?”
Ayya asked.
Who has yet answered that question of reason?

One day in Kailasa,
Parvati went to bathe,
gathered the dirt from her body,
gave it life,
and stationed that form as guard
so no man should enter
while she bathed unclothed.
Shiva, who had swallowed the deadly poison,
hurried in.
The form stopped him as a man.
Shiva grew angry
and struck off the dirt-born figure's head.

<!-- scan 301 -->

Parvati, seeing it, blazed in fury
and ordered her husband Shiva
to restore life to the child born of her dirt.
An elephant happened along the street;
its head was cut off
and fixed upon the dirt-born body.
That, the story says,
is elephant-faced Pillaiyar.
You know it, do you not?

If Mother Parvati carried enough dirt on her body
to make such a large child,
how many days must she have gone without bathing?
Was it not the old man of Erode
who planted such questions
and turned purana into an object of ridicule?

Was it not Periyar too
who cleared the weeds of ideas,
saying that the crop of common ownership
can grow only in a social field
from which weeds have been removed?

You who measure and declare that an “atma” exists —
where then did the tale of the next birth come from?
The question roared like *Murasoli*;
Sanatanists withdrew like turtles.

Seeing plants move in darkness,
blind minds cried “ghost, demon, spirit”
and died in fear.
There is no ghost, no demon — madmen!
Who lit the Self-Respect flame saying,
“Do not believe what swindlers tell you”?
Periyar!

Why did he affectionately call Kamaraj
a “green Tamil”?
So the backward classes might live!

<!-- scan 302 -->

Why did he wish
that Kazhagam rule should continue for all time?
So that the dreams he had seen
might be fulfilled step by step!

Was it not Periyar
who made Tamil feeling,
lying like the Lord of Srirangam,
spring up and thunder in all eight directions
and perform many valiant tasks?

Street stage, art stage, music stage, cinema stage —
everything once rang with
“ammāmi, aththimbēr,” a kind of *jala-tarangam*.
He changed that,
made it a Tamil stage, a stage of our own.
Is it not fitting to hold a poetry assembly
for the man who did so?

He had a beard like Tagore of Bengal,
a gaze raised like a charging tiger,
a body like a red hill,
and in eyes white as conch
two black beetles —
light remained in them until death.

Even a spinning top rests after circling;
he, even in extreme old age,
still went round like a top.

As volcano and burning flame,
as nature's own dance,
as thunder making opposition tremble,
as the great torch of ethnic consciousness,
as a sharp sword cutting down degradation,
as Ingersoll who denied even God,
as Socrates forever speaking in Athens,
as Voltaire with a diamond heart asking “Why?” —

<!-- scan 303 -->

our Father Periyar lived
and filled every atom of our thought!

A rain of reason would fall —
beauty flow, darkness go!
Clear speeches would shine,
ability sparkle!
Like the opened sea,
the mountain peak,
the sound of waves,
the cloud embracing them —
reason's rain would fall.
Arrogance would perish.
Slavery would end.

When Ayya spoke,
for Kazhagam people it was like eating *adhirasam*;
for villains, like an atom bomb exploding!

He did not die young;
he lived to fullness of age and then rested.
Yet if the Taj Mahal suddenly sank into the earth,
would we console ourselves
by saying, “It was built many years ago”?
If the Thanjavur tower fell,
would it be easy to bear
by saying it had grown old?
He was as tall as the Thanjavur tower,
a maker of history like the Taj Mahal.

A hundred years for that sun of awakening!
And for our Anna —
for the Kazhagam he created —

<!-- scan 304 -->

today is its thirtieth year!

Can a storm wind give birth
to a flower-soft breeze?
Such a child was born:
our Anna, the gentle wind,
a breeze that drove away even violent storms
with a smile.

Calling, “O fallen Tamil Nadu!”
he classified the greatness in which we had once lived
and gave us a deep treasury of ideas.
It was a treasury of thought —
not some broken-eared chest imagined by a black-money man
to overturn the Kazhagam,
but a camphor casket given by Kanchi Anna!

As Periyar's right hand,
as a motorboat carrying the poor in knowledge to shore,
as a sunbeam forgiving the errors of the small,
as a master who made fanatics bow,
he conducted politics with method and principle
and planned the removal of the begging bowl.
Some who once sat with “Sri” before their names
now wander the street abusing us.
If we fight a pimple, our own face is spoiled;
apply civet calmly and the boil will fall away.

Even when he differed in thought from E. V. R. Periyar
and wept as he parted to form another Kazhagam,
our Anna thundered to the enemy
that the two would remain like a double-barrelled gun.
Today, gathering cast-off pieces,
base men run with swords
to rip open the womb of the mother herself;
soon they will be crippled!

<!-- scan 305 -->

It feels as though it happened today —
that sight of the cow parting from the Kazhagam calf.
As though a spear that could pierce a hill
was aimed and tore our chest,
Anna, who would never leave us,
left us that day. Alas!
Eight or nine years have passed.

Avvai has a song called *Kondrai Vendhan*;
our king of principle has an imperishable song!
He showed in his new Tamil style
both the gait of the swelling sea
and the soft walk of maidens.
He trapped those who spoke evil in debate.
At the moment of taking snuff,
with a smile he ground his opponents to powder.
He drove out those of dusty character.
He placed a golden crown on Mother Tamil.

Anna held a pen —
a plough upon paper
to alter the fate written on the heads
of deceived Tamils!

Betel, areca and lime reddened his mouth;
if Hindi came to strike Tamils,
his eyes reddened.
He sent *agrasanadhipathi* away
and let “assembly president” walk the stage.
Yesterday's *prasangam* became “speech.”
*Namaskaram-kimaskaram* bloomed into good Tamil “vanakkam.”
Once a flag-hoisting ceremony exists in pure Tamil,
what work has *dhvajarohanam* there?
If “Truth alone triumphs” is said in Tamil,
does *Satyameva Jayate* grow angry?

<!-- scan 306 -->

The Sankaracharya says even worship
cannot be conducted in Tamil nurtured by the Sangam!
To meet the battle method of the Kanchi seat,
the leader from Kanchi did not leave us untaught.
If worship for the Lord in the temple
cannot be in the mother tongue,
we shall beat the drum and say:
we do not need that Lord either!

We are brothers to the young breeze called Anna;
at the same time,
we are grandchildren of the great storm called Periyar.
Those who impose the northern language
must not forget our history.
If they tread on the feet
of the fighting Tamil people,
our feet will tread upon
the head of language domination — take warning!

We are soldiers of a forty-year language war,
not heroes who obtained exemption
by saying, “What has it to do with us?”
Even when our young shoots
became victims to gunfire
and flowered as corpses in the middle of the street,
even when they entered fire
and became lamps that cannot be extinguished,
some who luckily escaped the nationwide earthquake
of the language revolution
by running and hiding
now speak for show:
“I shall abolish Hindi,”
and purchase valour at market price.
Even a party that sang off-key
in support of Hindi during the Emergency
now says Hindi must not come.
When cats themselves roar like leopards,
if we leopards rise in anger,
will there not be a war that shakes the earth?

<!-- scan 307 -->

Prison and torture —
for a Kazhagam man these are old stories!
Hindi for government,
Sanskrit for worship of God —
as long as even one Tamil remains alive,
he will not accept it!
Source-visible `ஓதிய மிலார்` —
