moringa branches cannot escape
if they come to strike iron pillars!

Do not imagine that because
Periyar and Anna are gone
you may run the country as you please.
Had they been here,
they would have stood as dams
against our river of feeling.
Waxen trees!
You might then have escaped without melting away.

Beating one's chest before a bull without a nose-rope
is the work of fools.
Even Anna, who asked us to be patient
like earth that bears those who dig it,
if he were alive today,
would surely thunder,
“Rise in fury, brother!”

Is government being conducted now?
Is it not jungle rule that continues?
How to destroy the opposition —
that alone is the thought.
Wherever one turns,
only slander against us;
cruel cases, murder cases —
they think the Kazhagam will tremble
if they file severe charges!
Will a temple tower fall
because a chicken pecks it?

<!-- scan 308 -->

As Ganga and Yamuna flood
and enter the northern plains,
illicit liquor floods
and pours across Tamil land!
They beat their chests saying,
“I shall drive out the liquor demon.”
Is he some demon without a form
to be chased away?
He is here,
laughing as an organiser of the ruling party!

Rape, murder, theft,
tear gas, firing,
bribery and corruption,
interference in administration,
interference even in justice —
proof exists by the thousand, one upon another.
We would need a separate poetry assembly
just for all that.

Covering everything up,
they act like Buddha and Jesus
and ask us to believe them!

Have you ever heard ministers before
speak of auctioning cattle?
Here speaks the cattle-man Velan:
if bullock-cart processions take place,
the carts will certainly be smashed,
and the bulls auctioned!

If the farmer who weeps
turns into a spark of fire,
will he not dare,
in opposition to auctioning cattle,
to auction the ministers themselves?

Because they have imagined Tamils
to be a sack of gooseberries,
the pressure in the chest grows.
We must cut it down!
When will all Tamils unite?
When will the domination of evil men
be driven back?

<!-- scan 309 -->

I know the question that rises.
There is black soil in the land,
weeds in the field,
chaff in paddy,
and a traitor among Tamils —
not far away, but near!

They want office,
a palanquin ride every day.
For that,
if the enemy's feet must be washed,
they race ahead to do it.
The pleasure such Vibhishanas find
is like the pleasure of prostitutes
who embrace a man's corpse for money!

Therefore,
do not fear Tamil traitors.
They are rusted nails.
Those nails cannot even hold
pictures of Anna and Periyar;
how can they bear their principles?

Let cowards move aside.
Let heroes continue.
We shall beat the drum
and raise the cry of the people;
we shall remove degradation.

Ayya lit the lamp of reason.
Anna's hand protected it
from being extinguished by wind.
Again a great wind blows today.
Anna's hand is not here;
in its place let thousands,
lakhs, crores of hands stretch out.
Every one of those hands

<!-- scan 310 -->

belongs to Anna —
the hands of pure brothers,
the hands of cadres!
All of them are Anna's hands.

`Agaram` is the first letter of Tamil;
Anna's name is the first letter for Tamils.
That leader of the first letter gave us
the Munnetra Kazhagam — the Progressive Federation.

If progress is to reach Tamils,
the Three Great Celebration alone is not enough.
To guard this great land,
to guard our language,
today itself we must take a vow.

We must gain Periyar's courage.
We must understand Perarignar Anna's clarity.
We need firmness that does not fear great opposition.
Death comes at six or at a hundred —
let what must happen, happen.
We need a heart of sacrifice
that does not count the sweetness of life.
We must open a brave chest
to bullets that pierce through.
We need the resolve of the revolutionary poet:
“The flower-grove where the mango-cuckoo sings —
a prison that thinks it can cage us.”

We must roll frauds away.
We must nurture newness.
We must crush counterfeits.
We must move as warriors of fighting spirit.
Periyar and Anna must live forever
in our hearts.

❀

## Translator notes

- The canonical Tamil title uses `கவியரங்கம் தலைமைக் கவிதை`; the contents witness uses the linked form `கவியரங்கத் தலைமைக் கவிதை`. The English title follows the canonical opening while both Tamil witnesses remain separate in metadata.
- `முப்பெரும் விழா` is rendered **Three Great Celebrations**, the movement observance linking Periyar, Anna and the Kazhagam. The Tamil phrase remains traceable rather than being reduced to a generic “anniversary.”
- Repeated passages about Periyar and Anna overlap earlier anthology items. The English keeps their rhetoric and key terminology consistent while translating this item as its own source witness.
- The Ganesha/Parvati, caste, Sanatana, temple-language, anti-Hindi, Emergency, prohibition and ruling-party passages are source polemic and contemporary political claims; they are translated as such, not externally harmonised.
- Source-visible difficult form `ஓதிய மிலார்` is retained explicitly rather than silently normalised from another edition.

## Item 33 — In a Changing Town

<!-- scan 311 -->

Under the title **“In a Changing Town,”**
on 10 August 1980,
Puducherry Radio arranged an Independence-Day poetry assembly
at the Puducherry Kamban Kalaiarangam.
Under Kalaignar's chairmanship,
six poets sang on the sub-themes
**equality, labour, surroundings,
home welfare, education and unity.**
This is the presiding poem he delivered.

Greetings
to the Honourable Chief Minister of Puducherry,
to the other ministers,
to the Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the Assembly,
to the respected state organiser
who plans and carries out his tasks,
and to this overflowing hall —
to all my life-blood siblings
who would give even their lives
to protect classical Tamil,
I offer my greetings!

Greetings
to the elders of rich Tamil
who have come to hear this radio *kaviyarangam*;

<!-- scan 312 -->

greetings too
to the smiling poets
who have come to sing in many modes.

It is a puzzle
that Puducherry Radio invited
this poet who writes
without caring much for *etukai* or *monai*!
Accept my thanks for that!

In the land where the volcanic poet of Ettayapuram
forged chartered poetry,
in the land where Bharathidasan,
the poet who made an earthquake of revolution,
was born,
we have gathered for a poetry assembly of freedom
that cuts away the destroying chains of slavery.

I salute the motherland
that lets freedom flash
in golden letters of history,
yet still carries poverty
upon its head.

Even after the fox-natured foreigners
left the country,
the benefit of our own rule
has not reached all who suffer.

In Puducherry,
like bottles emptied after drinking,
poor people lie here and there
in the middle of streets,
in fields,
by wells,
their bellies scorched,
walking corpses!

<!-- scan 313 -->

When will the tongue praise this
as a golden land?
There is no clothing
to protect human dignity,
no house in which to shelter.
What use is an honourarium
to mud, mire,
the insects and worms of the drain?
They do not need one!

Did we churn freedom
from the ocean of struggle
only so that self-respect might disappear?

Because they had nothing,
in Dindigul,
loving people who worship in Allah's name
rushed out of line to receive charity.
In the cruel mortar of death
they were ground to powder,
struck by the pestle called crowding,
and left this long world.

They ran seeking veshtis and saris,
driven by tormenting poverty.
They shattered like veenas,
and hung asleep
on the ladders of death,
without a lullaby.

Women of the mother-clan, flowers;
children, clusters of buds —
in the graveyard garden
they bloomed as plants of death.

Is freedom merely
to see crops like those of barren soil?

<!-- scan 314 -->

Is liberation something won
so that a few may live
while many wither?

Let us at least now make a rule
so those who have nothing may live
and hardship be removed.
Let us bid farewell to “fate.”
Let us march against
the supposed fruits of previous birth.
Let us, by righteous means,
break down the fortress of domination
that places barriers before life.
Let us make happiness swell
like a flood from an opened sluice.

Puducherry Radio has built this assembly
called “In a Changing Town,”
with poets steeped in Tamil.
Let us eat without weariness
the fruit of poetry
from this waterfall of honeyed taste!

Puducherry is the pilgrimage land
that stamped on me
a seal of sacrifice.
This is the sacred ground
where I died and lived again!
In this divine place
where many bathe in sacred water,
they once beat me down
thinking, “the fellow is finished.”
They lifted me and threw me into a drain.
Because the mosquitoes of death did not bite,
Puducherry kept me alive
and made me bear new trials.
Had it swallowed me that day,
I would have no trouble at all today!

There are beautiful towns
across this cooling earth;
in a changing town,
is there a greatness of newness?

<!-- scan 315 -->

Across the cooling earth
there are lovely towns,
waterfalls and rivers,
and deep seas in abundance.
A fawn leaps;
a tiger-cub rests in its mother's lap;
in the grove a cuckoo sings;
in delight a peacock dances.

Nature's abundance,
beauty's smile,
good and pleasant thought —
change in everything!
We need courage to act,
gentleness in action,
and we must shatter division.

The talk that birth makes one high or low
has been destroyed by the secretion of reason —
so we say.
Yet people do not forget division,
and religions do not renounce collision.

They let equality dance on the stage,
then secretly plot to break its leg,
and excuse themselves:
“It is God's doing — what can we do?”
Strike such a crowd with the whip!

To whom is the sermon
“You must labour” addressed?
To the many crores
already broken by labour upon labour?
They bake in the forge's hot fire;
they die in the deep mud of the field;
they perish in smoke-belching factories;
they perish among waves without fearing storms.
They turn deserts into groves.
They strike rocks to lay good roads.
Yet sweet sermons are preached at them:
how much more must they labour and tear themselves apart?

Labour according to strength,
comfort according to need —
people worn out by ceaseless work
need that at once!

<!-- scan 316 -->

Without it,
advice and sermons are bundles of wet firewood;
they cannot cook the socialism we need.
For the country to live,
we must prepare that *Nalapakam*
in practical life.

Surroundings, home welfare, education, unity —
so these do not become empty exteriors,
so victories may accumulate,
we must understand the greatness of labour
and establish equality without high and low.

**In a changing town —**

even an onion will take Draupadi's vow,
for peel after peel
another sari of skin appears!
Even an onion takes Draupadi's vow.

**In a changing town —**

a lump of mud praises itself as Karna.
Karna's heart melted with compassion;
a mud-clod melts in rain —
so is the clod Karna too?

**In a changing town —**

even the grinding-stone roller
will call itself the Lord
and demand worship;
in the temple it will make a commotion:
“Am I not also the Sivalinga?”
That equality is laughable.
Not every oil-press stone becomes a Sivalinga!

<!-- scan 317 -->

**In a changing town —**

a man who eats six times every day
will call himself a labourer,
because, he says,
he eats until sweat from his forehead falls to the ground!
Beat the great drum:
call him a madman!

**In a changing town —**

counterfeits become originals,
brass becomes gold.
To overthrow these lies,
in this changing town,
I call the poets steeped in Tamil.

Come into the poetry assembly,
you whose metrical chains caress the ear,
whose beauty surpasses an ivory statue,
whose heart is filled with delightful Tamil,
carrying the flame of love!

## Translator notes

- The title witnesses differ only in Tamil spacing: canonical `மாறி வரும்` versus contents `மாறிவரும்`. The English follows the canonical witness and preserves the contents form separately in metadata.
- `எதுகை` and `மோனை` are retained as *etukai* and *monai*, traditional Tamil sound-patterning terms, rather than replaced with an inexact English prosodic label.
- Kalaignar's Puducherry assault/survival passage is translated as first-person autobiographical rhetoric in this poem; no outside biographical harmonisation is added.
- The Dindigul crowd-death passage, poverty claims and political-economic critique are translated as source claims.
- The closing onion/Draupadi, mud-clod/Karna and grinding-stone/Sivalinga sequences are deliberate comic analogies attacking false equivalence; their Tamil cultural references remain explicit.

## Item 34 — Views of Society...!

<!-- scan 318 -->

Presiding poem delivered on 7 January 1980
at the poetry assembly held for the Golden Jubilee
of *Ananda Vikatan*.

Greetings,
poets who have taken the thread of imagination
and woven poems
on the Tamil loom — greetings!

Elders who have come to this hall
with enduring love for Tamil — greetings!

Swarms of bees who have flown here
to taste the honey of song!
We are fragrant flowers;
we welcome and salute you.
We are people who do not abandon affection,
people who do not forget human fragrance.

He was worthy of undying fame;
was there any undertaking of his
that did not flourish?
Arrogant words were scarce;
Vasan's heart was one
no one could hate.

<!-- scan 319 -->

Vasan's head never rose in conceit,
and that head never bent
before the unworthy.

★ ★ ★

In how many ways we nurture
Tamil, sweet as sugarcane!
Who nurtured *Vikatan* itself
through pearl-like humour,
essays and stories,
like a fresh cluster of flowers?
He is not with us today.
Yet the wall he built remains,
and the pictures remain;
let us drink them deeply
with the eyes of the heart.

★ ★ ★

Is *Ananda Vikatan* fifty years old?
No — there is a correction:
fifty-two is the truth!
Truthful Vikatan,
I ask only to tease:
what profit is there in reducing your age?
Are you still wandering the cinema world
looking for another opportunity?

The older you grow,
the more diamond-hard you become.
Taking on colourful roles,
you pour out thought.
Have you not heard
the cool moon, blazing sun and stars
claim ages of crores upon crores of years?
Live long like them!

O jewel of the eye, dark jewel,
golden jewel that makes us laugh with thought,
new jewel, jewel of fame!
With manners like a cluster of many gems,
you delight your reader-farmers
like a mound of harvested grain.
Lady, the excitement we gain from seeing you —

<!-- scan 320 -->

we do not gain even from a Kanchipuram sari
spread before our eyes!

After a musician's concert,
the vidwans turn the pages of *Vikatan*
to read the review.
A scholar feels proud
only when *Vikatan* gives his book a review.
Even a cartoon,
in Vikatan's pages,
under Mali's hand,
came alive and spoke.

Kalki's stories,
springs of multiplying word-wealth,
captured the mind.

Even when policies differed,
Vikatan showed in practice
that culture is needed in the way disagreement is spoken.
Even a pond may carry a little moss;
so too there may be a fault sometimes,
but not a crime.

When we said,
“Tamizh Nadu for Tamils,”
Vikatan answered,
“A rat-hole for rats!”
Perhaps Vikatan,
though it once ran a rationalist contest,
was also an expert in almanac astrology!
For just as it predicted,
we now see Tamil Nadu as a rat-hole —
yet there are no rats in it!
Eight-legged insects, snakes,
scorpions and centipedes
make an unbearable racket.
The insect called caste has eight legs —
no, no, I have said it wrong:
it has more legs than one can count!
Should we not seek out and destroy
these many-legged creatures that came halfway in?
The violence that writhes like snake and scorpion —
how few are the lives it devours? Tell me!

<!-- scan 321 -->

★ ★ ★

Balasubramanian is like a prop-root
to the banyan tree.
With affection he asked me
to sit as chair of this poetry assembly
so Vikatan's Golden Jubilee might be held with vigour.
He did not say,
“I will value you only if you hold office.”
Even if I lie in street dust,
he said, the red-black *kundrimani* seed
will not lose its colour;
he invited me with kindness,
black and red together.
I call Balu's character,
without like or dislike,
a lamp of light,
and praise it in wonder.

★ ★ ★

Do not be shocked!
I shall call *Ananda Vikatan* “Gandhari.”
Without even the help of a grinding-stone roller,
she gave birth to a hundred children!
Because they were not born from burning stomach-pain,
all those children acquired the qualification
to run separate magazines of their own!

★ ★ ★

As we say “Honourable Sastri,”
“Honourable Pillai,”
“Honourable Minister,”
Vikatan has never possessed
that kind of “weighty” honour at all.

<!-- scan 322 -->

The reason?
No one weighs Vikatan
and sends it to the old-paper shop.
When it is *bound* and carefully preserved,
where is the “pound” or kilo weight to speak of?

In women's hands,
Vikatan is a lover to their eyes;
unwilling to part,
they embrace it against their silk saris.
For men, Vikatan is a beautiful maiden;
they are enchanted even by
the “jacket” beauty of its cover.

Today Vikatan is the bridegroom,
so teasing wedding play is customary.
Do not grow angry for nothing!

Vikatan is a doctor:
when it is nearby,
every patient receives some comfort.

Vikatan is a wanderer:
you can see it on every train and bus.

Vikatan is a madman.
Why?
Wherever it is,
ten people keep circling around it!

Here too are ten people
who have come to sing sweet Tamil.
Even if you take them as ten people
circling around me — so be it!

<!-- scan 323 -->

Am I not mad myself
when it comes to Tamil?
Even if I were born a dog,
I would wag my tail
before a servant of Tamil.
Even if I wandered as a rabid dog,
would I not bark only
“Tamil! Tamil!”?

Tamil, my mother!
Tamil that raised me
as a cradle-lullaby!
Tamil sweet as a jackfruit segment
mixed with flesh, life and honey!
Until my breath dissolves into the sky,
I shall do my duty
to the soil in which I was born.

But while serving nectar-tongued Tamil,
our gaze toward social progress
must also remain sharp.
That is why Vikatan eagerly invites us
to take a **“view of society.”**

★ ★ ★

“Society” — poor thing,
a pitiable word!
By putting the blame upon it,
thousands upon thousands of injustices
are delivered by shameless villains.
Those orphan children then go
to law school in colleges of anarchy!

So that the laws they study
do not go useless,
they rapidly turn into exploding bombs.
If asked, “Is this not violence?”
the answer rises sharply:
“What better way did society teach us?”

<!-- scan 324 -->

Poor society — pitiable society!

When a burglar carrying a crowbar,
or a boy working scissors through a festival crowd,
is caught red-handed,
he cries:
“God, what can I do?
Blind society made me a thief!”

Poor society — pitiable society!

A man worships Mādan and Kādan as gods,
tears the heads from human buds,
and gives human sacrifice to a sorcerer.
If we cry, “You great sinner!”
he too replies:
“What can I do?
This destructive society made me like this.”

Poor society — pitiable society!

Even the robber
who breaks the arms and legs of ancient statues
and carries on international trade with them
plainly declares:
“Cruel society drove me
into this degraded trade.”

Poor society — pitiable society!

Because no green light was shown,
“I lit this light suited to my desire,”
says the lady of the red light.

<!-- scan 325 -->

If we spit,
“Shame! Is this a livelihood?”
she spits back:
“Shame on this ‘society’!”

Poor society — pitiable society!

★ ★ ★

Yet, taken as a whole,
some truth does shine here
in full light.

If we build no foundation of social progress,
then raise a mansion of socialist economics
and place international-political finials upon it,
it will crack and collapse.
Understanding this difficulty,
Vikatan asks us to look upon
“society.”

A gathering of human flowers
placed a sacred fence
around its own garden;
that fence is called society.

Human beings were not
the first life on earth:
worms, insects, fish, monkeys,
animals and birds came before.
Does the phrase “fragrance of a previous birth”
make sense to you now?
The “next birth,” and the birth after that —
are they not really the forms
that human beings may take
after crores upon crores of years of change?

<!-- scan 326 -->

The human species arose upon the earth.
I say with certainty:
at that time there was one caste alone.
After discovering fire,
restraining water,
and taking up family responsibility,
society was born.

The society that was born spread wide
and became a great society
with embankments of division.
“Birth is equal to all lives;
distinction comes from differing deeds” —
groups formed in harmony with this Kural.
Then small-minded men entered across the path
and created castes opposed to moral law.
Love fell;
quarrels multiplied.

The result?
In the darkness of ignorance,
man lived by eating man.
Today, in the light of science,
man lives by killing man!

Society is a garden of ordered flowers,
but every separate flower
must give fragrance.
A tiny drop becomes a great flood;
should every drop be a drop of poison?

Yet poison is drunk till dawn
as though it were honey — they call it liquor.
There are laws in the country to prohibit it;
there are also schemes here and there
to keep the mixing going!

Adulteration itself becomes a trade.
Clay pellets are married into grain.
Those who learn this art
and lose their moral standing
are the very people who sell
our country's currency for a good price —
and sell the currency of business honesty too!

<!-- scan 327 -->

If we ask,
“Can you do such poisonous deeds
that ruin health and nation?”
they move their tongues and say:
“What can I do? It is a defect of society.”

Poor society — pitiable society!

Goats, cattle, pigs and even donkeys
may brush against one's clothes
and circle the temple.
But if only a child of God
from the Harijan community touches them,
they cry “Tiruneelakanta!”
If asked,
“Is this cruelty right?”
they answer:
“It is justice that has existed in society from olden days.”

Poor society — pitiable society!
A society that must fall under our gaze!

I do not believe the story
that Shiva opened a third eye in his forehead.
Even so,
I wish that story would come true here
to burn these degrading social evils
to ash.
Let us open the eye in our forehead!
For the country to live,
for suffering to end,
let our gaze fall strongly upon society.

<!-- scan 328 -->

Poets who stand tall
like mountains of imagination,
raise your voices as one!
Let us protect rights!
Let us honour relationships!
Let us make truths speak!
Let us make evil hearts grow clear!

## Translator notes

- The title witnesses are exact; the ellipsis and terminal `!` are retained in English.
- The poem's Golden-Jubilee humour depends on Tamil/English magazine culture and wordplay: `கனம்` (“weight/honour”), *bound*/*pound*, the cover as a woman's “jacket,” and Vikatan as Gandhari with a hundred magazine-children. The English keeps these jokes explicit rather than replacing them with unrelated puns.
- Vasan, Mali, Kalki and Balasubramanian are retained as source-named figures without external biographical expansion.
- The repeated `பாபம் சமுதாயம், பரிதாபத்திற்குரிய சமுதாயம்!` is kept as **“Poor society — pitiable society!”** because repetition is the poem's governing satire.
- The source term `சண்டாளத் தொழில்` in the antiquities-smuggling passage is rendered “degraded trade”; the Tamil source is not altered.
- Evolution, caste, untouchability, alcohol, adulteration, crime and social causation are presented as Kalaignar's argument in this poem, not as a fact-check layer.

## Item 35 — Kalaivanar Arangam Poetry Assembly

<!-- scan 329 -->

On many themes about Anna,
poets have come taking words
sweet as jackfruit segments
to inscribe poetry
like letters on stone,
poetry time cannot erase.

At Kalaivanar Arangam,
today — an assembly of poet-lords!

I hear the mocking laughter of some:
“What use is a poetry assembly to the Kazhagam?
Have they no shame at all to hold a festival?
We stand here victorious,
swinging the sword of valour;
why do these useless men need
a Three Great Celebration?”
So an arrogant voice sounds over there.

I shall give one explanation
to them and to you.
Like a thief who puts out the lamp,
enters a house,
grabs what he can,
and runs away without being caught,
they have won the Aruppukkottai
and Nellai constituencies!

That is precisely why I said earlier:

<!-- scan 330 -->

under this “Idi Amin” rule,
we should not desire by-elections
even to the tip of a sesame seed!
Yet our dear siblings said with one voice
that in a battlefield
it is glory to receive an honourable wound.
Therefore,
against the army of money-rule,
our army of democracy clashed!

If even the great poet Kamban hesitated
to praise as a great warrior
Rama, the honoured Ramachandra
who hid and felled Vali,
then even in death,
was not victory Vali's?

And even if he ruled afterwards,
was not his brother Sugriva still a traitor?

Therefore,
those who fall bearing honourable wounds
will still be listed among heroes.

Even when brought low,
a Tamil will never lower his head
by losing self-respect.

He will not forget
the distinct culture
of gratefully honouring mother and father.

Because Anna came as mother to Tamils
and Periyar as father,
the DMK, which stands as their child,
without forgetting even a little of its debt of gratitude,
today —

<!-- scan 331 -->

heals with a smile
the wound received at the battle-front
and prepares the road to future victory.

Thinking Tamil,
wetting the heart with Tamil —
our Tamils once exercised mastery
over the sounding sea.
Yet like holding spit in the mouth,
brushing filthy teeth,
then rinsing and swallowing that same spit back,
Tamils themselves consented
to destroy their nectar-like ethnic character,
artistic greatness and literature,
and spat foul saliva back into the body.

The gut filled with filth must be washed clean.
For that we need
a good laundry-worker called medicine.

Bodily purity comes through medicine;
purity of the heart comes through truth.

Making a road for that truth,
placing explosives beneath the fortress of deceit,
for sixty years without fear
fought Periyar —
our Anna's leader!

Accepting Ayya Periyar's leadership,
Anna too set out
to break the chains of slavery.

Under the chairmanship of Ma. Nannan,
a rationalist of character
born, it seems, for the office
of judge in a *pattimandram* —

<!-- scan 332 -->

in yesterday's debate held here,
the team of younger brother Durai Murugan lost.

In one sense,
that was just!
One team argued
that politics was the greater cause
of Anna's lasting fame.
The other team established
that social service was greater.

Because this gathering accepted
the argument put forward
by younger Selvendran's team,
the courteous judge, Mr Nannan,
leaned toward society
and gave that as his verdict.

If politics is like taking paddy
that has come home after harvest,
husking it into rice,
placing it in the pot
and cooking a meal,
then social work is this:
watering the field where that paddy grows,
preparing the soil,
adding manure,
and removing the weeds growing nearby.

Understanding this,
Anna performed social work
and took politics into his hands
as a weapon for social progress!

## Translator notes

- The title witnesses are exact; **Kalaivanar Arangam** is retained as the venue name rather than translated into a generic hall.
- The Aruppukkottai/Nellai by-election, “Idi Amin” rule, money-rule versus democracy, and future-victory passages are translated as the poem's political rhetoric and claims.
- The Rama/Vali/Sugriva comparison is preserved as Kalaignar deploys it, without devotional or epic harmonisation.
- The spit/gut/medicine cleansing metaphor repeats imagery found elsewhere in the anthology; the English retains its intentionally harsh bodily register.
- `பட்டிமன்றம்` is retained as *pattimandram*, the Tamil public debate form. The closing paddy-field analogy distinguishes politics from the prior social labour that makes political progress possible.

## Item 36 — "Chithirai Festival" — Presiding Poem!

<!-- scan 333 -->

On Tamil New Year’s Day, 14 April 1990,
Chief Minister Kalaignar delivered on Madras Television
this presiding poem under the title
“Chithirai Festival.”

★

To the Tamil people filled with heartfelt love,
to the distinguished ones who have come to create lofty poetry,
and to my siblings dearer to me than life —
I heap my greetings and good wishes!

The television station sent word:
“Come and offer New Year greetings.”
The time when people said,
“Spit! Spit!! Why this fellow?” has changed;
now a time has come when they send for me —
is that not a wonder?

For the assembly of poets gathered here,
I am the leader!
A joke — a fine joke!
Without drinking the mother’s milk called grammar,
whenever hunger struck,
I drank the bottled milk of modern verse —
such a child am I!

And they have invited me
to preside over a poetry assembly!

<!-- scan 334 -->

When I wondered why,
I realised this:
I will not ask for *Vaikasi* —
“vai kāsu,” “put down money” — for my poem.

So perhaps they invited me
to bless Chithirai!
They ask me to give my blessing
to the Chithirai maiden
who has received her share in this poetry assembly;
to the Chithirai maiden born of Panguni,
they ask me to give my blessing.
Perhaps they dared invite me
because I will not offer a *Purattasi* —
a “purattu āsi,” a fraudulent blessing!

With a half-sari that does not slip from the breast —
*mār kazhiyā* —
the maiden Aavani,
a woman of a traditional household,
looked at me and said,
“Ah, is it you?” — *Aani*!
Then she danced and stamped, *thai, thai*,
flashing eyes that poured fire.

Why?
Because she said the first day of Thai
is the true Tamil New Year!
That may indeed be true;
but to keep her from melting in anger,
I told her:
if Tamils have two New Years in one year,
is that not twice the joy?
On the cool face of the tender maiden
I saw moonlight.

<!-- scan 335 -->

She embraced me!
Do not be startled and ask,
“Another woman too?”
It is only a *month* —
not a woman!

More than calling this merely a New Year,
I rejoice that this is the centenary year
of the Revolutionary Poet.
Ambedkar, the new man, arose,
lifted by the hand those who had lain
as though buried in pits,
set them walking,
and spread sunlight across their faces —
that history, on this colourful festival,
we must salute!
We must nurture new thought!

To the one who gave the Constitution
of the sacred Indian nation
as a treasury of thought;
to the one who stood tall as the Vindhyas
and checked the domination
of fanatics drunk on language —
yes, to lion-hearted Ambedkar
and lion-hearted Bharathidasan,
in this year when we celebrate their centenaries,
may the evil powers that torment us
turn and flee in defeat!

The sage Vallalar of Vadalur said:
“Do not sink into caste,
religion and the mire of scripture,
O human beings.”
We have not honoured his words!
We only raise arches that hide the sky
and hold festivals for him — without shame!
And for Valluvar,
the great one who said
that all lives are equal by birth —

<!-- scan 336 -->

we offer honours alone,
but refuse to put his words into practice.
For the daughters and sons we ourselves beget,
we search for marriage alliances
inside the circles of relatives and caste.
What profit is there
in preachers advising the whole town
about a principle they themselves will not live?
Why does the body burn with anger
when the truth is spoken?
If wealth born of thought
is called a reforming principle,
to leave it without action
is a great betrayal!

There are Tamils in Tamil Nadu
who learned, long before Ettappan’s age,
to praise, worship and sing hymns to betrayal.
Even if Kattabomman spits at them,
those calculating men do not care —
those “geniuses” who live clutching
the enemy’s feet!

To shout “principle!” and “doctrine!”
from a hilltop,
to swear “Even if my throat is strangled,
I will not yield,”
and then do nothing
to make even one of those claims real —
what use is it to climb a platform
and pose before the people as a great warrior?
Only when word and deed are one
will a government flower
and hang heavy with fruit!

Because yours is a government
that does what it says
and says what it does,
we shall labour to increase prosperity;
we shall say that, like the linchpin of a chariot,
the nation must stand behind the plough;

<!-- scan 337 -->

that beautiful cities and villages,
and a society without longing and deprivation,
will arise when industry grows;
that pure-minded plans are needed
to eradicate many cruel diseases
and build a clear, healthy life;
that if rulers move like a pouncing tiger,
hoarding rats will be crushed;

that those whose backs were broken,
who drifted in education and employment
like boats without oars,
will in the coming age gain prosperity
through revolutionary changes in reservation;

that the poor young girl educated through eighth standard,
who once sighed that family life
was a fruit beyond her reach,
shall have that despair changed,
and that five thousand rupees to help place the *thali*
on her neck is a boon obtained without penance;

that for those who have lost the head of the family
and cry out, crushed by poverty,
the sum given by the government
shall become a precious amount
that wipes their tears;

that children shall receive nutritious food with eggs;
that women must not be treated as mere hens;
that thirty per cent reservation is a first-stage concession,
and that in the end men and women must receive
an “Ardhanarishvara” share of rights —
that is, half and half, fifty per cent;
that only when women’s rights are secured
will the rights of the soil in this speaking land endure;
and that in parents’ property,
half shall belong to the daughter too —
a proud law of equal right;

<!-- scan 338 -->

that poor pregnant women,
inter-caste couples,
those who marry widows,
persons with disabilities,
and leprosy-afflicted beggars
must not stand bewildered,
without wealth or dignity —
we shall stand as patrons
and honour those lives;

that on three festivals each year
five kilograms of rice shall be free;
that not only Adi Dravidars and tribal people,
but also the most backward and denotified communities
struggling at the very bottom
shall receive house-sites,
acquired and given free;
that we shall work until there is no small village
without drinking water;
that enough of living
with the sky as roof and the earth as mat —
we shall continue daily the plan
to build sturdy houses for fishers,
Adi Dravidars and tribal people;
that those who became impoverished
while defending the country and language
shall receive welfare funds and opportunities;
that students going to school
shall receive generous bus-fare concessions;
that government employees, factory labourers,
teachers, weavers,
all working people and farmers —
people as dear as life —
shall have their just demands heard,
and we shall leap to remove their suffering.

Not merely words sweet as jaggery —
this is a government
that gives deeds a form for its words!

<!-- scan 339 -->

That is why we say:
let us grow thought — let us act —
let us increase prosperity and guard character;
with the light of truth in the heart
and resolve strong as steel,
let us win our rights;
let us learn the world.

I am a poet who gives freedom to poetry;
I have given the themes.
Those who come to sing upon them are
poets who praise victorious Tamil,
poets at the summit,
poets who have learned
and circle the world!

From the golden heap
of good Tamil they bring,
let the learned gain their chance;
let evil be gone,
let truth become ours;
let us plant the flag
of literary Tamil upon this earth!
With scientific Tamil,
let us touch the edge of the sky!

<!-- scan 340 -->

First —

Arasu Manimekalai.
If this government were Manimekalai,
with the inexhaustible bowl
we would scoop and scoop without decrease
and serve nectar every day
to everyone!

Never mind.
Even without the inexhaustible bowl,
to embrace five crore Tamil people
and give them a good life,
we think every day.
Now let us hear Arasu Manimekalai’s rare poem
about that thought!

Next —

Poet-King Ponni Valavan.
The Kaveri has another name: Ponni!
While we are thinking
how to act and solve that very problem,
here comes Ponni.
Let us see what thought she gives us!

Next —

Nirmala Suresh.
Do you know her theme?
It is about *seer* — order and excellence!
A theme suited to a woman!
Without thinking of increasing
wedding *seer-varisai* gifts,
we hope she will give good ideas
for increasing the country’s excellence.

<!-- scan 341 -->

Next —

our high-priced poet-king,
“diamond and pearl” — Vairamuthu!
His theme is “Let us guard character.”
If, after guarding character,
increasing excellence and refining Tamil Nadu,
we let it become an auction item for scoundrels,
honour will go;
even our name as human beings will go!
Gandhi was the gift of time;
Arignar Anna the gift of Kanchi;
and with them, to stand on the path
whose character Periyar shaped,
and to open the eyes
of the wailing *Othiya-milaar* men,
he will sing.
Let us drink its flavour!

Next —

Poet Tamilanban,
a reader of news on television,
a man who breathes classical Tamil.
What will he say so that rights may be won?
Let him read!
Let rights breathe!

Next —

Kavikko Abdul Rahman,
the man of laughter in a poetry assembly —

<!-- scan 342 -->

not one who stands beside us
only when things glitter,
and at times of commotion
moves many leagues away!
This steel poet comes to say:
“Let us learn the world.”
Will he learn it,
or merely tour it?
Let us watch and see!

### Concluding words

Even if I say nothing in conclusion,
no harm will come;
yet I wish to say one thing.
So that the lion-like gathering of Tamil youth
may think well and act,
I shall recite one passage
from my book *Sangath Tamil*.
Receive it!

Great men who build mansions
and live lulled by gentle breezes!
Money, money, money —
you bathe every day
in the worker’s sweat
and heap up wealth!
Like vultures that eat corpses,
you keep swelling your bellies!
You push the poor
into lives wrapped in darkness
and run after possessions,
with no mind that says “enough.”
O you who have made
increasing wealth alone your goal in life,
I ask you one question.
Answer me!

<!-- scan 343 -->

What do you eat from your leaf-plate?
A ruby stir-fry?
An emerald curry?
Do you fry diamonds,
and eat them with a cat’s-eye-gem *aviyal*?
Do you make rice
out of pearls and coral,
pound it, put it in the cooking pot
and fill your gut?

Arignar Anna once stood on a public platform
and asked this question, rich in meaning,
of men who pile up wealth like a mountain.
We have never forgotten it;
we still ask it today.
We proclaim across the earth
that the fragrance of common ownership must spread!
This cry is not new.
Poet-lord Nakkirar
beat the drum of commonality
in a *Puram* poem.
Listen!

For the king who longs
to bring the whole earth encircled by the southern sea
under the shade of his white victory umbrella,
asserting exclusive rule;
and for the unlettered rough man
who roams the forest day and night without sleep,
hunting animals as one throws a stone
to knock down fruit —
for either of them,
to guard their dignity,
are not two pieces of clothing,
one below and one above, enough?
And to remove hunger without stupor,
is not one *nazhi* of grain enough
for each to eat gruel or Pongal
in this ocean-girdled world?
Thus, if the truth is
“what we eat is one *nazhi*,
what we wear is two garments,”

<!-- scan 344 -->

is it justice that many wither in poverty
while a few dance in the pride of abundant wealth?
When all born into this world
have the same needs of body and mind,
why, without thinking this through,
should one man heap up possessions like a flood?
Why should many die
in lives hollowed out like pits?

Let us destroy private ownership
that says “everything is mine,”
and find the right way to drive out poverty;
let us share food and protect all life,
lay down the principle of common welfare,
and make joy on earth —
that is the use of having taken birth!
Nakkirar gathered this thought and gave it;
good people through this ancient world
have continued to give it ever since.

> “For the one who rules the sea-girt south
> beneath a single white umbrella,
> and for the unlearned hunter
> who sleeps neither midnight nor day,
> what is eaten is one *nazhi*;
> what is worn is two garments;
> all else is alike.
> The use of wealth is giving;
> if we say ‘we shall enjoy it ourselves,’
> many things will fail us.”
>
> — *Purananuru*, poem 189

This is a song I sang
when I was out of office!

<!-- scan 345 -->

Office is a garland of thorns;
literary work is for me a strand of jasmine.
Office is a deep pit;
public service is my Podhigai hill.
If you ask, “Honour or crown?”
I am a man who values honour alone.
If you ask, “Crown or head?”
I am not a fool who grins,
“Keep the crown; that is enough!”
That is why I said,
“Let us offer a hand to relationship
and raise our voice for rights,”
and was ready to renounce office,
spending more than ten years that way.

Office is only an opportunity to serve!
Office, splendour and pomp
will all burst like an inflated balloon.
Only service rooted in character
will endure like the sky!
Some who do not know this
writhe like fish thrown upon the bank,
thinking office alone
is the purpose of birth.

Including them too,
I offer New Year greetings,
thank the scholars, poets
and comrades of every section,
and take my leave.
Accept my respectful greetings!

## Translator notes

- The title preserves the canonical verified opening’s straight quote glyphs and terminal `!`; the contents witness uses curly quotation marks and no terminal exclamation.
- Scan 334 is built from Tamil month-name sound-play: `வைகாசி / வை காசி`, `புரட்டாசி / புரட்டு ஆசி`, `மார்கழி / மார் கழி`, and `ஆனி / ஆ நீயா`, plus the dance syllable `தை தை`. The Tamil forms are retained where an English substitute would invent a false pun.
- The social-welfare catalogue is translated as the poem’s 1990 governmental programme rhetoric; Phase 4 does not fact-check or modernise those claims.
- `அர்த்த நாரீஸ்வர` is retained through the poem’s own explanatory gloss, “half and half — 50 per cent.”
- `ஓதிய மிலார்` is a final-cleared source form already treated conservatively elsewhere in the anthology; it is retained here rather than silently normalised.
- The final common-ownership passage quotes *Purananuru* 189 as embedded by the source. The English is a translation of this anthology witness, not an imported external edition.

## Item 37 — Three Letters — Thoughts Three Times Three

<!-- scan 346 -->

### Three Letters —
### Thoughts Three Times Three

At the Kalaivanar Arangam in Madras,
as part of the Five Great Celebrations,
a poetry assembly was held under the theme
“Three Letters, Thoughts Three Times Three.”
This is the presiding poem sung there
by Chief Minister Kalaignar.

★

A great celebration — a sacred celebration;
for us, one celebration is the Three Great Celebration!
This year, two more celebrations
have joined it,
and it has become a Five Great Celebration!

Ayya,
Anna,
the birthday celebration of the Kazhagam;
the centenaries of
Ambedkar
and Paavendhar;
the Diamond Jubilee
marking the rise of the Dravidian movement;
and a magnificent victory celebration
because the Union government
has accepted the Mandal Commission recommendation!

<!-- scan 347 -->

A sacred celebration too,
to offer our heartfelt thanks
to the humanitarian V. P. Singh!
My greetings to all
who have come to see the festival.

To the lion of the knowledge movement,
our Professor of ethnic self-respect;
to my life-dear younger brother Nanjilar;
to dear friend Anbil;
to Mannai and Union Minister Maran;
to the honourable ministers;
to our Assembly Speaker Tamilkudimagan;
to the new poet T. R. Baalu;
and to all the old poets —
my greetings!

“Three letters,
thoughts three times three” —
thus they have arranged the poetry assembly.
And as the head sits
above the neck,
they have made me sit
at its head.

Can a head alone function,
without broad shoulders, arms, legs, trunk,
eyes, ears, nose, mouth, stomach,
heart or lungs?
It cannot.
Therefore none of these limbs
becomes arrogant.
They work with grace,
knowing the old saying
that for the eight-span body
the head is principal.

<!-- scan 348 -->

My limbs!
My beloved treasures!
My siblings dearer to me than life!
Is what I say not true?
Like a god who incarnates declaring,
“I alone am the chief organ,”
the head has never grown arrogant.
This head has never grown arrogant,
nor shifted from its position.
Therefore I see the Kazhagam
in the sight of neck to foot
bearing the head,
doing duty with dignity
and guarding discipline;
I rejoice,
bow my head,
and salute you.

The same blood that flows to the head
flows to the feet.
Yet if *varna-ashrama dharma* declares
that those born from Brahma’s head
are the high caste,
those born from his thighs are Vaishyas,
and we who came from his feet
are the lowest caste —
why is there such disorder
only in Brahma’s blood circulation?

What is Brahma — male or female?
Even if we suppose Brahma female,
did she stitch wombs
all over her body
in order to give birth?

<!-- scan 349 -->

Even lies need a limit.
We must know
that wicked Puranic tales are absurd.

If we say all this,
they warn us:
God will grow angry,
curse you,
and give you only a short life!
Yet Ayya Periyar,
who exposed this matter
far more sharply than we do,
lived to ninety-five!
Until he went to the grave
he roared:
“Let blind custom
be buried in the earth!”
This is Periyar’s birthday!

He gave rationalist thought
like solid refined gold.
Anna did not lock it in a box;
he made it into ornaments
and adorned the Tamils with it.
Periyar prepared
Self-Respect medicine —
a bitter medicine,
but one that saves life.
Anna mixed it into fruit,
dipped it in Tamil honey,
and gave it gently
to cure the disease.

Broad-browed king of knowledge Anna,
when the Kazhagam held
its first State Conference in Madras
in the year fifty-two,
when I spoke to second his selection
as conference president —

<!-- scan 350 -->

it was then that the magic
inside three letters
shone like the full moon
breaking through clouds!

On Tamil land encircled by three seas,
we are descendants of the three crowned kings
who beat three drums, raised three flags,
and nurtured the threefold Tamil,
the three fruits of our language.
Our language is `தமிழ்` — three letters!
The distinct `பண்பு` of Tamils — three letters!
From character rises `அன்பு` — three letters!
From love grows `காதல்` — three letters!
What lovers desire, `வீரம்` — three letters!
The `களம்` where warriors bear honourable wounds — three letters!
The `வெற்றி` seen on the field — three letters!
The seed of victory, `அறிவு` — three letters!
The treasury of that knowledge,
`அண்ணா` — three letters!
And the Kazhagam founded by that Anna,
`தி.மு.க.` — three letters!

Only after I spoke in this way that day
did the song come:
“In three letters there is a breath.”
Today, three letters
and thoughts three times three
have come into this poetry arena!

From ancient days,
as slaves, as servants,
as riding horses
for those who held domination —

<!-- scan 351 -->

the oppressed suffered,
wax thrown into flame,
writhing in agony.
To change that condition,
one rose like a blazing sun —
that worthy scholar
was named Ambedkar!

“What is caste? What is religion?
O humans who preach caste and religion!
If a thousand differences
in God’s creation
are truly his doing,
come — let us gouge out
that God’s eyes!”
Thus he protected people
who were dying like worms,
raised a battle flag
so they might rise again,
and declared:
“You no longer have the right
to suppress and rule us!
Only a separate electorate
can change our condition.”
He stormed like a cyclone,
shook like an earthquake,
and made revolution —
a new Buddha!

Combining the thoughts
of Ambedkar, Periyar and Anna,
Bharathidasan gave poems
as the smile of beauty
and the heat of flame;
a poet who dipped his spear
in volcanic lava
and forged poems of ideals!
His song makes us understand
the true meaning of the word “revolution.”

<!-- scan 352 -->

His similes ride the breeze in procession;
his truths spread wide
and pour a flood of light.
He is a son of Tamil —
he blossomed as Tamil itself!
Verses equal to nectar,
every one a flower rich with pollen!

(Here, in *The Smile of Beauty*,
the Revolutionary Poet sings of pigeons.)

> “Like a lotus that has opened
> and spread its petals,
> pigeons gather in a circle
> and eat their food.
> In their life there is no cutting,
> no stabbing;
> though each is distinct,
> there is no bondage,
> no blind custom of high and low.”

(Paavendhar speaks of the pigeons’ conduct.)

> “She is alone, without an equal;
> he commits a wrong
> that causes her pain.”
> Such an unworthy act,
> a few wayward pigeons
> among those where love dwells
> may perhaps have learned
> from sorrow-laden humans.

Who other than this poet
could call even darkness beautiful?
Tell me after you look!

(Wherever darkness exists, he says,
beauty would not be complete without it.)

<!-- scan 353 -->

> “At either side
> of the lofty beautiful nose you dwell;
> at the edge of a maiden’s fish-like eyes you are;
> in the middle of the ear
> and around it you spread;
> on a woman’s face
you make the marks stand out —
> O darkness, painters know your excellence.”

(He compares darkness with ignorance,
and argues: ignorance can produce knowledge,
but can knowledge produce ignorance?)

> “Knowledge is light — yes, yes!
> Ignorance is darkness — yes, yes!
> Ignorance can make knowledge;
> can ignorance be born from knowledge?”

(And an incident to explain it.)

> “A scorpion stung a boy;
> in midnight darkness a lamp was needed.
> They searched for a match
> and could not find one.
> Someone said it was in a box;
> there was no key.
> By then eight people
> had been stung by the scorpion
> and writhed in pain.
> ‘Cleanliness is compulsory’ —
> such knowledge you gave us,
> good darkness,
> how great is your glory!”

<!-- scan 354 -->

(Paavendhar sang of pigeons and darkness
with graceful simile and humour;
now look — as the Revolutionary Poet
he blows the conch of battle.)

> “Having scorched Tamil life in the north,
> the enemy came south
> intending harm.
> From that very day,
> O Tamil that guards justice,
> you said, ‘Subdue him!’
> Though there are stumbling stones here,
> you laughed: ‘We have broad shoulders!’”

To prove that Paavendhar’s song
has not gone false,
we have shown the whole nation
that in the far southern corner
the illusion and trickery
against the Mandal Commission
did not work.
We shall announce it
to the whole world too!

At a wedding house,
a banana tree,
not knowing it has been cut and tied there,
welcomes those who arrive.
So too many backward people in the north
remain unaware of their own condition.
That is why opposition to Mandal
is greater in those states;
but here the enslaved crowd
that stretches out its hand for *sundal*
and gathers around is very small!

<!-- scan 355 -->

Why is that?
Here is the explanation.

Seventy-five years ago
an organisation arose —
the great Dravidian movement
founded by the elder Sir Pitti Thyagaraya!
If a field is ploughed and prepared,
planted properly and guarded,
there is no need to weep at harvest.
From that day until this,
this is soil that has been seasoned,
land that has ripened —
wealth accumulated by the Dravidian movement.
If paddy is sown here,
grass will not accidentally grow instead!
In education and employment,
reservation on a social basis
is the noble demand of our movement.
That is why all the cries
from the citadels of domination
subside like a snake
that sees a stick.
If little foxes raise their tails
and call, “Come, let us fight,”
will lion-bulls run away
and hide in cave-cracks?
If mice dance here,
will tigers beat the rhythm for them?

<!-- scan 356 -->

The clamour of *alis*
will go only to others like themselves —
how can it defeat those men?

Golden Tamil given by the Sangam,
the Bay of Bengal swelling,
the young lion’s roar rising everywhere —
an people who lived saying,
“One is the clan”;
a people who from ancient days
gave and received;
then came a crowd to eat and grow fat,
tied up the tuft,
and ruined the order.
One caste — the Tamil caste —
became many castes;
therefore it became weak!

Intrigue won,
fraud succeeded;
“There will be no fall for us now,”
sang the worthless,
and danced in frenzy.
In learning,
in education,
in government employment,
a section of people hid away places,
claimed all as their own,
and tormented others,
seizing their lives.
Because we hesitated to fight them,
we once lay inert
like sculpted statues.

<!-- scan 357 -->

Then a day came
like daylight pushing away walking darkness;
the evil planet moved away.
“Only reservation
can set aside a share
for those who have been pushed aside” —
thus, swimming against the current,
the Dravidian movement rose!
It set out to cure
the South’s stupor.

Like clear nectar impossible to find,
like sweet musical song,
like honey syrup mixed with millet flour,
a feast whose taste never cloys,
like moonlight showing direction
to people who had none —
the good fortune that came unasked,
a great jewel:
communal proportional representation —
reservation —
a boon for the life of the backward!

<!-- scan 358 -->

Shall we lose what has come?
Shall we fall into a treacherous net?
People who eat what is cooked
and babble whatever comes to their mouths
write and speak of “merit” and “ability,”
subtly mixing poison,
confusing the minds of the people.
Paavendhar asked,
“If the knot is loosened beautifully,
will anyone simply let it go?”
I ask the same!

If all are equal in God’s creation,
did he make a brain of gold
only for the high caste
and place it in their skulls?
What sits inside the heads
of all other castes?
Clay? Lime?

Valmiki and Vyasa,
who gave poetry in the northern tongue
like the Ganges —
were they not children
born from Brahma’s feet?
Who is Valluvar? Who is Kamban?
Who are Ilango Adigal and Ottakoothar?
Were they born from Brahma’s head?
No!
Yet they are Himalayas of the Tamil language!

<!-- scan 359 -->

In the forty-three years
of independence we have had,
what exactly has been torn apart
by the opportunities given
to “merit” and “ability”?
What has been stitched?
What is this great thing
that has been torn and stitched — what, what, what?
We have never asked
why ability is needed
or whether ability itself is unnecessary!

But no scripture permitted Ekalavya
to learn the art.
Because he might defeat Vijaya — Arjuna —
with the bow,
what justice was there
in demanding his thumb as an offering?
Shambuka, the Sudra, performed penance;
“what qualification has he?” they raged,
and cut off his head —
is that not a story in the history
of Lord Rama?
If anyone today asks
for a thumb or a head
as such an offering,
his hide will be stripped;
his corpse-log will burn in the cremation ground!

Do you feel like asking:
“What is this unprecedented heat
inside a presiding poem?”
What else should there be?
When the very principal itself is in danger,
is it right to lie like a rabbit or tortoise?
When a man who has fallen
a thousand feet into a pit —

<!-- scan 360 -->

is being lifted by the hand toward safety,
if worthless men try to roll
a heavy boulder onto his head,
we must kick them!
We must chase them until they run!
Death may come at six or at a hundred;
let whatever happens happen —
even if this government itself must go!

Are we people who cannot live without a crown?
Were we born as infants
already seated on a throne,
as Sita was said to be born with a bow?
Royal umbrella or principle —
if you ask which we want,
we shall say:
“If you want someone to sell principle to survive,
find another person.”
Government comes and goes;
it is not permanent.
The principle laid down by Ayya and Anna
will not go — it lives as permanence.
Therefore, whether in office or out,
we shall value self-respect as life
and always guard Tamil ethnic dignity.
When he saw creatures abusing
the goal they had embraced,
Periyar would say in anger,
“Get lost, onion!”
Paavendhar would say:
“In prosperous great Tamil Nadu,
if non-Tamils stretch out their tails,
what they will receive is a kick.”

<!-- scan 361 -->

If he saw poisonous speakers
and obscene writers,
our Anna would mock them:
“beauties of an unshaking tongue,
beauties of a dung-like prose style!”
Unable to contain his anger,
Ambedkar himself once thundered
that if necessary
even the Constitution should be burned!

No generation will again
see leaders like these.
So that the thoughts of those leaders
may triumph,
let us take our oath!
Let us lift our radiant faces!
However many hardships come,
let us stand against them!
Let us see victory!

## Translator notes

- The contents witness encloses the Tamil title in quotation marks; the canonical verified opening does not. The English displayed title follows the canonical witness.
- The “three letters” sequence depends on Tamil grapheme/word forms (`தமிழ்`, `பண்பு`, `அன்பு`, `காதல்`, `வீரம்`, `களம்`, `வெற்றி`, `அறிவு`, `அண்ணா`, `தி.மு.க.`). Those Tamil tokens are retained because English letter counts would fabricate a different device.
- Embedded Bharathidasan passages are translated from this anthology witness only; no outside poetic edition is imported.
- The Mandal Commission, communal proportional representation/reservation, Dravidian-movement history, Ekalavya and Shambuka passages are translated as Kalaignar’s source-framed political argument, not as a separate historical adjudication.
- `அலி` is a source-visible gendered polemical insult. The transliterated plural *alis* is retained rather than replacing it with a modern identity label or silently softening the rhetoric.
- The closing Periyar, Bharathidasan, Anna and Ambedkar quotations/recollections remain attributed as the poem presents them.

## Item 38 — “On Arignar Anna’s Path”

<!-- scan 362 -->

As part of the Three Great Celebrations,
at the Kalaignar Marriage Hall in Anna Arivalayam,
on the evening of 16 September 1994,
Leader Kalaignar presided over the poetry assembly
and sang this poem.

Greetings!

August 15 —
the birthday of freedom.
September 15 —
for the Tamil,
the birthday of breath.
September 17 —
for the Tamil,
the birthday of heat.
And because on that very day
self-respect too was born,
we were born;
yes — the Kazhagam was born.

Joining all these together,
to explain a principle strong as iron,
we hold the pearl-like festival —
the Three Great Celebrations.

<!-- scan 363 -->

At this celebration,
my greetings to the poets
who have come to sing in the assembly,
and to the great Tamil people
who have come to see it.

One day all the flowers
gathered together,
held a conference,
and passed a resolution:

“Poets!
From now on,
do not summon our names
as similes in your poems!”

Looking toward the earth,
pleading with her eyes,
the white moon made a request:

“Enough of placing me in melody
and singing of me.
Leave me free in the sky!”

The clouds assembled,
held a procession,
and carried a petition:

“Poets!
Do not any longer show clouds
as similes for the hair
of enchanting beauties!”

<!-- scan 364 -->

Deer and peacocks,
and many others,
staged a blockade
and issued a demand:

“Poets! How long
will you drag us in,
shut us inside the cage of poetry,
and enjoy yourselves?
We want freedom!”
They leapt and bounded as they spoke.

Everyone in our gathering of poets
stood astonished.
“Why this demand?
Why this struggle? This procession?”
we asked,
looking toward the antelope,
the forest peacock,
the moon in the sky,
and the many-coloured flower.
Their answer was:

“By singing our praise
and showing us as similes,
again and again,
you have bored even us!”

Deer and honey, peacock and cuckoo,
sky and sea, sun-ray and moon,
loft-pigeon, paired parrot,
mango, jackfruit and banana,
dancing creeper, singing waterfall,
agil, cloud, mountain and statue —
all of them now announced
that without their cooperation —

<!-- scan 365 -->

“Very well,
you tell us yourselves:
what name is there
that never grows tiresome?”
We stood and asked.

There is one name
that never grows stale!
Think of it —
a name untouched
by sourness or bitterness!
A name that lives forever
as literature!
A name that has become
an incomparable history!

You call us flowers;
we wilt.
That name never wilts;
it spreads fragrance forever!

You call us the moon;
we wane and become a crescent.
That name, know this,
gives cool light
without diminishing!

You call us clouds;
only when wind blows
do we dissolve into rain.
Find one name
that without any wind
pours a rain of compassion
and, as the central substance of your poem,
brings it greatness.

<!-- scan 366 -->

“That name is the precious name
Anna!” —
so the waterfall, the breeze,
the beautiful flower
and all the familiar things
rejoiced within
and cried out in one voice.

Only those who do not know him
need Anna introduced to them.
Yet every day we see
this story acted before us:
even those who do not know him at all
believe they can move through the country
only by pretending
to know him very well!

When flowers and all the other
objects of simile themselves
become distressed
and protest:
“Do not drag our names into everything
until you make them tiresome,”
what is the name
that never tires us?
The name engraved
in golden letters in history,
a name that removes weariness
and gives delight —
Anna, Anna, Anna —
is it not that very name?

Even filthy operators
utter Anna’s name —

<!-- scan 367 -->

three hundred times before every breath —
and say they will fashion weapons
from iron that has not even ripened!
Anna does not refuse them,
not even for the sake of argument.
Perhaps our Anna still believes
one must possess a heart
that can bear anything!

They say Periyar’s ideas
were bitter medicine;
Perarignar Anna placed that medicine
inside sweetness
and gave it gently.

“Break Vinayaka,” Periyar declared,
and broke him!
“Do not needlessly break Vinayaka;
do not break coconuts for Vinayaka either” —
Anna stood holding this Kazhagam principle
like a lamp in his hand.

Yet those who today issue
Vinayaka Chaturthi greetings
make the skies shake
by shouting the name
of that brilliant Anna!

Ministers eat ritual earth,
roll inside Mariamman temples,
wrap themselves in neem-leaf garments,
celebrate goddess festivals —

<!-- scan 368 -->

and then say,
“We are driving out superstition.”
Instead of moving away in shame,
“Shame! Shame!”
the office-seeking chameleons
who go nearby to perform *paada puja*
are themselves called
lamps of rationalism!

Using Anna’s name,
they untie the knot so beautifully —
is this justice?

From childhood Anna lived
for many years in Kanchi.
History says
he never went to see
the elder of the monastery
in the neighbouring street.
Today, a visit to see the junior pontiff
is printed in eight-column headlines!
Anna, look at the suffering undergone
by this “evolutionary growth” of rationalism!

Once we heard Navalar explain
the difference
between those who merely saw Anna
and those who lived and moved with him.
Today we hear that very man
lean toward the side of those who only saw him
and sing a prefatory hymn!

Those who yesterday performed penance
longing for their face
to appear beside mine in a photograph,
today, Anna,
when they see a photograph
in which I sit beside you —

<!-- scan 369 -->

try to cover it over with ink!
All these petty men
want to rule the state.
Ah, Anna —
now I understand the meaning of your smile!

“You need a broad heart, little brother,
a cool heart that can forgive
even ungrateful sinners,”
you tell me.
Yes, Anna —
is that not why
I asked to borrow your heart
and received it?

Rationalism, equality, socialism,
ethnic consciousness, social justice,
love of language —
because they came to rule
in the name of all these,
new commentaries are now offered for each!
Those who received office from them,
and those who received many kinds of favour,
nod their heads:
“Yes... yes.”

If the demon Bakasura of Bharata
had teeth two *kathams* long
and gaps between them three *kathams* wide,
the temple-priest’s disciple
who beats the drum saying “yes, yes”
to Padavettamman —
what difference is there
between him and these people?

You stand as a statue,
and smile.
They slash principle with a killing sword;
you endure it.
Why? We understand that too, Anna:
there are younger brothers
who have sworn to labour without tiring
on Anna’s path —

<!-- scan 370 -->

standing as the flame of Self-Respect.
They will not go astray.
To the common people
who are deceived by counterfeits,
they will make truths understood
and falsehoods rejected.
You trust this, Anna.
So that your trust will not be wasted,
we shall walk on, Anna!

<!-- scan 371 -->

<!-- Source-visible blank/show-through verso retained as item provenance; no edition text. -->

## Translator notes

- The canonical and contents title witnesses are exact. `அறிஞர் அண்ணா` is rendered as **Arignar Anna**, retaining the familiar Tamil honorific rather than flattening it into a generic “scholar.”
- The poem’s opening is the 1994 Three Great Celebrations frame: Independence Day, Anna’s birthday, Periyar’s birthday and the birth of the Kazhagam are translated as the source presents them.
- The flowers/moon/clouds/animals “strike” against poetic simile is retained as an extended comic conceit whose resolution is that Anna’s name alone never grows stale.
- Periyar/Vinayaka, temple ritual, Kanchi monastery, Navalar and photograph passages are translated as Kalaignar’s polemical source claims; no outside political reconciliation is inserted.
- The line about having borrowed Anna’s heart deliberately echoes the anthology’s earlier elegiac language and is retained as an internal literary recurrence.
- Scan 371 is an item-owned blank/show-through verso. Its provenance marker is retained even though it has no edition text; structural scans 372–373 belong separately to the `கண்ணீர்த் துளிகள்` anthology divider and are not part of this item.

<!-- scan 372 -->

## கண்ணீர்த் துளிகள் — Tear-Drops

<!-- anthology divider: Tamil = கண்ணீர்த் துளிகள்; contents witness = கண்ணீர்க் கவிதை -->

<!-- scan 373 -->

## Item 39 — Panneerselvam!

<!-- scan 374 -->

In 1940, when Dravidian commander
A. T. Panneerselvam travelled by aircraft to London
to serve as adviser to the Secretary of State for India,
the aircraft fell into the Oman Sea and sank.
This is the tear-soaked portrait
that Kalaignar, while still young,
wrote about the departed Panneerselvam
in *Murasoli*, then published from Tiruvarur.

Those who say the people of the world
sit in a dark room with both eyes closed
and roll about —
let ignorance sink!
To make that truth known,
Periyar’s drum thundered.
Along that path came
a man... a beloved one... a comrade,
our precious Selvam...
a warrior of the knowledge movement.

When, in the good light of the sun,
the dusk says, “My time has come,”
and withdraws —
if, over there,
the morning sun that rose in the east
suddenly falls into the sea,
will not the world be shaken?
Will it not wither and waste away?

Just as this land was stepping forward
to cast off darkness and gain light,
a death-cloud of delusion
hid the young sunbeam!

O scion of the clan
with long shoulders that can lift mountains!
Lion-bull who came out of the cave
with a killing sword
to tear out the entrails
of the pack of little foxes!

<!-- scan 375 -->

O mountain-shouldered one!
O hill of ruby!
Your speech was a segment of ripe fruit;
your glance, a sweep of cool mist!
You have gone —
but will your fame go with you?

Panneerselvam!
Where is your milk-soft face?
Where is your Pandyan moustache?
Have the desolate waves
of the hunger-maddened sea
finally grown still after eating you?

“Let them eat... let them eat and rejoice...
let the people of the Dravidian land
think it so forever” —
where have you hidden yourself?

Born in wealth
and raised in wealth,
how did you learn
the character of the worker?

O noble one!
The tears we shed and shed
would conquer a thousand Oman seas!

Unblemished golden Dravidian land!
For all the people drunk on the liquor of old custom
to wake and rise —
to see that day
will be the offering to your work!

Though opposition circles us
and rages like a cyclone,
we have set out declaring:
“Victory is ours!”

We are not paupers —
we are descendants
of the guardian of Thanjavur!
Even if we must fall,
we shall sing of your deeds
and fall!

## Translator notes

- The canonical title compounds the name as `பன்னீர்ச்செல்வமே!`; the contents witness separates it as `பன்னீர்ச் செல்வமே` and omits the exclamation. The English title follows the canonical personal-name reading.
- The contextual note and the Oman Sea reference are translated exactly as this anthology witness frames A. T. Panneerselvam’s 1940 death; Phase 4 does not replace that source note with an outside biographical account.
- The poem’s young-sun, death-cloud, lion, mountain and sea imagery is intentionally heightened elegy and is retained rather than reduced to prose memorial description.
- `திருவிடத்தார்` / Dravidian-land rhetoric and the Periyar knowledge-movement frame are translated as Kalaignar’s youthful source voice.

## Item 40 — Mother Art’s Foremost Son!

<!-- scan 376 -->

A gaze cool as mist,
speech sweet as fruit,
a talent all his own —
a musician-poet
who gave beauty with sweetness!
Whenever people called,
he came to serve their laughter —
a good volunteer!
A Tamil who made Tamil flourish!
A generous giver
whose rain-like hand protected conferences!
A Tamil bull who held
that the one great fortune
was the fortune of labour!
Sole king of the world of art —
his heart itself an ocean —
what cruelty is this,
that leaving us shaken,
he has entered a sleep
from which he will not rise?

“Laughter alone is medicine
for human life —
mix it with the honey of thought
and drink it every day;
while I am here,
that feast shall never run short!”
So he spoke and lived —
alas,
he has fallen into consuming fire!

<!-- scan 377 -->

He made the poor laugh;
he made cowards stand upright;
and leaving all of us crying aloud,
he has gone to the world of death!

In cinema he made the fragrance of knowledge spread,
and stood as the nation’s strong right arm —
our **Nalla Thambi**, our “Good Brother”!

When a crippled thought rose
saying, “Let great wealth be hoarded,”
he broke the point of that thought
with **Panam** — *Money* —
the poet who gave us that work!

He turned the widow
into a **Manamagal** — a bride —
and let the birds of love
fly into a new sky,
a warrior of action!

Amid the stones thrown by villains
and the blows of words they dealt,
he kept explaining his principles
through *villuppaattu*,
in a way that made
even bullying troublemakers bare their teeth and laugh —
what a master he was!

Old Nandan goes
to the temple where dancing is done;
new **Kindan** goes
to the shrine where knowledge is sought.
We rejoiced that the Othiyamilar-hearted,
seeing you,
ran away.
Today,
having lost you,
our hearts melt!

Where is the sight
of you storing a smile
at the edge of your lips,
welcoming us with flower-like eyes?
Where is that small smiling face,
its lips red with beauty,
chewing a green leaf
like a parrot’s wing,
with fresh shell-lime and betel nut?

Did you become fire
so that Tamils might float on water,
O leader of art?

<!-- scan 378 -->

In this vast great Tamil land,
where waterways cut through the soil,
O king of art who lived with distinction —
if we had known a storm would blow
and uproot you,
countless crores of hands
would have held back that storm!

“I shall trip opposition
with a young smile
and sit beside my leader Anna,”
you said and rose.
Before that could happen,
you fell into death’s lap!

My life! My nectar!
What thought was in you
when you gave up life?
Did you say,
“I piled up a mountain of gold;
today I lie at the foot
of a mountain of dust,”
and then climb instead
to the summit of the mountain of fame?

New jewel! Pearl of fame!
You travelled on an artistic journey
to **Rusiya**, where common ownership was sprouting.
Did you conceive another journey like that
and set out on this great journey,
leaving a great blame upon us?

O art!
O imagination!
O beauty!
O knowledge!
O love!
O virtue!
Who is there now,
a good man like you?

You are there —
and your fame is there,
forever in the hearts of Tamils!

## Translator notes

- The canonical title separates `தலைச் செல்வன்!`; the contents witness compounds `தலைச்செல்வன்` and omits the terminal exclamation. **Mother Art’s Foremost Son!** follows the canonical elegiac sense.
- The poem is Kalaignar’s elegy for Kalaivanar N. S. Krishnan. Film/work titles embedded as puns — `நல்ல தம்பி`, `பணம்`, `மணமகள்`, and `கிந்தன்` — are kept recognisable in bold with brief English glosses rather than dissolved into ordinary prose.
- `வில்லடிப் பாட்டு` is retained as *villuppaattu*, the bow-song performance form used for reformist public explanation.
- `ஒதியமிலார்` is a final-cleared source form retained conservatively, consistent with earlier anthology translations.
- `ருசியா` is translated as the source-visible “Rusiya”/Russia reference in the poem’s common-ownership art-tour memory; no outside biographical wording is substituted.

## Item 41 — We Move as Your Shadow!

<!-- scan 379 -->

Written on the seventy-fifth anniversary remembrance of Anna.

★

Anna!
Our very life!
Our feeling!
King of our people's dignity!
Form of uprising!
Lovely grove
where thoughts bloom!
Heartbeat of your dear younger brothers,
bars of steel!

Seventy-five is born for you — yet
why did the count end at sixty?

Anna!
In beautiful Tamil speech
you surpassed waterfalls!
And that rain of English words —
a ceremonial fan before your power!
You heaped up sugar-candy letters like a mountain;
we turned into ants
and tasted them!

<!-- scan 380 -->

Seventy-five is born for you — yet
why did the count end at sixty?

Anna!
Magnetism in your eyes!
Calm in your speech!
Valour in your action!
Wisdom in your method!
Affection was your sibling;
compassion, its reprint!
A heart that could bear anything!
Sacred treasure found by the Tamils!

Seventy-five is born for you — yet
why did the count end at sixty?

Anna!
Before you came,
Tamils had a history — but
only after you came
did the Tamil understand that history in its true form!
Tamils had literature!
Yet only after you came
did you set it in motion
so this world would know it!
The Tamil people were a race with a distinct culture;
at a time when the Tamil people themselves did not know it —

<!-- scan 381 -->

Leader! Because you arose,
the Tamil became Tamil!

Seventy-five is born for you — yet
why did the count end at sixty?

Anna!
You were Father Periyar's commander!
The many kinds of harm
wrought by strange men —
you called them a potful of poison!
You were a scholar!
A teacher of knowledge!
You declared that nurturing the class division
that defines one as poor and another as rich
was evil,
and spoke standing on the path of equality!

Seventy-five is born for you — yet
why did the count end at sixty?

For this question that rises,
no answer alone has been found!
The grief within us
has not subsided even on this day.
Yet in the thought
that you have never gone away,
we move as your shadow!

## Translator notes

- The canonical title carries a terminal `!`; the contents witness does not. The English title follows the canonical witness.
- The repeated “seventy-five / sixty” question is retained as the poem's structural refrain rather than reconciled to outside biographical age-counting.
- `ஆலவட்டம்` is rendered as a ceremonial fan, preserving the image of English verbal fluency attending Anna's larger oratorical power.
- `மொந்தை விஷம்` is kept as the source's deliberately coarse “potful of poison” image; no outside textual variant is substituted.

## Item 42 — Long Live Jeeva

<!-- scan 382 -->

The good Tamil that makes our name fragrant is fragrant;
the name Nanjil is fragrant;
Boothapandi, fragrant with song, is fragrant;
and the mouth that sings the fame
of the noble Jeeva
is fragrant with honey!

At an age when the mist
of restless youth
had not yet cleared,
he set his heart on public life
and placed the nation first!
Though the garden was full of flowers,
like a bee that flew far away,
he said, “Service alone is my life,”
and flew throughout the land.

A few days he would stay at home;
many days he would languish
inside a prison cage!
On the stage he would beat the drum;
through the summer of life
he would keep travelling!
He would give us the flower *Thamarai*;
from it we would drink
Tamil nectar!

<!-- scan 383 -->

He would speak with arm raised —
and there
we would see a fierce storm blow!
He would call, “Come!” —
and at that yearning
we would thrill!

Where are those mountain-like shoulders?
Where is that strength that never diminished?
Where is that noble character?
Where is that nature that laboured for the nation?
Where is that face filled with courage?
Where is the light poured by those eyes?

That striped tiger
whose very blood
breathed the fragrance of revolution,
that Tamil poet
firm in principle —
has cruel death robbed him away
and rejoiced?
So the people of the land ask
and cry out in anguish!

Long live Jeeva!
Long live forever!

*Nam Nadu*, 21.3.1963

## Translator notes

- The canonical title has no terminal `!`; the contents witness does. The English title follows the canonical opening.
- The opening repeatedly uses `மணம்` / fragrance across Tamil, Nanjil, Boothapandi and the mouth praising Jeeva. English retains the repetition rather than replacing it with varied synonyms.
- `தாமரை` is both “lotus” and the title *Thamarai*; the English keeps the publication/title form in italics while retaining the flower metaphor.
- The final `வரிப்புலி` is rendered literally as “striped tiger,” preserving the revolutionary-animal image.

## Item 43 — The Fallen Hero

<!-- scan 384 -->

Sung in mourning when
Thoothukudi hero K. V. K. Sami was murdered.

★

“Joy in the grove, joy in the breeze — and then
I shall whirl my sword
to guard my own land!”
So roared the young bull.
While he was still
a flower of youth not yet opened,
he stood like a bright lamp,
raising a radiant face and saying,
“I shall scorch the enemy!”

A flower-maiden waited
to reach out, embrace and sport
with that still-unembraced body.
They called him a pearl
of the working people's clan,
a fruit rich with beauty and substance;
they called her
a flower wet with dew.

When would the two
become one?

“Put the drawn sword
back in its sheath for a while, beloved,”
she said,
blocking the lover who raised his sword
and rushed toward the field
where the war-drum roared.

“Do not think, woman,
that you can sacrifice me,
who leap toward liberation,
to your love!
I shall stand in the ranks
that have risen to give life
back to my sacred Tamil land,
ruined and mocked as low
by low men.
I want neither jeweled mansions
nor honeyed speech,” he said.

<!-- scan 385 -->

“Have you come to hate me altogether?” she asked.

“I will not say
I have no attachment to you.
But until the day comes
when there is no snake-pit
in the garden of the southern Tamils,
the lesson of love
is one I cannot study,” he said.
She stood trembling.

“Then let both of us
stand together on the battlefield.
Even if we are extinguished,
let the same fate come to us both.
What answer have you to that, beloved?”

“Lovers sometimes
follow such a course!
But... it will not suit me, my gold!
When I enter the field
and strike the advancing enemy,
my heart may hesitate for a moment,
wondering what has happened
to the slender woman
who entered my heart.
Within that moment
my army may falter.
No, woman!
Bid me farewell, my dear!”

“You refuse only marriage;
there is the flower-bed,
like a leaf spread out for a meal —
come there to this woman!”

“Let the milk-rice
remain in the pot.
Though I am hungry now,
I will not ruin you and go!
Give me leave without delay,
