my swan!”

“Is even the invitation
of mango-fruit cheeks forbidden?”

“It is forbidden.
I go carrying an army;
even if I return only as a corpse,
you must still be able to marry.
My love —

<!-- scan 386 -->

if I leave you merely ‘tasted’
and go away,
what profit is there for me?
The great loss would be yours!”

“No! One kiss...!”

“No...! After I return,
a whole shower of them!”

“Do not leave me crying, beloved...”

“We shall be happy all our days!
Put away your worry!”

“When you have won victory in battle,
give me leave to rejoice
that I too have won victory in love!”

“After we change the fate
of the people of the South,
who show a thousand colours
with every movement
yet wander without cohesion,
then, beautiful peacock,
there will be no bar
to our exchanging marriage garlands — know that!”

“Win and return quickly!
I shall take pearls
born from the roaring sea
and string a garland for you,
my lord of the southern Pandya land!
Until you return
I shall remain right here;
otherwise this very seashore
shall be the grave made for me!”

“Show me that pearl of a smile
that was never born in a shell.
I shall return,
singing the goodness
of refined Tamil
that lives within my thought,”
he said and parted,
leaving the maiden alone
on the white sand of the shore.

<!-- scan 387 -->

The great sea gives us pearls
through its beautiful shells;
its dancing waves give us salt
and raise us up.
Would anyone hate the sea
because its water is useless
even for rinsing the mouth?

So too,
the spear-eyed maiden
did not hate the noble great warrior
who laboured for the country
merely because
he had not embraced her at once.

The lion who had taken leave
went to see the leader,
spoke with him,
understood the situation,
and stood as commander
of the war being waged for justice.

At the head of a great host
the good warrior called everyone
to join against the corrupt men
who ruled the land.
Every street became a camp;
in the southern Pandya region
there was no quarter
without a battle-post.
Only the day for war
remained to be fixed.

“No one is equal to his shoulders!
Show our swords the way!”
So people gathered
in group after group.

News spread in every direction
that the Tamil people,
who had stood from the earliest age
upon this ancient earth,
the people of the South,
had risen.
A flood of ethnic awakening
made all directions stare in astonishment.
Battalions of youth,
ranks of working people —
all joined the formation
and sent word
to the commander-in-chief:
“Name the day!”

The leader held as his rule
the Kural's law:
act only after knowing
one's own strength
and the enemy's strength.

<!-- scan 388 -->

“The war will begin soon;
be patient,” he replied.
He rejoiced,
knowing only a few days remained
before the army would march.
Who knew that before then
such a cruel wonder would arise?...

Near the camp,
he stood alone,
absorbed in thought
about how the army should be led.
Then six or seven men
with the nature of jackals
ran forward to spring upon him.

“Othiyamilar!
Creatures useless for anything!
What do you want?” he asked.

“We want your life,” they said.

“I am already going to the field
to give this body
to dogs and jackals,
to vultures and hawks
that only wear human shape.
If before then
you have a craving for carrion,
take it away!
But know one thing:
you may be able
to take the life of this one man;
you cannot destroy
the feelings I have raised,
or the true warriors!” he said.

Those snake-men
without ears to hear Tamil
circled him with a *pada-pada* rush.
They hacked away
the warrior's limbs
like hanging roots of a banyan tree.
The fingers of the hand
raised to block the sword
fell to the ground
*pol-pol*, one after another.
As though setting explosives
to split a mountain,
they dared even
to split the great warrior's skull.
His head shattered;
he collapsed upon the earth.
On the lips his cousin-longed-for bride
had yearned to kiss —

<!-- scan 389 -->

on that chosen jewel-like chest —
the sweep of the sickle!
Before she could bury
her flower-face there,
a flood of blood.
The killers who came
for his life ran away;
the noble man gave
his last breath too
to the country
and lay down
in a cradle from which
sleep would never lift.

The beautiful woman,
standing by the sea-wave
with her pearl garland,
heard that the picture
had been shattered.
Those great sinners
had cut the garland itself!
The lion who was to lead the army —
limbs severed,
writhing and fallen
through betrayal —

“Alas!
Did I live all these days
only to hear this?
The mouth that told me,
‘Stand upon the white sand, woman!
I shall hurry back and marry you,’
has closed?
Have those legs
of majestic stride gone weak?
I lived believing
that those eyes which poured fire
would pour coolness
when you married me,
my fragrant honey!
They say those *chandala* creatures
have cast you
into the mire of death!
Why should I live any longer?” she cried.

She tore off the pearl garland
and hurled it into the sea.
Then she too,
remembering her beloved,
went down
to the bottom of the deep ocean.

## Translator notes

- The title witnesses are exact. **The Fallen Hero** renders `மறைந்த மாவீரன்` as an elegiac title without adding an outside biographical designation.
- Item 43 is translated as the anthology's dramatic elegiac narrative. Its lover-warrior dialogue, sexual/food metaphors and assassination sequence are not softened or converted into a historical fact-check layer.
- `எச்சில்படுத்தி` uses the source's food/tasting metaphor for sexually using and leaving the woman; the English “merely ‘tasted’” keeps that intentionally uncomfortable register.
- The source-visible `ஓதிய மிலார்` is retained conservatively as **Othiyamilar** rather than normalized from another edition. `முடை` is rendered contextually as carrion.
- The onomatopoeic `படபட` and `பொல பொல` are retained as sound effects in transliterated form.
- Near the close the canonical Tamil's lineation separates `சண்டாளப்` and `பிறவிகள்`; semantically they form the caste-marked abusive phrase `சண்டாளப் பிறவிகள்`. English renders it as “*chandala* creatures” and records the source's derogatory rhetoric without endorsing or silently sanitizing it. No Tamil source layer was changed.

## Item 44 — My Dear Friend! Why Did You Leave?

<!-- scan 390 -->

Written by Kalaignar when Kaviyarasu Kannadasan died.

★

My dear friend!
You who gave comfort to the heart
with poems of young summer —
why did you stop
your heart's own beat?

You blew like a breeze;
you also scorched
like fire inside my heart!
Even then,
our friendship shone
like the inseparable *anril* pair;
it never became
an extinguished lamp, my friend!

Kannadasa!
Dear one who sweetens
all my thoughts!
You were a flower-garden of poetry —
and the earthquake called Time
has shattered you
and levelled you to the ground!

You were a child
who stretched out your arms
and leapt toward every side
where someone held out a hand to caress you.
Did you leap in just that way
into the lap of the woman called the grave,
to drink the milk of peace
and fall asleep?

You were the cuckoo
who rejoiced singing
music for the movement —

<!-- scan 391 -->

once, they drugged you into a daze
and parted you away.
However many arrows of attack
you loosed afterward,
this heart of mine that bore them
was still your mother!

Even when you scolded,
because you scolded in Tamil,
I accepted it
like a sweet *pittu*.
Sweet poetic Tamil!
Why have you left me behind
in the midst of this separation?

Ah!
In that field of youth
we planted seedlings called love!
Believing our friendship
a crop for a thousand ages,
we dreamed a thousand crores of dreams!
Someone came for the harvest —
and cut away only you!

Your mind was never steady;
yet your fame became permanent!
To bring about this wonder,
sweet Mother Tamil
stood beside you!

My friend!
My dear companion!
You who sang
so many lullabies —
Mother Nature has now
rocked you and put you to sleep!
You who received
so much praise —
is Mother Nature's own caress
sweeter to you now?

You forgot me!
You hid yourself from us!
Yet because we cannot forget you,
you have filled every corner
of our hearts!

## Translator notes

- The title witnesses are exact. `பிரிந்தாய்` is rendered as “did you leave?” to retain the elegy's direct spoken grief without adding an explicit object absent from the Tamil.
- `அன்றில்` invokes the traditional image of inseparable paired birds; the Tamil term *anril* is retained rather than replaced with a generic modern species name.
- `பிட்டு` is kept as *pittu*, preserving the source's wordplay between a scolding received as Tamil and a sweet morsel.
- The movement-song / later-arrow passage is translated as the poem's autobiographical friendship rhetoric; Phase 4 does not convert it into an external political-history gloss.

<!-- scan 392 -->

## மலர்த் தோட்டம் — Flower Garden

<!-- anthology divider: Tamil = மலர்த் தோட்டம் -->

<!-- scan 393 -->

## Item 45 — Today Is Your Birthday

<!-- scan 394 -->

Written by Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi
on Kamaraj's birthday, 15.7.76.

Today is your birthday —
a festival everywhere
for that mountain of principle!
Born in Virudhai,
you grew into a warrior
and stood as a leader
who filled history!

The cruelty of prison,
torture itself —
you accepted them
with a smiling face,
a man of sacrifice.
We had a thousand
clashes of opinion — yet
shall we ever forget
your imperishable service?

Sacred one who came
to make Tamil soil fragrant!
Embodiment of public service,
sweet as nectar!
Thought dwells through your very being — and in the heart,
for as long as thought remains,
it remains there to labour!

<!-- scan 395 -->

Khadi clothed your body;
your arms embraced
the crying poor.
You would launch harsh words
at political parties —
yet if hardship came
to a party leader,
you would tremble with concern!

Your tears
at Periyar's grave!
Your tears too
when Perarignar and Mutharignar passed away!
And when I lost
the mother who gave me birth,
you came like kith and kin
and shed your tears!

Great man!
Did not our tears for you
flow like the swelling
of a mighty river?

Your life was not
the life of a private man;
it was a chapter
in the history of self-respect.
From Kumari to the Himalaya
you made your flag fly
and inscribed the fame
of splendid Tamil Nadu.

“Man of virtue!
Tender shoot of a noble lineage!” —
so our Anna,
bridegroom of culture,
called you.
The Father of Rationalism,
calling you a *pachai Tamilan*,
a true Tamil,
affectionately kissed
the crown of your head.
Did not this sea-girt nation
fold its hands before you
as the “Black Gandhi”?

Toward the edge of your life
you showed affection toward us too!
You established your very life
as a lesson for everyone!

Today is your birthday;
forever it is a special day!

— Published in *Murasoli*, 15.7.76.

## Translator notes

- The canonical title uses `உன்றன்`; the contents witness uses `உன்`. Both mean “your” in this context, so the English title is the same while the Tamil lexical variant remains explicit in metadata.
- `விருதையில்` is retained conservatively as **Virudhai**, the source's place form, rather than silently harmonized to an outside modern place-name spelling.
- The verified source repeats the unusual form `கருத்திருக்கும்` in `கருத்திருக்கும் உன்றன் உடல் ... நெஞ்சில் / கருத்திருக்கும்...`. English preserves the repeated “thought” sense rather than silently emending the Tamil to a different-looking word or imposing an unsupported death/body interpretation.
- `பேரறிஞர்` and `மூதறிஞர்` are retained as the source's honorific identities **Perarignar** and **Mutharignar** rather than silently replacing them with outside names.
- `பச்சைத் தமிழன்` is retained once as *pachai Tamilan* with an immediate contextual gloss, and `கருப்புக் காந்தி` is translated directly as “Black Gandhi,” preserving the poem's source epithets.

## Item 46 — There Is No One Day Called His Birthday!

<!-- scan 396 -->

There is no one day
called his birthday!

He is the king of hearts
of this southern-tip Tamil Nadu.
Our Anna — lord of a golden age —
who surpassed even sweet jaggery
and flowers opening their petals
with the savour of his words
and the fragrant perfume
of his writing!

Even the serpents that come hissing,
setting decaying ideas
like venomous fangs,
he makes coil themselves,
with the heart that bears anything
serving as his snake-charmer's flute —
such a great-hearted man!

For Anna, whose name shall not fade,
archanai after archanai is offered every day!

In restraint he is like a tortoise;
he endures as though mute!
But if some evil comes upon the Tamils,
he leaps up like fire!

Like a hen brooding over her clutch—

<!-- scan 397 -->

he, for all of us—
the very way we marshal our ranks today
is surely the training he gave us!

From coral-red lips
what spills are pearl-words!
In eyes where anger does not dwell
what gleams is diamond-light!

There is no one day called his birthday:
every day
that nectar-Tamil is born
is his birthday!

## Translator notes

- The canonical title reads `என` (“called / as”), while the contents witness has `ஏன்` (“why”); the English follows the canonical opening and does not combine the two witnesses.
- `அர்ச்சனைகள்` is kept through the religious image of repeated *archanai* offerings because the poem deliberately turns everyday remembrance of Anna into a ritual metaphor.
- Across the scan-396→397 join, the final-cleared Tamil has the source-visible sequence `அடைகாக்கும் கோழி போல / அவனைத்தான் நம்மையெல்லாம்-`. The English preserves the broken rhetorical syntax rather than silently changing the Tamil to a smoother presumed reading.

## Item 47 — Precious Remedy! Anbazhaga, Beloved Sibling!

<!-- scan 398 -->

My sibling,
it is your sixtieth-jubilee!
A festival for our family —
by the fragrance of Tamil on your tongue
the Kazhagam garden flourishes;
by the power of your speech
all treacherous enmity takes flight;
your army of constructive work guards the Kazhagam
and awakens Tamils from their long sleep!

You are a radiant gem,
a Tamil bell that rings without rest!

Could a pit be allowed
in the life of the Tamils? —
your heart boiled and rose at the thought;
your speech became a new flood!
The breath of a race that acts with valour!
The sweep of a sword that brings forth victory!

Rare Tamil literatures,
arrayed as *akam* and *puram*,
drip honey;
a gathering of poets
longs to drink it from you!

If some little fox-like men

<!-- scan 399 -->

dare to raise some petty argument,
the Professor's sharp spear will split it!
Even an incurable illness will be cured,
and the Tamil people will survive!

We are Periyar's children;
we are Perarignar Anna's younger brothers;
we are the inseparable two-coloured flag —
while this great family leaps
in an ocean of great joy,
precious remedy!
Anbazhaga, beloved sibling!

As you complete sixty in this jubilee,
may you live for many years more,
to see celebration after celebration
one upon another!

## Translator notes

- The canonical title's `அன்பழக` is preserved as the direct address **Anbazhaga**, carrying the personal-name/affection resonance of the source. The contents witness instead reads `அன்புறவு`; the two are not merged.
- `அகம் / புறம்` remains *akam / puram* because the line invokes the classical Tamil interior/exterior literary categories rather than merely “inside/outside.”
- `இருவண்ணக் கொடி` is the movement's “two-coloured flag” image exactly as the poem deploys it; the translation does not expand it into a historical explanation inside the verse.

## Item 48 — Rationalist Pandianar!

<!-- scan 400 -->

Our dear Pandianar!
Was *soundaram* — beauty — only in your body?
Your heart too was beautiful!
Firmness itself was your tiger-like strength;
your bearing had a grandeur like Panneerselvam's —
so Anna Alagiri would often say
of Pattiveeranpatti Pandianar;
I too was among those
who savoured those words from close by.

I, Anbil and Nagore Hanifa
rejoiced in the hospitality of Pattiveeranpatti,
a feast where honey and milk seemed blended.
We ate and delighted
in fruits of rationalism.

The next morning,
you arranged for us to be shown and taught
all about the fragrant cardamom,
vegetables and flowers
in the hillside estate;
it remains in memory even now!

P. T. Rajan, who recreated Ayyappan,
and Soundarapandian, who said
“*Poyyappa kadavul*” — “false, father, is god” —
lived as friends,
their hearts one within the other
like ghee within butter —
what a quality that was!

One day at Arur,
at a meeting we young Dravidians conducted,
you took part;
the speech you gave there
was worth a thousand pieces of gold!

<!-- scan 401 -->

“In the name of the Self-Respect Movement,
whatever Periyar says,
I shall accept as command;
let us hold the coral-jubilee conference
in ancient Madurai,”
you said!

That one event conducted by the DMK
under the name **Pandian Nagar**
is proof that this is a movement
that does not forget to turn back
and look at history!

When a splendid bridge was built
at a cost of eighty lakh
in Cheranmahadevi,
the constituency of Pandian,
who had served as Assembly Speaker,
I gave it the name
of sweet-Tamil Soundarapandian.

To the tooth-baring crowd
that fixes its principles
on whichever side comes to power,
stands worshipfully enslaved to the ruling party,
your words would have delivered
thousands upon thousands of whip-lashes,
Pandianar!

Those Mahamagam men
who chant the Hari-name mantra,
smearing it over as “evolutionary development,”
now unveil the handsome sacred form
of the commander of the rationalist movement
who lived like a lion —
what “appropriateness” indeed!

At the Chengai Self-Respect Conference
that Ayya conducted in 1929,
the far-seeing resolution
that women too must have equal rights in property
was adopted under your leadership;
remembering that,
we duly made it law
under the DMK government.

Not only Nadars —
the people of the land themselves
praise you and honour you.

<!-- scan 402 -->

Only when caste is abolished
can society advance —
never for even an atom of time forgetting
the Tirukkural of Valluvar,
the primal son;
O joint of sweet sugarcane!
O unwavering lion-bull!
May you live forever!

## Translator notes

- `சவுந்தரம் / சவுந்தர்யம்` is allowed to carry both the name-sound and the ordinary sense “beauty”; the English exposes that wordplay rather than replacing it with a flat adjective.
- `அய்யப்பனை ... / பொய்யப்பா கடவுள்` is a source polemical wordplay. `Poyyappa kadavul` is retained before a compact gloss so the sound-play with Ayyappan is not erased.
- The poem's claims about personalities, conferences, statutes and political conduct are translated as source claims; this English layer is not a historical fact-check.

## Item 49 — The Scales of Justice

<!-- scan 403 -->

You will put medicine on a wound
and seek a way to make it heal!

You will understand
that a widow too has a heart within her,
like the pupil within the eye!

“My wife has died;
I want another marriage,”
you will say!

Then, if you yourself
reach the edge of death,
must your wife sacrifice
her love as an offering?

## Source note

Published in **1945**, according to the verified source page record.

## Translator notes

- The source lineation splits `கைம்பெண்` (“widow”) across lines as `கைம் / பெண்`; the English restores the lexical unit only at translation level and does not alter the Tamil source layer.
- The final question is retained as a direct challenge to the gendered double standard around remarriage rather than softened into explanatory prose.

## Item 50 — Would They Accept?

<!-- scan 404 -->

Dharman and Bhima
and the other three were not enough —
Panchali loved Karna too.

Ah!
Lakshmi and Parvati,
and Kalaimagal, lady of music,
did not dry the ocean up
because chastity had been lost!

Ahalya,
woman of a rishi lineage,
joined herself to Indra;
Tara,
the ascetic woman,
desired the Moon.

All these Puranas
the pandits of this land
fashioned and gave us
in Muttamil —
because their spines were bent!

Women who speak the heroic words,
“Even if a mountain comes against us,
we shall not let our chastity crumble” —
if all the women named above are gods,
would people with self-respect
accept what you say?

## Source note

Published in **1945**, according to the verified source page record.

## Translator notes

- The mythological and sexual claims are translated as Kalaignar's source polemic, not harmonised with outside epic/Puranic traditions.
- `முத்தமிழ்` is retained as **Muttamil**, the threefold Tamil conception, because a generic “Tamil” would lose the source's literary-cultural charge.
- The unusual `கடலைதனை வறுக்கவில்லை / கற்பிழந்த காரணத்தால்` construction is kept close to the source rather than supplied with an explanatory mythology absent from the Tamil.

## Item 51 — Know It as a Storm!

<!-- scan 405 -->

Eripathar slew his enemies
with the battle-axe!
Mother, father,
wife and children were hacked down
by Kotpuli!
And you call Saivism
the ground on which love stands!
In the killing-field,
Jains died through the prison of caste!
You self-styled true devotees,
raving in swollen pride!
The mythology you nurtured
to snare us in its net—
we shall smash it against the charnel ground
and bring it down in an instant!
We are not a breeze
entering a garden of flowers!
Know us as a storm!

## Source publication note

Published in **1945**.

## Translator note

The poem invokes violent episodes and sectarian polemic from Saivite hagiographic tradition as Kalaignar's source argument. The English translates that rhetoric as written rather than reconciling it with devotional accounts.

## Item 52 — Have You Heard?

<!-- scan 406 -->

Have you heard?

“Landlord of three hundred,
four hundred *veli* of land—
has even one poor person
risen because of you?”
My comrade of this land,
who asks this at every hour!
I welcome and honour
the question from your stout heart;
I grow into a tree
the moment your question rises!
A thousand *veli* of land
belong to stone in the temple, friend!
Yet not even a blade of grass
is of use to the poor,
who go on lamenting!
Through clenched teeth, one word—
O hero of Bharata,
have you ever heard it?

## Source publication note

Published in **1945**.

## Translator note

The canonical title `கேட்டுண்டோ?` is deliberately retained as distinct from the source-body closing `கேட்டதுண்டோ?`; the Tamil archival layer is not normalised. *Veli* is retained as the historical land-measure term.

## Item 53 — Varna or Death?

<!-- scan 407 -->

In the green Tamil land
that lived with grace,
singing many a *parani*,
a few selfish tricksters,
without even a grain of honour,
may gather and devise
scheme after scheme,
trying somehow
to establish *varna*;
but at death’s very summit,
to guard your honour—
O martial Tamil,
fight!

## Source publication note

Published in **1945**.

## Translator note

`வருணம்` is retained as *varna* because the poem's political force depends on the caste-order term and its sound opposition to `மரணம்` / death.

## Item 54 — When Does Defeat Come?

<!-- scan 408 -->

Because a little mouse has come,
a mighty cat hides
beside the stove.
At the sight of a snake fleeing,
a mongoose stands trembling,
drenched in sweat.
If such enormous spectacles,
unseen anywhere in the world,
should arrive in rich-Tamil land,
then every lion-like Tamil bull—
long crowned with victory,
never weary of a fight—
will hide like a fox
frightened by a crab
and hand victories
to foreign okra-warriors!

## Source publication note

Published in **1945**.

## Translator note

The deliberately comic animal reversals and the source insult `வெண்டைக்காய் வீரர்` are kept literally enough to preserve the satire; “okra-warriors” is not an outside historical label.

## Item 55 — Still This Clamour?

<!-- scan 409 -->

Still this clamour?

On the banks of the Cauvery,
in the surge of the Pennai—
there is no history! No history!
Alas, Tamil Nadu;
they say the beginning is in the Ganges!
Who has seen that the Pandyas ruled?
Who carved the Cheras’ prosperity?
Has anyone told of Chola abundance?
The tale of the sandal’s rule alone
is made the beginning of history!
All who reject it, they say,
have sold their honour.
Students have come to reshape it!
Cadre-warriors will flash forward!
O slack-jawed ones—
still this clamour?

## Source publication note

Published in **1945**.

## Translator note

The canonical title `இன்றுமா கூச்சல்?` is preserved separately from the source-body closing form `இன்னுமா கூச்சல்?`; this Gate-3/Gate-4 title decision is not normalised in English metadata. Historical assertions remain source rhetoric.

## Item 56 — Green Parrot

<!-- scan 410 -->

Put a chilli fruit
on the head of a tender bottle-gourd,
set jasmine leaves as wings at its sides,
and place a screw-pine leaf behind as a tail—
green parrot!
A creation like you! A creation like you!
Round head! Red beak!
Little wings! Long tail!
Sugarcane-sweet parrot—
is my description wrong?
You refuse to bless me after hearing the portrait;
even hearing words of praise,
you will not praise me back.
I know the reason, little parrot!
Because I put you in a cage,
you writhe like a worm on a hook.
I shut you in prison and took delight;
now I understand—
prison is cruel, prison is cruel!
Forgive me, forgive me!
Golden-speaking doll, forgive me!
When my prison ends, I shall break yours;
through prison I have learned prison’s pain.
Little enslaved bird! Lovely darling!

<!-- scan 411 -->

I have gained experience—gained experience.
When I return, I shall give you a life of freedom!
Fly, fly, fly high!
Your bound feet will be freed; at once
fly upward as far as you can reach!
Beautiful-hued girl, fly everywhere!
I shut you in the cage, parrot!
Forgive me! I have understood
the cage’s pain. Because I have understood
the cage’s pain, you caged parrot—

<!-- scan 412 -->

I shall open it and set you free. But, parrot,
people who know the cage’s pain
have come here to rule.
They put us in cages to die!
Why, parrot? Why, parrot?
Give me an answer too, green parrot!
“You caged me,
so they caged you,” you will say—
well said, parrot;
but whom did you cage
that I caged you?

## Translator note

The poem deliberately turns a playful parrot portrait into a prison-and-political-rule allegory. Repetition around `சிறை` / prison and `கூண்டு` / cage is preserved rather than condensed.

## Item 57 — Fountain of Imagination

<!-- scan 413 -->

*(Written spontaneously by Kalaignar after seeing a beautiful fountain in an Orlando lake while touring the United States in 1971 at the invitation of the U.S. government.)*

In a rare lake in the city called Orlando
I saw a lovely firework-like fountain.
To the rhythm set by the coloured lights,
without missing a beat,
it dances—
what can I say of the grace
of that fountain-dancer’s performance!
One sight: as though milk-white ice
were rolling across a floor of green grass—
and while you are still looking,
*pat!* it changes into another sight!
As though this is how the Apollo here once launched,
arms of water climb the sky,
ripping open the dark.
In a goldsmith’s workshop,
yellow light spreading gold—
then, like an arrow loosed from a bow,

<!-- scan 414 -->

it shoots upward with a whirr.
When people of Stone-Age belief
cut down a living creature as sacrifice
before their gods,
red colour leaps and hisses
like the blood that spurts out.
On the Margazhi festival days in our towns,
women spread designs before their thresholds,
and in each street lay pumpkin flowers
upon the *kolam* they have drawn—
I saw a sight just as rich for the eyes.
Luckily—
I had the chance to enjoy it alone;
that is how I escaped!
Had a woman been beside me,
she would have counted every colour
in the fountain—and the very next day
asked for one sari of each kind!

## Translator note

The poem moves rapidly between dance, rocket, goldsmith, sacrificial-blood and Tamil *kolam* imagery. `அப்போலோ` is retained as Apollo in the source's U.S.-travel/space-flight context; the closing gendered sari joke is translated as source humour rather than modernised.

## Item 58 — O Sky, Pour Down!

<!-- scan 415 -->

*When the sky failed and drought spread across Tamil Nadu, Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi wrote this in* Murasoli *on 23 March 1975, pleading for rain.*

The forest-fire of drought—
put it out with the tears
you distil!
Where is your flashing smile?
Where is your thundering speech?
For so many long days,
without your rose-water bath,
my precious Tamil land
has yearned—understand that!
O sky, pour down,
to wipe away our people’s torment—
upon the martial Tamil people
who hold honour greater than all else.
Some at the Centre think
that by fastening stains upon us

<!-- scan 416 -->

and hurling
the knife of slander,
they can snatch away the crown—
pour down, O sky,
to sharpen their wits!
Hindus, Muslims,
and the Christians who live here—
our kin—are pleading:
“Like ancient Tamil’s three rains,
let it pour!”
Good people call you;
the ungrateful wish
you would show no mercy—
stretch out your water-hand
so their burning hearts may cool!
O sky, pour down—
that the Tamil world may live!

## Translator note

The poem joins drought imagery to contemporary political accusation. `மும்மாரி` is rendered as the classical “three rains” rather than replaced with a generic “abundant rain”; claims about the Centre remain source rhetoric.

## Item 59 — A Letter in Verse!

<!-- scan 417 -->

Sibling! I shall tell you one thing—
keep it in mind!
We are indebted for all time
to guard the country.
Those who stand in contradiction
have no work in this Kazhagam.
Those who lived by grabbing a place
will go somewhere else;
brothers who do not leave the track
will stand as one.
Whatever quarter of Indian soil
may send danger,
at once we shall raise
shoulders of valour
like the Vindhyas and Himalayas
and overcome it.
If harm comes to the country,
the DMK will spring up—
setting aside that very instant
its own resolution and demand,
never forgetting to beat its chest
to guard the honour of the soil.
This is the witness of our Kazhagam’s history;
I am not saying something new today.
Our duty, always, is service to the country.
Let us guard peace
so the people may live well;
let us make a way
for good principles to endure.

## Translator note

`கழகம்` is retained as Kazhagam where the poem speaks in movement-family language; `தி.மு.க.` is rendered as DMK. The national-duty claims are translated as the source's political self-description.

## Item 60 — Will He Realise? Who Knows?

<!-- scan 418 -->

Will he realise?
Who knows?
Sibling,
he abuses me to his heart’s content—
he abuses me to his heart’s content—
expecting that I,
taking a word heated in a furnace of fire,
will not fail to burn him back;
so he abuses me to his heart’s content!
To sit in an office
that shines like silk,
he tried many paths,
found one path—
and abuses me to his heart’s content!
“Just watch how I attack him!
Can Karunanidhi escape?
I shall obey your every word,
even scratch your feet!
I shall not dream of self-respect!
Show mercy and grant me a post!”
He clasps
the feet of those in the Garden;
that is why
he abuses me to his heart’s content,
whirling a tongue of fire!
To answer chaff,
do we need leopards from the thicket?
If I answer such people,
that very answer becomes a scheme
for them to gain a post!

<!-- scan 419 -->

That is why
he abuses me to his heart’s content;
he clasps their feet!
I know too that the “ten-headed ones”
stretch out a hand,
saying a post is not needed—
money-aid will do!
Let them go, let them survive,
let their business prosper;
let them abuse me for it—
I utter not one opposing word
and leave them be!
To attack an elephant is a tiger’s valour;
to attack a “white-nosed horse”
is shameful, is it not?
That is why I say: let some abuse me;
I utter not one opposing word
and leave them be!
Is it wisdom
to use a spear
to bring down an ant
that a finger can crush?
To keep what one has gained
from being snatched away,
and to leap and leap
for the sake of gaining a post,
he abuses me to his heart’s content,
and clasps their feet!

> “Beside a minister who plots ruin,
> there gather foes worth seventy crores.”

Though I failed then
to grasp the meaning of that Kural,
so that pain may not surround us again—
will he realise it today?
Who knows?

## Translator note

The poem's repeated attack/office-seeking refrain is structural and is not compressed. Quoted source expressions such as `பத்துத்தலையோர்` (“ten-headed ones”) and `வெண் மூக்குக் குதிரை` (“white-nosed horse”) remain source metaphors rather than being assigned an outside referent. The Tirukkural is translated for sense while remaining visibly a quotation.

## Item 61 — Let It Whirl as a Battle-Sword!

<!-- scan 420 -->

Treasure from the southern quarter,
that dances in honeyed flowers
and comes making the street fragrant—
gentle southern breeze!
Seven of my siblings,
who loved me across the breadth of the sky
until flesh and life would melt,
clasped a mass of flame
tight as though embracing you!
To guard the language,
in fire that spins and races
until the eyes scatter,
once eight bathed in flame;
today, declaring that Tamils at least hereafter shall rise,
seven of undying fame have leapt!
Koyiladi Brindavan, king of principle;
the resolute youth Manohar in Tiruchi;
tireless worker Arur Kittu;
lion-hearted Muthupandian;
Chennai Mary, one from the clan of mothers;
Kallavi Rajendran, never slack in duty;
and Mel Mayil Jaganathan,
whose eyelids would not flicker even at a charging spear—
why must these young trees
be scorched to death in blazing flame?

<!-- scan 421 -->

Wearing fire-blisters
all across their bodies by choice,
Namakkal’s brave young Ramasamy,
Sikkalur lion Ezhumalai, Ramayya,
Tiruppur tiger Thiruvenkatam, Muthusamy
are receiving treatment—
and proudly declare:
“Would lions fear
a pack of little foxes?”
Though this is not a method we can accept,
when they receive burning fire
as though it were a shower of snow,
we are defeated before them in sacrifice—
we bow,
we weep,
we rise;
let their valour whirl in our hands
as a battle-sword—
let the sharp blades of betrayal
break into dust, into dust!

## Translator note

This is a memorial/political poem about self-immolation and injuries in language agitation. The source itself says `ஏற்கும் முறையல்ல இதுவெனினும்`—“though this is not a method we can accept”—and that qualification is retained. Personal names and the source-visible rhetoric are not historically harmonised in the translation.

## Item 62 — Whose Names Have Still Not Appeared?

<!-- scan 422 -->

Whose names,
whose names have still not appeared?
Sibling,
only by receiving the funds
I ask you for as a right
have we steered the Kazhagam boat
across fierce storms!
Whenever funds were asked,
you were like generous Pari,
never saying no;
even when you had nothing,
you were like Kumanan of Kongu,
ready to say,
“My head is still here—take it!”
Beloved apple of my eye,
that is how you shone then
and how you shine today!
Perhaps Muthuvel and Anjugam
knew it beforehand
when they named me *Nidhi*—fund—
so Perarignar once said
before the Mutharignar,
and received eleven lakhs.
When I ran about and came seeking you,
you searched me out
and brought the fund!
Do you remember
that event whose green freshness
has never faded from the heart?

<!-- scan 423 -->

The Kazhagam treasury has not become pregnant—
what can be done?
Family planning
where it is not needed!
Some treasuries swell and rejoice,
not through lovers’ quarrel and union,
but through corruption!
Those are no infants—
Central Investigation doctors say
they are great abdominal tumours.
From that day to this,
to serve the rare aim
of taking only in measure
and living in prosperity,
the Kazhagam has remained
a treasury unstained!
The five and ten in your hand
at once become Kazhagam election funds
and place you before me.
The moment I see your name,
I bless you from my heart!
Nor do I fail to count
whose names have still not flashed
in *Murasoli*.
One word for tomorrow—
you know me!
If it came whirling
with the sweep of a sword,
would that be pleasant for you?
Even if I merely ask in sweet speech,
“What did this person give?”
would it not strike like thunder?
So today itself, sibling,
without making excuses,
send the Kazhagam fund at once!

<!-- scan 424 -->

Periyakulam must be crossed by swimming—
with your precious labour.
Give the Kazhagam fund!
When this Karunanidhi asks,
O jewel of the Kazhagam,
have you ever kept your fist closed? Hmm!
Before an eye can close and open,
heap it up—
like a hill!

## Translator note

The poem repeatedly plays on `நிதி` as “fund” and as the final element of Karunanidhi's name; English exposes that wordplay instead of inventing an equivalent. The treasury/pregnancy, family-planning and corruption-tumour imagery is intentionally comic and political. Named exempla and monetary claims remain source claims.

## Item 63 — A Drop of Honey!

<!-- scan 425 -->

Sibling,

> “They heed not hunger, sleep not with their eyes;
> for them, their task itself is the eye.”

That is an old song—
it will be engraved in your mind!
To see a new turning,
to tear away the disguise of the “holy,”
to make wicked deeds fall,
we have donned the garb of battle!
This election is one stage of it!
They shower money-bullets;
decorations leap and dance;
coloured lights steal the eye!
With black money they will cover even “the pond”!
But you, apple of my eye, do not worry—
take the Kazhagam flag in your hand!
Wear the Rising Sun upon your clothes!

<!-- scan 426 -->

Go street by street!
Go house by house gathering votes!

★

Between one another,
among yourselves,
let there not be even one drop of bitterness—
for that tiny drop
can become a great flood!

★

Why are you waiting?
Whom are you waiting for?
Even if someone comes,
will he not give only a drop of honey?
Think that it has not come,
and bend your body
into a bow!

★

In the tents of those
who have looted honey and milk
by the pot, variety upon variety,
heap upon heap,
you plead with your eyes, my dear!
Where is such a pot here?
There is only the “crown”
of being Anna’s younger brother!

★

To heal wounded Tamil Nadu,
to end the cruelty of those
arrogant in ruling the land,
to create a new condition—
O storm, rise!
Rise at once and act
like a tiger cub!

★

<!-- scan 427 -->

The days remaining are few;
fly like a weapon, my dear!
Is victory a commodity sold in a shop?
Without slackening your stride,
hurry around the constituency!
Shake off weariness! Show your strength!
With not even time
to wipe away your sweat,
O life of the Kazhagam
that has spread like a banyan—
fly like a spear
and seize victory!

## Translator note

The `★` divisions are source structure and remain explicit. `பணத்தோட்டா` is rendered as “money-bullets,” preserving the cartridge/bullet campaign image. “The pond” is kept in quotation marks because the Tamil itself marks `குளத்தையே` that way; no outside referent is supplied. The poem's honey/drop, bitterness/drop and pot imagery is intentionally recurrent and is not flattened.

## Item 64 — Let It Sprout as Seed and Put Forth Roots!

<!-- scan 428 -->

Let it sprout as seed
and put forth roots!
In the womb of Mother Paddy-field,
made pregnant by the farmer’s sweat,
she came as a daughter—
the maiden Grain-ear,
cherished by everyone;
on Pongal day she stands before us!
Like a woman in the bridal chamber,
her head bowed,
those called Tamils rejoice;
they leap crying *thai, thai!*
With light’s arrival darkness disappears;
chisel-carved sculptures glitter!
On Pongal day,
so that new joy may appear everywhere,
the Sun has risen!
To approach everything with civility
and overcome it,
the Rising Sun has climbed high today!
Let us change the condition
that asks,
“Has the life of the Tamil people
become only a story?”—
let our feeling of ethnic self-respect
sprout as seed
and put forth banyan roots!

## Translator note

`விழுதுகள்` evokes the prop/aerial roots of a spreading banyan, not merely generic roots; the closing rendering makes that image explicit. `உதயசூரியன்` retains both the natural Rising Sun and its movement/political resonance. The contents title's `விட்டோம்` is not merged with the canonical imperative `விடட்டும்!`.

## Item 65 — He Calls the Sun an Ice Cube!

<!-- scan 429 -->

He calls the Sun an ice cube!
Sibling,
I am supposedly weeping
that the government may be lost!
*Ananda Vikatan* laughs!
Ah—what satisfaction for it!
“Even if the sceptre is snatched away,
I have not the slightest worry—
no one can snatch
the writing-pen from my hand!”
I thundered at the Muthamil Peravai.
To elder-scholar Vikatan,
it seems that sounded like *Mukhari*,
a lamenting raga!

★ ★ ★

“So long as the writing-pen
remains in his hand,
Karunanidhi will not grieve
even if the sceptre goes”—

<!-- scan 430 -->

that was the line
poet Kannadasan roared many times;
at an award ceremony for artists,
I merely said it again
with pride in stately Tamil.

★ ★ ★

They say I spoke in a trembling voice—
as though I declared myself ready
to take blows like a *thavil*;
as though, uttering an analogy
in the tone of one being punished,
I went away with my head hanging—
this week the high-quality Vikatan,
counting that worthy,
has offered a criticism!
Even in its editorial it abuses me
and lays down a grammar
for supreme courage!

★ ★ ★

Because the Prime Minister said
the days when Chennai ordered Delhi
were gone,
I did not, it seems,
snarl and pounce!
Vikatan prods me!
Only if I had in fact issued such orders
would there be cause to rage:
“How can this be said?”

★ ★ ★

<!-- scan 431 -->

For some reason Vikatan has forgotten—
during the Emergency,
Delhi sent an emissary
to my own house:
if I abandoned opposition to the Emergency,
no hardships would approach,
no defamation case would be filed,
and the government’s life
would be extended by one year—
thus they tried to kindle desire!
Had I yielded,
for a year in MISA prisons
five hundred of my precious dear ones
would not have suffered!
The government too would have continued—
but one thing:
honour would have gone!

★ ★ ★

Even if crores of enemies cry,
“Come, join us,”
we are those who raise our shoulders
and say, “I shall not touch it”—
we remember not only
Paavendar’s song that showed this,
but his path as well!
Vikatan mocks us!
The contradiction
between imitation and truth—

<!-- scan 432 -->

if that gentleman who laughs in delight
would only think,
he would understand!

★ ★ ★

In the election of ’67,
Anna, Rajaji, Quaid-e-Millath,
Ma. Po. Si., and
P. Ramamurthi who stood in our alliance—
was it not Vikatan
that drew them all seated on a donkey
in a cartoon
and laughed?
The fragrance of camphor,
the firmness of the Kazhagam—
he cannot so easily understand them!
That is why
he calls the Sun an ice cube!

## Translator note

The poem is a direct rebuttal to *Ananda Vikatan*. `செங்கோல் / எழுதுகோல்` is preserved as sceptre/pen; `முகாரி` as the Mukhari raga; and the *thavil* image remains literal. The Emergency/MISA and 1967-cartoon passages are translated as Karunanidhi's source recollections, not independently fact-checked here. The closing camphor line carries the familiar donkey/camphor proverb resonance after the source's donkey-cartoon reference. The contents witness shifts the title to second-person `என்கிறாய்!`; it remains separate from canonical respectful third-person `என்கின்றார்!`.

## Item 66 — Don't Stop Your Stride!

<!-- scan 433 -->

Sibling,

dogs will go on barking.
Do not stop your stride for that!
There is no need to tremble, shrink,
or move aside to the edge.
We are not going to loot, after all;
we are going to stop the looting!
To spread the principle—to reach the goal—
to climb the hill of victory and plant the flag!

The seeds of discord scattered by enemies
will grow into thorns.
Pure-hearted one, they will pierce your feet;
so that they do not wound the feet
of those who follow you,
lift them out,
break their points,
and fling them far away!

Do not panic at the wild torrent
that cuts across the path.
Reckon how your grandfather, great-grandfather,
mother and father crossed it;
build a bridge,
or show the strength of your shoulders and swim—
reach the bank without letting the journey
be stopped halfway!

One pack of howling foxes will go to the right;
another will go to the left.
What if they go right? What if they go left?
Only see that they do not leap upon you
and tear at you!

Beasts and great pythons will writhe in the bushes—
only to clear away such dangers
do you need a weapon in your hand!
To destroy human enmity
that heaps itself like chaff,

<!-- scan 434 -->

carry the sword called civility
and the shield called love!

When, with resolve, you climb
peaks covered in frozen snow,
footwear without inner chafing will serve you well—
it will never, ever slip!

Your journey is to remove
the filthy thoughts and burdens of domination
piled up for thousands upon thousands of years.
Do not forget that purpose
and leave the Tamil a refugee!

Enemies will appear as friends:
they will sing a prelude in your praise,
show you office,
even bear your palanquin.
Guard yourself from falling into that pit,
and guard the people too.
Beat the drum and sing, “Long live Ayya Periyar!”
Sound the conch and dance, “Long live Arignar Anna!”
Then every hard journey can continue without fatigue.
Even the tiny ant scorns those who live without labour!

Know your duty;
flower in dignity and win.
Let the Kazhagam journey go on
without discipline breaking down!
Vow that there is not even a moment's rest.
Sibling, rain upon Tamil Nadu
so that prosperity may spread!

If you must, throw a little food now and then
to the rats and termites along your path;
but take care not to feed certain newspapers!

The Kazhagam is a crop of a hundred thousand seasons.
Watch out for the tricksters
who offer honey mixed with poison
to uproot it completely!
For the principle joined to our flesh and life,
we shall conduct a procession of victory!

## Translator note

The poem sustains one political-journey metaphor from barking dogs and thorns through torrents, snow peaks and treacherous hospitality. `உட்பூசலற்ற காலணி` carries both the physical sense of footwear without inner chafing and the movement sense of freedom from internal feud; the English keeps the footwear image and leaves the political resonance audible. `கடமை / கண்ணியம் / கட்டுப்பாடு` remains the movement triad “duty / dignity / discipline.”

## Item 67 — A Backwater Full of Ignorant Folk!

<!-- scan 435 -->

A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!
If the one or two decent people there grow alarmed
and rise to speak against them,
they will drag them down
and throw them into the burning fire!
Are they not a gang grown fat and roaming free?
A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!

★

Knowing that little dogs will bark,
they throw scraps of bread,
shut their mouths and trap them in the net!
A certain “Maya” in the middle of the hall
speaks meltingly—and hides the truth!
A trick learnt from the womb itself!
A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!

There is no useful plan to make the village prosper;
Maya's work is to cultivate corruption
so that she alone may live in splendour!
Say, “This will not help,”
and they will flay your skin!

<!-- scan 436 -->

Every day, a brand-new law—
and still, because sleep has not cleared,
they beat the drum of swagger!
A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!

★

They say chaste Kannagi once blazed in fury
and burned Madurai; so,
“Look, I too will burn something in return!”
and Panchali Devi burns a newspaper.
Even after propping a spine into their backs,
the fool-crowd, it seems, lies hunchbacked!
A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!

★

“I shall place this whole village on a leaf
and finish eating it, cooked and uncooked alike—
this is my decision!”
Even when that Maya says so,
the yes-men are ready still
to support her and fall at her feet!
A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!

Declaring contradiction itself to be scripture,
they say the drumstick tree is fit for building a house!
They say that if a wall of wax candles is raised as a fort,
fire itself will flee in fear and hide!
“I shall commit a thousand sins,” she says—
“God is on my side!” No, not even that:

<!-- scan 437 -->

“I myself am that God!” she says!
A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!

★

When everyone stands like hand-raising dolls,
and lifts left hand and right hand together,
would wicked Maya's delight not boil over?
Before it collapses like a long wall that cannot stand,
even the unlearned must learn—
so that wickedness may swiftly be destroyed
on the path of righteousness!
A backwater full of ignorant folk—
and on its panchayat board, most of them are the same!

## Translator note

`மாயை` is kept as the personified “Maya,” allowing both the ordinary sense of illusion/deception and the poem's political personification to remain active. The Panchali/Kannagi, wax-wall, drumstick-tree and self-deification passages are source satire and are translated as such rather than rationalised or fact-checked.

## Item 68 — Tamil Nadu Is Being Looted

<!-- scan 438 -->

Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother,
by the torment of corruption-bandicoots!
Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother—
by the cruelty of a ruinous government;
in life, even daytime turns dark with grief!
By the torment of corruption-bandicoots,
Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother!

Did she not climb to power
being called the Rani of Jhansi?
Yet she bought the government's TANSI land
and broke the law!
She dances at the summit of arrogance—
and now,
to wipe out that conceit,
the people are speaking!
By the torment of corruption-bandicoots,
Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother!

They say there is corruption of six crores in Omni buses;
they sell the government beer factory
and pocket two crores.
Brother, if we do not wake even now,
they will turn our country into a cremation ground
and stretch prison bars across it!
By the torment of corruption-bandicoots,
Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother!

<!-- scan 439 -->

With a rowdy force they run an entire realm;
newspapers that criticise them
are burned in the street.
Government property is sold without tender;
many bribes are taken underground!
By the torment of corruption-bandicoots,
Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother!

For revenge alone, they say, “Supreme-Court appeal,”
and run all the long way to Delhi;
but then they say a High-Court verdict is enough
and hand a medical college over to private hands!
By the torment of corruption-bandicoots,
Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother!

TAMIN, SPIC-share corruption, they say;
rice-mill corruption,
teacher-training-school corruption, they say;
lavish-tour, wasteful-expense corruption, they say—
can all of it even be told today!
By the torment of corruption-bandicoots,
Tamil Nadu is being looted, mother!

## Translator note

`பெருச்சாளி` is a large rat/bandicoot and functions here as the poem's repeated metaphor for corrupt operators, so the refrain keeps “corruption-bandicoots.” TANSI, Omni bus, the government beer factory, TAMIN/SPIC, courts and the other allegations are translated strictly as claims made by the poem; Phase 4 does not independently adjudicate them.

## Item 69 — What Kind of Country Is This?

<!-- scan 440 -->

What kind of country is this?
What kind of country is this—
a fraud that gives life only to cheats!
Affection and attachment here are like water and oil!
Ah, friendship that sheds false tears
and then cuts your throat!

Every heap of rubbish imagines itself a sharp sword;
with drums and war-drums pounding,
it dances in swagger!
When those who came to rule strike through plunder,
the crowd of servants beating time grows fat!
To stretch out a hand and receive money and office,
they stand baring every tooth in their mouths!
With bags full of bribe-money,
lakhs upon lakhs poured out,
if told, “Put your head at my feet,”
they will place it there without hesitation!

What kind of country is this—
a fraud that gives life only to cheats!
There is no boundary to the sky in this world;
there is no boundary to poverty in India!
Ten scandals come and go in a year;
the Harshad Mehta scandal is a badge of disgrace!

“We stand firm in policy,” they say;
“We stand for conduct,” they say;
“The flag and symbol belong to us,” they say!
Then they beat their chests for wages
in return for the money they were given;
until the giving hand closes,
they are scorpions that keep jumping!

<!-- scan 441 -->

The dominant classes have come to power;
harm may come in many forms,
yet the press applauds!
What kind of country is this—
a fraud that gives life only to cheats!

“I alone am the heir to Periyar and Anna,” one says.
If a fox asks us to believe it is a lion,
people still say, “Long live the name,
for this one rules the land!”
“We prospered because of that person;
we shall be subjects to none,” they say.

Once, men would not live
if even a kunrimani-weight of honour were shed;
today, even after a mountain of honour is lost,
men as great as mountains bend
without shrinking at all!
Has the mountain sunk and the ridge risen—
is that story now a serial without end?

The three-letter word called gratitude
is turned into filth and eaten;
the land has become a sewer
where pigs too wallow!
What kind of country is this—
a fraud that gives life only to cheats!

A child for whom a mother fasted first,
carried three hundred days in her womb,
now hurls a spear to make that belly collapse!
The DMK is the beautiful moon
that flowered after driving away dusk;
because it lies beyond the monkey's reach,
the monkey howls until the street laughs!

To resist Hindi,
to guard our people and language,
this fortress stands;
there is a plan to destroy it!
“I am always first for the feast,
you are always first for the battle”—
the clinking small coins run about
thinking and saying just that!
Like Gandharis who ask Kunti for a child
and stab their own bellies—such is their desire!

<!-- scan 442 -->

What kind of country is this—
a fraud that gives life only to cheats!

If it is the leader's birthday,
by all means let a garland be placed!
But at every shrine,
ministers carrying kavadi—
is that the work of rationalism?
They shave their heads;
they leap crying “thaththom”;
they roll wearing leaves;
women too walk through fire!

All the rays of knowledge
that the Self-Respect Movement
and the cry of dignity for our people
caused to grow in Tamil Nadu—
have they all stumbled and fallen
because the servant-mind climbed into the head?
When will a new awakening rise in Tamil soil?

Do not hesitate—set out, Tamil!
Slap your broad shoulders
and rise!

## Translator note

The poem's repeated `எத்தன்` points to a cheat/trickster, hence the refrain “a fraud that gives life only to cheats.” Its political, corruption, ritual and mythological accusations are preserved as source rhetoric. The Kunti/Gandhari passage is intentionally satirical and is not harmonised with outside epic versions.

## Item 70 — Come, Let Us Tear Off the Mask!

<!-- scan 443 -->

They grow matted hair—
open shops of devotion;
with a powerful following
they make even governments
that rule by force bow down!
Women with lightning waists too,
without obstruction,
fall into their illusion!

In olden days they tied up their hair,
built huts and lived as sages.
Even those men, who had not wholly renounced,
kept attachment to women
and fathered children!

Buddha of the Bodhi tree,
Mahavira,
Adi Shankara,
Ramanuja—
they preached what were called lofty principles.
Yet those who claim to have come in their path
have slipped into violence,
and some have planted poisonous seed
in the mire of varnashrama.
Because such people have multiplied,
ethical discipline and ascetic discipline
have withered like a scorched sapling;
social progress has spilled onto the ground
like milk running away!

Whoever says, “Come, I shall show you God,”
and calls you to the bed,
is declared the choicest ascetic,
a sadhu, a swami!
Whoever looses Cupid's arrows
claims compassion;
he says, “I shall show you heaven,”
and wastes women themselves!

As though that were a chapter of spirituality,
with no objection raised,
a crowd of women believes his desire to be worship.
Is being deceived a chapter—or an outrage?

How many mahatmas, how many Sai Babas,
how many miracles have they shown!
Though people know that a magician
can reproduce all of it in a moment on a stage,
what a cruelty that they still become
stubborn fools!

Between God and human beings,
what broker is he?
If one has received divine grace,
why dakshina, offerings and daily worship?
If he has renounced everything,
why rosewater to bathe every limb of his body?
Why a maiden?
Is the sign of having attained nakedness
that he delights in photographing women naked?

In place of staff and water-pot,
there gleam a sword and liquor;

<!-- scan 444 -->

there is a body fed and swollen,
and a guru taking pleasure
while many women massage him.
Seeing all this again and again,
why does the crowd still remain
without clarity of reason?

One ashram for printing counterfeit notes;
another ashram for digging pits
and burying gold.
These small-minded thieves are believed,
worshipped and bowed to—
and correcting the innocent women
who trust them becomes a mighty labour!
When a town is deceived and plundered,
what difference does male or female make?

<!-- scan 445 -->

Those who set fire to a thatched house
are placed upon a chariot
and given a festival!
A shawl is wrapped around a rat snake,
while rational thought is beaten to death!
For promotion,
for piling up mountains of money,
many officials wash the golden feet of swamis!

To end the grief of childlessness,
a woman circles the sacred fig tree
and then goes to this mendicant monastery;
he bestows his “hand-grace,”
covers her body with his own,
turns himself into a husband,
and grants the boon the woman sought!

Murder, robbery, abduction,
sexual assault and outrage—
though such reports arrive in waves every day,
governments exist to embrace and protect the ashram
so that not even one hair falls
from its matted locks!
If ministers themselves become servants of swamis
and sit enthroned without fearing the town's smouldering,
then, to prove that there is no smoke without fire,
fire will spread—spread in every direction!
The deeds of thieves living
behind a screen of divinity will burn away!

Holding a festival for the Revolutionary Poet,
researching new books upon books,
and conducting stirring programmes—
that alone is not enough, sir!
Did not that poet ask with feeling:

> “Is it right to call the idle deceivers
> the very highest saints?”

With that rebellious heart,
let us beat the drum and set out,
like a pride of Tamil lions rising!
Come—
let us tear off the masks
of fraudulent ascetics!

## Translator note

The poem is an explicit rationalist polemic against fraudulent religious authority. Names, caste-order terminology, sexual exploitation and criminal allegations remain source claims and are not softened or independently fact-checked. The closing quoted challenge is retained as a quotation attributed by the poem to the Revolutionary Poet.

## Item 71 — What Is the Answer? Tell Us!

<!-- scan 446 -->

Among all lands, a good land—
our flourishing Tamil land!
But scalding water was poured at its roots,
and ancient culture burned and collapsed!

Within that town stands a poisonous tree—
like those who run to eat its fruit,
thinking it has ripened for food,
and lose their lives,
the people cast their votes
and brought ruin upon themselves!

It is easy for some
to put on the costume of the virtuous;
it is hard to recognise it
and remain awake!

The rule of those who once said
“ten-and-a-half-touch pure”
now bears witness by sweeping away
all the wealth there is!

“Everything to private hands”—
they pour the country away
and go to welcome ruin without restraint!
Hereafter there is no life here for the poor;
they have even blown the conch
to clear the way for outsiders!

Young people,
rising with a shiver like leopards,
like lion cubs!
Brothers who turn into
an erupting volcano, a storm,
and make history!

<!-- scan 447 -->

Shall we say, “Enough of endurance,”
and rise in a surge?
Or shall we become donkeys
whose sense has died,
and carry still more loads?

## Translator note

`பத்தரை மாற்று` is the same old gold-purity register encountered earlier in the anthology; “ten-and-a-half-touch pure” keeps the source metaphor rather than replacing it with a generic claim of honesty. The privatisation and electoral accusations are translated as source claims.

## Item 72 — Kā, Kā, Kā!

<!-- scan 448 -->

Kā, kā, kā—
brother, did you hear that song
in *Parasakthi*?
Udumalaiyar's song,
a lesson even for rationalists!
For the crowd that beats its chest for wages,
changes track for gain—
that song is a blast!

Kā, kā, kā—
that is the old song, brother;
now hear the new song!

Kā, kā, kā—
now they're all catching *kākkā*;
is it such a terrible fault
to point it out?
Put up a “cut-out”—*kākkā!*
Cut away traditions and throw them out—*kākkā!*
Print thunderous advertisements in the papers,
as though trampling people underfoot—*kākkā!*
Occupy the Speaker's chair—*kākkā!*
In the middle of the Assembly,
fall at this person's feet or that person's—*kākkā!*
Shout in the House,
“My caste is a higher caste!”—*kākkā!*
For the Mahamaham bath too—*kākkā!*
Even if a hundred people died there—*kākkā!*

<!-- scan 449 -->

Even if ministers eat rice off the ground—*kākkā!*
Even if white fly-whisks wave
and neem-leaf garments are tied on—*kākkā!*
Do *anga-pradakshinam*—*kākkā!*
Even if the price of rice rises unjustly—*kākkā!*
For bus-fare hikes—*kākkā!*
Even if every price reaches the summit—*kākkā!*
For plunder—*kākkā!*
For killings—*kākkā!*
For brutal highway robbery—*kākkā!*
Even if crores upon crores are piled up—*kākkā!*

Brother, how can I tell you the cruelty?
Now *kākkā* even sets fire to policy itself!
Again and again invoking the names
of Periyar and Anna,
*kākkā* lifts up those who stage deceit!

Now the stork pecks even at the fish
called “reservation”;
and for this too,
*kākkā* shouts “Jai!”
and hails “J”!

## Translator note

The poem depends on layered Tamil sound-play. `கா` is the crow's caw; `காக்கா` evokes “crow” while `காக்கா பிடித்தல்` is colloquial sycophancy/currying favour, and the repeated refrain also sounds like “protect.” The English therefore retains *kā / kākkā* rather than pretending the wordplay has a single equivalent. The closing `ஜே / ஜெ` is preserved as “Jai / J.”

## Item 73 — Let Us Rise in the East Like the Sun!

<!-- scan 450 -->

Let us rise in the east
like the sun!

Each day, when they turn the pages of the newspapers,
Tamil people writhe at news that stings like scorpions.
A rule that destroys the lives of the poor like a sword—
and what a sight: those who carry the papers
show not even one drop of shame!

Every day comes news of theft in temples;
even the idols of the Lord are stolen—
a red fire in devotees' hearts.
Cholera too, they say, spreads
as food for the hunger of “Death”;
in police stations, rape and killings
continue day after day!

Those who are in government
certainly need protection;
we are not so ignorant as to say they do not.
But for that,
must thousands upon thousands of police
line up and stand like motionless tall trees,
doing penance along every road?

The people of Kumari groan
under the damage of floods;
without even a mustard-seed of concern in the heart,
a flower-strewn path is laid
for the Chief Minister's car.
They certainly know how to curry favour—
but will the country prosper?

<!-- scan 451 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

Full-page advertisements
for schemes that never happen;
a proclamation:
“I bear the Dravidian lamp!”
Those who lie Nandi-like
for money, office and concessions say,
“Good, good—you alone are the Mother of Dravidianism!”
What shame!

Bus fares make eyes bulge;
the quantity of ration rice shrinks sharply;
the cruelty of prices rises into a giant form;
Tamil people breathe out fire in long sighs!
No—people turned to embers!

With fertiliser prices rising,
and diesel, petrol and cooking-gas prices rising too,
the eyes of farmers burn with heat;
so do the eyes of those who labour beside them,
and those of traders!

Professional tax, land tax, crop tax—
taxes in pile after pile.
Seeing the acts of rulers who bring such distress,
people have risen today saying,
“Even patience has a limit!”

Bribery and corruption—crores upon crores—
spread their beds, sing and dance in delight;
without even a little shame,
they sit in the Fort and scold others!

To watch and endure all this—
is this Tamil Nadu,
or a burial ground, a cremation ground?
So, vowing that we shall endure no longer,
all our siblings,
so that the country may live
and decline may end,
will join shoulders
with the ranks of our allied parties
and set out!

<!-- scan 452 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

To teach a lesson to the deceivers
who abandoned in midstream
the people who trusted them and voted,
first comes the November protest—
a struggle on the path of nonviolence!
The next stage,
as decided by all parties:
the arrow we launch will be a total shutdown!
We can lie down no longer.
As a people's uprising,
let us rise in the east like the sun!

## Translator note

The poem moves from a newspaper catalogue of public grievances into explicit opposition mobilisation. Temple theft, police-station violence, flood response, prices, taxation and corruption are retained as the poem states them. `காக்காய் பிடிக்கின்றார்` is rendered “curry favour,” while the source's image of supporters lying “Nandi-like” for money, office and concessions is kept rather than smoothed away.

## Item 74 — Is This Diversion Justified?

<!-- scan 453 -->

Rolling the earth itself up like a mat,
the wicked Hiranyaksha hid inside the sea!
The gods were startled
and stood praying to Thirumal.
Thirumal took the form of a boar
and entered the surging sea!
He brought back the globe of the world
after destroying the asura!

In joy, Bhuma Devi thanked
the grunting boar
and said with feeling,
“I have fallen in love with you.”
The Lord in boar-form agreed at once.
What happened next...?

> “Like one flood joined with another flood,
> the two became veena and music.”

To the God of gods, Thirumal,
and to his divine consort Bhuma Devi,
was born an asura with a warrior nature!
His naming, they say,
was Narakasura.

The man and woman belong to the clan of gods—
yet the son born to them belongs to the asura clan!
One feels like asking, “How can this be?”
But once we begin asking questions like this,
must we not also ask
who could ever roll this earth itself up like a mat?

When the world was rolled up like a mat,
would not the seven seas too
have been inside this very earth?

<!-- scan 454 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

The demon who lifted the earth with all seven seas—
in which sea did he hide that earth?
We say, “The one who taught letters is God.”
If raising the questions “Why?” and “How?”
is itself opposition to God,
can one who does not know how to explain a doubt
remain a great teacher?

If the struggle between asuras and devas
was the Aryan-Dravidian struggle—
not only Ayya Periyar and Arignar Anna,
but also, the poem says,
the Light of Asia, Nehru, wrote so—
do scholars reject it?
If they do,
would that not mean rejecting old history itself?

The research book written
by the famed historian Srinivasa Iyengar
again and again says
that the Deva-Asura war of those days
was an Aryan-Dravidian struggle.
Is it just to divert that meaning elsewhere?
Then—can the Diwali purana be accepted?

## Translator note

The poem first retells the Hiranyaksha/Varaha/Bhuma/Narakasura story and then turns its internal logic into a rationalist challenge. Its Aryan-Dravidian historical interpretation and the attributions to Periyar, Anna, Nehru and Srinivasa Iyengar are translated as source claims; Phase 4 does not independently verify them.

## Item 75 — It Is Over—a Comedy Drama!

<!-- scan 455 -->

It is over, mother—
a comedy drama!

A thousand thunderous salutes roar and roar—ah!
Processions march to the beat of drums, march and march.
Welcome arches, like hunchbacks with broken backs,
along every road of the city
bow and offer praise, praise and praise.
Flags and festoons hide the clouds above,
swaying gently as though holding up a parasol.
Nine elephants stand holding garlands, stand and stand;
at the mahout's command
they place them around the neck, place them and place them.
The new hall gathers still more splendour, splendour upon splendour;
a flood of light like the moon in the sky
spreads everywhere, spreads and spreads.

The Queen reads a speech prepared for young people,
reads and reads—
but the crowd gathered there
falls about laughing, laughing and laughing!

> “Young people! Spears of the younger generation!
> This very day, take your vow and set out!
> Enjoy the springtime called life—
> and dedicate at least a little of your youth
> to service of the country!”

As soon as this passage is heard,
the hall shakes and shakes;
the great crowd bursts into loud laughter
and rises, rises and rises!

<!-- scan 456 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

The startled Queen asks what has happened,
asks and asks.
Someone there says,
“Madam, the speech you read is wrong,”
says it and says it.
The Queen flares up:
“Why? How?”—question after question.
The man trembles like a startled deer,
trembles and trembles:

“I am sorry, Mother!
Your speech was prepared
to offer greetings to the young.
But those summoned to this durbar
and gathered here
are people advanced in age,
with hair gone grey!
Looking at them and addressing them as
‘Young bulls!
Indrajits!’—
that is why mockery and ridicule have overflowed.
Who could do anything but laugh, Mother?”

The dignitary who gave that answer slips away,
slips away and slips away.
Poor lady—the enraged grande dame
embraces sorrow, sorrow upon sorrow.

It is over, mother—
a comedy drama!

## Translator note

The source deliberately repeats verbs such as “march,” “read,” “laugh,” “ask” and “tremble” to create a stage-farce rhythm; the English preserves that repetition rather than compressing it. The poem calls the central figure simply the “Queen,” so no outside identity is inserted.

## Item 76 — There Are Some Countries!

<!-- scan 457 -->

There are some countries!
....
From village rule to national rule,
the child called democracy
is never even “born”!
While it still writhes in the womb
like a worm,
they abort it and rejoice!
If, by some mistake, it escapes,
is born and begins to crawl,
they cut off its head
and make “soup”!

Let the dignity of people's rule become dust!
Become dust! Die away!
Or else become barren forever—
for that, they stand and pray
for divine grace!

There, a change of government
happens in half a second!
On the ruler's chest,
bullets become garlands—
ornaments and necklaces!
On the next man's chest,
a heavy shield is pressed;
if he too lies down,
who knows whether he will rise!

Guns themselves
serve as the pillars
that hold up the tents of rulers—

<!-- scan 458 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

they do the work;
they “finish the job”!
Coronation ceremonies
are conducted at the front
by drawn swords themselves!
A royal anointing means
bathing the ceremonial halls
with the red water
brought in pots of blood!

There are some countries!
There,
the child called democracy
is never “born”!

One who rides an elephant beneath a royal parasol—
his very intestines are pulled out,
made into a garland,
and beautifully placed
upon the next sacrificial goat!
Those who raised him up and praised him
will take even his bones!
“This is our beautiful sceptre,” they say!
The one who holds that sceptre—
when will he burn
like a stack of straw?
Who can know?

If it is monarchy,
perhaps its meaning is:
“End that rule!”—
and finish it with a cruel club!

<!-- scan 459 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

Ended and ended,
yet never ending—
there are some countries
that remain monarchies!
There,
the child called democracy
is never “born.”

The dalliance and pleasure of dictatorship!
On the pleasure-bed of autocracy,
its exclusive entertainments—
in the middle of that revel,
if anyone merely says “mm,”
it means prison!
If anyone asks “why,”
it means exile!

Dictatorship is a banana tree—
its own shoot will bring it down!
I do not know why:
the banyan tree of democracy
never sends down roots in that soil!
Why? I do not know.
They have cut the trunk itself
and pulled out the taproot!

On the flanks of India,
two countries sprouted!
There,
only in fields of blood
do crops of government grow!
“Mujibur's life is ended”—
and blood ran from all our eyes!
Yet one day dawned:
Bengal's earth

<!-- scan 460 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

flowered! Flowered!
The world rejoiced!
They hailed him as Bangabandhu—
and then rolled the head
of that golden *bandhu*
across the ground like a ball,
and played in delight!

Now here is another ball game
in the arena of Bangladesh!
The skull-ball of Ziaur Rahiman,
rolling past the “goalkeeper,”
was caught in a net of conspiracy,
struck the iron post
and broke apart!

Dictatorship—
it is a banana tree;
its own shoot will bring it down!

A little breeze of democracy
blew in Bangladesh.
Even that is being blocked,
and the work of spreading
a jewel-red carpet
for a military durbar
moves swiftly forward!

There are some countries!
There,
the child called democracy
is never “born”!
If, by some mistake, it is born and begins to crawl,
they cut off its head
and make “soup”!

This, today,
is the painful news
Bengal gives us!

## Translator note

The source's central metaphor is the unborn/killed “child of democracy,” set against coups and blood-soaked coronations. `வங்க பந்து` activates a Tamil `பந்து` wordplay between *bandhu* (kin/friend, in Bangabandhu) and “ball”; the English retains *bandhu* and explicitly carries the ball image. The source-visible name `ஜியாவுர் ரகிமான்` is rendered conservatively as “Ziaur Rahiman” rather than silently normalised to an outside spelling.

## Item 77 — You Bless Your Footwear

<!-- scan 461 -->

Today is your servant of love's
fifty-seventh birthday!

Scholars say that looking back
at the path already walked,
and not straying from the path now being walked,
is the gaze of a lion.
This humble one is no lion;
yet, keeping the road already travelled in mind,
I shall continue my service to the Tamil people!

Koḷili is the town that sowed me—
sweet Tamil also calls it Thirukkuvalai!
Raised there like a seedling,
I learnt the breeze-like Tamil of Thiruvarur!
At the school of Erode,
I received education in the dignity of our people;
at Kanchi, with knowledge as charioteer,
an education in ideals!

To oppose Hindi imposition forever,
in the Tamil army that first set out,
I saw the youth wing even then
and became inseparably joined
to the movement's course!

From that day until today:
Ayya Periyar's embrace!
And, enfolding me,

<!-- scan 462 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

the moist tenderness of Anna's heart!
Beloved sibling,
what thought do I have
other than you?

Even when I lay upon the railway track,
even when solitary prison roasted me,
the tears shed by the Anna who saw me
were rosewater sprinkled
to wash away my weariness!

When, after I had crossed twenty years of age,
they tormented me in Pondy,
the medicine Father Periyar rubbed on me
was an ambrosial feast
that cooled my mind!

My mother Anjugam
and Muthuvel,
when they gave birth to me,
waited thinking:
“He will sing music
and make us happy!”
But this one
was caught fast
in the midst of a politics
that sings abuse!

An ideal is not jasmine
that one can simply reach and pluck!
Nor is it a mango or coconut
to climb and pluck!
It is swimming against the current!
Planting a flag upon a volcano!

Government that appears along the way
is only shade offered by nature
so that the fatigue arising on the journey
may pass.
The journey toward the ideal
must continue!

<!-- scan 463 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

Birds of a pond whose good water has dried
may act like kith and kin,
then seek the office to which they had clung
and sell themselves.
Even so,
when a crowd that vows,
“We shall always be arrows in the bow;
we shall live as spear-points,”
keeps its word with firmness
and flashes like letters carved in stone,
why should I worry?
The Kazhagam itself is our east!

They say a horse threw its rider down
and then dug a pit for him.
Here, those who made us the horse
and rode upon us
have come to dig a pit for us!
The story of kicking away
the ladder one climbed!
The story of killing gratitude!

All these torments
that have become stories—
apple of my eye, sibling,
I endured them
by thinking of you!

You bless me!
I bow to you!
What you bless is not a crown!
Whether a crown exists or not,
you are the sibling
whose nature is to bless.

For you,
you bless this footwear
that keeps labouring!

<!-- scan 464 -->

⟦continuation from previous page⟧

For this alone
you polish it!
You are the poor, humble sibling!
Even when this footwear wears thin,
you will not say,
“Stop now and rest!”

On this birthday, sibling,
I give you my pledge:
never leaving you,
I shall labour without rest!

*(Written by Kalaignar as his message for his 58th birthday.)*

## Translator note

The source body opens by calling the day the speaker's `57`th birthday, while the source's closing parenthetical identifies the text as Kalaignar's message for his `58`th birthday; both witnesses are preserved exactly rather than reconciled away. `காலணி` is deliberately retained as “footwear”: the closing conceit rejects the crown and casts the political worker as footwear worn down in service to the sibling. Place-names and movement-history references follow the final-cleared Tamil witness without importing outside biographical wording.
